HU XUEYAN
By Gao Yang

The Red-Topped Merchant

CHAPTER ONE

“Your Excellency,” said the bodyguard, saluting Zeng Guofan, who was enjoying his customary game of go after dinner, “a dispatch officer from Zhejiang requests an audience. Will Your Excellency receive him?” Zeng was in the middle of a ko fight. The survival of his entire southeastern quarter of the board depended on it; he could not afford to abandon the struggle. Yet in the end he put down his stones and rose. “Of course I must see him. Tell him to come in.”

The dispatch officer wore travelling clothes that were all but in rags. It was late in the ninth month, long past the time for changing to a winter hat, yet he still wore a summer one. Its bright blue finial showed that he was a military officer of the third rank. “You Tianyong, assistant brigade commander of the Zhejiang governor’s own troops, pays his respects to Your Excellency.” He hurried forward a couple of paces, knelt and kowtowed. A great hole in the back of his coat exposed a patch of yellow-brown skin. “Get up, get up!” His face looked as though it had never been washed, and Zeng could scarcely bear the sight. “Take Commander You somewhere to rest,” he told the bodyguard. “Give him a meal, then ask him back to speak with me.” “If I may report to Your Excellency,” You interrupted, “Governor Wang ordered me to reach Anqing within a fixed time and deliver these dispatches. I beg Your Excellency to read them first.” “Very well, give them to me. Get up and speak.” “Thank you, Your Excellency.”

You stood and retreated a couple of paces. Turning a little sideways, he opened his coat and drew out an oilpaper packet he had carried against his skin. It was thick and heavy, evidently containing more than a letter of a few sheets. The oilpaper had split, but the large envelope within, a foot or so in length, remained as clean and sound as new. The moment Zeng took it, he knew it held cloth or silk, not paper. He turned it over to read the address: “For the personal and exclusive attention of Governor-General Zeng at Headquarters, Anqing.” Below stood: “Respectfully sealed in his own hand by Wang Youling.” He broke it open. As he had expected, it contained a folded square of snow-white Hangzhou silk. With an idle flick of his hand he shook it out, then started. The writing was black tinged with red, and several purplish-red spots were plainly bloodstains. Wang Youling had written in blood just four characters, each as large as a great bowl: “We await your mighty reinforcements.” Beneath them ran a line in smaller characters: “Wang Youling, Governor of Zhejiang, respectfully leads the millions of officials and people of this province in a hundred prostrations, and begs you with tears.” Zeng had cultivated himself all his life towards the ideal of an unshaken mind. Now, however, his expression changed despite himself.

The civil advisers and military officers at headquarters were all visibly moved by this startling, extraordinary appeal. They watched Zeng, waiting to see what he would do. Slowly he rolled up the silk. “You have had a hard and arduous journey,” he told You. “Go and rest first.” “Thank you, Your Excellency,” You replied solemnly. “Nothing could comfort me more than to have reached you. All my hardships are forgotten now. I beg only that Your Excellency send troops at once.” “I have my own plans.” Since You would not leave to rest, Zeng began asking about Zhejiang. “When did you set out?” “I left Hangzhou on the twentieth of the ninth month. Yuhang had already fallen. I imagine Hangzhou is under siege by now.” “Hangzhou has very strong walls and moats. If I remember the Comprehensive Gazetteer of the Empire correctly, it has ten gates.” Zeng recited, “‘Awaiting the Tide’, one hears the ‘Clear Waves’ sound; ‘Surging Gold’ and ‘Qiantang’ make ‘Great Peace’ secure. In the reign of Emperor Renzong of the Song, the recluse Xu Zhonghui wished that his descendants might never leave Qiantang. He said it would always be spared the ravages of war. I should think Hangzhou could hold out.” You had heard the verse: it strung together the names of Hangzhou’s ten gates, such as Houchao, Awaiting the Tide, and Qingbo, Clear Waves. But this talk of some man from the Song dynasty meant nothing to him. He understood only that, by saying Hangzhou could hold out, Zeng seemed to have no intention of sending troops. Desperately anxious, he had to speak.

“Hangzhou’s walls are strong, certainly. But the city cannot hold out for long.” “Oh?” Zeng spread his fingers and combed them through his beard. “Why is that? Tell me.” “Hangzhou has insufficient stores of grain—” Prosperous though Hangzhou was said to be, its households did not keep large stocks of rice. The rice market of western Zhejiang was at Chang’an, a hundred li northeast of the city. Each year the landlords retained only enough of the grain collected as rent to feed their own families, sending the rest to Chang’an to await a favourable price. Thus the city had not even ten days’ provisions. That spring and summer, in the lean months before the harvest, rice prices had soared. Hangzhou had been captured and stripped bare in the second month of the previous year, and the inhabitants who remained were struggling to survive. Where could they find money to buy and store grain? They had meant to lay in proper reserves when the new crop came in, but no one had foreseen the army’s headlong collapse. The grain lying in abundance across the fields had all fallen to the Taiping forces. Zeng sighed deeply. “If only Zhang Yuliang and Li Dingtai in eastern Zhejiang had been willing to fight with all their strength and hold them off for a while!” Then he asked, “Ample provisions are the first requirement in defending a city. Has Governor Wang done nothing?”

“Governor Wang has been doing everything he can. Last year he issued a proclamation inviting merchants to buy supplies, promising exemption from likin duties wherever they passed. But the roads were unsafe, and the rice merchants dared not come. When I left, I heard that he intended to ask Intendant Hu to go to Shanghai to purchase food and arms. I do not know whether they have arrived.” “Which Intendant Hu?” Zeng asked. “Hu Yuanbo?” “No, Hu Xueyan.” “Ah, yes, him! I hear he is a very capable man.” “He is indeed. All the great local gentlemen in Hangzhou have either fled or gone into hiding. It is entirely through Intendant Hu’s efforts to borrow grain and raise contributions that the imperial troops have been kept going.” Zeng nodded and considered Hangzhou’s situation for a moment. “What about the south bank of the Qiantang? Zhejiang’s military funds now come from Ningbo and Shaoxing. That route is still open, I take it?” “Yes. We depend entirely on it. But—” “Go on. Why should you hesitate?”

“If I may report to Your Excellency, once one crosses the Qiantang, Xiaoshan, Shaoxing and Ningbo are all under Commissioner Wang. He and Governor Wang do not get on. Things—” You shook his head slightly, unable to continue. Commissioner Wang was Wang Lüqian, the imperially appointed commissioner for militia organisation. Zeng knew him well and had received a letter from him complaining that Shaoxing and Ningbo prefectures raised a hundred thousand taels a month for the provincial capital, yet Wang Youling had not sent a single soldier across the river. From what You was now saying, the truth seemed otherwise. But whoever was right or wrong, discord among commanders and hostility between soldiers and civilians could bode no good. The situation in Zhejiang was profoundly discouraging.

“Go and rest.” A man in Zeng’s position had no need to tell You what measures he proposed, much less explain his reasons. He merely gave his orders. “Get a good night’s sleep. Collect my reply tomorrow, then return to Hangzhou at once and report. I shall have someone prepare the dispatches, a horse and your travelling expenses.” “Yes, Your Excellency.” You rose and saluted. “Thank you, Your Excellency.”

The steamer plying between Shanghai and Anqing was the Siming, owned by the British firm of Swire. Her comprador, Xiao Jiaji, came from a wealthy Shanghai family and had been born with a taste for seeking out the strange and unexplored. Curiosity had first led him to study English under Gu Yingchun. Through his teacher’s wife, the Seventh Lady, he had come to know “Uncle” You Wu—the name he used in imitation of her child—and so acquired connections with the Grain-Boat Guild. Yet, like Hu Xueyan, he understood everything that went on inside its “threshold” while remaining an uninitiated outsider. Zeng Guofan had sent Li Hongzhang with troops to relieve Shanghai, and the Siming had consequently made several trips to Anqing in succession. By the time the business was over, it was late autumn before Xiao could find time to visit Gu.

Gu Yingchun was doing very well. His partnership with Hu in silk and tea had brought him a tidy fortune. When the worsening situation in Jiangsu and Zhejiang cut off supplies, he had turned to land. Wealthy families seeking refuge poured into the foreign settlements at Shanghai, driving land prices higher and higher and swelling his profits. Being on the spot also made it easy to choose a site, engage workmen and buy materials, so he had built an exquisite residence on the newly opened Second Road. His family of three—the Seventh Lady had borne him a son—employed more than ten servants. Teacher and pupil had always been close. Naturally, they began with easy conversation about Xiao’s travels. They had exchanged only a few remarks when they heard the Seventh Lady’s voice; then she appeared before them. Her broad, round face was heavily made up, the white exceptionally white and the red exceptionally red. Her dazzling jewellery made her almost too brilliant to look at. “Are you going out, Madam?” Xiao rose to greet her. “Yes. Two relatives have come from far away, and I am taking them to see something of Shanghai. We shall browse in the foreign shops and take a little air—”

“In this cold weather?” Gu interrupted, laughing. “You must be mad!” He delighted in picking holes in his wife’s expressions. Long accustomed to it, she ignored him and continued. “At noon I shall treat them to a foreign meal, and in the afternoon we shall see the Western circus. Nothing is settled for the evening. Shall we dine together?” “No need to arrange anything. Come home for dinner. The crabs are good just now; I shall get a basket.” “Yes!” she exclaimed with delight. “We have not had a proper feast of crabs this year.” Then she sighed. “What a calamity! With all this war and turmoil, even the supply of crabs has been cut off. There is no pleasure in being alive these days.” “There, there! You should be content,” Gu said. “You live in the foreign settlement, with no worries about food or clothing, yet you say life has no pleasure. What of the people trapped among the Longhairs?” “It is precisely because people are trapped among them, with no news coming through and no knowing whether they are alive or dead, that one cannot stop worrying. That is why I say there is no pleasure in life.” Her face clouded. “We cannot attend to everything,” Gu said consolingly. “Go out and look around; take your minds off it. Come back this evening for crabs.” She said no more, but went out with her head lowered.

Gu, too, grew despondent. “Things are very bad.” He shook his head. “I fear Hangzhou may be finished within the next few days.” “And Mr Hu?” Xiao asked. “I wonder how he is managing there.” “No letter has come.” Two tears suddenly rolled down Gu’s face. “Such a good friend, and we can only watch him being trapped inside! Who knows whether we shall ever see him again? These last two nights, whenever your teacher’s wife and I have spoken of him, neither of us has slept till morning.” “Heaven protects good men,” Xiao said to comfort him. “Nothing about Mr Hu—his features, his temperament or his conduct—suggests a man destined for disaster. Besides, with his insight and ingenuity, how could he sit helpless in a beleaguered city, unable to think of anything?” These words helped a great deal. Gu thought for some time, then nodded. “I cannot see the signs of a short life in him either, however I look.”

After a meal at Gu’s house, teacher and pupil went out in the same carriage. Gu dropped Xiao at the shipping company, then went to his property firm and sent its senior outside clerk to buy crabs. His instructions were explicit: they must be good, whatever the price. With that authority, the task was easy. The clerk was an able man. He waited at the inland-waterway wharf until a boat from Jiaxing arrived with a dozen or more baskets of crabs. Quick of eye and hand, he laid hold of a good basket and refused to release it before discussing the price. “Half a tael each, large or small. There are thirty-two in this basket. I shall be especially generous and ask only fifteen taels.” “Fifteen taels—and you call that generous?” “Take them or leave them. You ought to understand: crabs are not dear in Jiaxing, but we have risked our lives bringing them all the way to Shanghai. Surely that is worth half a tael each?” He reached to reclaim his goods. The clerk would not let go, but neither would he pay the asking price. He took out twelve taels in silver and pressed them into the owner’s hand. The man refused. After much coaxing and noisy bargaining, they settled at fourteen taels.

The Seventh Lady had just returned when the crabs reached the house. She took them out to look at them: golden-haired, purple-backed, extraordinarily large and strong. Set upon a round Fujian lacquer table as smooth as a mirror, they braced themselves on their eight legs and scuttled imperiously in every direction. The sight made the clerk swallow. Kind-hearted by nature and skilled at putting people at their ease, she said at once, “You have bought too many. Take a few back to the firm and share them with your colleagues.” She lifted a string from the basket—five with pointed abdominal flaps and five with round ones, ten in all—and handed it to him. He refused, but she insisted that everyone should have a taste, and he finally took them away. She then went into the kitchen herself to direct the cook in steaming the crabs with perilla leaves. Afterwards she opened a chest and brought out a set of silver implements: tiny pincers and hammers, exquisitely made.

They were eating with great animation when shifting shadows appeared outside, accompanied by voices and footsteps. The Seventh Lady feared neither heaven nor earth, but such a commotion terrified her. Her limbs suddenly weakened and her face went white. When she was six, her family’s house had caught fire; this was exactly how it had begun. Nearly thirty years had passed, yet the impression had never faded, and the old terror remained. “Do not do that!” she cried, at once angry and alarmed. “What are you all rushing about for?” Before she had finished, she saw the male servants helping someone inside. Her alarm grew, though she was somewhat reassured to see at a glance that Gu was unhurt. He hurried ahead to lift the door curtain himself, calling repeatedly, “Hold him steady! Hold him steady!” Then he found a moment to call into the room, plainly addressing her, “Get someone to bring a reclining cane chair, quickly!” Recovering a little, she asked, “Who is it?”

Xiao Jiaji appeared from somewhere. “Mr Hu!” he answered. “Which Mr Hu?” “Who else? Little Uncle!” At that her heart contracted. She hurried to the door just as the servants brought Hu there. In the lamplight she could scarcely recognise him. “Little Uncle?” “Seventh Sister!” His face was covered in beard, and he looked dreadfully worn. He managed a faint smile that exposed a startling row of white teeth. “It is I.” “It really is Little Uncle!” Tears streamed from her eyes. “How have you come to this?” “This is no time for questions!” Gu urged impatiently. “Where is that cane chair?”

She turned at once and told the maids to bring it and lay a padded quilt upon it. The male servants crowded around to help Hu recline, and only then did she notice the injury to his leg. “Fetch a doctor! Bring ginger broth!” Gu gave his orders in rapid succession. “Put some congee on!” Taken by surprise, the Seventh Lady had lost all presence of mind. Xiao was calmer. “Sir, let Mr Hu settle himself first.” By the time Hu was resting in the chair, a maid had brought a bowl of ginger broth with red dates. He drank it between gasps, his hands shaking and his legs twitching. At the sight, the Seventh Lady’s vision blurred with tears.

“He is utterly exhausted!” Gu told his wife. “He must not eat much at a time yet. Bring out that old wild ginseng.” Hu had not eaten a full meal for two months. He had escaped all the way in a little boat, but because he carried official dispatches he dared not show himself. Hiding by day and travelling by night, he had passed through one Longhair stronghold after another, with scarcely any chance to find food. Even when he found some, he could not eat his fill: his stomach and intestines were so weakened that a sudden full meal would have been impossible to digest. It was said that whenever the soup kitchens opened in winter, a few starving men died on the first day because they ate too greedily. The Seventh Lady understood this too. She hurried to fetch the old wild Jilin ginseng she had treasured for so long, originally obtained from the imperial palace, and gave it to Hu to chew and swallow, to sustain his failing strength. “Little Uncle,” she said, looking at his injured leg, “let me see the wound.”

She reached to lift his foot, but he hastily drew it away. Near Jiaxing, while the Longhairs were questioning him, a single unfortunate answer had earned him a sword cut. Without doctor or medicine, he had found some incense ash in a deserted country temple and roughly covered the wound, then bound it with strips torn from his underjacket. Now it was festering, filthy with blood and discharge, and he would not let her soil her hands. “Seventh Sister, do not touch it.” The few words left him breathless. After a pause he managed two more: “I’m hungry!” “I know, I know! The congee is cooking.” An idea occurred to her. “I shall get Little Uncle something to eat first.” She went into the kitchen herself, ladled out a bowl of ready-made chicken broth and skimmed off the fat. She tore off a piece of breast meat, chopped it to a paste and stirred it into the broth. Then she took a rice-flour milk cake, crushed it and soaked it in the soup until the whole became a bowl of gruel, which she carried to Hu herself. The smell alone made him swallow repeatedly. Once the bowl was in his hands, he longed to gulp it down at once. But he thought that an open display of his ravenous hunger would wound the couple’s feelings. Forcing himself to exercise restraint, he pretended to be composed and ate delicately, spoonful by spoonful.

He finished the great bowl of gruel without leaving a trace, yet it was nowhere near enough. “Seventh Sister,” he said helplessly, “the temple of my five organs is still in revolt.” “Little Uncle,” Gu urged, “wait a little before you eat again!” “Oh!” Hu nodded, but his disappointment was plain. The Seventh Lady could scarcely bear it, though she could not disregard the condition of his stomach. “I know. Let us find something that will do you no harm.” She brought out several plates of small delicacies—pine nuts, almonds, honeyed dates and candied kumquats—to appease his craving. Just then the doctor who treated injuries arrived. After examining the wound, he pronounced it serious: Hu would need at least a fortnight before he could move about.

“That—that is impossible,” Hu said anxiously. “I must go back in three to five days at the very most.” “What?” the Seventh Lady asked urgently. “Little Uncle, you are going back? Back to Hangzhou?” “Yes. Think how many mouths are open there, waiting for me.” “Governor Wang has entrusted Little Uncle with a special mission to buy rice in Shanghai,” Gu explained to her. “Lives depend on it. He really cannot afford to linger. I have already sent someone to ask Brother Five to come and discuss matters. But—” He turned to the doctor. “Sir, whatever happens, I must beg you to do your utmost. No medicine is too costly. Please find some way of getting Little Uncle on his feet within three to five days.” “Indeed!” The Seventh Lady joined her pleas to his. “Doctor, you must perform this act of kindness. Every day sooner that Little Uncle gets back means many more lives saved in Hangzhou. You will earn untold merit for such a deed. In all your life, you have never treated a patient more important than this one.” That last sentence had great force. Deeply moved, the doctor examined the wound from every angle, frowning and clicking his tongue for a long time. At last he spoke. “There is a way. I fear only that the patient could not bear the pain.” “Never mind that!” Hu clenched his teeth. “I care nothing for pain, so long as I recover quickly.”

“Easy enough to say.” The doctor shook his head. “You are exhausted to the very limit. Severe pain could make you faint. Let me think.” He turned to Gu. “Mr Gu, do you not know a foreign doctor?” The question reminded Gu of something he bitterly regretted forgetting. Hu’s appearance had so shocked him that he had been too bewildered to think of summoning a Western physician. Now it would be awkward to seek another doctor’s services. “Yes,” he answered, having little choice. “Foreign doctors take their time over a case, but they have a couple of very useful medicines. Could you obtain some painkiller from them?” “Well—” Gu looked troubled. He knew that Western physicians differed from Chinese practitioners: they would not readily prescribe for a patient they had not examined. Moreover, there was more than one kind of painkiller, some applied externally and others taken internally. “Which kind would you want? I should at least need its name.”

“I cannot tell you its name. I cannot make sense of that foreign gibberish.” The doctor paused, then made up his mind. “Never mind! We cannot keep wasting time. I shall begin.” From his medicine chest he took a cloth bundle. Unfolded, it revealed several knives and forceps of different sizes, gleaming brightly. He cleaned the wound with fresh cotton, then cut away the dead flesh. The pain brought sweat pouring from Hu’s head. Gu and the Seventh Lady were trembling with alarm and sweating with him, yet they had to pretend to be calm and find something to say to comfort him. Like someone coaxing a child, she kept repeating, “It does not hurt, it does not hurt. It will soon be over.” At last it was over. The doctor applied Jiangxiang Powder to stop the bleeding and ease the pain, then bandaged the wound carefully. He too breathed a great sigh of relief. “Fortunately it has not developed into tetanus,” he said. “‘When a blade wound has bled heavily, a weak and fine pulse foretells survival.’ He must now be looked after with the utmost care. There must be no neglect.” He went on to enumerate many prohibitions: no exertion, no anger, no loud talk or laughter. Hu must also restrict his diet—nothing salty, sour or spicy, no hot wine or soup, not even hot congee. “Oh dear!” the Seventh Lady exclaimed. “He has just had a great bowl of hot chicken soup.” “He has already drunk it. What is the use of mentioning it now?” Gu said. “We must be careful from now on.”

Once the doctor had left, Gu proposed bringing in a Western physician instead. The Seventh Lady objected, and Hu too said it was unnecessary. He felt the pain had already diminished, which proved that the doctor had some skill; they ought not to replace him. “I feel much stronger,” Hu said. “We must attend to the important business. Why is Brother Five still not here?” “One of his pupils is marrying again today, and he is at the wedding feast. I have sent someone after him. Little Uncle, give me your instructions first.” “Very well.” Hu put a hand inside his coat and felt about for a long time before bringing out an oilpaper packet. He handed it to Gu.

Inside were two appalling letters written in Wang Youling’s blood. One was addressed to Qingduan, Governor-General of Fujian and Zhejiang. Besides begging for reinforcements, Wang hoped he would press Li Yuandu, who was still lingering at Hengzhou, to lead the Hunan recruits he had raised towards Hangzhou. This would hold the Longhairs in check and ease the pressure on the city. The other was for Xue Huan, Governor of Jiangsu, asking him to raise funds and provisions. Enclosed was the draft of a memorial, which Wang asked Xue to have copied out and forwarded to the throne on his behalf.

The memorial described in detail how Hangzhou had been besieged and deprived of food. It laid the blame on Wang Lüqian, the militia commissioner stationed in Shaoxing, who had colluded with corrupt local gentry to monopolise power and regarded the provincial capital’s peril as indifferently as the people of distant Qin might regard the troubles of Yue. Still more shocking, he had actually incited ruffians to assault an imperial official. On the twenty-fourth of the ninth month, the Longhairs had overrun Xiaoshan, across the Qiantang from Hangzhou. Liao Zongyuan, prefect of Shaoxing, sent gunboats to intercept them. Hopelessly outnumbered, the imperial troops were forced to retreat. Wang Lüqian and the people of Xiaoshan and Shaoxing had long been on bad terms with the government forces and distrusted them deeply. Now they supposed the gunboats had gone over to the enemy and were returning to guide the Longhairs. Wang ordered the boats surrounded and their crews captured, with no penalty for killing them. When Liao heard the report, he knew that, even if the accusation was not deliberately false, it was a grave misunderstanding. He hurried outside the city in person to restore order. With a roar the mob dragged him from his horse and beat him savagely, while Wang stood by, silently approving. This internal strife gave the enemy their opportunity. The Longhairs swept forward and took Shaoxing without shedding a drop of blood. The day before they entered, Wang had fled with his family and baggage from Shaoxing to Ningbo, and thence by sea to Fujian. Hangzhou’s supply route had thus been severed. Naturally, Wang Youling wished to impeach him. His language was exceptionally severe, even declaring, “Your servant could not close his eyes in death.” One could imagine the depth of his hatred.

“What shall I do with these two letters?” Gu asked. “Send them both to Governor Xue—” “Very well.” Before Hu had finished, Gu was already making to rise. “I shall take them tonight.” “There is no need for that. First thing tomorrow will do. I have more to say.” “Of course. Tell me.” “I want you to see Governor Xue in person.” Weak though Hu was, his low voice still carried great resolution. “I shall find the rice myself. As for boats to carry it, I must ask Brother Five when he comes. It would be best if we could manage without troubling the authorities. But the governor must detail troops to escort us.” “Is there a route open?”

“There is one way through. You would not know it, but Brother Five does. We shall discuss it when he comes.” Hu continued, “There are some poems for Governor Xue as well. Tell him that my injured leg prevents me from calling on him. If he wishes to know how terrible things are in Hangzhou, he need only read them.” Again he felt inside his coat for a long time before producing several badly crumpled sheets. Gu spread them on the table, smoothed them out and read carefully. They were entitled Poems Recording Events in Hangzhou in the Xinyou Year, by Zhang Yinju: twelve quatrains of seven-character lines, each with explanatory notes. At the fifth, Gu read aloud: “Dignified officials gather in the commander’s hall; / The ninth month’s clear autumn breathes an air of ease. / Suddenly countless evil mists press close; / Henceforth no military dispatch passes the ten gates.” The note beneath read: “On the twenty-sixth of the ninth month, several hundred thousand rebels surrounded the city. All ten gates were shut, and official communications ceased. The inhabitants were like birds in a cage or fish in a cauldron.” Gu counted on his fingers. “Today is the fifth of the eleventh month. They have been under siege for forty days.” “Forty days is not so long, but the shortage of food began much earlier. By the tenth day of the siege, the people were already in a panic.” Hu sighed. “Read on.” Next came: “Ten city gates besieged on every side; / Which minister understands the art of war? / Your people await you like the harvest—why the helmet, lord? / Word comes that Zhang Xun is marshalling his men.”

The note explained: “On the sixth of the tenth month, General Zhang Yuliang arrived to relieve the city and won a great victory. That afternoon he sent Deputy Commander Kuang Wenbang inside to see Governor Wang Youling, requesting that troops move out during the night and establish a camp outside. They could then link up with General Zhang and open a route for provisions. General Rao, who was present, opposed the proposal, saying, ‘There will still be time tomorrow.’ Unexpectedly, the rebel Li Xiucheng erected a wooden fortification during the night, cutting off the supply route from Zhang’s camp. Earthen works were also built across the moat all around the city’s ten gates. When General Zhang sent Deputy Commander Kuang to see the governor and pledged to destroy the rebels, the people had craned their necks in eager expectation, all declaring that the enemy would surely be crushed.” After reading the poem and its original note, Gu asked, “Who is General Rao?” “Rao Tingxuan. He became famous through Madam Shen when he relieved Guangxin Prefecture. In truth, he is useless.” Hu sighed. “How many times have I told Governor Wang that Rao’s achievements depended on others, and that the man himself is a coward! Governor Wang would not listen. That was the one chance of saving Hangzhou. Once it was lost, not even an immortal could save the place.” “And Zhang Yuliang?” Gu asked. “Everyone says he is no good. What is the truth about him?” “Read on. The next poem explains.”

The poem explained it thus: “Marquis Huan, unrivalled in courage and strength, / Would never yield before the flying cannon shot. / Ten thousand horses silent, the whole army weeps; / A commander’s star, huge as a grain measure, falls into the Yangtze.” “What? Was he killed?” “Killed in battle.” Hu shook his head. “He too did enormous harm through his failures. After his defeat at Jiaxing, he could not hold Jinhua or Lanxi either, and Hangzhou was placed in danger. Still, we owed something to him in the end.” “The poem compares him to Zhang Fei. It praises him highly.” “He died fighting for his country. Naturally they must speak well of him.” Hu’s face darkened. “I urged Governor Wang to withdraw for a while. It is like banking at pai gow or playing the dice-shaking game: if luck runs against you, stop for a spell and begin again. But he refused. He said that he had once told He Genyun that an official was bound to defend the territory entrusted to him and must on no account abandon Changzhou lightly. If he now acted contrary to his own words, how could he justify himself?” “It seems Governor Wang is a loyal servant of the throne after all.”

“A loyal servant?” Hu gave a bitter laugh. “How many coins a catty are loyal servants worth? I think he—” His voice choked and nearly failed. Gu had never heard him speak with such angry bitterness. That he should now dismiss loyalty as utterly worthless revealed the pain and indignation within him. Yet Gu could think of nothing to offer him in comfort. “Let us eat first,” the Seventh Lady said. “However great the trouble, we must fill our stomachs before we can think what to do. And Little Uncle really is hungry.” “How can I eat when we are talking of Hangzhou?” Hu looked up, his eyes full of tears. “Read the last two poems.” Gu read them closely and his face changed. The Seventh Lady could not understand it. “What is it?” she asked. “What do they say?” “Listen.” He read aloud, pronouncing every word distinctly.

“Men come to carve flesh, not to heal a wound; / The starving vie to eat it—grief beyond words. / A breastful of hot blood, three measures of blood, / Forced down as though it were dragon’s liver or phoenix breast.” “What?” the Seventh Lady cried in horror. “People eating people?” Gu did not answer at once, but read out the note word by word. “Soldiers pillaged at will. The inhabitants sounded gongs, seized them and brought them to Fengshan Gate, where Governor Wang was usually stationed. The governor established the facts and, invoking the emperor’s authority, had them all beheaded. Their bodies lay heaped by the roadside. Soldiers fought for their hearts and livers to eat with wine, and starving civilians likewise competed for morsels of their flesh. ‘Eating human flesh’, something formerly encountered only in histories, I now witnessed with my own eyes.” Everyone within hearing, before the hall and behind it, turned pale. The Seventh Lady shook her head again and again. “The world has changed! That such things should happen!” “I can scarcely believe it myself,” Gu said. “Little Uncle, is it really true?” “Not only true; there is scarcely anything surprising in it.” Hu’s face was bleak as he struggled for breath. “People will eat anything when they are starving.” He went on to describe the famine in Hangzhou. Western Zhejiang had enjoyed a bumper harvest that year, but just as the crop was being gathered, the Longhairs had swept in like a tide. The imperial troops retreated at every turn, and the grain ready for harvesting supplied the enemy instead, enabling them to maintain a long blockade. Otherwise their hundreds of thousands of men could not have sustained themselves, and Hangzhou’s siege would have collapsed of its own accord.

The comfortably off households in Hangzhou naturally held some grain in reserve, but people who lived from one small measure of rice to the next felt the danger immediately. The rice shops had closed even before the gates were shut. Hu therefore took the lead in establishing soup kitchens: forty-seven throughout the upper, middle and lower divisions of the city. They served twice daily, in the morning and late afternoon, cooking one shi of rice at each serving. There was too little congee and too great a crowd. The old and infirm, women and children could not force their way to the front; some went three or four times and came away empty-handed. Before long, the kitchens had to close. Government grain was still sold at controlled prices in quantities allotted by household size. When the rice was gone, they sold beans; when the beans were gone, wheat. The rich had another source of rice: they secretly exchanged gold and opium with the Eight Banner troops for their military rations. Soon the rice, wheat and other grains were all exhausted, and people turned to medicinal roots and dried provisions. Prepared rehmannia, Job’s tears and Solomon’s seal could all serve in place of meals. Dates and chestnuts became treasures, while sea cucumbers, shark’s fins and other delicacies of the banquet table became the food of the poor.

After that they ate bran, leather trunks and hobnailed shoes—the shoes were made of oxhide—then duckweed, grass roots and tree bark. The people of Hangzhou were devoted to Buddhism, and old ladies of wealthy families particularly loved to purchase animals and spare their lives. At a place called Little Cloud Tower, cattle, sheep, pigs and ducks were kept for this purpose. Naturally, they were all swept away. “The people in Hangzhou are no longer people. They are ghosts, every one of them a bundle of bones. Look at a face and you see three holes: two for the eyes and one for the mouth. In the streets they are like empty eggshells blown by the wind, swaying and drifting, unable to keep their feet.” Hu paused for breath, then continued with great effort. “Two people might meet in the road and talk, both too weak to raise their voices. As they speak, one suddenly falls down for no apparent reason. The other tries to help him. It would be better if he did not: the moment he bends to lift him, his own head swims, his eyes blur, and down he goes too, unable to rise. Who can count the bodies left in the roads like that? It is fortunate that this is winter. In summer, plague would have broken out long ago.” “Then what of your own family?” the Seventh Lady asked urgently.

“I do not know whether they are alive or dead.” Tears fell as Hu spoke. “Back in the eighth month, my mother thought we would be safer taking refuge in the country. I sent the whole household, young and old, to Upper Tianzhu below the Northern Peak. Once the city gates shut, all news of them ceased.” “They will be all right,” the Seventh Lady said. “Yours is a family that has accumulated merit. Your mother is always doing good and helping people. Heaven protects the virtuous. They will certainly be safe.” “Ah!” Gu sighed. “What a catastrophe!” By now the clock was striking eight. The great crabs had been steamed and had grown cold, but after learning that Hangzhou, so often called a Buddhist sanctuary, had been reduced to the horror of people eating one another, no one in the household could swallow a meal. As hostess, the Seventh Lady was obliged to urge them to eat; they merely went through the motions and soon left the table. The only man who had eaten his fill was You Wu, who arrived after hearing the news. At his pupil’s wedding feast he had naturally been treated as an honoured guest. When he learnt that Hu had come, he was eager to leave, but still had to drink a good deal more before he could get away. Now the sight of Hu drove most of the wine from his head. He could only stare.

“Little Uncle, how have you come to this?” “Brother Five, do not ask him about it. It was truly hell on earth; he escaped by the narrowest margin. Let us discuss what must be done.” “Please come inside,” the Seventh Lady said. “I have everything ready for Little Uncle.” She had prepared a small room beside Gu’s study as Hu’s bedroom. The windows were papered a spotless white, a great brazier was burning, and a Western brass bed had been furnished with thick quilts and bedding. She had also prepared a decoction of ginseng alone, together with nourishing, easily digested food, so that he could eat while they talked.

In fact it was Gu who spoke for him. “Brother Five, the people of Hangzhou are starving to death. Governor Wang has entrusted Little Uncle with the task of buying rice in Shanghai. The more the better, and the sooner the better.” “The Zhejiang provincial treasury allocated twenty thousand taels,” Hu said. “But I could not bring silver with me. I have come empty-handed, and I do not know what has happened to my bank either. Brother Five, this account will have to be settled later.” “Money is a small matter,” Gu put in. “I shall advance it.” “There is no need even for that,” You replied. “Tongyu has a thousand shi of rice in its warehouse. I can get another thousand at any time. If that is not enough, we shall find more. The rice itself is easy enough. The question is how to transport it.”

“The Grand Canal is blocked. We cannot get past Jiaxing,” Hu Xueyan said. “There is only one route left: by sea, through Biezimen.” Biezimen lay in Haining, at the mouth of the Qiantang River; in the Ming dynasty it had been Hangzhou’s first line of defense against Japanese pirates. Brother You knew the canal well, but the sea routes were another matter. “That is beyond me,” he admitted frankly. “We shall have to find the Sea-Junk Guild and ask them to work something out.”

The Sea-Junk Guild operated along the coast. Ever since the proposal to ship the tribute grain by sea, relations between it and the Grain-Boat Guild had verged on open hostility. Asking Brother You to approach its members now was asking a great deal. Hu drank his ginseng broth, still turning over in his mind how best to proceed, but Gu Yingchun spoke first. “I have met Chief Yu of the Sea-Junk Guild once. Things have come to such a pass that I cannot stand on ceremony. I shall go!” He rose as he spoke, but Brother You caught his arm. “No hurry,” he said deliberately. “Let me think.”

Hu’s wits were as quick as ever. Seeing what Brother You had in mind, he struggled to rise. The Seventh Lady hurried to support him. “What do you want, Little Uncle?” He did not answer. Getting to his feet, he cried, “Brother Five!” and dropped to his knees. Brother You recoiled in alarm and sprang well out of his way. “Little Uncle! Little Uncle! What are you doing? You do me far too much honor!”

Gu and his wife helped him up together. The Seventh Lady was about to speak, but Hu waved her into silence. “I am begging Brother Five on behalf of the people of Hangzhou!” “Little Uncle, why must you do this?” Brother You had no choice but to speak plainly. “You have only to say the word. Even if Chief Yu were my bitterest enemy, with no hope of a reconciliation, I should still have to go and ask him nicely.” In fact, Chief Yu was just such an enemy. His name was Yu Fuhua, and he lived at Qiaojiabin, inside the Little South Gate. Coastal shipping had made him a considerable fortune. Originally, one man worked the sea routes and the other the canal: river water and well water never crossed, as the saying went, and there had been no cause for either friendship or enmity. The transfer of the southern tribute grain to sea transport had changed everything. Brother You himself was reasonable enough to recognize an inevitable development. Yu Fuhua had not deliberately sought the contract in order to take the Grain-Boat Guild’s livelihood away. His younger followers, however, saw it differently. To make matters worse, the sea-junk sailors strutted about, spending lavishly in teahouses and taverns. The grain-boat men looked poor beside them; jealousy bred resentment, and clashes were frequent.

On one occasion the two guilds fought a pitched brawl, and in fairness the grain-boat men were more at fault. Even so, disputes among men of their world ought to have been settled within it. Instead, Yu Fuhua listened to several minor expectant officials at the Jiangsu Maritime Grain Transport Bureau and had some of Brother You’s men hauled before the Shanghai county magistrate. Liu Xungao was one of Jiangsu’s capable officials. He also knew that the Songjiang Grain-Boat Guild was like a hundred-legged insect that went on wriggling even after death, and he had no wish to stir up trouble. Gu Yingchun, meanwhile, had some influence at the county yamen and, at Brother You’s request, went to intercede. These considerations together led Liu to turn a major affair into a minor one and the minor one into nothing at all. He summoned Brother You, admonished him in open court, and required a written pledge that there would be no further disturbances. Then he allowed him to take his men away.

This only deepened the grudge. As Brother You saw it, even the county magistrate knew better than to provoke the Songjiang guild and had left an opening for mercy. Yu Fuhua, by contrast, had shown none of the loyalty their way of life demanded. Had he forgotten that they all lived by the water and belonged to the same fraternity? Very well, then: there would be no further dealings between them. Brother You summoned the heads of the wharves under his authority and formally announced that the Songjiang Grain-Boat Guild was to take no part in anything connected with the Sea-Junk Guild. Any man who changed allegiance and became a sailor on a sea junk would be considered expelled; thereafter, even if they met, he would no longer be acknowledged. Yu Fuhua knew that he had made a mistake and was deeply uneasy about it. Several times he sent intermediaries to express his goodwill and seek a reconciliation. Brother You ignored them. Now he would have to break his own rule and bow to the other man.

“For you, Little Uncle, I would grit my teeth and take three knife thrusts clean through me. But these past two years I have flown my colors so high that I cannot bring myself to lower the sail all at once. That is the difficulty.” “Brother Five, you would be doing it for the people of Hangzhou,” Hu said. “With my leg injured, I cannot go and negotiate with Chief Yu myself. Besides, the sea passage to Biezimen is safe enough; the danger begins once we are through it. Chief Yu may well refuse. Only if you ask him will he feel bound to do you a favor and agree, however unwillingly. As for your having flown your colors so high that you cannot lower the sail, that is true too. But I have thought of a way to let you lower it—and save face into the bargain. What do you say?” “There is no need to ask me, Little Uncle. Whatever you say goes. I was only talking, after all. If there were no other way, I should have to swallow my pride and see Chief Yu just the same.” “We shall not make you swallow too much.” Hu turned to Gu. “Old Gu, I should like you to write a letter for me—to Governor-General He—” “To Governor-General He?” Gu repeated. “Who knows where he is hiding now?” “Surely we can find out?”

“We certainly can,” Brother You put in. “He may have been dismissed and be facing an investigation, but he was a governor-general, after all. Someone must know where he is. But, Little Uncle, he no longer has any say in Jiangsu’s affairs. Why write to him?” “He has no authority now, but people will still show their former superior some respect,” Hu replied. “I want him to speak to Governor Xue or the Shanghai circuit intendant and have one of them mediate between you and Chief Yu.” “No, no!” Brother You waved both hands. “I am no match in status for an official in office. Such occasions are terribly awkward. Better to avoid an unnecessary complication.” Gu, however, thought Hu’s suggestion very sensible. “Better to settle a feud than to prolong it. If a senior local official mediates, both sides will save face. You ought to take the opportunity to lower your sail.” There was another reason too. “And that is only part of it. For Little Uncle’s mission, Chief Yu’s junks are indispensable. But the risk is so great that, even if he agrees out of regard for you, it will be hard to repay the favor. If the authorities intervene, it will be half an official requisition. That would make your debt of gratitude much lighter, would it not, Brother Five?” Hu nodded repeatedly. “Exactly, Old Gu. That was precisely what I meant.”

With both of them taking this view, Brother You naturally agreed. Hu dictated the substance of the letter, and Gu wrote it out for He Guiqing. They arranged to go their separate ways early the next morning and have everything settled by noon. The details of transporting the rice would have to wait until Brother You and Yu Fuhua had made their peace.

By the time the Seventh Lady had settled both sets of guests and gone to bed, it was already midnight. She asked her husband about Hu’s mission. When she heard of the elaborate plan to write to He Guiqing, she fairly leaped up. “At a time like this, can you still afford to carry the coffin round the city wall—taking every possible detour? Never mind that the people inside Hangzhou are nearly all starving to death. One look at Little Uncle, coming in that condition to beg for help, ought to have set you working through the night!” She was positively bristling. “Honestly! You call yourselves grown men, and yet you go about things like a pack of dithering women!” Gu laughed. “Do not stamp your feet at me. Ask your brother! If I had not persuaded him, Brother Five would not even have agreed to settle his quarrel with Chief Yu.” “I shall go!” she declared without a moment’s hesitation. “I shall go and speak to Brother Five.” She had no need to. Brother You happened to have something private to discuss with his brother-in-law, and she met him as she opened the door. Her angry expression astonished him. “What is it? Who has upset you?”

At this, Gu hastened to intervene. “Seventh Sister, speak gently to Brother Five. He has difficulties of his own. If you put your case reasonably, he will listen.” “Very well, I shall be reasonable. Come in and sit down, Brother Five. Let me ask you one thing: which matters more, your friendship with Little Uncle or the prestige of some governor or governor-general?” “What do you mean by asking that?” “I have my reasons. Answer me first, and I shall explain.” “Of course my friendship with Little Uncle matters more.” “Good!” Her expression softened. “Now let me ask you something else. Put the lives of the whole population of Hangzhou on one side of a scale and our guild’s quarrel with Chief Yu on the other. Which weighs more, Brother Five?”

Brother You was silenced; he had no answer to that. Gu was delighted, but also a little uneasy. Close as he was to his wife’s brother, courtesy still imposed a certain reserve, and there were things he could not say outright. Now this female Zhang Fei, with her fearless plain speaking, had said enough to convince Brother You. Yet Gu feared that, having proved her point, she might refuse to leave it there and provoke resentment by pressing too hard. He hoped she would know when to stop. The Seventh Lady had grown a few years older and had a child now; she was naturally no longer quite the woman she had been. Her voice grew calm. “As I see it, Little Uncle wants to help you save face, but his plan is not a very good one.” “Then you must have a better plan?” Gu prompted her. “Why not explain it calmly to Brother Five and see whether it will work?”

“If you bring officials in to mediate, there is something of a punishment cup about it: you have to swallow it, whether you like it or not—” “Exactly!” Brother You slapped his thigh in hearty approval. “A’qi has put her finger on it. I could hardly contradict Little Uncle, but that is precisely how it feels.” “Let men of our own world settle their own affairs,” the Seventh Lady continued, warming to her subject again. “Go and see Chief Yu tomorrow, Brother Five. Tell him plainly that, for the sake of every life in Hangzhou and your friendship with Little Uncle, you have come to bow your head and ask for help. When word of that gets about, who will fail to praise your humanity and generosity?”

Brother You considered this intently, then rose abruptly and left without a word. The private matter he had meant to discuss with his brother-in-law was his feeling that they need not involve the authorities: could they not find a better way? There was no longer any need to ask.

As soon as he reached Qiaojiabin, inside the Little South Gate, he could see the Yu residence from a considerable distance. It was both new and large. Soon after Yu Fuhua had completed it, the Small Swords Society had risen in revolt, and Liu Lichuan had occupied the house for a period extending over three calendar years. On New Year’s Day in the fifth year of Xianfeng’s reign, Jiangsu’s governor, Jirhangga, had recaptured the walled city of Shanghai with the help of the French admiral Lani’er. Yu recovered his home and had it extensively refurbished, making it even more splendid than before.

This was Brother You’s first visit to the Yu house. He had come without pomp and with few attendants, and no one recognized him. He never carried a visiting card; he merely pointed to himself and said, “My name is You. I am from Songjiang.” His strength lay hidden beneath an unimposing exterior, and his clothes were very plain. The servants looked down on him, assuming that he had come to beg assistance or seek employment. “Our master is out,” one said indifferently. “Come back tomorrow.” “No, I have something extremely urgent to discuss. I must see him. Please send someone to find him. I shall wait here.” “And where are we supposed to look?” The servant’s tone had become unpleasant.

Brother You had great self-control. Having already resolved to humble himself in pursuit of his purpose, he found it easier to put up with a slight. “In that case,” he said reasonably, “you have benches here already. Might I sit down and wait?” In the entrance passage stood two benches, each more than a zhang long, provided for visiting servants and sedan-chair bearers to rest their feet. If he wished to sit there, why should he not? He was welcome to do so. He had sat there for the better part of a two-hour watch when a handsome carriage arrived. A smartly dressed servant riding on the shaft jumped down and placed a step beneath the carriage door. A young man in rich clothes emerged and strode proudly inside.

This was Yu Fuhua’s eldest son, Yu Songnian, known as “the Yu Family Licentiate.” For all his wealth, Yu Fuhua had felt something lacking so long as none of the younger men in the family held a scholarly degree: they were rich, but not distinguished. He therefore engaged eminent tutors and drove Songnian to study in seclusion. But examination success did not necessarily follow literary merit, and the young man repeatedly failed to obtain even the lowest degree. His father consequently donated fifty thousand taels toward repairing the Confucian temple. The court usually rewarded such public benefactions in one of two ways: it might order the local authorities to erect a commemorative arch for the donor, or it might increase the quota of successful candidates admitted as licentiates. Yu Fuhua wanted the latter reward and, after making the necessary arrangements, obtained it. The donation benefited the locality, but he had certainly considered his own interests too. With the quota enlarged, admission became easier, and Songnian at last earned his scholar’s blue collar. The official term for a licentiate was shengyuan, and there were various distinctions within that grade; those admitted to supplementary places were called zengsheng. Whatever the category, however, a licentiate was a licentiate. Calling Songnian “the Yu Family Licentiate” implied that his place had been purchased by the family’s huge donation, and was naturally a little disparaging.

Yet Songnian was no fool. Though he had some of the habits of a rich young idler, he sincerely aspired to learning and was courteous and well mannered. Amid the servants’ cries of “Young Master!” he had already passed through the screen doorway when he sensed that something was wrong. He stopped and looked back. Sure enough, there was Brother You. “Uncle You!” He hurried over, bowed respectfully, and asked in mingled surprise and delight, “What brings you here, sir?” “I have come to see your father.” Brother You was too generous to complain of the servants. “I hear he is out, so I am waiting for him.” “But why are you sitting here?” Songnian turned on the servants angrily. “Where are your manners? Master You has come to see us, and you leave an honored guest sitting here instead of asking him inside?”

Only then did the servant who had spoken earlier realize that he had failed to recognize a great man under his very nose. He had heard more than once of his master’s quarrel with Brother You and his unsuccessful attempts to mend it. Now that the visitor had come of his own accord, the servant had treated him discourteously. If Brother You took offense, it would be an unforgivable fault; he might have smashed his own excellent rice bowl. The thought drained his face of color. Seeing this, Brother You decided to carry his kindness through to the end. “Do not scold him, do not scold him,” he said, stepping quickly between them. “Your man invited me inside more than once. I chose to wait here myself; I thought it more convenient.” At this Songnian said no more. “Please come inside, Uncle You. Father is inspecting the city wall. I shall send for him at once.” “Thank you, thank you. It really is rather urgent; otherwise I would not presume to trouble your father.” “Please do not say that, Uncle You. We could hardly have hoped to bring you here even with an invitation.”

Songnian ceremoniously led him into the reception hall. In the center hung a blue plaque with four large gold characters in the emperor’s own hand: “Meritorious Service in Protecting the People.” The plaque explained why Yu Fuhua was inspecting the wall. When Shanghai was retaken, foreign warships had fired from the southern wharf on the Huangpu to support the assault, wrecking a long stretch of wall between the Great South Gate and the Great East Gate. Since Liu Lichuan had occupied the Yu mansion, the court considered its owner open to suspicion of having supported the rebels and fined him one hundred thousand taels to repair the damage. After the local officials had pleaded his case, however, the emperor also bestowed this plaque. Rumors were now circulating that the Longhairs would attack Shanghai. Yu feared that the stretch he had rebuilt might not be strong enough. If an enemy broke through there, the responsibility would be serious, so he had spent several days inspecting it and taking precautions. When Songnian had finished explaining, Brother You took the opportunity to prepare the way for his request. “Your father has always been devoted to the public good. Excellent, excellent! In saving others, one saves oneself. That is what has brought me here today.” “Indeed,” Songnian said respectfully. “Would you care to give me your instructions first, Uncle You, or would you prefer to wait for Father?”

“I can tell you first; it makes no difference.” Brother You began a detailed account of Hu’s perilous journey in search of grain. Halfway through it, Yu Fuhua came home and interrupted them. “Brother You!” He was a plump man of medium height, and his haste had left him breathless. He spoke between panting and laughing. “What wind has blown you here? What a rare pleasure!” “One does not visit a temple without a purpose. I have come to ask a favor. I was just telling your eldest boy.” “It is about shipping grain to Hangzhou—” Songnian began. Yu Fuhua was quick-witted and exceptionally smooth in his dealings. His son’s few words were enough for him to guess most of the story. He hastened to interrupt. “Of course, of course! Anything concerning Brother You can be discussed. First, settle yourself comfortably. We have not met for so long; let us hear how you have been. You are looking well, Brother You. Good fortune must be on its way!” “Thanks to your good wishes. Chief Yu, I have come today—”

“I know, I know.” Yu Fuhua would not let him begin the business. Turning to his son, he said, “Go in and tell your mother that Uncle You is here. Ask her to prepare a few dishes for him—with her own hands. Bring out the pot of wine-lees stew that is ready, and we shall have a drink. Your Uncle You and I must have a proper talk today.” Brother You had long heard that Yu Fuhua was worth a million and lived in great luxury. Now he was asking the wife who had shared his early years to go into the kitchen and cook for his guest herself. Clearly he did not intend to put on airs because of his wealth. This gesture of remembering friendships formed in humbler days was genuinely touching, and Brother You gladly accepted the hospitality.

The wine was brought out, and host and guest sat facing each other. Yu Fuhua had adopted the style of an official household, with the younger members standing by to wait on their elders. However much Brother You urged him, Songnian dared not join them at the table. Once he had filled the guest’s cup, Yu Fuhua solemnly raised his own in both hands to the level of his nose and offered a toast to Brother You alone. Naturally, there were a few important words to be said. “Brother Five, I have been remiss in many ways these past few years. I beg you to overlook it all. River and sea belong to one family; north, south, east, or west makes no difference. From now on, I shall rely on your guidance and help.” He tipped back his head, drained the wine, and turned the empty cup toward his guest. Since Brother You had come to make peace, he drank his own cup dry as well. “Chief Yu,” he said, showing that it was empty, “let us wipe out the past today. River and sea do belong to one family. Still, in certain matters, I must ask you to have your men show us a little consideration.” “You are too kind, too kind!” Yu said with an air of trepidation. Then he turned aside. “See whether Fuquan is here.”

Brother You knew the man: he had been an able assistant to Yu Fuhua when the latter was building his fortune and was now a prosperous, round-faced gentleman himself. Summoned into the room, Fuquan did not wait for his employer to speak, but immediately made a deep bow. With a broad smile he offered his greeting. “Uncle You, you still remember me, I hope?” “Oh!” Brother You deliberately blinked and assumed an expression of delighted surprise. “Brother Fuquan! You have filled out.” “I would not presume to be called your brother, Uncle You. Just call me Fuquan.” “Indeed, they are all your juniors. Such politeness only puts a distance between us, Brother You.” Yu then turned to instruct Fuquan. “Pass the word: river and sea are one family. The men of the Songjiang Grain-Boat Guild are to be treated as our own, with respect and consideration in everything. Whatever Uncle You orders is exactly as if I had said it myself.” Fuquan acknowledged each instruction. His manner was not merely solemn, but sincere. In their world, a man repaid a foot of kindness with ten feet in return. Seeing this, Brother You could hardly fail to respond with assurances of trust, friendship, modesty, and mutual consideration.

Yu Fuhua was delighted to see a long-standing feud dissolved in a single meeting. In terms of their respective interests, although the Sea-Junk Guild was prospering for the moment, the Grain-Boat Guild had deep roots and a different order of influence. Reconciliation therefore mattered even more to the sea-junk men. Yu was an exceptionally calculating man, and he felt that the occasion deserved a splendid public celebration. If word spread that Brother You had come voluntarily to restore friendly relations, would that not add greatly to Yu’s prestige and standing? Having made up his mind, he immediately announced that he would choose an auspicious day within the next few days and give a grand banquet as a modest token of respect. He spoke so earnestly that Brother You could neither refuse nor properly wish to. He thanked his host for the meal before it had even been prepared, and the matter was settled.

Their friendship now seemed closer still. Yu Fuhua drank freely and talked merrily, in the highest of spirits. Brother You, however, was growing anxious. He had urgent business to discuss; how could he give his mind to reminiscence? Yet he could hardly spoil his host’s pleasure. If this went on until Yu was hopelessly drunk, would the whole visit not have been wasted? He waited and waited, restraining himself again and again. Just as his patience was about to give out, Songnian noticed and reminded his father, “Father, Uncle You has something to discuss with you.” “Oh, yes, of course.” Yu could no longer pretend to have forgotten. He turned to his guest. “Please tell me again, Brother You.”

“It is this. There is a consignment of rice for which we need your boats. It must go by sea, enter Biezimen at Haining, and travel up the Qiantang River to Hangzhou. The people inside the city are no longer merely eating roots and bark. They are eating human flesh. We absolutely must have your help, Chief Yu, and the sooner the better.” “For anything of yours, Brother You, you have only to say the word. But you know our circumstances. The Sea-Junk Guild has never taken the Biezimen route. We do not know the waters and might run aground. That could ruin the whole undertaking.” He hurried on. “Of course, your grain-boat men could pilot us, but taking sea junks into the river is not the proper way to use them. Let me send for some people at once and discuss it. We must find a plan that is safe in every respect. May I give you an answer tomorrow?”

Brother You was distinctly displeased. In such a time of war and disorder, he thought, where could one go without taking risks? Even in peace, with treacherous winds and waves, no one could guarantee freedom from mishap. Was this talk of finding a wholly safe plan not simply a deliberate excuse? He thought so, but there was naturally no question of losing his temper. Still, he had to give the man a hint. “Chief Yu, even brothers keep clear accounts. Friendship is friendship, and business is business. Work out the figures carefully. The freight will be paid from public funds. Why leave the river water unused when you could wash your boat in it?” “Please, please! You misunderstand me, Brother You. I am certainly not calculating my advantage in that respect. My concern is—” Whatever he said seemed wrong. He also needed to learn more about conditions along the route, so he changed tack. “I have long heard of that Circuit Intendant Hu. Might you introduce me to him?” Circuit Intendant Hu was Hu Xueyan. Over the past few years, purchased rank and recommendations for promotion had carried him smoothly upward, making him a peculiar figure in Zhejiang’s provincial capital: part official, part merchant. Brother You had intended to bring the two men together in any case. Now that Yu had asked, he agreed without hesitation. “We can go this moment, if you wish. I am entirely at your disposal.”

“Today would be too hurried. For one thing, I have been drinking; for another, a hastily arranged call might seem disrespectful. I shall certainly visit first thing tomorrow. Where is Circuit Intendant Hu staying?” “At the house of my relative, Gu Yingchun. I shall come and fetch you in the morning.” “So he is at Old Gu’s! We know each other too, and I have been to his house. Do not trouble yourself. I can go on my own.” That brought their discussion to a close, and they had drunk enough as well. Brother You rose to take his leave. When he returned to Gu’s house, the Seventh Lady came forward to meet him. Even before she spoke, her eyes expressed a concern stronger than words.

“Well?” Brother You did not answer, but asked after Hu’s injury. Here the Seventh Lady had good news. She praised the doctor’s skill in treating wounds: Hu was in far less pain, the wound was healing quickly, and in three days he should be able to get out of bed and walk. “Part of it is that his mind has been at rest since he arrived here,” she added. “Good news lifts a man’s spirits. If Chief Yu will help, it will do him more good than any medicine.” “He is willing to help, but these things cannot be settled so quickly. Let me speak to Little Uncle first.”

Brother You began his account. The Seventh Lady stood listening beside them, smiling throughout the first part. When he reached Yu Fuhua’s promise to give an answer the next day, she sprang up. “That is plainly an excuse!” Hu hurried to restrain her. “Seventh Sister, he has difficulties of his own. Do not be anxious yet. Let us talk it through.” “It is for you that I am anxious, Little Uncle!”

“I know, I know,” Hu replied, as composed as ever. “If I were Chief Yu, I too should have to consider the matter carefully. There is little to fear at sea, but once we pass through Biezimen and enter the Qiantang, the Longhairs are on both banks. Naturally, he has every reason to be worried. There is only one way to proceed. On the one hand, we must tell him the truth: where the danger lies, where it does not, and what we shall do if trouble comes. On the other, we must ask him to put friendship before caution and take a risk. As the saying goes, spend the first half of the night thinking about others and the second half thinking about yourself. For the moment, let us consider our own part: what can we do for him that will make him willing, out of friendship, to take that risk?” “Quite right!” Brother You was full of admiration. “Little Uncle, that is both reasonable and fair. If we approach it that way, we can get the thing done.” “Very well!” The Seventh Lady could do nothing with them. Then she reflected that, as a woman, she need not concern herself with such weighty affairs. “If the sky falls, there are taller people to hold it up. It is nothing to do with me. You can discuss it yourselves.” She turned to go.

“Seventh Sister!” Hu called after her. “There is something I simply must consult you about. Please come back!” Naturally, she stopped. He had only called because her words sounded rather petulant; in fact, he had nothing to ask her. Now that he had spoken, however, he had to find something. An idea flashed into his mind. “Seventh Sister, I have not been in Shanghai for six months. Have any good restaurants opened lately?” “Yes!” she replied. “There is a new one called Taihe. It serves northern and southern dishes from all over the country. I have eaten there several times. The food is first-rate.” “And the place itself—is it spacious?” “Spacious? It is more than that! Qingxinglou, Fuxinyuan, Hongyunlou—none of the big restaurants you can name is so splendidly fitted out.” She looked at him. “Are you thinking of giving a banquet, Little Uncle?”

“Nothing escapes you, Seventh Sister.” Hu smiled; the compliment was deliberate. Then he turned to Brother You. “Brother Five, since you have already humbled yourself, do it to the end for the sake of all the people of Hangzhou. Give a banquet in your name and invite every man under Chief Yu, great or small. It would be best if all our grain-boat men were there too. Let us pay him every possible honor and see what he says.” “Certainly. You have only to ask.” “Then we must trouble Seventh Sister to book the tables at Taihe. The invitation will be in Brother Five’s name, and I shall pay—” “You need not tell me that,” she broke in. “What I need to know is how many guests there will be, and how many tables to order.” That, of course, was for Brother You to answer. “If we are going to invite them at all,” he said slowly, “we need not worry about how many. I shall send a single invitation to every brother in the Sea-Junk Guild. We shall take the whole restaurant and serve a continuous banquet: as many tables as are needed.” “That is a generous way to do it. Agreed.” Hu clasped his hands toward the Seventh Lady. “I leave it in your capable hands.”

The Seventh Lady loved a grand, lively occasion and agreed at once. Beneath her expansive manner, however, she had an eye for detail. After a moment’s thought she asked, “What day?” “Are we not in a hurry?” Brother You replied. “If we want to be quick, make it tomorrow.” Without a word, she took down the almanac hanging behind the door and turned its pages. “Tomorrow may not be possible. It is an auspicious day; someone is sure to be holding a wedding feast there. The day after is an ordinary day: ‘Suitable for sacrifices and the making of covenants; unsuitable for other undertakings.’ Would that do?” “Certainly,” Hu answered at once. “We can call this the making of a covenant.” There was no time to lose. The Seventh Lady took a wrapped packet of silver dollars and drove to Taihe herself to arrange the banquet. Brother You asked Mr Zhao, the Gu household’s accountant, to write out a full formal invitation on red paper and sent it to the Yu residence at Qiaojiabin. At the same time he summoned a favorite disciple, Li Delong, to help with the arrangements.

While brother and sister were busy, Hu lay alone in bed and thought things through. By the time Brother You returned, his plans were ready. “Brother Five, let us take things one at a time. What about the rice?” “I have passed the word. We can have it settled this afternoon. The more the better, of course, but we must also see how many boats Chief Yu can provide. In a word: if he has the boats, I have the rice.” “Good. Now let us discuss the boats. Whatever else he fears, Chief Yu is most afraid of the Longhairs. But that need not stop us. They are on shore, and we shall be on the river. They have no gunboats, so there is no need to fear them. At worst, they can come out in small craft and attack with foreign rifles. If we have a force of our own, with a few dozen good guns, we can open fire as soon as necessary and rout them. I have thought of where to find the men too. Is Old Gu well acquainted with Yang Fang?” Brother You understood his intention and nodded. “Very well acquainted. And even if he were not, it would not matter.” “Why is that?” Hu asked.

“Little Uncle, do you mean to borrow some men from Ward, the foreign commander?” “Exactly. Is Yang Fang not the intermediary in all the foreign commander’s negotiations with the Shanghai circuit intendant?” “Quite right. Yang is a director of the Siming Guildhall. Ningbo is in Zhejiang too; he has every reason to help his home province. Even without a personal acquaintance, we could still ask him.” “I know little of the man’s background. Of course, a mutual friend would make the matter easier. But this is only my idea; I do not yet know whether it will work. We must ask Old Gu first. When will he be back?” “There is no need to ask him.” Brother You pointed. “We have someone here already.” He meant Xiao Jiaji, who had gone out with Gu early that morning. Hu had instructed them to deliver Wang Youling’s two letters written in blood to Xue Huan in person, so Gu was still at the Jiangsu governor’s temporary headquarters in Shanghai, waiting to be received. Fearing that Hu would be anxious, he had sent Xiao back ahead with a message.

“You see,” Hu said to Brother You, “this is just why I have been thinking of using the foreign commander’s men. In official business, unless you have the right connections, you get nowhere. If even obtaining an audience with Governor Xue is so difficult, how can we hope for troops to escort our grain? Even if he agreed, they could not be on their way in two or three days.” He emphasized his next words. “My mind is made up. We shall find our own solution.” Brother You explained their plan to Xiao, who listened quietly without speaking. “Well, Jiaji?” Hu urged him, already sensing that he had another idea. “There is a way. It looks troublesome, but in fact it is easier. Why not ask the British or French admiral to send warships as an escort?” “But—would that be possible?” Brother You was the first to express doubt. “It would,” Xiao said. “Foreigners have another set of rules. Fighting a war is one thing; saving civilians is another. If this rice were military provisions, they could not properly escort it. If it is to save the common people, of course they can.” Hu was delighted to hear this. Still, there was a question. “How should we put the request, and whom should we approach?” “I can go myself,” Xiao volunteered, but immediately added, “Only I must consult my teacher first.”

“Your teacher will certainly approve,” Brother You put in. “But I am still doubtful. I fear there may be no such good fortune.” “Then we can try both approaches at once,” Hu said, asking Xiao, “What if we paid Ward ourselves to send a few dozen men to protect us? Could we try that?” “There is no harm in trying,” Xiao replied. “But I think it would be difficult, and for two reasons.” First, Wu Xu, the Shanghai circuit intendant, had spoiled Ward’s mercenaries. Even a great deal of money might not persuade them to risk their lives. Second, they were infantry; whether they could make their strength felt on the water was very doubtful. “That is sound reasoning.” Hu was always ready to acknowledge another man’s merits, and he greatly admired Xiao. “Jiaji, I must ask you to give me all the help you can with this.” “You are too kind, Mr Hu. Whatever needs doing, you have only to tell me.”

One man appreciated the other’s ability; the other looked up to him. Seeing this, Brother You conceived a plan of his own. He kept it to himself for the moment, intending to wait until Gu returned. “I saw Governor Xue,” Gu reported, plainly unhappy. “Little Uncle, if Mr Xuexuan hopes for any substantial help from him, I fear he is dreaming.” “What did he say?” Hu asked steadily.

The question was enough to make Gu angry all over again. Xue Huan had recited a long catalogue of his own troubles. He blamed Wang Youling for failing to raise and train troops in Zhejiang and for sending all his military funds to southern Anhui and Jiangxi; now that the situation had deteriorated, Shanghai too was under pressure. He also recalled how overbearing and obstinate Wang had been during his time in Jiangsu. The clear implication was that Wang had brought his present plight on himself. “And one can hardly blame Xue for being in a bad temper,” Gu added. “Beijing has been turned upside down. Two princes have lost their lives, and Prince Gong is now in power. A new sovereign brings a new set of ministers. Zeng Guofan will soon be coming to Liangjiang too. Both seals in Governor Xue’s hands—acting governor-general of Liangjiang and substantive governor of Jiangsu—look in danger of slipping away. Naturally he is unhappy.” “I know,” Hu replied. “Before you came back, Brother Five, Jiaji, and I had already discussed things. We never intended to rely on him. But he is still the governor of Jiangsu, and we have no choice but to ask him to forward Mr Xuexuan’s memorial to the throne. Has he done that?” “He would not dare refuse. He has even given orders for the dispatch urging Li Yuandu to hurry. I was afraid the clerks might be slow, so I spoke to them specially. They promised to send all the official papers out together tomorrow.” “Then let us leave that alone and discuss our own plans.”

Brother You and Xiao Jiaji gave Gu a full account of their earlier discussion. It was a great comfort to him. Wasting so much valuable time waiting to see Xue Huan had left him not only angry but seriously anxious. Now it appeared that several courses were open; they were by no means at a loss where to begin. His worries lifted, and his spirits revived.

“In that case, we must first decide what we mean to do. As for an escort, if we can persuade the British and French admirals to send troops, we shall have a force strong enough to make the voyage safe, and it will cost us nothing. But there are two difficulties. First, they may still require an official request from the governor of Jiangsu. Second, it cannot be arranged in three or five days.” “A delay is out of the question!” Hu Xueyan answered at once. “Every day seems like a year to me. I wish I could leave tomorrow.” “Then the only quick solution is to hire some of Ward’s men. That will probably cost a considerable sum.” “How much?” “It depends on how many we hire. At least thirty taels a man, with a thousand in compensation for each man killed. For fifty men, that means fifteen hundred at the very least. And if—” If they were all killed, another fifty thousand would be payable in compensation. Only as the words reached his lips did Gu Yingchun realize how ominous they sounded. If that happened, Hu himself would surely not survive. He swallowed the rest of the sentence.

Hu took no offence. “Fifteen hundred it is, then. The officer in command will need an extra payment, of course. I do not mind. But”—he pointed to Xiao Jiaji—“his concern is sound. Men accustomed to fighting ashore may find, once they are aboard a boat, that all their strength and courage are of no use.” “We shall have to ask them. A generous reward may bring brave men forward, but money cannot buy back a life. Unless they feel reasonably certain, they will not agree rashly.
