We are outsiders; there is no need to imagine that we know better.” Gu’s last remark had something of a master’s admonition to his pupil about it, and Xiao, who had been preparing to offer an opinion, could hardly speak now.

“I know Yang Fang, as it happens. We are not intimate, but he has been kind enough to think well of me. I shall call on him tonight.” “Good! We shall divide the work. Let us settle it now: I shall see to the rice and the sea-going junks; you arrange for the foreign officers.” Brother You turned to Gu. “One other thing: you must get some ready funds together.” “That presents no difficulty.” Hu took a ring from his hand and gave it to Gu. “You know the firms with which I do business. Find out how much money I have with them and draw on it as you need.” The ring was of pure gold. If it did not weigh a whole tael, it weighed at least eight mace. It was enormous, and equally ostentatious. In fact, however, it was a seal. The four characters on its face, “Seal of Hu Xueyan,” cut in relief in seal script, could summon eighty or a hundred thousand taels almost at a word. Gu’s own resources were sufficient without it. “There is no need. You never take that ring off. Keep it yourself.” “No,” Hu replied. “I have another reason. If all goes well on this return to Hangzhou, well and good. But if we never meet again, I entrust everything to you. Tomorrow I shall give you a list of what others owe me and what I owe them.” This provision for his own death distressed everyone. Gu was even more unwilling to accept the seal ring. Taking Hu’s hand, he tried to force it back onto his finger. They were still struggling over it when the Seventh Lady returned and naturally asked what was going on. Knowing how deeply she felt things, and that the truth would grieve her, they exchanged a glance and put her off with some casual explanation.

“The food is ordered: seafood banquets at eight taels a table. We have booked forty tables,” the Seventh Lady announced. “The proprietor says he will lose money, but he wants to make his name through this engagement. Since he attaches so much importance to it, we shall look foolish if too few people come and the occasion falls flat. Brother Five, I am a little worried.” “What is there to worry about? We are inviting people to a gathering and a feast. Do you suppose we cannot find enough guests? I shall speak to Li Delong presently.” “And Chief Yu?” “You need worry even less about him. This plan of Little Uncle’s is just what he could wish for. Plenty of his people are sure to come.” Brother You added, “If you are still uneasy, I shall have Li Delong spread the word that we have taken over the Taihe Restaurant to entertain the whole Sea-Junk Guild. Anyone who stays away will be showing contempt for us.” “Good. I shall send word for another ten tables to be held in readiness.” With that, she went out again.

“Sister Seven is a delightful woman,” Hu said with a smile. “So fond of a lively gathering—she is surely born to good fortune.” “Never mind that. There is another matter. Yingchun, what do you think of this?” Brother You continued, “Little Uncle needs men to help him. To be frank, neither you nor I would be much use on the journey. I shall send Li Delong, and you can send Xiao Jiaji to accompany him all the way to Hangzhou.” “Well…” Gu looked hesitant. “No, no, there is no need,” Hu hastened to say, sensitive as ever to other people’s difficulties. Gu was genuinely in a quandary. His relationship with Xiao differed from those in the Grain-Boat Guild, where disciples were received in a formal initiation, a master’s command carried absolute weight, and men thought nothing of risking death. Xiao was merely his apprentice in foreign languages and business. In a matter of life and death, he could not compel him; he must ask the young man himself. Yet his friendship with Hu was so deep that, wherever he could help, he was bound to do all he could. He could not bring himself to refuse. Moreover, although Hu said there was no need, his face betrayed disappointment, which made Gu feel worse. After considering the matter, he could only speak frankly. “Little Uncle, if I had a brother of my own, I would certainly send him with you. Jiaji may be my apprentice, but he belongs to the Xiao family. Let me ask him.” Then it struck him that this too was ill judged: if Xiao proved afraid and refused, would it not make his apprentice seem unworthy? He added an excuse to cover a possible refusal. “His mother is ill in bed. If her condition permits, I am sure he will go.” He had scarcely finished when Xiao’s voice came from outside the door. Arriving just in time to hear, he volunteered at once. “I shall go! Of course I shall go!” This solved Gu’s difficulty and did him credit as well. Hu, however, still had to make a polite refusal. He knew that the sick mother was an excuse Gu had provided out of consideration for his apprentice, leaving him a way to retreat. But even men who understood one another still had to observe the proper forms; and this particular formality could not be left to Gu. Hu must say the words himself.

“Jiaji, I know you are loyal to your friends. But loyalty and filial duty come first. Since your mother is unwell, you ought to stay and look after her.” “It is nothing serious!” Xiao, quick to take the hint, answered promptly. “She has an old stomach complaint. She will be better in two or three days.” “Then it is settled.” Gu rose. “Since you are going, you had better be involved in the arrangements from the beginning. Come with me to see Mr Yang at Daji.” Daji was the name of Yang Fang’s firm, which dealt exclusively in goods for the foreign trade. When master and apprentice arrived, Yang was giving a large dinner for visiting merchants. They could quite properly have accepted his invitation to join the company and exchanged the usual courtesies, but Gu wished to make clear the urgency of his business. He declined politely but firmly, adding that he had an awkward favour to ask. Naturally, it would have to be discussed in private. “Certainly!” Yang readily agreed. “Come this way.” They sat close together in a corner of the reception room. Gu came straight to the point. Yang drew in his breath, looking as though the request presented considerable difficulty. “Brother Yang, forgive me for saying something more that I ought perhaps to leave unsaid. Eastern and western Zhejiang share their fortunes and their troubles. For the sake of the people of your own province, you must somehow find a way.” “Of course I shall try. Of course I shall.” Yang repeated the assurance several times. “The difficulty is that Ward has recently quarrelled with Intendant Wu. Foreigners can be very stubborn. Get on the right side of him, and anything is possible; cross him, and it is hard to make him listen. The only thing to do now is send someone to arrange a meeting with him tonight. Once I have finished attending to my guests, we can go together. Meanwhile, stay and have a little food and wine.”

At this, Gu could hardly decline again. He joined the dinner and exchanged courtesies while turning over in his mind the problem of persuading Ward. Suddenly Xiao said, “Master, I think I ought to go back for a while and return later. I must question Mr Hu carefully.” “About what?” “Foreigners are very thorough in their work, and this involves fighting. They are sure to want a precise account of the conditions along the route. Otherwise they will never agree.” “Exactly!” Yang said approvingly. “This young fellow has excellent judgement. Go quickly, then. Will two hours be enough for your questions?” “Quite enough.” “Good. I shall arrange to meet Ward at nine. Whatever happens, be back here by half past eight.”

Xiao returned before the appointed time. Besides obtaining answers to all the questions he had thought necessary, he brought a message from Hu. “Master, Mr Hu asked me to tell you: ‘A challenge will move a commander more readily than a request.’” This had the true flavour of a strategist’s advice: directing events from the command tent, he had sent out a secret prescription for victory at a distance. As the carriage jolted along, Gu pondered Hu’s brief maxim over and over. Ward was encamped near Jing’an Temple in western Shanghai. Yang was a familiar visitor. The sentry at the gate held up a lantern, took a look, and waved them through. The carriage drove straight to Ward’s office.

After the introductions, the four men sat around a small round table. Yang began by saying that Gu was one of the representatives of the Zhejiang authorities and had a favour to ask. Gu then took over, first clarifying his position. The authorities’ official representative, he explained, was Hu Xueyan, a highly successful merchant who held office under the Qing government; he himself had been appointed to act on Hu’s behalf. At this, Ward raised his first question. “Why has Mr Hu sent a representative?” “He has been wounded, quite seriously. He hopes to return within three to five days, and to do so he must obey the doctor’s orders and remain absolutely still. He is recovering at my house.” “I see.” Ward appeared to understand. “Please go on.” Gu explained his purpose. Along with his request, he offered a generous compliment: Ward, he was sure, would support this charitable undertaking on humanitarian grounds, and his brave men would certainly carry out the mission successfully.

Before Gu was halfway through, Ward was already shaking his head. As soon as he finished, Ward answered coldly, “I am sorry. You have my sympathy, but I can do nothing to help you.” “That is a great disappointment. May I ask why you cannot help?” “Certainly. First, Zhejiang lies outside the area in which I am supposed to deploy troops. And the mission is dangerous. I cannot be sure of success.” “Your first reason hardly seems valid. As I have already explained, this is a charitable mission—” “No!” Ward interrupted. “I have my responsibilities.” “Are they not to support the established authorities—to assist the Chinese government?” “Yes,” Ward said reluctantly. “But Shanghai must come first.” “Detaching fifty men would scarcely impair your strength.” “Whether it would or not is for me to judge.”

“Colonel,” Yang put in soothingly, “everyone has placed great hopes in you. You should not refuse so categorically.” “No!” Ward continued to shake his head. “The mission is too dangerous. It is a worthless risk.” “It is not dangerous.” Gu pointed to Xiao. “He can explain all the circumstances.” “No. I do not need his explanation.”

This flat refusal to hear them, with its unmistakable suggestion of contempt, angered Gu. Remembering Hu’s advice, he immediately saw what to do. With a cold laugh and a face like frost, he turned to Yang. “You cannot believe what people say. We are told that these foreign officers uphold justice, hasten to help those in distress, and are full of loyalty and courage. Who would have thought it was nothing of the kind? They are merely a pack of cowardly, money-grubbing mercenaries!” At the last words, Ward’s expression changed violently. He sprang up and glared down at Gu. “Whom are you calling cowardly, money-grubbing mercenaries?” “You ought to know.” “I certainly do!” Ward roared. “You must apologize. We are not mercenaries.” “Then you are regular troops?” “Of course.” “Regular troops must take orders from someone. Tell me, are you not subject to the Chinese authorities? Is it Xue or Wu? Just give me the name, and I shall find a way.” This touched Ward’s most vulnerable point. If he admitted that someone could command him, they could simply have that person issue an order. Would that not diminish his own standing? Gu continued, “To be fair, perhaps I went too far in accusing you of greed. But I do not admit that I was wrong to call you a coward.” “That is your greatest mistake. Do you realize what an insult it is to call a soldier a coward?”

Gu gave no ground, meeting him word for word. “If it is an insult, your own conduct has invited it.” “What!” Ward seized him by the shoulder and shook him hard. “Tell me! How have I shown cowardice?” Seeing violence threaten his master, Xiao immediately stepped between them. Yang also tried to intervene, but Ward was strong and furious, and would not let go. Gu remained perfectly composed. “Cowards are always brave enough in private quarrels,” he said coldly. At that, Ward released him. Turning to Yang, he declared, “I must maintain my authority. This man has insulted not merely an individual but the entire force. It is a serious matter. Unless he has a satisfactory explanation, he will be responsible for all the consequences.” Yang had no idea what Gu was trying to do and could not help reproaching him. “This is hardly the way to proceed. We came to ask a favour. Why provoke an open quarrel? It will be difficult to put matters right now.” He had spoken in Chinese, and Gu answered in the same language. “Do not worry. This is precisely the position I wanted to force him into. Of course I have a satisfactory explanation.” Yang knew nothing of the further purpose behind Hu’s clever prescription of challenging a commander rather than petitioning him. He could only urge Gu repeatedly, and with great earnestness, to keep calm and avoid a dispute.

“Set your mind at rest. Unless he refuses to listen to reason, I shall convince him.” Having said this in Chinese, Gu turned to Ward and continued in English. “Colonel, hundreds of thousands of people in Hangzhou are on the verge of starvation. They need food as you and I need air at this moment. With your help, we could risk this passage and carry grain into the city, saving hundreds of thousands of lives. Is that a ‘worthless risk’?” The question silenced Ward. He rolled a cigarette, lit it at the lamp, and sent his answer out through a thick cloud of smoke. “There is no possibility of success.” “Let us leave the possibility aside for a moment. Answer my question first. If the risk brought success, would it be worth taking?” Cornered, Ward could only admit, “If it succeeded, of course it would.”

“Excellent!” Gu seized on the admission. “I regard you as a man of worth, and naturally you would wish to do something worthwhile. You will recall that I told you the mission was not dangerous. Xiao could explain all the circumstances. Yet you refused even to consider it. At the mere mention of the Taiping forces, you lost your nerve—” “Who says so!” Ward protested. “You are slandering me.” “I should like you to demonstrate your courage by your actions—and show your worth.” “Very well!” Stung into agreement, Ward blurted out, “Let me hear the circumstances first.” He rose and went over to a map. Delighted by the change and filled with admiration, Yang hurriedly picked up the lamp from the table and motioned Xiao to explain. Gu joined them. In the lamplight, they all studied the map pinned to the wall, drawn on a sheet of thick foreign paper. It was far more complicated than a Chinese map, and the many little red and blue triangular flags made it harder still to understand. Xiao, however, often looked at navigation charts aboard steamships. After studying it briefly, he understood it.

“We shall encounter no enemy at sea. The possible danger begins here.” Xiao pointed to Biezimen. “In fact, there is only one stretch that presents much danger. The sea is wide, and the Taiping forces have no gunboats, so they cannot threaten our vessels. Only here, where hills on the north and south constrict the channel, is there a narrow passage. This is what produces the famous Zhejiang tidal bore. Once we have passed through it, the river widens again, and the danger is over.” “Then how wide is the river at that passage?”

“It has never been measured. But even if a shot fired from the shore reached a vessel, it would have lost its force.” Ward shook his head. “Rifles do not worry me.” Then he asked, “Are there any batteries?” “Certainly not,” Gu answered from beside him. “Even without permanent batteries, there must be temporary gun positions. If I were there, I should certainly deploy artillery at this point.” “You must not rate the Taiping forces too highly,” Gu said. “They cannot understand your methods of warfare.” Ward thought there was truth in this. He had fought the rebels many times and knew something of their abilities. They were not even fully proficient with foreign firearms; naturally, they would not understand the tactics of holding a strategic passage by concentrated fire. Moreover, even if they did understand, there would be no reason to defend this particular passage. The Qing forces in the area were weak, and there was no naval force likely to pass through it to relieve Hangzhou. To station guns there would be to put valuable weapons where they served no purpose.

Still, the principle that victory favoured the side which planned more carefully was common to Chinese and foreign military thinking. Ward preferred to take the safer course. “Is this narrow passage very long?” he asked. “No,” Gu estimated. “Eight or ten li at most.” “And what sort of vessels will you use?” “Sea-going junks.” These were the flat-bottomed trading vessels known as shachuan. Ward was trained for land warfare and knew little about ships, but he understood the effect of a favourable or contrary wind. Pointing to the map, he said, “This is the season of northwesterly winds. Sailing from east to west, you will have the wind against you.” “On that point,” Gu answered cautiously, “I do not think you need be too anxious. Besides sails, there must be other ways of helping the vessels along. These sea-going junks are large and strongly built. Their hulls alone provide considerable protection. I should think them fairly safe.” “I must look into that further. I shall have to discuss it with the commander of the flotilla. Ideally, we should slip through the passage at night and avoid a direct engagement with the Taiping forces.” His tone already suggested agreement to provide an escort. Yang spoke with delight. “Thank you, Colonel! Shall we settle the matter today—the number of men and the contribution you would like towards their pay?”

“You asked for fifty men. I shall give you fifty. Discuss the other details with my quartermaster tomorrow.” “Certainly!” Yang replied gladly. “We shall do exactly as you wish.” Hostility had given way to friendship. Gu too smiled, offered his thanks, and took his leave. “Gu,” Yang said admiringly in the carriage, “you really know how to handle a situation. We must work together more often.” “It was good luck, with some guidance from a wiser man. Intendant Hu gave me one piece of advice: a challenge will move a commander more readily than a request. And it did move Ward.” “So Intendant Hu is skilled in dealing with foreigners as well.” “He knows little of foreign affairs. What he understands through and through is human nature.” “I must meet him sometime,” Yang said. “Ward’s quartermaster is Chinese, and I know him extremely well. Tomorrow evening I shall invite him to dine with courtesans. Everything will be easy to discuss.” “Excellent. I ought to be the host. Tomorrow morning I shall send the invitations to your house and ask you to arrange it.”

“It makes no difference whether you give the dinner or I do. Let us leave that aside. There is something I should like to ask you. Is Hangzhou not under siege? Once the grain boats get there, how will the rice enter the city?” Gu was taken aback. “Ah!” he said, as though waking from a dream. “Of course. I had not thought of that. I shall ask when I get back and let you know.” “Could you give me a definite answer tonight?” It seemed premature to worry about what would happen once the boats reached Hangzhou. Besides, whether the rice could enter the city was no concern of Yang’s. Why did he want an answer so urgently? There seemed to be another purpose behind it, and Gu felt he must find out. “Why the hurry?” “I have another idea. If the rice can enter Hangzhou, there is nothing more to say. Otherwise—” Yang broke off to ask, “Could you introduce me to him now? I should like to discuss it with Intendant Hu himself.” “Why not?”

The carriage changed direction and drove straight to Gu’s house. As soon as it stopped, Xiao ran inside to announce the visitor. A great stickler for courtesy, Hu wanted to get out of bed and receive him in the reception room. The Seventh Lady firmly opposed this. They finally compromised: he might get up, but must remain in his bedroom and receive the guest there. Naturally, the women withdrew. By the time Gu ushered Yang inside, Hu had put on a long gown and riding jacket and was waiting at the doorway, leaning on Xiao’s shoulder. Each had long known the other by reputation. Once the formal greetings were over, they spoke with great warmth and mutual admiration, their conversation continuing for some time. Gu found an opening to give a brief account of the negotiations with Ward. Hu had already heard the outline from Xiao, but now he naturally thanked Yang most earnestly. “It concerns our home province. I ought to do what I can. But”—Yang turned abruptly to the business that concerned him—“once the grain boats reach Hangzhou, how is the rice to enter the city?”

At this, Hu’s face immediately clouded. He sighed. “That was another mistake. Before the city was closed, the high officials disagreed. Some wanted all ten gates shut; others thought one or two should be left open. In the end, they shut them all. As soon as that happened, the rebels dug trenches and put up wooden barriers outside. They sealed us in completely.” Having said this in one breath, he paused for air, then continued. “Someone had proposed building a sloping causeway from the city wall right down to the river, to serve as a supply route. It sounded so extraordinary that everyone laughed. The work would also have been enormous, so nothing was done. Looking back now, it was an excellent idea. It would have taken some trouble, but with the supply route open, Hangzhou could have held out.” He gave another long sigh. Gu was the first to show alarm. “Little Uncle, if that is so, will all our efforts be wasted?” “Not necessarily. Once the grain boats arrive, the people inside will fight their way out at all costs and escort the grain into the city.” Yang nodded, glanced at Gu, and seemed about to speak, then stopped. Watching his expression, Hu knew he had something to say.

“Brother Yang, we have met as though we were old friends. Please speak freely.” “The situation is this. Naturally, I too want our fellow provincials in Hangzhou to have enough food to stay alive. But one must allow for the worst. Suppose you get the grain boats to Hangzhou after endless hardships, only to find all communication between the city and the outside world cut off. What will you do then, Mr Hu?” “What would you advise?” “Speaking as a businessman, you can hardly hand all that rice to the rebels or throw it into the river. If it cannot enter Hangzhou, could I ask you to divert it to Ningbo?” So this was why he had been so eager to meet Hu. Gu thought to himself that Yang was a formidable fellow, shrewd in every calculation, and not to be underestimated. He listened closely for Hu’s reply. “That is a practical suggestion. If the rice cannot enter Hangzhou, of course I shall send it elsewhere. So long as it does not fall into rebel hands, any destination will do.” Then Hu qualified his answer. “But I cannot promise what you ask just yet. Why? Because I do not know the situation in Ningbo. If I gave you my word and then could not fulfil it, I should be breaking faith.” “The situation in Ningbo is much like Shanghai’s—”

Ningbo too had a foreign settlement. Just as wealthy Jiangsu families had fled to Shanghai, the rich households of eastern Zhejiang had taken refuge in the Ningbo settlement. As early as the summer, Ningbo’s gentry had submitted proposals to the local authorities, offering to raise five hundred thousand taels to hire British and French warships to defend the city. After Xiaoshan and Shaoxing fell, the Taiping army swept eastward, taking Yuyao, Cixi, and Fenghua in rapid succession. Ningbo might fall at any moment. The British, French, and American consuls therefore conferred and decided to send representatives to Fenghua to meet the Taiping commander, Fan Ruzeng, and urge him to postpone an attack on Ningbo. Fan gave no direct answer to the request, but promised to protect foreigners. The three consuls had consequently issued a joint proclamation guaranteeing the safety of the settlement. Yet land communications were virtually severed, travelling merchants dared not move, and the city was suffering a severe grain shortage. Yang’s purpose was partly to help his native place. It was also to obtain a good price and make a profitable transaction out of the opportunity.

His private interest, of course, was something Yang had no intention of revealing. “Mr Hu, I understand that many Hangzhou people have taken refuge in Ningbo as well. If you cannot achieve your first object, sending the grain there is really the only alternative.” “And when it reaches Ningbo? What if it cannot be landed?” “That will not happen. Any one of the three consuls—British, French, or American—can arrange protection for you. Naturally, I shall make the necessary contacts.” “In that case—” Hu sprang to his feet. A plan had occurred to him, and in his excitement he forgot his injured leg. He fell to the floor, pain bringing beads of sweat as large as soybeans to his forehead. Xiao quickly stepped forward to help him up. Gu, badly startled, examined the injury. Only after the commotion subsided could Hu finish what he had been saying. “Brother Yang, in that case, let us make a bargain. Hangzhou is short of grain; so is Ningbo. We can work together. I shall undertake to send a consignment of rice to Ningbo and find both the rice and the vessels. In return, could you ask the foreigners to intervene on charitable grounds and escort this consignment of mine into Hangzhou?”

“That proposal…” Yang looked at Gu, visibly uneasy. “Little Uncle, when it comes to business and ingenuity, we must acknowledge you as a Zhuge Liang,” Gu said tactfully. “Unfortunately, you are something of a novice in foreign affairs. This cannot be done.” “Why not?” “Foreign consuls need grounds for intervening. Grain can be sent to Ningbo in the name of protecting their nationals, since foreigners must have food. Hangzhou is different. There are no British, French, or American residents there in need of relief. As for feeding Chinese civilians, it depends on the place. In a war zone, there is no way to distinguish food for civilians from provisions for troops.” When Gu had finished, Yang added, “In the eighth month, the British government sent its minister orders to maintain strict neutrality. That means helping neither side. Your proposal is excellent, Mr Hu, but unfortunately it is impracticable.” Hu was disappointed, of course, but did not want to show it. Returning to Yang’s request, he declared generously, “Then it is settled. If this rice cannot enter Hangzhou, we shall divert it to Ningbo. But Chief Yu must be told beforehand. If the junk owners refuse to discharge at a different destination, we shall have an argument on our hands.” “Of course I shall ask Brother Yingchun to make that clear. What I should like your instructions on is the price of the rice—”

“That is no difficulty!” Hu cut in firmly. “We can settle it however you wish. If this were business, every fraction of a tael would have to be reckoned. But this is not business.” “Yes, yes.” Yang felt an inward pang of shame. Almost to himself, he said, “Of course, we are doing a good deed.” With that, the discussion reached a conclusion. Afraid that Hu was tiring himself and delaying his recovery, Gu invited Yang into the reception room. There, with Xiao, they settled the details of their further dealings with Ward. The party did not break up until late at night.

Next day everyone went off on his separate business. Hu alone remained at Gu’s house to recover, with nothing to occupy him. Unable to move freely or leave his room, he found solitude oppressive. He sent especially for the Seventh Lady and could not help voicing a mild complaint. “I did not dare disturb you, Little Uncle. I wanted you to rest and let the wound heal,” she explained. “Talking takes strength too.” “Ah, Sister Seven, you do not know what is on my mind. Left alone, I keep turning things over until I cannot even sleep. Someone to talk to would help the time pass.” Yet they could not talk too deeply. There was no news of any member of Hu’s family; to speak of them was to touch his sorest wound. The Seventh Lady, ordinarily so talkative, found herself tongue-tied, at a loss for anything to say. “Sister Seven,” Hu asked, “have you had any dealings with Madam He lately?” Madam He was A’qiao. After Hu had arranged her match with He Guiqing, she had accompanied him to Tongzhou. Before long, He had indeed left his post as granary vice-minister to become governor of Zhejiang, then governor-general of Liangjiang, rising steadily in the world. For a time A’qiao had enjoyed considerable splendour. “I have not seen her for a long while,” the Seventh Lady said with deep feeling. “Who did not call her fortunate then? When Mr He was in Changzhou, I visited once. She sent an official boat to fetch me from Songjiang, with a hundred soldiers for protection. I enjoyed a grand occasion myself! Mr He was very courteous when we arrived. His wife was often ill, and A’qiao managed everything. Wherever she went, a whole crowd of maids and serving women followed. What airs and grandeur! She had grown prettier too, her hair full of pearls and jade. She looked every inch the wife of a first-rank official. Who could have imagined that Mr He would come to grief? Not long ago someone told me she had aged beyond recognition. They say Wu Zixu’s hair turned white in a single night as he struggled to get through Zhaoguan Pass. It seems such things really do happen.” “Then she has a conscience after all.”

“You mean she has worried herself into this state over Governor-General He?” “Yes. Governor Wang told me the charges against him are extremely grave.” “How grave? Surely they would not behead him?” Hu looked at her and slowly nodded, as if to say that, unbelievable though it might seem, it was quite possible. “They would execute an official as high as that?” The Seventh Lady found it almost inconceivable. “Of course they should!” Hu suddenly became agitated in a way she seldom saw. “Unless one or two heads are taken as an example, the country will never be put right. In ordinary times they lord it over everyone, demanding grain and money, claiming it is all for the court and the people. When the moment comes for them to do their duty, they gather up their valuables and run. If men like that can live comfortably on the money they have squeezed out of others, what justice is there for the men who do their utmost and die fighting?” She had never seen him so fiercely indignant, and the outburst affected her deeply. Thinking too of his own position, she felt a grief she could not express.

“Little Uncle,” she said involuntarily, “I do not think you need return to Hangzhou. Let Brother Five’s disciple and Jiaji escort the grain boats. Stay in Shanghai and let your wound heal. Find a way to locate your mother and bring the whole family here. Would that not be better?” “Thank you, Sister Seven. You are thinking of my interests. But it cannot be done.” “Why not?” she argued vigorously. “On the voyage it makes no difference whether you are there or not. Li Delong and the Sea-Junk Guild will handle the vessels. Jiaji will deal with the troops sent by the foreign commander. You are wounded and need looking after yourself. What help could you give? You would only make more trouble and become a burden.” “That is true. But once they reach Hangzhou, without me to make contact with Governor Wang, the whole undertaking might be ruined.” On reflection, she could see the force of that. “Once you have reached Governor Wang, and the city sends troops out to escort the grain inside, there is nothing more you need do?” “That is so.” “Then do this. Do not enter the city. Return to Shanghai on the same boat. We can discuss the next step and how to find your mother. You could even make enquiries outside Hangzhou yourself. In any case, having got out, you must not walk back into the trap.” “There is truth in that too—”

His tone suggested that an objection was coming. Before he could voice it, the Seventh Lady broke in. “Of course there is! You are a great businessman, Little Uncle. You purchased your rank as intendant. You are not like Governor-General He; you have no responsibility for defending territory.” “It is not entirely a matter of public duty. It is friendship,” Hu replied. “Everything I am today is due to Governor Wang’s support. He is in such desperate straits now, suffering as though he were in the deepest hell. If I will not share his danger, my conscience will never be clear.” “That is loyalty, certainly. But loyalty of that sort serves no purpose. You could do him more good by helping from outside.” Her argument was sound, yet Hu still felt he could not act on it. Ordinarily he was decisive and not easily swayed by sentiment. Even when sentiment did move him, the course of action usually had to make sense in his own calculations first. To put it plainly, he might be said to make use of sentiment. With Wang Youling, however, it was different.

He sighed. “Sister Seven, do you think I cannot see that your advice is good? Good not only for me, but for Governor Wang as well. Yet I simply cannot do it.” “That is strange! If it is good for you and good for him, why not do it? This is not like you.” “No, it is not. But in this one matter, however much I think, I cannot find a way through. First, I fear Governor Wang will say to himself: ‘Hu is no true friend. At the critical moment, he abandoned me and saved himself.’ Second, I fear other people will say I understand wealth and honour but know nothing of friendship that stands the test of life and death.” “Oh!” Growing impatient, she spoke without choosing her words. “Little Uncle, you are being stubborn. How can you listen to everything other people say? As for Governor Wang, if your friendship runs so deep, he ought to understand your heart. Besides, you would not be abandoning him. You would still be working for him outside.” Now she felt she had a great deal more to say. “People must be reasonable. Even the Three Bonds and Five Constant Virtues are useful only when they make sense. I cannot bear those moralizing hypocrites and their unreasonable doctrines: ‘If the ruler orders his minister to die, he must die; if the father orders his son to perish, he must perish.’ Just think! If the loyal minister is dead, who will serve the emperor? If the son dies and the family line ends, where is the filial devotion in that?”

Hu laughed. “Sister Seven, listening to you reason is what we Hangzhou people call crisp and clear. How refreshing!” “Do not flatter me. If you think my words have any sense in them, take my advice!” Having dealt with ruler and minister, father and son, she turned to friendship, the fifth of the human relationships. “I have heard storytellers recite the Three Kingdoms. When Liu Bei, Guan Yu, and Zhang Fei swear brotherhood in the Peach Garden, they say they do not ask to have been born in the same year, month, and day, but wish to die in the same year, month, and day. That makes no sense either! If one loyal friend dies and all the others follow him to the grave, will there not be a world without decent men, with only scoundrels left?” “True enough,” Hu said, thoroughly interested. “One must not examine everything too closely. Follow things to their foundations, and a great many sayings cease to make sense.”

“Exactly! Little Uncle, these last nights I have been thinking about your situation. And if I think of you, naturally I must think of Governor Wang. You once told me that he urged Governor-General He not to flee Changzhou, warning that flight would ruin his name and standing. That prediction has come true. If Hangzhou cannot be saved, I am sure Governor Wang will never flee. He will be a great loyal servant of the dynasty. But consider this for his sake: what friend has he to take care of his affairs after his death? Who but you?” Hu looked up, visibly moved. “Sister Seven,” he said uneasily, “you have reminded me of something important.” “Thank Heaven!” She pressed her palms together at her breast and let out a long breath. “At last you have seen reason.” “Not quite. But this really is something I must think about carefully.”

He continued talking with her while turning the matter over in his mind. It appeared that she was entirely right: Wang Youling was determined to fulfil the role of a loyal minister. Only from outside could one see how perilous Hangzhou’s position was. Those shut inside the besieged city had only one thought: once reinforcements arrived, the siege would be lifted. Yet even if Li Yuandu’s newly raised army at Quzhou could fight its way to Hangzhou, it might not be able to drive off the many surrounding rebel forces. The city’s fall was only a matter of time; so was Wang’s death in the cause of loyalty. Quite apart from the eyes of the whole city, which were fixed upon him and would not permit him to flee, he could not run even if an opportunity presented itself. Escape would make all his suffering meaningless. And if he lived on as He Guiqing now did, what pleasure would there be in life? “I have thought it through,” Hu said. “Governor Wang is bound to die. I must make his death count.”

“Exactly!” The Seventh Lady was immensely relieved. “I always said you were an exceptionally clever man. How could you fail to grasp so simple a truth? People speak of friendship through life and death. If, after a man dies, someone still treats him as faithfully as when he was alive, he is a fortunate man.” “Yes. Once he dies for his duty, everything will fall to me. My only fear is—” Though he stopped, his meaning was as plain as if he had finished. It was not that he himself shrank from mentioning an ill omen; he feared distressing her. But in such circumstances a woman of her temperament would naturally have something direct to say. “Little Uncle, set your mind at ease on that score. If anything happens to you, my brother, my husband, and I will take everything upon ourselves.” “Good!” Hu drew a great breath. “With that assurance from you, Sister Seven, there is nowhere I would fear to venture.” This again was unwelcome language. Uneasily, she asked, “Little Uncle, what sort of venture do you mean?” “I certainly do not mean rushing down a road to death. I mean facing a difficult passage with courage. To tell you the truth, I was rather frightened whenever I encountered the rebels on my way here. Now I am not afraid. The more frightened one is, the more likely one is to make a mistake. Facing things boldly may prove the safer course.”

CHAPTER TWO

They sailed out of the Ji River into the Yangtze, then passed south of Chongming Island and entered the sea. There were eighteen sea junks in all. The foreign escort, as finally agreed, numbered a hundred and twelve: a hundred soldiers, most of them men from Luzon, and twelve officers—seven from Luzon, three Americans and two Chinese serving as liaison officers. They occupied two junks in the middle of the convoy. Hu Xueyan travelled aboard the leading vessel, together with Xiao Jiaji, Li Delong, the skipper Li Qingshan whom Yu Fuhua had sent, and a liaison officer surnamed Kong. These five settled every question of movement and action aboard that vessel and issued the orders. As soon as he came aboard, Hu was warned of the many taboos observed when sea junks sailed at sea. The little shrine on the steering deck, containing a spirit tablet of the Queen of Heaven, was especially sacred. Remembering the saying that trouble comes from speaking too much, Hu said little. In his spare time he merely lay on his bunk, absorbed in thought. The others behaved differently. Xiao was accustomed to life afloat, but steamships had none of these taboos, and Kong cared even less about them. Li Qingshan and Li Delong knew which subjects to avoid; they refrained from saying what ought not to be said but otherwise talked as usual. Beside them, Hu, normally so lively and entertaining, seemed weighed down by cares and sunk in the deepest gloom.

Kong suggested mahjong. Hoping to relieve Hu’s loneliness, Xiao went specially to invite him to join them. “How can five of us play? Unless one man has a—” Hu stopped short. He remembered travelling on a floating pleasure boat, where the word “dream” was taboo and had to be replaced by “golden millet”. “Unless one man has his golden millet,” he finished. Xiao stared, then understood. “There is no need,” he said. “I do not wish to play. Come, Mr Hu. It will relieve the tedium.” Indifferent either way, Hu joined the game. Halfway through, a gale and heavy seas forced them to stop. He returned to his bunk to sleep. He was anxious enough already; now, unused to the violence of the wind and waves, he began vomiting miserably. His whole being was so unsettled that life seemed intolerable, while death offered no escape. “There is nothing to worry about, Mr Hu!” Xiao came again and again to comfort him. Nor did he offer words alone: he attended to all Hu’s needs, with the same affectionate respect he showed Gu Yingchun. Hu was deeply touched. There was much he wanted to say, but he felt too ill to make the effort.

By nightfall the wind and waves had subsided. A bright moon rose out of the sea, and Hu’s seasickness vanished without medicine. Now, however, hunger tormented him. He remembered the box of foreign biscuits that the Seventh Lady had put into his mesh basket with her own hands. He got up to search for it and woke Xiao, who had been sleeping soundly. “It is only me,” he said apologetically. “I was looking for something dry to eat.” “You feel better, Mr Hu!” Xiao said delightedly. “They kept some congee for you in the stern cabin. I shall fetch it.” He lit a storm lantern and went aft, returning with the congee, a plate of salted fish and a salted egg. Hu ate everything, wiped his mouth and smiled. “In times of war and famine, one cannot be too particular about how one lives.”

“What matters in a man’s life is not that sort of thing, but his heart,” Xiao said. “Governor Wang showed sound judgement in choosing a friend like you.” “It is no use!” Hu’s face darkened. “We do what we can and leave the rest to Heaven. Even if we reach Hangzhou, who knows what we shall find? The city may be falling at this very moment.” “No,” Xiao said consolingly. “We must always look towards the brighter possibility.” “True!” Hu was easily encouraged. “Hope is what keeps a man alive. Jiaji, tell me: what do you mean to do in the future?” Xiao felt that at last he had found someone who understood him. Not even Gu had ever asked him that question. All his great ambitions had remained unspoken; now, unexpectedly, he had a chance to pour them out. “One day I mean to compete with the foreigners. I am always asking myself: why should we not do what they can do? Chinese minds are no worse than theirs. We simply fail to work together. I want to find a few men who share my aims, join forces with them and see how we measure up against the foreigners.” “That is ambition!” Hu exclaimed in admiration. “Count me in. Tell me how you propose to compete.” “In business, naturally. They come here to trade; we can go to their countries and do the same. For the present, Chinese business ought to be conducted by Chinese, and Chinese money earned by Chinese. So long as the advantage stays in our hands, I need not be the one to prosper.” Hu considered this for a moment. “What breadth of spirit! I shall certainly help you. What business could we begin taking from foreign hands now?” “Steamships, first of all—”

From that day, Hu and Xiao discussed plans for establishing a steamship company. Only when the junks were about to enter Biezimen did their discussions cease. According to their plan, they slipped through under cover of darkness. Once through the narrows, they were out of danger and sailed southwest along the Qiantang. A favourable northeasterly wind soon brought them to Hangzhou, where they anchored in midstream. But Hu did not know how to communicate with the city. Looking from the river towards Fengshan Gate, they saw the Longhairs massed outside it, apparently stretching unbroken for tens of li. No one dared go ashore rashly. “The arrangement was that Governor Wang would send someone to contact me. He warned me on no account to land,” Hu said. “All I can do is wait, and wait, and wait!” Wang Youling expected the grain boats to arrive shortly. His whole effort now went into opening a passage to the riverbank through which the provisions could enter the city. But all ten gates were tightly besieged, and the defenders had exhausted their resources for both attack and defence. Anxiety and anger made him ill. His liver fire rose, and he repeatedly vomited blood, a bowlful at a time. The loss left him dizzy and faint, his face as yellow as gold paper. Yet he would not leave the wall to rest. Rest would do no good; better to force himself to hold on, and at least encourage the troops by his presence.

The defenders’ spirit, born of desperation, was not bad. Yet the saying was sound: even an emperor does not send hungry soldiers on a mission. Fighting requires strength. With empty bellies and weapons in their hands, the men could scarcely run, let alone kill an enemy. Each day they sallied out, but the moment the Longhairs drew back, the imperial troops sounded the gong to recall their own men. This stalemate continued for a long time without achieving anything, while more and more people starved inside the city. At first, charitable souls who could not bear to see the bodies exposed dug graves and buried them. Eventually the dead were too numerous to bury, and they had to leave them where they lay. Bodies beyond counting filled the streets and alleys. Fortunately it was winter, so no epidemic had yet broken out, but the stench throughout the city was torment enough. By the end of the tenth month, the Longhairs had overrun all the imperial camps outside the walls except one: Deputy Commander Zeng Desheng’s battalion stood firm outside Houchao Gate. Its survival seemed a miracle, though the explanation was simple. Food was easier to find outside the city; when none could be found elsewhere, the men sought it in the Longhairs’ camps. Death in battle and death from starvation were equally death. Better, then, to seize the enemy’s provisions and seek a chance of life in mortal danger. Zeng’s men accordingly fought with a courage that made them appear to welcome death as a homecoming. Strangely enough, the Longhairs lost heart at the sight of the flag bearing the character “Zeng” and often fled without fighting. Yet this single battalion could do no more than preserve itself. The disparity in strength was too great for it to launch an offensive and break the enemy. It was powerless to do more.

Wang nonetheless placed enormous hopes in it. He specially ordered Wu Baofeng, magistrate of Renhe, to move a cannon weighing three thousand jin from City God Hill to Zeng’s camp, at immense effort. Trained on the Longhairs’ fortifications, it thundered away until no enemy remained in that immediate area. Still they could not reach the riverbank. Beyond the cannon’s range the Longhairs remained as numerous as hairs on an ox, blocking every approach in layer upon layer. The encirclement could not be broken. Just then a spy was caught. Spies were easy to recognise: the people inside the city were either bloated in the face or wasted to skin and bone; they could scarcely shuffle a few inches and spoke in voices too weak to hear. Anyone of normal complexion who walked easily and could be heard without straining one’s ears must have slipped in from outside. What purpose could draw a man into such a hell on earth? There was no need to ask. Sure enough, a beating soon brought out the truth. The man admitted that the Longhairs had sent him with a letter for a battalion commander under Rao Tingxuan, fixing a date for a coordinated attack from within and without. He also revealed that more than a dozen large vessels, laden with provisions, lay in the Qiantang. Plainly Hu’s grain boats had arrived. Wang’s spirits revived at once. He went immediately to Ruichang, the garrison general of Hangzhou, to discuss how to force a bloody passage through which the grain might be brought into the city. Little discussion was needed. They decided to entrust the task to Deputy Lieutenant-General Jiechun. Indeed, there was probably no one else equal to it. Jiechun was a Mongol whose ancestors had served in the Hangzhou garrison for generations. He had begun as a cavalry officer of the senior sixth rank. Highly skilled in arms and deeply trusted by his men, he had risen through service to the senior fourth rank as a banner commander, and Ruichang relied heavily upon him.

In the spring of the tenth year of Xianfeng, when the Longhairs first breached Hangzhou, Ruichang had prepared to cut his throat and die for his country. Jiechun urged him not to throw away his life. The enemy had advanced rapidly with light cavalry from Guangde in Anhui and had no reinforcements behind them; there was no cause for despair. They should hold their ground and await relief. Ruichang accepted his advice and withdrew into the Manchu garrison beside West Lake. In effect it was a city within the city, commonly called the Manchu City. Its defences were so skilfully managed that six days of Longhair attacks achieved nothing. Jiechun’s eldest son died defending the walls. His father laid out the body without tears, believing that his son had died as a man should, at the proper moment. On the seventh day Zhang Yuliang’s reinforcements arrived. Jiechun burst forth on a charging horse, sweeping aside all opposition. In concert with the relieving army, he launched a great counterattack and drove the Longhairs more than ten li beyond the city. For this service he received the right to wear a peacock feather and was promoted to deputy lieutenant-general of Ningxia, though he remained in Hangzhou as Ruichang’s right-hand man. During the present crisis his distinguished services earned him the title Baturu, “the Brave”, and a transfer to the deputy lieutenant-generalship of Zhapu, an important coastal-defence post. But Zhapu had fallen to the Longhairs, so he stayed to defend the provincial capital. Of Hangzhou’s ten gates, Wulin in the north and Fengshan in the south were the most important. Wang had originally commanded at Fengshan in person. Only recently, exhausted by vomiting blood, had he handed its defence to Jiechun. Hu’s grain ships lay in the river outside that very gate. To send Jiechun to force a passage was therefore the obvious choice, both for the man and for the position he held.

The Longhair commander besieging Fengshan Gate was Chen Bingwen, whose title in the Taiping hierarchy was Langtianyi. He had been about to leave: the Longhairs too were beginning to run short of provisions. Li Xiucheng had already drawn up plans to return to Suzhou for the New Year and resume the campaign the following spring. But Chen learnt from refugees escaping the city that its food was gone and its inhabitants had begun eating one another. He abruptly changed his mind and insisted on staying. Knowing also that Fengshan Gate was the key to the city’s defence, he massed his forces there in layer after layer all the way to the river, concentrating as many as forty thousand men in that tiny area. When he saw the grain boats in the distance, his eagerness to seize them grew fiercer. He prepared against a sortie from the city to collect the provisions while trying every means of capturing the vessels. But the river was broad and Ward’s men guarded them closely. Whenever a small boat drew near, a volley met it. Even if the boat did not sink, its occupants could scarcely escape death or injury. Three days passed without success. Finally someone proposed copying the method by which Cao Cao’s army had been destroyed at Red Cliffs: load small boats with brushwood, soak it in oil and send them downstream from above to burn the grain ships. Chen thought the scheme feasible. But the upper reaches were outside his sector, so he had to make contact with the commanders there and submit the proposal to the Loyal King for a decision. Such arrangements could not be completed in a day or two. Moreover, the weather had grown warmer, the wind was changeable, and many of their own small boats were on the river. What if the clever plan backfired and they burned themselves instead? He hesitated to act. Meanwhile, the men aboard the grain ships could wait no longer.

For Hu, the three days had felt like years. He could neither eat nor sleep. Kong, liaison officer with the foreign escort, believed they were in a dangerous position and that failure to decide quickly might have disastrous consequences. He kept urging Hu to sail for Ningbo, as originally agreed, if the provisions could not be landed. Li Qingshan of the Sea-Junk Guild said nothing, but his face betrayed considerable anxiety. This made Hu still more frantic. His eyes reddened, and he muttered unintelligibly to himself all day. He looked close to madness. Unable to comfort him, Xiao turned to Li Delong. “Brother Delong, we must find some way out of this. Something will go wrong if we remain bottled up like this.” “Yes, I have been thinking the same thing. But what can we do? We are trapped in the middle of the river.” Li pointed ashore. “The Longhairs are like ants. They have sealed Hangzhou as tight as an iron barrel. How can anyone get out?” “That is precisely the point. If the people inside cannot get out, we must find a way in and obtain a definite answer. If it can be done, good; if it cannot, Mr Hu can make other plans at once. We are waiting like a fool for a wife. How long can this go on?” Li too was young, impatient and willing to take risks. He naturally approved of Xiao’s proposal and volunteered to swim ashore and carry a message into the city. “Brother Delong,” Xiao said calmly, “this is not a question of proving our loyalty, still less of making polite offers to one another. We have to get the task done. Whether you go or I go makes no difference; what matters is which of us is better suited. You swim better than I do, you are more agile, and your skill in a fight is far beyond mine—” “Enough, enough!” Li laughed. “Stop flattering me. The higher you praise me now, the harsher the words that follow. Tell me plainly: can I go or not?” “I am not trying to be roundabout. At a time like this we must not allow feeling or pride to interfere. We are good brothers; why should we not discuss it calmly and frankly? Let me first ask you this, Brother Delong: have you ever been to Hangzhou? Do you know the name of the gate in front of us?”

“No. I have never been to Hangzhou.” “Then you are not quite the right man. Hangzhou was the capital of the Song dynasty. It is a large city, and without knowing it you will have trouble finding your way. But that is the lesser difficulty. The chief point is this: you heard Mr Hu say how strictly they are hunting spies inside the walls. Hunger has inflamed people’s tempers, and they will not listen to reason.” Xiao paused. “Brother Delong, do not be angry if I speak frankly. You have a quick temper, and you are not as ready with words as I am. It would not be wise to send you.” Li was straightforward, willing to listen to reason and accept sound advice. Recognising the truth of Xiao’s words, he answered at once, “Very well. You go.” The two then discussed how Xiao should land, pass through the Longhairs’ positions and make contact beneath the walls to enter the city. Only when these details were largely settled did they take their proposal to Hu. “Mr Hu,” Li began, “there is something we must report. We have worked it all out, and we cannot afford any more delay. Once I have told you, I beg you to agree and not refuse. Please write a letter to Governor Wang. Jiaji will take it into the city.” In fact, Li had misjudged Hu. Hu had long understood that he himself must stay where he was, lest someone from the city should reach the boats after terrible hardships only to miss him, with irreparable consequences. Apart from that, anyone who could possibly carry a message into the city ought to go. At Li’s words, his whole manner changed.

“Steady!” He had recovered his customary calm in the face of great affairs, utterly different from the restless man of the past two days. “First tell me how you intend to get there.” “Swim ashore—” “No, no!” Hu shook his head at the very first sentence. “What will you do once you land, soaked to the skin, even if you do not fall ill from the cold? Do you imagine there is an inn where you can dry your clothes by a fire?” “We meant to act as circumstances required.” “At a time like this we cannot simply trust to luck. We must think everything through before acting,” Hu said. “Listen to me.” He had no prepared plan himself. But he had always been quick-witted; thinking as he spoke, he soon worked out a complete scheme. Its most important point was how to deal with any Longhairs Xiao encountered. Hu suggested a stratagem: pose as the representative of a British merchant in Shanghai and offer to sell them arms.

“You speak English, and you know the foreign firms in Shanghai. You are clever too. You will certainly make a convincing show,” Hu said. “Remember, the Longhairs are provincial fellows as well. Use the foreigners to overawe them.” Once his instructions were given, however, he wrote no letter. This too showed his care: if a letter were found in a search, the whole undertaking would fail and Xiao would lose his life. Yet Xiao needed some token to prove his credentials when he met Wang Youling. The gold ring on Hu’s hand would have been unmistakable, but he feared it would catch a Longhair’s eye and be stolen. After long consideration, he could think of nothing but an oral message. “Before I left, Governor Wang talked with me for a long time. He entrusted all his affairs after his death to me. His youngest son, whom he loves most dearly, is called Tiaoyun and is only five this year. He wanted to place the boy under my name. I said we should discuss it after I returned from Shanghai. No third person knows of this conversation. Tell him, and he will know that I sent you.” This was the best possible proof. Xiao asked exactly how the two characters of Tiaoyun’s name were written and committed them to memory. But he could not yet leave: first they had to find a small boat.

There were boats about. Ferries carrying refugees passed from time to time, but with the foreign soldiers patrolling the junks, loaded guns in hand, none dared approach. Here they needed Li Delong. Borrowing Kong’s telescope, he picked out an empty boat in the distance and swam towards it. He caught hold of its side and raised his head to face the boatman. Before saying a word, he drew a dripping horseshoe-shaped silver ingot from his clothing and handed it up. Truly, a handsome reward will always find a brave man: he hired the boat without difficulty. Dusk was falling and visibility was poor, making it easier to slip ashore. Hu pointed out the direction himself. Just as they were about to cast off, another thought struck him. “Wait, boatman! What is your surname?” The man pointed at the water. “Jiang—River.” “Old Jiang, thank you for your trouble,” Hu said. “Take my friend ashore, then come back and bring me word. I shall give you another ten taels. I will not deceive you. If I do, may I fall straight into the Qiantang and die a dreadful death.” Such a terrible oath seemed to move the boatman. “What is your honourable surname, sir?” he asked.

“Wang.” “Rest assured, Mr Wang. Once I have landed this young gentleman, I shall certainly come back and report.” “I am relying on you!” Hu bowed from the junk. “I shall have the silver ready and wait for you here. Even if it is the middle of the night, come back without fail. Have you a lantern on your boat?” “A lantern, yes.” Jiang was quick-witted too and understood his purpose. “But if I hang it outside at night, the wind off the river will blow it out at once.” “Quite right. Come, then! I shall scratch your back with six fingers—an extra favour for you.” Hu gave him a storm lantern burning Mobil kerosene. He was to hang it at the bow as a signal when returning with news, lest the foreign soldiers, unaware of his purpose, fire on him by mistake.

Once Xiao had gone, Li Delong could not help asking why Hu had treated the boatman in that manner, even swearing by the gods as though afraid of being disbelieved. Was he worried about Xiao? “Once I have let him go, there is no point worrying,” Hu replied. “It is the boatman I am guarding against. He might land our man and then go to the Longhairs to inform on him for a reward. I tied his feet with ten taels so that he would come straight back. Naturally I had to swear an oath, or he would not believe me.” “Mr Hu, I truly admire you. You leave nothing to chance. But why did you tell him your surname was Wang?” “That has another cause. The Qiantang ferrymen all hate me. My real name would have caused trouble. Let me tell you why. Twenty years ago—” Twenty years before, Hu had still been an apprentice in a bank. On one occasion he was sent to Xiaoshan County on the south bank of the Qiantang to collect a debt. He failed to collect it, and in a little eating house he lost almost all his scant travelling money at dice, keeping only the ten copper coins needed for the ferry. “When the boat reached midstream, they collected the fares,” he said. “The man came to me, and I put my hand in my pocket—but could bring nothing out.” “Why?” Li asked. “Misfortunes never come singly. A hole had worn in the pocket, and all ten coins had slipped out without my noticing. The Qiantang ferrymen were notoriously fierce. I had heard of people actually being pushed into the river because they could not pay. Naturally I was desperately embarrassed. I could only tell the truth and promise to fetch the money from my bank as soon as we landed. I might as well have talked my mouth off. They insisted on stripping off my clothes.” “The villains!” Li exclaimed indignantly. “But surely the whole boat did not just stand by and watch?”

Of course not. Someone lent him ten cash, sparing him the humiliation of being stripped. Yet the experience stung him so deeply that, once ashore, he swore that if he ever prospered and had the means, he would buy two boats, engage some boatmen and establish a charitable ferry to carry passengers between the banks without charging a single coin. “To tell the truth, I could have fulfilled that wish long before I did. Who would have thought one could be prevented from doing good? Delong, what do you suppose the reason was?” “Easy enough. When one man does good, another loses his livelihood.” “Exactly. The Qiantang ferrymen joined forces against me and would not let me establish the free crossing. Fortunately Governor Wang helped me in the end.” At the time Wang had been transferred to the post of prefect of Hangzhou. His personal friendship made helping Hu a duty; equally, as the local official, he was bound to benefit the people and encourage charitable acts. He issued a proclamation forbidding those who earned their living by ferrying passengers to obstruct the scheme, and also applied for an official reward for Hu. Countless people benefited from the free ferry. Even though Hu had not set out to fish for praise, his reputation spread everywhere, and he became known as “Hu the Benefactor”. But most Qiantang boatmen gnashed their teeth at the mention of that name. That was why he had concealed his true surname from Jiang. Now that his spirits had lifted, Hu had recovered his usual gift for telling a story. He made this little incident so entertaining that Li listened with delight—and with growing admiration.

“Mr Hu, there must be such a thing as moral recompense. After the good you have done on this river, you surely cannot come to harm upon it. We shall all share in your good fortune.” This was exactly what Hu wished to hear. His face lit up. “Thank you, thank you! May your gracious words come true.” Nor was he the only one encouraged. Everyone aboard took heart from Li’s remarks, believing that with a good man among them, calamity would turn to blessing. At once the oppressive atmosphere of the past two days—their sense of being trapped, their anxiety and despondency—lifted. Dinner was a cheerful affair. Even Li, who was no drinker, agreed to take a cup.

“One really cannot disbelieve in spirits and gods,” Kong observed comfortably, cup in hand. “But true goodness is doing good without letting anyone know. There is nothing remarkable in charity performed merely to gain a reputation for virtue.” “I disagree. If everyone would do good for the sake of a good name, this world would be a fine place. Some people are simply charity swindlers,” Hu said. “As the saying goes, ‘Since the Three Dynasties, the fear should be that men care too little for a good name.’” “Charity swindlers?” Li laughed. “That is a new name to me.” “It means cheats who use charity as a cover. They are the hardest sort of swindler to guard against, though in time people always see through them. In anything a man does, he cannot counterfeit his heart for ever. Some imagine themselves so clever that everyone will fall into their trap. In the end their true nature shows, and they ruin themselves. A man’s worth depends on whether he keeps his word. Take Governor Wang. To speak frankly, his reputation as an official in Zhejiang was not among the very best. But now he says he will not leave, and he stays. He means to share the fortunes of Hangzhou’s people, to live or die with them. In that alone he is worth far more than Governor-General He.” At this, everyone’s thoughts turned naturally to Xiao. When would he enter the city? What would Wang do on receiving the news? How should the boats respond? “He is certain to take action. But—” Hu suddenly held his cup without drinking. His face grew bleak. After a long silence he sighed. “I really cannot see how we are to get this rice ashore. Even if they fight a passage through, how can they keep the supply route open?”

“Mr Hu, I wonder whether this might work,” Li said. “Does Hangzhou not have water gates? Could we get a few little boats, divide the rice among them and smuggle it into the city?” “I fear not—” Hu had scarcely begun when a shot rang out. Immediately someone shouted, “Do not fire! Do not fire! The messenger is coming back!” Hu and Li hurried out of the cabin. Sure enough, a point of light was slowly approaching. As it drew nearer they could see a storm lantern hanging at the bow. When the boat came alongside, Li called, “Boatman Jiang!” “It is I!” Jiang answered, throwing up a rope. Li caught it, made the boat fast, helped him aboard and invited him into the cabin. Hu had already placed a gleaming silver ingot on the table. “The young gentleman has gone ashore,” Jiang said. “I have come to report.” “Thank you for your trouble.” Hu pushed the silver towards him. “A little something for your New Year’s entertainment.”

“Thank you, thank you.” Jiang took the silver, then hesitated before speaking. “Mr Wang, there is something I think I had better tell you. As soon as the young gentleman landed, the Longhairs took him.” Capture itself was nothing to fear; what mattered was how he had been taken. Hu asked calmly, “Were they very rough with him?” “No, not particularly. He is a brave young gentleman. He did not try to run when he saw them, so they treated him rather politely.” Half relieved already, Hu asked casually, “Is there any news from inside the city?” “I do not know.” Jiang shook his head, his face suddenly full of distress. “Inside and outside are two different worlds.” “Then what about outside?” “Outside? You mean the Longhairs, Mr Wang?” “Yes. What news is there on their side?” “I do not know much. A few days ago they were saying they would return to Suzhou. Some of them were selling their plunder from roadside stalls for next to nothing, as though anxious to turn it into cash. But these last two days I have heard no more about leaving.” Hu understood. The Longhairs too were finding their provisions hard to maintain. For the moment they were locked in a stalemate with the defenders. If the city could be supplied and hold out for another month or two, the enemy might retreat without a fight. Yet there was another side to it: since the Longhairs were short of food, the dozen or more grain-laden boats lying on the river must tempt them greatly. If they massed small craft and attacked regardless of their lives, the danger would be severe. That night Hu was once more too anxious to sleep. His every thought was a hope that Xiao would get into the city and Wang would assemble enough men to fight a passage through and hold the supply route. A single day would suffice: they could bring the junks alongside the bank, unload the grain and carry it into the city.

Xiao did indeed find his way inside. From the moment of his capture, the Longhairs had regarded him differently. Every civilian they seized ordinarily trembled with terror. Only this “new fellow”—their usual term for a newly captured civilian—was different. The others had their hands bound, and one man’s queue was tied to the next man’s to prevent them escaping. Xiao, however, was treated with considerable courtesy, just as Jiang had reported. They escorted him to the officer’s “residence”, where even the questioning was polite. “You look as though you come from elsewhere. What is your name, and what is your business?” asked a minor commander in a yellow robe and cap, speaking with a Hubei accent. “My name is Xiao. I have come from Shanghai,” Xiao answered calmly. “To tell you the truth, I hope to conclude a large piece of business. If it succeeds, Hangzhou will be unable to hold out any longer.” The breadth of his claim instantly impressed the commander, who changed his form of address. “Mr Xiao, what great business is this? Why should Hangzhou become unable to hold out once it succeeds?”

“I really cannot discuss it with you,” Xiao said. “Please send me to see the Loyal King.” “I do not know where His Highness is stationed, and I cannot gain an audience with him myself. I can only refer you to my superiors. But, Mr Xiao—” The man hesitated. “You will not get me into trouble, will you?” “How could I?” “If what you say is untrue, will not all the blame fall on me?” Xiao laughed. Seeing that the man was simple and easily deceived, he deliberately assumed a look of contempt. “What an amusing thing to say! Why should you suppose I am not telling the truth? I was living very comfortably in Shanghai. Why else would I travel all this way? Let me be frank: the British have commissioned me to come here. I must see the Loyal King and lay an important matter before him.” Suddenly he asked, “May I know your name?” “Lu Deyi.” “When I see the Loyal King, I shall put in a good word for you. You are certain to receive a handsome reward.” Li Xiucheng managed his army differently from the other Taiping commanders. He had always set great store by attracting able men. At Xiao’s words, Lu was more anxious than ever to show him proper attention. “Mr Xiao,” he answered sincerely, “thank you for your kindness. But it is late today. Stay the night while I send a report to my superiors. They will send someone to fetch you. Would that suit you?”

It would not do to press too hard. Xiao thought that a night’s stay would allow him to question this obliging man and learn more about the situation, giving him a firmer basis for action. He therefore replied with apparent satisfaction, “Very well. I shall stay the night.” Lu treated him as an honoured guest and entertained him with wine. Xiao knew the Yangtze ports well, and Lu came from Hanyang. Recent events in Hubei supplied a topic, and they soon fell into congenial conversation. Eventually they came to conditions inside Hangzhou. Lu showed himself a man of genuine kindness. His face fell. “Truly a calamity!”

He sighed. “The thought of it spoils one’s appetite. I only hope the city falls soon, so that its people may have a chance to live. If this siege goes on, I fear they will all perish.” “Yes,” Xiao said, seizing his opportunity. “Naturally, the great business I have come to arrange will help your side, but it will really benefit Hangzhou’s people too. Yet there is something I do not understand. When the Loyal King took Suzhou, his humanity and generosity won everyone’s gratitude. Why, then, does he not give the people here a way to survive?” “It is hard to dismount once one has mounted a tiger,” Lu replied. “I hear the Loyal King had an arrow shot into the city with a letter urging surrender. It was most earnestly expressed, but no answer came.” “Oh? What did the letter say?” “That soldiers and civilians, Manchus and Han alike, might surrender if they wished, and those who did not wish to surrender would be allowed to leave. The Loyal King has already sent a memorial to the Heavenly Capital, asking the Heavenly King to pardon the Manchu troops and let them return north. The journey there and back takes more than twenty days, and the imperial rescript has not yet arrived. Once it comes, someone will be sent to negotiate peace with Ruichang. Things may look very different then. I have been to many places,” Lu added, “but the Manchu soldiers in Hangzhou seem to be the fiercest of all.” This recalled Hu’s words to Xiao: with food in store, Hangzhou could hold out for six months or a year. He felt the weight of his responsibility more keenly than ever. Lying that night in Lu’s “residence”, he imagined every possible turn of events again and again. How could he gain Li Xiucheng’s trust and free himself from surveillance? Once he encountered the imperial troops, how could he convince them that he was no spy and have them take him into the city to see Wang? He tossed and turned until he heard the fourth watch being called, and only then drifted into an uneasy sleep. He had no idea how long he had slept before he woke with a start. Voices clamoured and footsteps hurried outside, as though something momentous had happened. All sleep vanished. He sat bolt upright and listened intently, catching a cry: “The demon wind is up! The demon wind is up!” He seemed to have heard the expression somewhere. Clenching his teeth, he searched his memory until at last he remembered: in an idle moment aboard the junk, Hu had told them that the Longhairs called the Qing troops “demons”. “The demon wind is up” meant that imperial troops were attacking.

Alarmed and delighted at once, Xiao hurried out of bed and dressed himself securely. Still he dared not act rashly. Opening the door a crack, he peered out. Longhairs were pouring outside with weapons of every kind—red-tasselled spears, ash-wood staffs, great chopping swords and foreign firearms. Gunfire had begun already, mingled with shouts that seemed now near, now far, now east, now west as the wind rose and fell. The attacking imperial force appeared considerable.

What should he do? Xiao Jiaji asked himself. If he meant to escape, this was an excellent opportunity. But he knew nothing of conditions outside. To blunder into a hail of bullets and die without anyone even knowing what had happened—what a senseless end that would be! Yet if he stayed, there was at least one obvious difficulty: he could hardly expect to meet Li Xiucheng at once. Apart from the time he would lose, Lu Deyi might be killed in this very battle, leaving him without such a reasonable man to deal with. The consequences did not bear thinking about. While he wavered, another group hurried past outside the courtyard. Their footsteps and voices were light, but urgent. “Quick, quick!” someone urged. “Get away from the Longhairs! It does not matter where we end up!” “Getting away from the Longhairs” was a common expression; Xiao had often heard Hu use it. It meant escaping from their hands. But “it does not matter where we end up” was a revelation. “Escape!” he told himself. “If I do not, am I really going to sell arms to Li Xiucheng?”

His mind made up, he wasted no time, though he moved quickly rather than rashly. Making sure no one was about, he slipped down the narrow passage and emerged into an open space. The smell of cooking reached him. He looked up: a kitchen. It was large and apparently deserted. He examined it carefully, crossing the courtyard a step at a time. Only when he reached the stove did he discover a man sitting beside its base, warming himself. The man was terribly thin, with enormous eyes; coming upon him so suddenly, Xiao found his appearance frightening and recoiled two paces. The man merely stared, as if half-witted, his large, lifeless eyes showing no expression. “Who are you?” Xiao asked. “Do not bother asking me,” the man replied feebly. “I am not running away. Run where you will, you never get out of their hands. I shall leave it to fate.” Half of Xiao’s courage ebbed away. He stared at the man, unable to speak for some time.

“By the look of you, you are not from here. Where have you escaped from?” The man had a kindly face. His voice was weak, as though life held no interest for him, and Xiao’s fear and suspicion subsided. “I have come from Shanghai,” he answered truthfully. “Is there not a foreign settlement in Shanghai? Everyone fleeing the troubles wants to get there. Why have you come into this place instead?” His voice sounded hollow with despair. “Heaven’s road lay open, and you would not take it; hell had no gate, yet you forced your way in. Why bring such trouble on yourself?” “I had no choice.” Xiao used the opportunity to sound him out, though he dared not yet tell the truth. “I have a friend in Hangzhou, a man bound to me for life and death. I wanted to go into the city to see him.” “You are mad! People are eating each other inside Hangzhou. Your friend is probably dead of starvation already. Where would you go to see him? And even if you found him, you could not save him. You would be trapped yourself and starve to death. What sort of reckoning is that? Truly, it must be fate.” His reproaches revealed a good heart. Xiao decided to tell him the truth, first asking, “May I know your name, sir?”

“Everyone calls me Old He.” “Old He, my name is Xiao. I shall be honest with you. I have come to save the people of Hangzhou—or rather, it is not I, but a philanthropist of great renown in your city who is doing this good deed. He has brought a large quantity of grain from Shanghai and sent me to see Governor Wang with a message.” Xiao paused briefly, then assumed the manner of a man staking everything on a single throw. “I have told you what is in my heart. If you belong to the Longhairs, then it is my fate to die here, in this year, this month, this day, and this hour. If you do not, please show me a way through.” Old He listened and sat in thought without speaking. After a long time he raised his head. Xiao saw that his eyes had changed.
