They no longer had the dull, almost dying look of before. They shone with determination, as though all the strength in his body had gathered within them.

He held out his hand. “Where is the letter?” Xiao was taken aback. “What letter?” “Did you not say that this great benefactor had sent you with a message for Governor Wang?” “Yes, but it is a spoken message. If there were black words on white paper and they fell into the Longhairs’ hands, would that not be disastrous?” “A spoken message?” Old He hesitated. “That would be rather difficult to carry.” “What do you mean, Old He?” Xiao understood him. “Were you intending to take it for me?” “Yes. I should have a better chance than you. But looking as I do, no one would believe me if I said I had an oral message for the governor.” “Then let me ask you”—Xiao bowed to the ground—“to take me into the city.” “It will not be easy. I might slip through alone, but someone who looks like you could never get in.” “Then what must I do?”

“First of all, your face is pink and white, as if you ate fish and meat every day. If you got inside looking like that, there would be trouble. If you really mean to enter the city, you must put up with some unpleasantness.” “That does not matter. I shall put up with anything.” “Very well.” Old He nodded. “I have one foot in the grave already. If I can do something like this first, it will have been worthwhile. Let us see what is happening outside.” They observed quietly. The voices were still noisy, but the gunfire had slackened. “The government troops have been beaten,” Old He said confidently. “This is the right time to leave.”

Xiao found it extraordinary that the man could judge the outcome of a battle merely by listening. This was a crucial moment; a single wrong step might be fatal. He could not help asking, “How do you know, Old He?” “I knew it from the beginning. The government troops are so hungry that their sight is going black. How can they fight? All they can do is make one charge.” That explained the dwindling gunfire. On reflection, Xiao found it reasonable, and followed Old He out through a side gate of the Longhairs’ headquarters with renewed confidence. Sure enough, the rebels were returning to their quarters. They filled the main streets like ants, walking, talking, and laughing with all the air of victors. Fortunately, Old He knew the neighborhood intimately. Keeping clear of the main streets, he led Xiao through one narrow lane after another until they reached a dilapidated temple to the God of Wealth. Inside, seven or eight beggars were huddled together, gambling with dice.

“So you are not dead yet, Old He!” one of them said. Old He ignored him and addressed a man whose clothes were slightly less ragged than the others’. “A’mao, take off that tattered padded jacket.” “What for?” “Lend it to this friend.” “And what am I to wear?” “He will give you his clothes in exchange.” At that, several men clamored to make the exchange themselves. “Me! Me!” they cried in confusion.

Old He had made up his mind to deal only with A’mao. Though the padded jacket was a little more presentable than the rest, it was coated in such a thick layer of grease and filth that it looked like a butcher’s apron. The sight alone made Xiao nauseous. “There is no other way,” Old He said. “Without this, we cannot succeed. Worse than that, we shall be in danger as soon as we go out. And it is not just you: I must change too.” Xiao had no choice. Gritting his teeth, he put on the jacket, along with torn shoes and socks. His whole body crawled as if insects and ants were moving over his skin. Never in his life had he endured anything like it. Yet now that the clothes were on, there could be no question of taking them off. Looking at Old He, he saw that the man too had exchanged clothes with someone and was wearing garments even dirtier and more ragged than his own. Why was another man, with no obligation to him, taking on this misery as well? Thinking of that made it easier to bear.

“A’mao,” Old He asked, “what is today’s password?” “I do not know.” “I know,” someone answered loudly. “What do you want it for, Old He?” “I have a use for it, naturally.” Old He turned to Xiao. “Have you any silver dollars? Take one out.” Xiao did as he was told, and Old He bought a password with the coin. “But what is this password for?” Xiao asked.

“For entering the city,” Old He replied. “The gates may be closed, but they still send a few beggars out to gather intelligence. Not that it does any good.” To Xiao it was immensely useful. Now he also understood why he had had to submit to this ordeal. They had not gone far when a timber fort came into view in the distance. Xiao knew they were still only halfway to the city gate. Hu had told him how, after Hangzhou’s ten gates were surrounded, Wang Youling had strained every effort to open a route to the river. Vastly outnumbered, he had lacked the strength to do it. Just then Zhang Yuliang had withdrawn from Fuyang. Wang immediately sent men to contact him and proposed an advance by fortified stages: Zhang would build camps inward from the riverbank, while the defenders built outward from the city. Each position, once established, would be held firmly. They would seek no quick result, but proceed methodically until the two lines eventually joined and a route to survival opened.

Wang’s handwritten appeal had been exceptionally urgent: “The survival of Hangzhou depends upon this one undertaking. We must not lose the opportunity and ruin the attempt.” Zhang Yuliang had therefore dared not delay. Fighting as he advanced, he built more than a dozen camps from the outer embankment by the river, until another waterway barred his way. He sent men through the siege lines into the city, asking Wang to launch a coordinated attack and enclosing a clear map of his positions. Wang immediately instructed Rao Tingxuan to dispatch a large body of troops into the city. But Rao delayed for a night, and the secret leaked out. Li Xiucheng set his men to work overnight and erected a timber fort halfway along the route, with cannon mounted on it. Outside stood an earthen wall pierced with firing holes for muskets. This cut off Zhang’s forces from Rao’s, and Zhang himself was killed by cannon fire. That was the position when Hu left Hangzhou. The timber fort still stood and was plainly impassable. Old He led Xiao well around it, keeping clear of the Longhairs, until at last they saw a city gate.

“This is Houchao Gate.” “I know.” Xiao recited, “‘Awaiting the Tide’, one hears the ‘Clear Waves’ sound; ‘Surging Gold’ and ‘Qiantang’ make ‘Great Peace’ secure.” The couplet wove together the names of five of Hangzhou’s gates, a piece of lore known only to local people. Hearing it, Old He felt a sudden warmth toward him. For the first time, a smile appeared on the withered, shrunken, wrinkled face that scarcely seemed human. “So you know that! Where did you hear it?” Xiao smiled in return. “This may be my first visit to Hangzhou, but I know a good many of its stories.” “You have a bond with Hangzhou,” Old He said, much comforted. “Everything is sure to go well.”

As they spoke, they approached a ditch. Men were patrolling on its inner side; on the outer side, others lay with their ears pressed to the ground. Xiao did not know what they were doing and stopped in surprise. “Who are those men?” “They are blind,” Old He replied. “Blind men have particularly keen hearing. Wine jars are buried underground too, so they can hear any noise all the more clearly.” “Ah, I understand!” It dawned on Xiao. “These are the listening jars I have heard of. They are afraid the Longhairs will dig tunnels and plant explosives.” “Exactly. Come along—the soldier opposite is raising his gun.” Old He lifted both hands and hurried forward. Xiao copied him, stopping only when they reached the edge of the ditch.

“Password!” the soldier challenged them from the other side. “The sun and moon shine bright.” Without another word, the soldier went over to a windlass and turned its handle. A plank that had been standing upright came down across the ditch to form a drawbridge.

Though the soldier looked haggard and exhausted, as if even speaking demanded too much effort, Xiao thought his continued devotion to duty worthy of respect. Perhaps the government troops’ discipline was not quite as dreadful as people said. If so, why make trouble for himself by trying to slip into the city? He spoke as soon as the thought occurred. “Old He, would it not be easier if I explained my purpose and asked the officer commanding here to send some men to escort me inside?” Old He considered. “Everyone says Deputy Commander Zeng, who guards Houchao Gate, is a good man. You might try. But remember”—he added a warning—“a scholar confronted with soldiers may find that reason gets him nowhere. You know the facts yourself. Do not contradict yourself and invite trouble.” “I shall not. Every word I say is true. All that white rice is waiting in the middle of the river. Could anyone invent that?”

Hearing this, Old He changed his plan. He gave the sentry a brief account and asked to see an officer. They were passed up the chain from a bazong to a qianzong, then to a garrison officer, until at last, at Houchao Gate, they met Zeng Desheng, a deputy commander under Rao Tingxuan. “We know Circuit Intendant Hu went to Shanghai to buy rice,” Zeng said after hearing the story. “But you have no letter, and you speak with an outsider’s accent. I cannot make out exactly who you are. How can I take you to Governor Wang?” Xiao understood his concern. “Sir, search me. I am no assassin, and a man openly asking for an audience is surely no spy. I could not risk carrying a letter through the enemy’s lines. Once I see Governor Wang, I can tell him something that will prove Hu sent me. If he does not believe me, you may have my head. I shall leave a written military pledge with you.” “A military pledge? That is something out of a novel. I shall take you.” Xiao had made Zeng laugh, though the smile looked more painful than weeping. “Thank you, sir!” Xiao answered briskly, then immediately made a request. “Please give me a padded uniform like your soldiers’ to wear.” He was desperate to shed those filthy rags. But his relief lasted only a moment. Once inside the city, the reek of corpses rose about him so powerfully that he nearly fainted.

Wang Youling had lost hope. Jiechun had launched an attack early that morning—the very moment when Xiao had heard the gunfire. More than a dozen boats of life-saving white rice lay waiting to be unloaded. If even that incentive could not summon the soldiers’ strength, who could possibly open the supply route and win them a chance of survival? Wang therefore decided to write his final memorial to the throne: “Your servant Youling previously entrusted Governor Xue Huan of Jiangsu with the task of reporting on my behalf that Hangzhou was surrounded on all sides, the route to the river blocked, and the troops and people trapped within the city. I do not know whether that report has reached Your Majesty. All ten gates are now closely besieged, and the rebel host grows ever larger. Repeatedly we have led our starving troops into battle and secretly arranged coordinated attacks with the camps along the riverbank. In several dozen engagements, great and small, by day and night, we have failed to open even a single supply route. The city’s food is entirely exhausted. Horses have been slaughtered to feed the soldiers, followed by cats and rats; roots and tree bark are eaten. The roads are strewn with the starved dead, whose numbers increase daily. Officers and men endure hunger and hold their posts, but they no longer have the strength to wield their weapons.”

“At first we feared that the exhaustion of supplies would bring mutiny within. But after we pleaded with the men in tears and sought to comfort them, all pledged to follow us to the death, without a disloyal thought. We have discharged our duties so poorly that troops and people must sit awaiting death. Long have we suffered such anguish that life is unbearable.”

At this point the pain in Wang’s eyes became so sharp that he had to lay down his brush. He had suffered from the ailment for a whole year. At first it had been described as “an excessive depletion of the heart’s blood, upward pressure from the liver and bowels, affliction of the spleen channel, and weakness of the lung’s qi.” It had then developed into “wind and fire flaming upward.” Without a moment’s peace of mind, his eyes had swollen into wartlike lumps; when pressed, they shed blood with the tears. Everyone who saw them was horrified. Later, a renowned eye physician had treated him with medicines and other remedies, and there had been some improvement. Since the siege began, however, the old trouble had returned, worsening day by day. Wang bitterly resented it. He had long ceased to care about his life, but the failure of his eyes was a cruel affliction.

Other official papers could have been dictated to a secretary, a son, or a nephew. But Wang did not wish anyone to see this last memorial. Forcing open eyes that felt as though needles pierced them, he continued: “While a breath remains in us, our sole thought is to kill one more rebel and hold out one more day. We reflect with tears that, after last year’s devastation by war, few households in Hangzhou retained any reserves. Rice and other provisions were all brought from Shaoxing, and much of our military funding came from there as well. After the fall of Jin and Lan, we planned at once to defend the Ningbo–Shaoxing supply line in strength. First we ordered Zhang Jingqu, intendant of the Ningbo–Shaoxing–Taizhou circuit, and later repeatedly directed Salt Commissioner Zhuang Huanwen and the nominated circuit intendant Peng Siju to lead troops and militia to establish defenses. Wang Lüqian opposed and obstructed every measure. He also shielded the wealthy gentry, exhausting all possibilities of obtaining contributions or loans. Clinging obstinately to his own views, he thwarted us at every turn. Still intent upon making defensive preparations, we noted that Liao Zongyuan, together with Zhao Jingxian of the Huzhou gentry, had held a city in peril for more than a year. We transferred Liao to act as prefect of Shaoxing and had him devote every effort to organizing its defense. But he went against the wishes of the great local gentry, who did not want defenses established. They falsely accused him of colluding with the rebels and beat him savagely, while Lüqian stood by without intervening. When the city fell, Lüqian fled; Liao Zongyuan ultimately died with the city. Thereafter, the districts of Ningbo and Shaoxing fell one after another, leaving Hangzhou an isolated last stake, beyond all hope of rescue.”

Wang could not release the resentment that filled him. He thought of Wang Lüqian taking his family and baggage out to sea from Ningbo and sailing away to Fujian, free and at ease, while the entire population of Hangzhou endured a catastrophe without precedent. Even if he and the garrison general, Ruichang, died to repay their sovereign, their deaths could do nothing to remedy the situation. He pressed on furiously with his brush: “Wang Lüqian has ruined the whole undertaking. I shall die unable to close my eyes. Our funds are exhausted and all hope of relief is gone; destruction may come at any moment. We have betrayed Your Majesty’s gracious trust, and our guilt admits of no pardon. We beg only that Your Majesty select and dispatch a mighty army to sweep away the rebels without delay. Then the day of our death will be as though we lived again. At present our memorials cannot pass through, and it will be still harder to smuggle them out hereafter. We therefore set forth jointly in this draft memorial the circumstances of Hangzhou’s impending collapse, for secret delivery to Shanghai, where Governor Xue Huan of Jiangsu may have it transcribed and submitted to the throne on our behalf. Looking up toward Your Sacred Majesty as toward Heaven, we are overwhelmed by anguish and fearful awe.”

Before Wang could finish the memorial, his family had heard his grief and gathered about him. He looked at his five-year-old son in the nurse’s arms, his skin sallow and dark, his body thin as a bundle of sticks. The sight cut him to the heart. He gave a cry of anguish and lost consciousness. When they revived him, his eldest son, Juyun, stood before him, forcing a smile through his tears. “Father, Uncle Hu has sent someone.” “Oh!” Whatever else might happen, this was good news. Wang felt his strength return. “Where is he?” “Waiting in the reception room. There is no need for you to go out, Father. Let him come to your room.” “Very well. What use is ceremony at a time like this? Besides, anyone Uncle Hu sends is one of our own. Ask him in. What is his name?” “Xiao. He is very young, and he says he is Gu Yingchun’s pupil.”

Entering the inner room, Xiao Jiaji offered the full ceremonial obeisance. Wang was too weak to return it and could only protest, “Gallant Mr Xiao! Gallant Mr Xiao! I cannot possibly accept such an honor.” Respecting his lonely suffering and steadfast loyalty, Xiao nevertheless knelt reverently and touched his forehead to the floor three times. Juyun returned the courtesy on his father’s behalf, then brought a chair and asked the visitor to sit beside the bed. “Governor Wang!” Xiao got no further. It was not awe of an official that silenced him. Everything he had seen and heard along the way had been more appalling than he could ever have imagined. Now, above all, every member of the Wang household seemed half dead. They drifted weakly about like wavering ghosts, until he could no longer tell whether he was still among the living or already in hell. His mind grew dazed, and for a moment he did not know where to begin. Wang therefore took the initiative and asked about Gu. “I have met your teacher. We are, in fact, connected by an adopted family relationship. I trust he has been doing well?” “Yes, yes. Thanks to your good fortune, sir!”

Only after the words escaped did Xiao realize that he had begun badly. With Wang in such a state, what good fortune could anyone borrow from him? It almost sounded like mockery. Anxious to cover his mistake, he hurried on. “Your loyalty and righteousness are known to all, Governor Wang. No one who knows the situation in Hangzhou can fail to be moved. Everyone compares you with Governor-General He—” Another blunder! He Guiqing had abandoned his territory and fled; the comparison was naturally intended as a contrast, yet it seemed to demand that Wang perish with Hangzhou. Ashamed, remorseful, and angry with himself, Xiao broke off. Normally so fluent, he had become tongue-tied and hardly dared speak again. Yet his words produced an unexpected result. Far from being offended, Wang smiled. “Shanghai gathers people from every quarter, and everyone has an opinion. How do they compare me with Governor-General He?”

Since Wang pressed him, Xiao had to answer. He composed himself. “They all say that you are a truly loyal servant of the throne. Set beside Governor-General He, the difference between worth and unworthiness becomes all the clearer. Everyone prays that danger will turn to safety for you and misfortune to good.” “Ah!” Wang let out a long breath. “If that is what people say, then justice does indeed live in human hearts.” After a pause he asked, “Xueyan must have sent a letter?” “Mr Hu feared that I might encounter the Longhairs on the road, so he wrote nothing. He entrusted me with a spoken message.” Xiao remembered the private conversation Hu had described, in which Wang had asked him to care for his child after his death. Its purpose had been to establish Xiao’s credentials. Now that Wang already accepted his identity, he need not mention it and cause further grief. He went straight to the business. “The rice we bought arrived four days ago and is lying in the middle of the river. Because you told Mr Hu that you would send someone to contact him as soon as the grain boats arrived, he has not dared leave them. He waited until yesterday without news. Anxiety has robbed him of his appetite and his sleep. He therefore sent me ashore at great risk to bring this message: please dispatch troops quickly to open the supply route so that the rice can be landed.” Before he had finished, tears were streaming down Wang’s face. He shook his head repeatedly and sobbed, “We learned of it yesterday. We sent troops out this morning. It was no use! The Longhairs have penned us in until we have not a scrap of strength left. We can see it, but cannot reach it. Food is there, yet we cannot put it into our mouths. How can I close my eyes in death?” He broke into a great cry of anguish. Everyone in the household, old and young, wept with him, calling upon Heaven and earth. Xiao’s heart ached; tears poured down his cheeks, and he too sobbed aloud. Tearful eyes met tearful eyes as they comforted one another and at last brought their grief under control. Xiao then returned to the interrupted discussion. He needed a definite decision.

The question brought Wang fresh anguish. This was the only road to survival for hundreds of thousands of people. Even knowing that it lay beyond their reach, how could he take the terrible decision to say, “There is no hope. Go”? And what if the boats stayed and waited for a chance? If that were possible, it would naturally be best. A painted cake could not satisfy hunger, but the thought of plums might ease thirst. With so much rice lying in the Qiantang, the troops might find fresh courage and work a miracle. Wang had seen such a miracle in childhood: when a neighbor’s house caught fire, a man who had been bedridden with crippled legs somehow leaped up and ran out of the flames. The hidden strength summoned by a desperate will to live could be astonishing. Yet such things might occur; they could not be commanded. All that rice was sure to tempt the Longhairs. Even if they had enough food themselves, they would devise every possible means of depriving the besieged of their last hope. Whether by open assault or a secret raid, one attack might very well send Hu Xueyan to his death in the Qiantang. He would join Wu Zixu beneath its waters, his bitter grief sounding in the tides morning and evening for a thousand years. At the thought, Wang wept sorrowfully, shook his head, and sighed. “Why drag another man down with me when I die? Mr Xiao, tell Xueyan that our will outstrips our strength. We can only sit and await death. Ask him to leave at once.” This was, in fact, the answer Xiao had come hoping to obtain. But now that Wang had spoken it, he could not bring himself to assent.

“Governor Wang, think it over once more. There may be a way.” “There is nothing left to think over. I hope all day and plan all night. Even sending someone to contact Xueyan is almost beyond us. Ah!” Wang was in mortal anguish. “I did nothing wrong except in two matters. First, someone advised me at the beginning to build a ramp from the city wall down to the riverbank and guard it with a strong force to secure our supply route. I feared the burden on the people. The undertaking was so vast that I worried we might have to abandon it halfway, wasting all their labor. I did not accept the proposal. Now I see how terribly wrong I was.” It really had been an excellent plan. Such a route would naturally have been attacked by the Longhairs, but even if nine attempts failed and one succeeded, the city could have prolonged its existence a little. It could never have been trapped as completely as it was now, without the smallest opening toward life.

To say so, however, would only deepen Wang’s grief. Xiao therefore argued the opposite. “Not necessarily. From what I saw on the way, there are so many Longhairs that even with such a ramp, I doubt it could have been held.” “Let us leave that. The second mistake was the worst.” Wang spoke dejectedly. “When the siege began, some said we must close the gates; others said we should open them and let the people out. There was endless argument and no agreement. I should not have listened to those who urged closure. The proper course was to throw all ten gates wide and let the people escape.” “You must not torment yourself, Governor. If the gates had been opened, the Longhairs might have charged in and Hangzhou might have fallen long ago.” “That was precisely what we feared. We thought relief would come in ten days or a fortnight, and that we might as well hold out. Opening the gates to let the civilians go would shake the army’s resolve. Who could have known that, in the end, we should fail to hold the city anyway? If it was to come to this, why did we do it? I have failed the people of Hangzhou!” Again he broke into loud sobs. Xiao wept with him, but a plan had already formed in his mind. Choking back his tears, he called, “Governor Wang! Please listen to what I have to say.”

When Wang’s grief had eased a little, Xiao proposed a plan. It might have been called a promise, though in truth it was a hope: that the grain boats could remain safely where they were for another three days, and, more urgently still, that within those three days the troops inside the city could fight their way to the river and bring the rice ashore. “May it be so!” Wang forced himself to rally. “Let us work together, inside and outside, and stake our lives on these next three days.” “Yes!” To encourage the defenders, Xiao ventured another promise. “If the troops from the city can reach the riverbank, the foreign soldiers aboard the boats will certainly support them. There are not many of them, but their firearms are powerful and highly effective.” “That would be best. If Heaven grants our wish and Hangzhou is relieved, I shall be responsible for rewarding the foreign troops. I may not be able to manage more, but twenty thousand taels!” Wang struck his chest. “Even if I must sell my modest property to make up the sum, I shall not mind.” “Very well.” Xiao rose. “I must take my leave and return as quickly as possible to arrange matters.”

“Thank you, gallant Mr Xiao.” Wang spoke with heartfelt gratitude. “Hangzhou is no longer merely a city in danger; it is a place of certain death. You have risked your life to bring this message. For such boundless generosity, it is not only I who thank you, but every civil and military official, every soldier, and every citizen of Hangzhou. Mr Xiao—” He struggled shakily to his feet. “Allow me to bow to you.” “I could not accept it!” Xiao hurried to support him. “Governor Wang, this is a duty I could not in honor refuse.” One insisted on offering thanks, the other steadfastly declined the honor. After a long impasse, Wang’s eldest son finally made the obeisance on his father’s behalf. Xiao was deeply moved. Reflecting that this parting in life was almost certainly a parting in death, he felt hot tears fill his eyes and a lump rise in his throat. He managed only, “Take care of yourself, Governor Wang,” then turned away.

He had stumbled through the inner gate when he heard voices calling from within. “Please come back!” Wang had another important trust to place in him. Recalled to the room, Xiao received the governor’s final memorial. This time Wang’s voice was quite calm. “Mr Xiao, please give this to Xueyan for safekeeping. While the city stands, I live; when it falls, I die. News that Hangzhou has fallen will mean that my life is over. Ask Xueyan to take this final memorial in person to Governor Xue of Jiangsu and request that he have it transcribed and submitted to the throne. It concerns the final accounting of my entire life. Mr Xiao, I entrust it to you with all my heart.” Faced with such grave solemnity, Xiao dared show no hesitation. “I shall,” he answered firmly. He folded the draft into four and placed it in the pocket of the short undergarment next to his skin. Anxious lest it slip out and be lost, he pressed it twice through his outer clothes to make sure it was secure.

“Oh, one more instruction. Please have this final memorial submitted under the names and titles of both General Rui and myself. The general and I have agreed to die for our duty together. Neither will survive the other.” He spoke so calmly and firmly, with such readiness to meet death, that the respect Xiao felt overwhelmed his sorrow. He took his leave with composure. “Governor Wang, I shall not fail your trust. May this draft remain forever in Mr Hu’s keeping!” “May it be so,” Wang said in a low but perfectly clear voice. “And tell Xueyan again that he must not grieve for me.”

CHAPTER THREE

How could Hu Xueyan not grieve? His eyes reddened as soon as he received Wang Youling’s final memorial. What hurt most was the knowledge that Wang had abandoned hope. Hu could imagine it all: Wang had pinned his hopes on the grain boats, first fearing that Hu might not reach Shanghai safely, then that, even if he obtained the grain there, he might fail to overcome the obstacles on the journey back to Hangzhou. Who could have foreseen that, after all those hardships, the food would arrive only to remain within sight but beyond reach? There was nothing left to hope for. With that last thread of hope severed, the last thread of life had snapped. No sorrow is greater than a deadened heart. Wang’s heart had turned to ice; the days left to him were merely a wait for death. Such an extremity of human suffering seemed without equal, a grief to be mourned for all time. Yet Hu had to search for hope in hopelessness. Perhaps a miracle would occur within these three days. It was a tenuous hope, and even to cling to it seemed difficult. Overnight, the situation had grown more threatening. Boat after boat of rebels circled them, keeping just beyond gunshot. Their intention needed no explanation.

The foreign escort kept urging them to settle the matter and leave. “It will be difficult to persuade them to wait three days,” Li Delong said. “While the man we sent had not returned, we could argue that we must wait for definite news. Whether they liked it or not, they could hardly dispute that. Now Jiaji is back. After what was just said about Hangzhou, everyone knows the situation. They will not stay here in great danger, waiting uselessly for something hopeless.” “They must agree, whatever it takes. Having come all this way, I cannot bear to turn back just like that. Besides, it is only three more days of danger. We have already faced far greater risks. What are three days more?” “Then we should tell them promptly. The Sea-Junk Guild does not look very willing either.” “Once the foreign soldiers agree, the boatmen will have protection and nothing more to say. We must approach this from two directions. A generous reward will always find brave men. Ask the liaison officer to pass this on: I have two proposals, and they may choose between them.” Hu considered whether two proposals were enough—whether there might be a third course that took everyone’s interests into account. After much thought, he could find only the two.

“The first is this. If the troops in the city can fight their way out, I ask the escort to help them. The twenty thousand taels Governor Wang has offered as a reward will be paid as soon as I reach Shanghai, and I shall add ten thousand of my own. For anyone killed or wounded, I shall pay twice the compensation laid down in their regulations. They are to wait three full days and nights on these terms. If nothing happens, we sail for Ningbo, and I give them three thousand taels.” “That is a generous reward. It would be worth risking their lives for,” Li said. “But men differ. Suppose they refuse and insist on sailing?” “Then there is my second proposal: let them push me into the Qiantang River before they sail.” As he spoke, Hu’s face was utterly bloodless. Li and Xiao stared at one another in dismay and remained silent for a long time. “I am not trying to frighten them. I never make empty statements. If, after I have done everything that loyalty and friendship require, they still refuse, tell me what course remains to me except death.” His determination to sacrifice himself both moved the foreign soldiers and alarmed them. Through the liaison officer, however, they imposed one condition: he must keep his word. Once the three days were over, he must devise no further excuse for delay. “We do what we can, then submit to Heaven’s decree,” Hu said. “These three days are only to make sure that we have done all that lies in our power. I know they will be of no use.”

For all his words, Hu forgot both food and sleep, fixing his entire mind on the deliverance he expected. Day and night he stood at the bow and gazed into the distance. The moaning river drowned his stifled sobs, but its cutting wind and the bitter winter nights brought on such violent coughing that it could be heard even inside the cabins. “Mr Hu,” Xiao urged him, “Governor Wang’s affairs, in life and death, depend on you. Your own family is waiting for you too. Your life is too precious for you to neglect yourself like this.” The reproach, coming from a younger man, made his concern all the more touching. Hu had to yield. Yet even lying in his berth, he strained his ears. Whenever a patrolling foreign soldier fired a shot, he would insist on going out to see what had happened. Though every day seemed a year, the three days finally passed. The foreigners allowed no latitude. When the full three days and nights expired, at eight in the evening, they insisted on sailing. Hu could do nothing. Turning north, he bowed several times, offering what was, in effect, a farewell tribute to a man still alive. Then they departed, and he broke into uncontrollable weeping.

Only when they reached the vicinity of Zhenhai, at the mouth of the Yong River, did they learn that the Taiping commanders Huang Chengzhong and Fan Ruzeng had advanced from Cixi and Fenghua respectively, and that Ningbo had fallen two days earlier, on the eighth day of the eleventh month. Ningbo, however, had a foreign settlement, British and American consuls, and British and French warships. The two consuls had marked out an area for foreign residence and commerce and were negotiating with Huang and Fan to keep the Taiping troops from entering it. “What do we do now?” Hu asked weakly. “Return to Shanghai?” “There is no reason to do that. You are exhausted, Mr Hu. Leave this to me.” Xiao hired a small boat and approached a British warship. After an exchange between the vessels, he was allowed aboard and met its captain, Kaobaituo. He explained that he wished to contact Yang Fang’s Ningbo firm and asked for an escort from the warship. He also told the captain that they had a large quantity of grain with which to supply Ningbo. The news was warmly welcomed. “There are seventy thousand Chinese refugees in the neutral zone. Food supplies have become an enormous problem. You and your grain have arrived at just the right moment. But I am very sorry,” the captain added with a shrug. “At present I cannot do what you ask. Could you stay aboard my ship for two or three days?”

“Why?” “The consular body is negotiating with the occupying forces. We want them to respect the neutral zone and keep the markets functioning. Once an agreement is reached, your grain can be brought in openly. For the moment, however, we must abide by the existing understanding. We cannot escort any Chinese person ashore.” “Could you send a letter for me, then?” The captain considered. “Yes. Write a letter, and I shall ask the consulate to have it delivered. I must also tell our consul this good news.” Xiao did as he suggested. The captain acted promptly and efficiently, sending a junior officer ashore in a small boat with the letter. At the same time, he ordered him to call on the British consul at Ningbo, Xiafuli, and report the welcome arrival of a large consignment of grain.

While waiting for a reply, Xiao was tempted to accept the captain’s invitation to stay aboard. Yet he was uneasy about his own vessels. Though several dozen foreign soldiers guarded them, a clash with the Taiping forces would cause serious trouble. If he could ask for a British flag to fly, that would provide excellent protection. But he feared that the men serving under the American Ward might regard this as an insult and refuse. After turning the problem over, he could see no alternative but to return and keep watch himself. Just as he rose to leave, the captain received a report informing him that Ward’s men were present. He wished to establish contact and asked Xiao to introduce them. This unexpectedly solved Xiao’s difficulty. Delighted, he readily agreed. A lifeboat was lowered from the warship, and he accompanied a British officer back to his own vessel. After the soldiers had conferred, Ward’s men accepted the British suggestion. The grain boats hoisted British flags and came under the captain’s protection. Now they were truly safe. Proud of how well he had managed the negotiations, Xiao hurried off to tell Hu. But when he entered the cabin, he found him looking utterly drained, with a dreadful colour in his face.

“Mr Hu!” he exclaimed in alarm. “What is wrong?” “I am going to be ill.” Xiao laid a hand on his forehead. It was burning hot. “You are ill already. Lie down at once.” Once Hu lay down, he could not rise again. He burned with fever and rambled continually, calling now for Xuexuan, now for his mother. In his delirium, he knew only that he had been taken ashore; where he was lying ill, he had no idea. One night he woke at midnight and saw someone sitting by a lamp—a woman. The slender figure, seen from behind, seemed familiar, yet for the moment he could not remember who she was. “Am I dreaming?”

Though he had only murmured to himself, the woman heard. She turned and raised the lamp’s flame so that he could see her face. Now it truly seemed a dream. He could scarcely find his voice. “You—you look so like A’qiao!” “I am A’qiao.” She wiped away a tear and forced a smile. “You never thought it would be me, did you?” Without answering, Hu struggled to sit up. He was too weak and swayed as though about to fall. A’qiao hurried forward to support him. “What do you want? Some tea?” “No,” he said with difficulty. “I want to see whether I am dreaming. Where is this? Are you really A’qiao?” “Yes, I really am. I came especially to see you. Lie down. We can talk slowly.” There was so much to ask that he did not know where to begin. In truth, his head was too clouded even to think. He could only lie back, look up at the canopy, then turn his eyes towards A’qiao. First he must discover what had happened between the onset of his illness and this moment.

“Where is he?” he asked without explanation. “Do you mean young Mr Xiao? He is sleeping in the outer room.” Xiao had already heard their voices. Hastily throwing on his clothes, he came to the bedside. Hu was haggard, but his eyes had regained a clear look. Surprised and delighted, Xiao asked, “Mr Hu, do you recognize me?” “You?” Hu replied in bewilderment. “Are you not Jiaji?” “And this lady?” “She is Madam He. Why are you asking me these things? You make it sound as though I could not recognize anyone.” “Exactly!” Xiao said, smiling with relief. “For the past few days, you really did not recognize people. This damp-fever illness came on terribly fiercely. At last you have turned the corner.” “As bad as that?” Hu could scarcely believe it. “I had no idea,” he said, catching his breath. “How many days?” “Eight.” “Where are we?” “In the British settlement, at Mr Yang’s firm. You are extremely weak. Do not talk too much. Have some rice gruel, then your medicine, and sleep. When you feel stronger tomorrow, we shall tell you everything.” His “we” plainly included A’qiao, who added, “Young Mr Xiao is right. The first thing is to get well.” “It does not matter. I know nothing of what has happened, and it weighs on me. What news of Hangzhou?”

“None.” Hu turned towards A’qiao to question her, but she was already rising. “I shall warm the gruel,” she said, going out. Watching her still graceful figure recede, then noticing Xiao’s curious half-smile and studied air of indifference, Hu again felt as though this meeting had taken place in a dream. “How did she come here?” he asked quietly. “She arrived yesterday and came straight to me. I had met her once at my master’s house, with his wife, so I knew her. She said she had heard you were seriously ill and had hurried here especially to nurse you. She wanted to stay. My master’s wife knew about it, so I could hardly refuse.” Hu stared blankly for a while, then frowned. “I do not understand. Trying to think makes my head ache. How could such a thing happen?” “No wonder you are puzzled. It is a long story, and I do not fully understand it myself. She says she was visiting my master’s wife just as my letter arrived. When she heard how ill you were, she wanted to come and nurse you. Naturally, my master’s wife approved and asked my master to make the arrangements. He sent someone to escort her on a British steamer.” “Strange!” Hu said. “She belongs to the He family, and I am a Hu. Why should a concubine of the He household come to nurse me?” “Surely that is obvious. She must have left the He household. Anyone can see that. But she was embarrassed to explain, and I was embarrassed to ask. You can question her yourself presently.” Hu now understood the broad outline. A’qiao could hardly have run away in secret, he thought, or Gu and his wife would not have sent someone to escort her to Ningbo. Yet—

“But can we trust what she says? How do you know your master’s wife approved of her coming?” “There is no doubt about it. Her escort was Old Huang, the senior outside clerk at my master’s firm.” Hu was reassured. He too knew the man, also called Old Huang of Ningbo. He was about to ask more when he heard footsteps. Falling silent, he looked towards the doorway. The curtain stirred, and A’qiao’s back appeared first. Carrying a tray, she had no free hand to lift the curtain and had slipped in sideways.

Xiao helped place a small table across the bed and took the tray from her. It held a pot of fragrant rice gruel and four small dishes, light but delicately prepared. One was an egg preserved in wine lees, a particular favourite of Hu’s. At the sight, his mouth watered and his stomach began to rumble. “Mr Hu,” Xiao said, making a point of explaining where the food came from, “even the rice for the gruel was brought from Shanghai by Madam He.” “Young Mr Xiao,” A’qiao interrupted, “please call me A’qiao.” This was a still clearer sign that she had left the He family. “Madam He” had already begun to sound jarring, so Xiao was happy to comply. Out of respect for Hu, however, he felt he should not simply call her by her personal name, and glanced at him for guidance. “Call her Sister A’qiao.” “Yes.” With a warmth tempered by respect, Xiao addressed her: “Sister A’qiao!” “There!” she replied readily, quite like an elder sister. “Now we sound like a family.”

Neither he nor Hu felt able to respond to that. A’qiao said no more. Lowering her eyes, she ladled out the gruel. It was steaming, so she pursed her moist lips and blew on it until it was cool enough, then set it down. Taking a white handkerchief from beneath her arm, she wiped a pair of ivory chopsticks and placed them before Hu with the bowl. “Would you like me to feed you?” she asked. This reminded Xiao that, with such an attentive person to care for Hu, there was no need for him to stand there in the way. He smiled and quietly left. Both Hu and A’qiao watched his retreating figure until it disappeared. Then they looked back at one another and remained silent for a long moment. Suddenly her eyes reddened. She quickly lowered her head, took up the handkerchief, and pretended to blow her nose while wiping away her tears. Hu too was filled with conflicting emotions, but was reluctant to question her. Since she did not want him to see her tears, he pretended not to notice and picked up his chopsticks to eat.

Once he began, he could think of nothing else. He had not eaten a grain of rice for days and was ravenous, slurping the gruel with extraordinary energy. When the first bowl was empty, A’qiao was already waiting with a filled ladle. As she served him more, she laughed happily. “Worse than a hungry ghost let out on the fifteenth of the seventh month!” For all her teasing, once he had finished the second bowl she would allow no more. His illness had only just begun to recede, and overeating might upset his stomach. Hu was not satisfied, however, and after much persuasion she gave him another half-bowl. “Ah!” he sighed, laying down his chopsticks. “At least I have had my fill.” A’qiao knew what lay behind the words. She too understood Hangzhou’s plight, but feared that mentioning it would distress him. Without replying, she gathered up the dishes and went out. “A’qiao, do not go. Let us talk.” “I shall be right back. Your medicine is simmering. It should be ready now.” Soon she brought in the decoction, helped him drink it, and urged him to sleep. Hu refused, saying that he felt quite alert and that the scar on his leg itched unbearably. “That is a good sign. New flesh is growing over the wound, and you are recovering. Let me wash your feet. You will feel more comfortable.” Before she said this he had scarcely noticed, but now he felt itchy all over and longed for a good soak in a public bath. Like the people of Yangzhou, he had long enjoyed “water inside the skin in the morning, skin inside the water in the afternoon”—tea first, a bath later. Since Hangzhou’s situation had grown critical, he had not visited a bathhouse. On reaching Shanghai, his injured leg had again prevented him from bathing. A male servant at Gu’s house had washed him once, and he had changed from head to foot into new clothes that the Seventh Lady had a tailor make specially. But on the hazardous voyage since then, cold sweat from worry and fear had broken out and dried, then broken out again, countless times. His whole body was coated in grime. He was deeply uncomfortable and longed to bathe, yet could hardly ask A’qiao to undertake such a task.

While he was thinking this, she had already gone to put her words into action. Somehow she found a brand-new wooden footbath on high legs. She brought a kettle of hot water, poured it into the basin, and lifted the quilt to take hold of his feet. “No, no!” Hu drew them back. “I have not washed them since I boarded the boat in Shanghai. They are filthy.” “What does that matter?” she said without hesitation. “I have come all this way to nurse a sick man. Nothing would make me happier than to see you recover properly.” Her words filled Hu with both gratitude and reflection. Through war and chaos, he had narrowly escaped death. Thinking of his missing family, the people of Hangzhou on the verge of starvation, and Wang Youling trapped in a hopeless position, moving step by step towards hell, he had often asked himself what people lived for. Was it merely to endure suffering worse than death? Now he felt differently. Life held joy as well as sorrow. Which one a person experienced depended on his own actions. It was just as the Treatise of the Exalted One on Response and Retribution said: misfortune and happiness have no fixed gate; people bring them upon themselves. With this change of thought, his heart seemed to come back to life, like a withered tree touched by spring. His mind grew active too. Where he had been too weary to think about anything, thoughts now came readily and quickly. Once A’qiao had washed his feet, he smiled. “A’qiao, since you have started a good deed, see it through. Give my body a wipe as well.” “That would not be wise. You are weak and must not catch cold…”

“It does not matter!” Hu thrust out his withered arm and punched the air twice, trying to look vigorous. “I feel well enough to get up.” “Nonsense! Lie down properly.” She tucked his feet and hands beneath the quilt, pressed him back onto the bed, and pulled the covering snugly around him. “Truly, A’qiao, I am well again.” “How can you be? After an illness like this, you do not recover just because you say so. Even an elixir could not work that fast.” “Good fortune lifts the spirits. You are my elixir. Once it arrives, every illness vanishes.” “Hmph!” Her lips curled slightly. “You are good at sweet talk. Go to sleep.” With a slender finger she brushed his eyelids shut. As soon as she turned away, his eyes opened again. He stared at the canopy and thought. There were many things he wanted to know, but for the moment, A’qiao was the one person he wished to talk to.

She did not return for a long time. Unable to restrain himself, he called out. It was Xiao who answered. “Mr Hu, you must not tire yourself. It is two in the morning. Please rest.” “Where is A’qiao? Where is she sleeping?” Large wholesale firms commonly provided guest rooms and beds, but they did not ordinarily accommodate women. Some merchants and their customers even objected to a woman’s presence, believing that a menstruating woman might offend the God of Wealth worshipped on the premises. Yang’s Ningbo firm, also called Daji, specialized in wholesaling seafood and sundries. It was comparatively broad-minded and did not forbid women to come and go, but it had few private guest rooms. Xiao had therefore arranged for A’qiao to stay at a clerk’s house, where she had shared a bed with the man’s newly married wife for one night. “That will not do tonight. It is the clerk’s turn to sleep at home, and he gets only one night in ten. Sister A’qiao said, ‘Such a fresh, sweet young bride! How could I keep the two of them apart?’ The clerk was very considerate and insisted it did not matter, but she would not agree.” “Then where will she sleep tonight?” “There.” Xiao pointed to a door panel and two benches standing to one side. “I have everything ready to make her a temporary bed. Still—” He smiled and said no more. Something knowing in his expression invited suspicion, and Hu naturally pressed him. “Still what?” “This bed looks quite large. Why not let her sleep at the foot of it? She wants to stay in this room so that she can look after you. Would sharing the bed not be more convenient still?” Hu could not tell whether he was serious or joking, nor did he know what A’qiao herself intended. He could only smile without answering.

In the end, Xiao did set up a temporary bed for her. Naturally, she arranged her own mattress, quilt, and pillow. Once she had given the patient his medicine and closed the door, Hu spoke. “Your mattress is too thin, and you have no bed curtains. You would do better to sleep on the inner side here.” He patted the place beside him. A’qiao, who was removing her hair ornaments and make-up, said nothing. She brought her quilt over, but took the outer side so that she could get up easily. Ningbo people took pride in their beds. This one, carved from boxwood, was enormous, with more than enough room for two. An oil lamp stood on a shelf at the inner side, its flame turned low. Its faint light fell on her close-fitting, light lined jacket of pale green satin. Looking at her, Hu once again felt that their reunion was a dream. “A’qiao, surely it is time you told me what has happened to you?” “It is a long story,” she replied gently. “You have tired yourself tonight and have just taken your medicine. Sleep well. We can talk tomorrow.” “I feel very alert now.” “It is good that you do. But listen—the cocks are already crowing. These last hours of sleep before dawn matter most. Go to sleep. I am here now. What is there to hurry about?” There was a depth of meaning in this that gave Hu plenty to think about. But his illness had only just begun to recede, and his strength was limited. Soon he grew drowsy and slept until daylight.

When he woke, she woke too and hurried to get up and attend to him. Hu wanted her to rest a little longer. Holding her back, he said, “It is too cold. Do not get up yet. Let us have a proper talk.” “About what? I only want you to get well soon. Everything else can wait until we are back in Shanghai.” “I thought it over last night. If I come safely through this, I shall never be an official again. I shall mind my own business and be content if I can meet a few good friends regularly for a chat.” “You think only of your friends,” she said with a trace of reproach. “You never make plans for yourself.” Plans for himself would naturally have to include her. The implication was delicate. Hu showed no reaction, but calmly asked, “How did you leave the He household? Can you tell me now?” “Of course I must tell you. But you are always thinking of your friends. I am afraid it will distress you.” She was counting He Guiqing among Hu’s friends—and his situation was now pitiable indeed. One mistaken decision at Changzhou had brought dismissal and an order for his arrest and investigation. For two years the matter had dragged on, largely because Xue Huan had contrived excuses and delays on his behalf. Yet he had suffered more than enough of a fugitive’s existence.

“It was no life for a human being,” A’qiao sighed. “With his status so compromised, he could go nowhere. Whenever a visitor was announced, we first had to find out exactly why he had come. Most frightening of all was a call from the Shanghai magistrate: we feared he might have come to make an arrest. I have learned the truth of that saying, ‘Do no wrong by day, and a midnight knock will not frighten you.’ It could not be truer. If I felt like that, imagine how he felt himself.” “In his place, I should have steeled myself and surrendered.” “He often said the same. But saying it was all he could do; he could never bring himself to act. Now…” Now even those days of constant apprehension were nearly over. Since the late emperor’s death, the accession of a child sovereign, and the two empress dowagers’ assumption of government behind the curtain with Prince Gong in high favour, a new spirit prevailed at court. To restore morale, civil and military officials who had lost armies and dishonoured the state were to be dealt with severely. Worst of all, Zeng Guofan had been appointed governor-general of Liangjiang and placed in command of military affairs in Jiangsu, Anhui, Jiangxi, and Zhejiang. All officials in those four provinces, up to governors in the civil administration and commanders-in-chief in the military, were subject to his authority. He Guiqing had sent intermediaries to ask for an opportunity to redeem himself through service. The reply had been brief: willing to help, but unable to do so. “Half a month ago someone brought word that Mr Zeng had recommended an intendant named Li to lead troops to defend Shanghai. They say that as soon as he arrives he will take over from Governor Xue. He is Mr Zeng’s pupil and will naturally obey his teacher. Governor Xue will no longer be able to help, however much he wishes. Because of that—” Because of that, He had to make his final arrangements. He settled his household affairs and dismissed his concubines. That was how A’qiao had left his household.

Remembering how well he had treated her, she had felt uneasy about leaving him at such a moment. But he would not change his mind, and she had no choice but to obey. “He must have tried to provide for the rest of your life before you left,” Hu said. “Though how much could he have had left? Two years of living on his capital must have brought him close to the end.” “Ordinary expenses did not amount to much. Most of it was swindled out of him. One man said he could work through influential contacts in the capital; another said a gift must be sent to someone or other. Who knows how much money went down those holes? Before I left, he said he would scrape together two thousand taels for me. I would not take it.” “That was loyal of you. But in times like these, to be frank, you are better off relying on yourself than on others.” “I have not left myself entirely without provision. I entrusted a little chest to the Seventh Lady. The things in it must be worth thirty to fifty thousand taels. When we return to Shanghai, I shall give it to you.” “What for? I have no heart for managing investments for you just now.” A’qiao said nothing. She blinked and bit a fingernail, as though considering something of great importance. Ever since Hu had bade his distant farewell to Wang across the Qiantang River, he had felt that every desire was extinguished. He had neither wished nor been able to think about anything, and had sunk into illness. Though he was now out of danger and recovering quickly, the old listlessness remained. He did not care to guess her thoughts. Turning his face towards her, he merely admired her at leisure, as he might contemplate a piece of ancient jade he loved.

It had been six years since he had looked at her in this way. Six years of war and separation was a long time. How many people had vanished, their survival uncertain, their whereabouts unknown? How many had risen or fallen, their fortunes utterly changed? Yet A’qiao as she had been six years ago seemed only a night and a dream away. Her appearance then came vividly before his mind. Comparing the two, he saw what had changed and what had not. The most obvious change was in her figure: she was fuller now. Before, she had been rather too slender, so the change had made her more beautiful. She was also more composed and worldly-wise. What had not changed was the feeling in her eyes, as deep and pure as ever. It seemed that, in her mind, there was no one but Hu Xueyan—not even herself. At that thought, a spark appeared among the cold ashes of his heart. It was the beginning of a rekindled fire, and he himself felt how precious it was.

Without quite realizing it, he reached for her hand. “I came close to death on this journey,” he said with feeling. “I do not mean that I was timid or feared the dangers on the road. I mean what happened to my heart. From Hangzhou to Ningbo, it grew utterly cold. I lay in bed all day asking why one person should care for another. Without affection, he could be himself and I could be myself, and I need never fret over him. So I told myself that, once my mind was at peace again, I would be more distant towards everyone.” He had said this in a single rush and had to pause for breath. A’qiao did not realize that he had more to say. Taking it as an indirect message to her, she changed colour. “Are you saying this especially for me?” “Yes—” Only then did Hu notice her expression. He understood her mistake at once and hastened to add, “I have never said this to anyone else. There is a reason why I am telling you alone. I wonder whether you can guess it.” The meaning remained obscure, but she could see how anxious he was to correct the misunderstanding. Half reassured, she waited for what he would say next. “Do not make me guess. You know I can match wits with other people, but I am far behind you.” Hu smiled. It was not a handsome smile. His wasted face made his mouth seem large, and the two pointed canine teeth looked almost like fangs. But it was a happy smile, and she was glad to see it.

“You still know just what to say.” He grew serious. “I spoke of my state of mind because there was one thing I wanted to tell you: I have changed my mind now.” “How?” “People must have feelings after all. Even if they suffer for them, even if they die for them—” Before he finished, a warm, soft hand covered his mouth. “With so many things you could say, why talk so recklessly?” “Very well, I shall not say it. So long as you understand.” He asked, “You seemed to be thinking hard about something just now. Why not tell me?” “There is much to say. But as things are, you have no heart to listen, and I have no heart to speak. Nothing need be hurried. We can talk once you are well.” “I shall not be fully well for some time. Fortunately—” He had meant to say that at least he would not die, but remembering her objection to the word, he stopped himself. After a pause he continued, “In a day or two, I mean to return to Shanghai.” “How can you?” “Why not? Here in Ningbo, news is slow and there is nothing to do. A healthy man would fall ill from sheer boredom. How can a sick one recover?”

“It cannot be helped. You have only just begun to improve. If you travel in the bitterest winter cold, exposed to the sea wind, and your illness returns, there will be no one to help in the middle of the ocean, however loudly we call. It would mean two lives lost.” “Why two?” “Have you not thought? If anything happened to you, what could I do but throw myself into the sea?” Faced with such devotion, extending to life and death, Hu could no longer bear to oppose her. Privately, however, he thought that with care over his diet and A’qiao’s attentive nursing, there would be no great risk. Yet without the doctor’s agreement, he could not silence her objections, and Xiao would probably object as well. He decided to ask Xiao to sound out the doctor privately. But he could never find an opportunity. From the moment she got up, A’qiao scarcely left his side. Snow had begun to fall again, and Xiao suggested that she set up all her operations in the room. With a brazier burning brightly, she prepared medicine, cooked gruel, and made dishes there. Hu found the bustle lively and amusing. Using a Hangzhou saying, he teased her about conducting a grand religious ceremony inside a snail shell. But it meant that, although Xiao was right before him, there was no chance for a private word.

The time was not wholly wasted. Helping A’qiao prepare the food, Xiao told Hu everything that had happened over the past few days. Apparently the negotiations between Huang Chengzhong and Fan Ruzeng and the British consul, Xiafuli, had gone well. The commanders had promised to do all they could to protect foreign residents. Two rebel soldiers who had attacked a British missionary had been arrested and executed. Proclamations had also been issued to reassure the population, allowing trade outside the four gates. Business in Ningbo had largely returned to normal. “Our rice made the difference. Food is the people’s first necessity. Once there is no panic over grain, it is easier for them to settle down,” Xiao said consolingly. “At least that should ease some of your regret, Mr Hu.” “It is a good deed that earns unseen merit,” A’qiao added. “For that deed alone, the Bodhisattva will surely protect your mother, turn danger into safety, and bring her through her troubles.” Hu remained silent. Joy and sorrow mingled in him, and he could find no words adequate to express what he felt.

“There is something important I must ask you,” Xiao continued. “The people at Daji wanted to know how the rice was to be accounted for. Should they sell it and divide the proceeds with us, or take it over at an agreed price? I told them to receive the rice first. The terms could only be settled after I had asked you. If they would not agree to that, I had no authority to do otherwise, and the rice would have to return on the same boats. Daji accepted. Now I must ask you. In my view, sharing the proceeds would be the more advantageous arrangement.” “No,” Hu answered firmly. “I do not want money.” What did he want, then? Rice—and vessels to carry it. Once Hangzhou was recaptured, he wanted them available within three days. His purpose was easily understood. When the city was recovered from the rebels, the roads would surely be strewn with those who had starved, and the city full of distressed survivors. What they would need was rice. “Why arrange it like that?” Xiao urged. “Speaking strictly as a businessman—and everyone admires your calculations—would this not leave your capital tied up and idle? Besides, the recapture of Hangzhou may be a long way off.” “Not necessarily. Fortunes will turn,” Hu said with some agitation. “The rebels’ conduct is erratic and unreliable. I do not think they can last. In three or five years they will be finished.” “Three or five years is a long time. With interest compounding, one dan of rice can become two or three. Even for the sake of Hangzhou’s people, you ought to consider the calculation.” “That is true.” Hu grew calmer again. “But I have my own reasons. First, I have not entirely given up hope. Reinforcements may arrive, and Hangzhou may not fall. If the supply route can be opened, I shall want to send rice at once. If it were not readily available, we might lose the opportunity. Second, if the city does fall, keeping rice ready means it can be dispatched whenever the place is recovered. It is a hope with which I comfort myself. To put it plainly, I am deceiving myself, but at least I shall have done what I can for Hangzhou.” “That makes sense too. I shall negotiate with Daji on those terms.”

“There is no hurry about that,” Hu said. “When does the doctor come?” “Every day after lunch.” “Then let us eat early, so she has time to clear everything away.” He turned to A’qiao. “When the doctor comes, will you need to withdraw?” Women of the household did not receive male visitors, but doctors were an exception; she need not necessarily hide herself. His question, however, implied that he wanted her to withdraw. Understanding perfectly, she gave the answer he desired. “I shall listen from behind the screen.” Hu had deliberately asked because he knew she would offer to leave. But listening behind a screen hardly amounted to leaving at all: he had lured the tiger aside without getting it off the mountain. He needed another plan. That was simple enough. First he asked Xiao to write a letter for him, keeping his hands occupied. Then he said he fancied some sweet soup and sent A’qiao to the kitchen for foreign sugar. Once she was out of the way, he could speak privately. “You need not write the letter, Jiaji. There is something I want to tell you. Come here.” Xiao came to the bedside. “I have decided to return to Shanghai at once,” Hu said. “Speak to the doctor. Whatever happens, I must go.” “Why?” Xiao asked in surprise. “What is the hurry?”

“No particular reason. I simply must go. Only in Shanghai can I find out what is happening.” Hu continued, “My heart had gone completely cold. But after talking with A’qiao this morning, I feel very differently. I have two things to do now. The first is to save Hangzhou. However hopeless its condition, I shall treat even a dead horse as though it could still be saved. The second is to attend to my business. In business one cannot afford to fall a single step behind. The sooner I reach a place where news travels freely, the better. Do you understand?” “I understand the second point, but not the first. How are you going to save Hangzhou?” “There is no time to explain.” Hu glanced anxiously towards the window. A slender figure had just passed outside; A’qiao was about to come in. Seizing the few moments left, he instructed Xiao to negotiate privately with the doctor and let her know nothing of it. Xiao could not readily understand his purpose, but there was no time to ask. He could only do as he was told. By the time Hu had finished a bowl of longan-and-egg soup sweetened with foreign sugar and A’qiao had put everything in order, the doctor arrived.

The physician enjoyed a considerable reputation, distinguished as much for his professional integrity as for his skill. Though Hu was still thin and weak, his eyes shone brightly. The doctor was astonished: in a single night, more than half the illness seemed to have gone. He acknowledged that he had seldom seen such a thing in forty years of practice. “That is naturally due to your skill, sir.” Somewhat apologetically, Hu asked, “May I know your surname?” “Dr Zhang,” Xiao answered for him, adding a flattering introduction. “The foremost physician in Ningbo. His is a family of scholarly doctors, seven generations in the profession. Dr Zhang himself holds a scholarly degree.” Presumably that meant he had qualified as a licentiate. “I have been wanting in respect,” Hu said. “I have no degree at all.” “You are too modest, Your Excellency. Wherever one goes in this great world, a man entitled to the hat button of the third rank is above ten thousand others.” “A pity there is still more than one man above me!” Hu laughed loudly at his own expense.

He laughed too suddenly, caught his breath the wrong way and began coughing violently. Xiao hurried to pat his back, but the cough only grew worse. Dr Zhang too was helpless and could only wait for it to stop. A’qiao became desperately anxious. She knew Hu’s complaint: his throat needed stroking, and honey water would quiet the cough. Xiao was using the wrong method, so naturally it did no good. Honey water could not be procured at once, but the other remedy was within reach. “Mr Xiao,” she could not help calling from behind the screen, “tilt his head back and stroke his throat.” The voice was soft and sweet, with the gentle inflections of the Wu dialect. Curious, Dr Zhang turned to look and asked directly, “Is there a lady here?” A physician might ask anything without offence. Xiao, however, found the question awkward. As he stroked Hu’s throat, he replied indistinctly, “Mm, yes.” The doctor seemed about to say more, then stopped. Once the coughing ceased, he took Hu’s pulse, inspected his tongue and asked carefully about his food, sleep and daily habits. He then announced with satisfaction, “The illness is no longer dangerous. He needs only nursing and rest. He should recover in about a fortnight.”

“Thank you, thank you!” Hu clasped his hands. “Jiaji, take Dr Zhang to your room to write the prescription.” Xiao understood. When the prescription was finished, he raised the question of Hu’s wish to return to Shanghai. The doctor was deeply perplexed. “It would be inadvisable even to move him to another bed. His grave illness has only just begun to improve. How can he act so rashly?” “There really is important business in Shanghai that cannot be managed without him,” Xiao said. “The journey takes only a day or two. If you would prescribe several doses to support his recovery, and we were especially careful in looking after him, surely he would come to no harm?” “Look after him? Who would do that? If the illness returned, I should not be aboard. What would you do then?” “Quite so,” Xiao said. “We shall have to abandon the idea.” But Hu had sharp ears. Listening from the next room, he had already found an answer: invite Dr Zhang to make a professional visit to Shanghai and care for him during the voyage.

Once the doctor had taken his leave and entered his sedan chair, A’qiao no longer needed to remain hidden. With Xiao present, Hu revealed his idea. It was not inherently impracticable. Wealthy households often paid handsomely to engage a doctor in this manner and sent special carriages or boats to fetch him. But at this particular time and place, both A’qiao and Xiao thought it would be difficult. “It would be ideal if he agreed,” Xiao said. “I only fear he will not. First, Ningbo is still unsettled. He may be uneasy about leaving his home. Second, the New Year is approaching. Ningbo people attach great importance to the family reunion. Even those doing business elsewhere hurry home. Who sets off on a long journey then?” “There is still time before New Year. I shall certainly send him back beforehand,” Hu replied. “It is for me to ask and for him to agree or refuse. Why not go and speak to him?” “Of course I can. I must go to his Qingyi Hall to have the prescription made up anyway, so I can see him there.” “He owns a pharmacy too?” Hu said. “Excellent! Even if he will not come to Shanghai, I should like to talk with him.” Everyone knew Hu wanted to open a pharmacy. An idea occurred to Xiao, and he nodded. “You might well find much to discuss.” “That is another matter. Jiaji, so long as he agrees to come, we shall accept whatever terms he names. And appeal to his particular interests. You understand?” “I understand.” Xiao smiled. “But I fear you must invite him here and speak to him yourself.” More than two hours later he returned. Dr Zhang had accepted an invitation to dinner, he reported, and was fond of calligraphy and painting. Asked about inviting him to accompany them as physician, Xiao said he had not yet found it convenient to raise the matter. He added that A’qiao ought to make the request. It was an unreasonable favour to ask; a lady’s appeal would be more likely to succeed.

“That is true. When a man speaks, his word must mean what it says. If he is rebuffed or has to climb down, his words will carry less weight afterwards. A’qiao, will you ask him?” “I ought to refuse. So a woman’s word is worth nothing, and it does not matter if she is rebuffed or has to climb down? What treatment! Still—” She deliberately assumed a helpless expression. “Ah, there is nothing for it. I shall have to do the asking.” That settled, they had to prepare dinner for their guest. Hu thought ordering dishes from a restaurant would be more respectful. A’qiao welcomed the saving of trouble and readily agreed. Xiao was less enthusiastic. He had something else to say to her, out of Hu’s hearing. “You need not trouble about these small matters, Mr Hu. I shall discuss them with A’qiao. Let me go with you to look at the kitchen first.” When they reached a secluded stretch of the corridor where Hu could not hear, Xiao stopped. “A’qiao, do you really mean to help Mr Hu carry this through?” “Yes. I did not approve at first, but if he insists, I can only do as he wishes.” “If you mean to do as he wishes whatever happens, there is something I must say. But you must not strike me.”

“I shall not. Tell me.” “Dr Zhang is very interested in you. I do not know how he discovered it, but he knows you as Madam He. It was ‘Madam He’ this and ‘Madam He’ that; he never stopped asking.” Xiao paused to watch her face. Naturally, she did not look pleased. She flushed scarlet with anger. “A doctor like that! A heartless beast! He deserves to be cut into a thousand pieces!” “There, you see?” Xiao said calmly. “I knew you would be angry.”

That reminded her that he had more to say; if she carried on like this, she would never hear it. On reflection, her own awkward position was to blame. Madam He appearing in a man named Hu’s quarters—people would hardly suppose her a respectable woman. Since that was so, she might as well use a little crooked ingenuity herself. As these thoughts passed through her mind, her expression softened. “Let him talk whatever nonsense he pleases. So long as I behave properly, that is enough. Go on.” “Mr Hu must leave, and he cannot leave unless Dr Zhang comes too. So I had to try a little trick. You are an understanding woman, A’qiao, and for Mr Hu’s sake I am sure you will not blame me.” This sounded ominous and rather alarmed her, though she kept her wariness to herself. Outwardly she took a different tone. “Of course not. I know you are doing it for him. Tell me, and we shall discuss it.” “I thought that if I simply asked him to accompany us to Shanghai, he would refuse. Once he had said no, we should be at an impasse. So an idea came to me. I said, ‘Madam He specially asked me to invite you. She will cook a couple of dishes herself this evening and hopes you will join her for wine. She begs the honour of your company.’ He was delighted and said, ‘Certainly, certainly!’” It was a beauty’s lure. A’qiao naturally disliked it, but the thought that it was for Hu’s sake kept her from resenting Xiao. “What else did he say?” she asked calmly.

“He said more, of course. ‘Why should Madam He invite me?’ I told him, ‘To thank you for curing Intendant Hu. She also has another favour to ask.’ He kept asking what it was, but I would not tell him. Now everything depends on you.” A’qiao nodded and considered all he had said. A plan formed in her mind, but one point had to be settled first. “When he asked why I was here, what did you tell him?” “I said, ‘Madam He has now been dismissed from her household. She is Intendant Hu’s sister-in-law, the elder sister of his wife. Suzhou is occupied, so she cannot return to her own family and has had to seek refuge with a close relation.’ He said, ‘That explains it! In troubled times one cannot be overparticular about appearances. It is only right that a wife’s elder sister should nurse her brother-in-law.’” A’qiao understood. This supposed sister-in-law relationship implied that she had a younger sister who was one of Hu’s concubines. The arrangement made them close enough, yet not too close: it explained her freedom to come and go in his rooms and care for him, while leaving the amorous doctor enough hope to take the bait.

She greatly admired Xiao’s ingenuity, but could not help laughing. “You dreadful boy! How ever did you think of such a thing?” she said in the broadest Suzhou dialect. Xiao laughed too. “It appears that he would like to become Mr Hu’s brother-in-law by marrying his wife’s sister. With so close a family connection, anything could be discussed.” He reminded her, “This play is certain to come off beautifully. But whether you ought to prepare Mr Hu first is for you to decide.” After considering it, A’qiao concluded that she must tell Hu something, but not everything. She had known the world of the pleasure quarters. Other workings of a man’s mind might escape her, but in this one respect she understood him perfectly. A man might be generous by nature, yet he could not endure a rival taking the woman he loved. It was not merely jealousy; his pride was involved as well. Having made some arrangements for the food and wine, she found a moment when Xiao was absent. “Must you really have Dr Zhang accompany you to Shanghai?” she asked Hu. “Yes!” His answer was decisive. “Unless he comes, you will not feel easy. We must find a way to persuade him.” “Xiao and I have settled on a way. But there is something I must say first. We can only proceed if you agree.”

Hu could hear at once that there was more behind her words. He trusted them both and might as well be magnanimous. “You need not tell me. Do whatever you think best.” “Oh, how generous you sound!” Her voice held a warning. “Do not turn mean afterwards.” That deserved some thought. Hu prided himself on his generosity and his readiness to accept a loss, and did not relish being called mean. Yet A’qiao knew the world and was a shrewd judge of men; she would be especially unlikely to misjudge him. There must be a reason for the word. Though his mind was not as quick as usual, it was still quicker than most. He recalled Xiao’s hesitation on returning, his air of having something awkward to say, and soon grasped the clue. Deliberately, he said, “If you think I shall be mean, you must be planning to give him something I treasure. Is that it?” “Yes. What do you treasure?” “Only one thing.” Hu smiled. “A living treasure.” “You are the living treasure!” A’qiao smiled radiantly and said no more about it.

Dr Zhang arrived early. Naturally he first went to see his patient, and Hu offered the customary courtesies. “You have taken so much trouble that I do not know how to thank you. This simple meal is only a goose feather brought a thousand li—a small token of heartfelt regard. But I hope the day will come when I can repay you properly. Let us be friends, Dr Zhang.” “Then the honour is mine,” the doctor said. “I do feel there is an affinity between us. With the same illness and the same medicine, one patient responds to a single dose, while in another the drug sinks like a stone into the sea.
