That is the difference between a doctor and patient who have an affinity and those who do not.” “Indeed,” Xiao interjected. “You have an affinity with all of us.” “Life is all a matter of affinity,” Hu declared, launching into a little discourse. “I never dreamt that I should come to Ningbo. Even after arriving, I never imagined I should fall ill and owe my life to you—” “You are too kind!” Dr Zhang protested. “Medicine cures those not destined to die. Heaven protects a good man, and so the medicine takes effect. I dare not claim Heaven’s credit.” Just then the door curtain lifted. Even Xiao felt that the room had brightened. A’qiao had taken great care with her appearance. Though her make-up was light, the effect was extraordinarily striking. Above all, her clear, liquid eyes drew the doctor’s gaze irresistibly wherever they glanced. Xiao knew that she had not explained matters to Hu in sufficient detail. Afraid that their accounts might differ and expose the deception, he seized the opportunity of an introduction to remind them of their “relationship”.

“Dr Zhang,” he said, indicating A’qiao, “this is Madam He, His Excellency Hu’s sister-in-law.” Hu almost laughed aloud. Xiao had endless devices. How had he invented such a family connection? A’qiao, however, seemed entirely untroubled. Smiling gracefully, she gathered her skirts in a courteous salute and said with perfect composure, “Please sit down, Dr Zhang.” “You are too kind, too kind.” He hastened to return the courtesy, though his eyes would not leave her. “We all call Madam He A’qiao,” Xiao continued, busily drawing them together. “You should do the same.” “Yes, yes. A’qiao.” The doctor asked, “May I ask your youthful age?” “Youth? I am old now, a faded pearl without value. Thirty-two this year.” “One would never guess it. I know a little of physiognomy. Let me look at you carefully.” Whether he truly knew the art or merely wanted a pretext for admiring her at leisure was uncertain. Naturally, A’qiao had to take him at his word. She sat upright with a faint smile, allowing him to read her features. Her dignified ease betrayed neither that she had been a concubine nor that she had come from the pleasure quarters.

Dr Zhang looked her over from head to foot. Her feet were hidden beneath the folds of her skirt, but he could ask to examine her hands. Reading the hands was a normal part of fortune-telling, and she could hardly refuse. Ordinarily a man showed his left hand and a woman her right, only one in either case. She chose to be generous and held out both. Her fingers were like tender scallion shoots, long, white and slender. The nails too were long, with a delicate pink tint as though dyed with balsam flowers. Gazing at them, the doctor longed to reach out and clasp them.

“Excellent!” he said. “The signs of refinement and distinction. Your blessings will increase as you grow older.” A’qiao glanced at Hu, smiled faintly and ignored his predictions. “There are only a few dishes. I fear we shall not entertain you properly, Dr Zhang.” With that she rose and left. He felt an inevitable sense of loss. Men and women did not dine together, especially when the man was a stranger. By appearing, she had fulfilled the courtesies of a hostess. Indeed, by ordinary standards he had already enjoyed more than he could expect; there would be no further opportunity to see her. “But does she not have another favour to ask?” The thought reassured him. Whatever the request might be, he resolved that there could be no discussion unless she made it in person.

As expected, only Xiao kept him company at dinner. The dishes were delicately prepared. When the hotpot was brought in at the end, A’qiao spoke a few polite words from behind the curtain, then vanished again. They talked for some time after the meal, until Dr Zhang could bear it no longer. “Did you not say that A’qiao wanted me to do something for her?” “Yes. Let me go and ask.” The doctor watched the screen, straining his ears. After a long while someone emerged, but it was only Xiao. Behind the screen, however, a slender shadow could be seen. “A’qiao says you will certainly refuse, so she had better not mention it.” “Why should she not?” he blurted out. “What makes her think I shall refuse?” “Then I shall tell you!” came the answer from behind the screen.

A’qiao appeared with the words. She had changed her clothes. Earlier she had worn a black satin fur jacket edged with grey squirrel and a pleated skirt of palest blue silk. Now she was in informal dress: a silk-padded jacket of bamboo-green ning silk. Too fond of looking pretty to wear bulky padded trousers, she would not bind the cuffs at the ankles as northern women did either. Instead, her loose-cuffed trousers were of soft black satin, trimmed with very broad ornamental bands. For warmth, the jacket had been cut to fit closely at the waist, making her figure all the more graceful and alluring. Dr Zhang was half-tipsy, yet at heart he remained a cautious, proper man. Her beauty was so dazzling that he dared not look closely. Turning his face slightly aside, he asked, “Whatever you wish, A’qiao, please tell me. Is it a particularly careful prescription for a tonic paste?” “That too, naturally,” she replied. “But there is no hurry. My younger sister entrusted me with this duty. She was uneasy about leaving our dear relation alone in Ningbo. I cannot look after him constantly, and even when I do, I cannot be as attentive and considerate as my sister. I have been thinking how fortunate it was that, in such a grave illness, he found you. Your skill has brought him back to health as surely as spring revives the earth. He must be out of danger now. But if he travels to Shanghai by boat without you to care for him, I really cannot feel easy. So there is nothing for it but—” She drew an embroidered handkerchief from beneath her arm and smiled with her lips pressed together, as though too embarrassed to finish. Dr Zhang was intoxicated by the clear, oriole-like notes of her voice. Absorbed in the sound, he had paid too little attention to its meaning. Her sudden silence puzzled him. “Why have you stopped? Please go on. I am listening carefully.”

Her meaning was already perfectly clear. If he had listened carefully and still failed to understand, his mind must have been elsewhere. Seeing how thoroughly he had lost his senses, Xiao gave A’qiao a glance: she need not wheel her horse and draw her bow any longer, but should come straight to the point. “Very well, I shall. But, Dr Zhang—” Her large eyes flashed with a lively, almost girlish innocence. “Once I have said it, you must not turn me down!” This was a serious demand. The doctor collected himself and went over her words in his mind. Only then did he understand, and he found agreement rather difficult. “There, you see?” A’qiao deliberately spoke softly to Xiao. “I said I had better not ask. Now I have only invited a refusal—” “Not at all,” the doctor broke in uneasily. “I understand what you mean. The question I am considering is not whether I ought to accompany him.” “Then what is it?” “Whether the patient can travel. A sea voyage in weather like this—if his illness returned, it would be no laughing matter.”

His objection was reasonable, but it was hard to know whether it was sincere or merely an excuse. A’qiao was formidable herself and deliberately pressed him, though indirectly. Looking at Xiao, she asked, “Did Dr Zhang not say that, with him to look after the patient on the journey, there would be nothing to worry about?” “Certainly. With Dr Zhang there, what need we fear?” Their exchange made him seem unwilling to help, which distressed him greatly. Still he dared not act rashly. Rising, he said, “Let me examine him again.” In the next room, Hu had heard every word. Partly because the prospect of having his wish fulfilled delighted him, and partly because he meant to conceal his remaining symptoms, the examination naturally showed a marked improvement. Only now could Dr Zhang consider his own circumstances. Amid war and disorder, with the year drawing to its close, it was hardly the time for a long journey. But he had spoken too generously to retreat or qualify his offer. At the same time, romantic fancies filled his mind. He could not bear to relinquish the chance of sharing a sea voyage with A’qiao. At last he made up his mind and agreed.

Hu was naturally grateful beyond measure. But it was A’qiao’s gratitude that Dr Zhang wanted. Hu understood the delicate distinction perfectly. He wrapped a hundred taels in red paper and asked her to present them with her own hands. “A physician should have a heart ready to sacrifice his own flesh,” the doctor said, waving both hands in refusal. “To speak of money would obscure the sincerity of my intentions.” Fine words—but A’qiao understood those “intentions” only too well, and was the more determined to give him the money because she could not and would not repay them in the manner he desired. Naturally, she kept that thought deep within herself. “I understand your kind intentions, Dr Zhang. This is a small token from my ‘brother-in-law’. He says that if you refuse it, his mind will be uneasy and his recovery will be slower. In that case, he would not dare ask you to undertake the journey.” The doctor savoured every word. “I understand your kind intentions” was branded on his heart as if with a hot iron, never to be forgotten. “In that case, I can only be shameless enough to accept. But—” He went no further. To show A’qiao that he had agreed entirely for her sake, he decided to repay Hu’s gift with one of his own: a couple of valuable restorative medicines. But there was no need to announce it beforehand. To do so would spoil the gesture, so he checked himself.

“Then may I ask, Dr Zhang,” Xiao interposed, “when should we prepare to leave?” “The sooner the better. I must return before the New Year.” “You will certainly be back in time.” Xiao considered the arrangements and made a proposal. “Let me have all tomorrow to prepare. Shall we leave the day after?” “The day after tomorrow must be a good day.” A’qiao knew few characters, but could manage an almanac. Confidently pointing to the entry for the first day of the twelfth month, she read, “‘Auspicious for setting out.’”

CHAPTER FOUR

They spent a full day making the arrangements. Early on the third morning, everyone went aboard; after they had settled themselves, the gong sounded and the anchor was raised. Dr Zhang brought a bundle wrapped in blue cloth to Hu’s cabin. “Your gift was far too generous, Mr Hu. I feel ashamed to accept so much. I have brought two medicines for you to keep.” “Thank you, thank you! You are too kind.” Hu assumed that they were ordinary medicinal ingredients. Only when the bundle was opened and revealed two brocade-covered cases did he realize that they were precious tonics. In the long case lay a complete ginseng root, every fine rootlet intact, secured with red and green silk threads. A red label on gold paper bore eight characters: “Finest Wild Mountain Ginseng from Jilin.” “This root was an item of tribute. It came out of Minister Zhang’s household—genuine imperial-palace stock.” As he spoke, the doctor opened the square case. Inside were two sections of deer antler, each a little over a cun long and covered with fine white hairs. Nothing about their appearance told Hu whether they were good or bad.

“Everyone knows that velvet antler comes from a deer’s horns, but not every deer’s horn is velvet antler. Old antler is useless; inside it is all fibers and channels. What one wants is newly grown antler, filled with vital essence and blood, like this.” Dr Zhang went on, “There is an art to harvesting it too. If the man lacks skill, one stroke may take off the deer’s head—” He had seen the process himself. At the turn of spring into summer, when everything flourished, deer were driven into an empty enclosure and chased without respite. Timid creatures by nature, they fled with all their strength, driving their blood and vital energy upward into the newly growing antlers. A gate was then opened, just wide enough for one deer to escape at a time. Beyond it was a curved passage formed by rails. At one end a man waited with a sharp axe, concentrating intently. As the deer was about to emerge, he judged his moment and struck, severing precisely the newly grown section of antler. Only velvet antler gathered in this way was of the finest quality. Hu listened with great interest. “They say, ‘No one may see how medicines are prepared, but Heaven knows the maker’s heart.’ Yet good or bad quality will become known in time. One person tells ten, and ten tell a hundred; that is how a reputation spreads. Dr Zhang, I hear that you have a pharmacy too. It must be a large concern.” “I could hardly call it that. It is a family business that I merely keep going. My medical practice keeps me so busy that I have little time for it.”

At this, Hu could hardly pursue the subject. Liu Bucai had become so absorbed in gambling that he took little interest in Hu’s plan to open a pharmacy. Hu had thought of asking Dr Zhang’s advice, but a man who could scarcely tend his own affairs would hardly leave his own field to cultivate another’s. There was no point in discussing it. Still, the doctor was an honored guest specially invited to accompany them, and they must do their best to entertain him. What diversion could they find? Conversation with the hands would be better than mere talk: the most suitable amusement, and almost the only one available, was a game of mahjong. Ningbo mahjong was as famous as the Cantonese variety. It was said that the transformation of madiao cards into mahjong had resulted from Ningbo players’ continual experiments and improvements since the Ming dynasty. Dr Zhang was a devotee himself and readily agreed to Hu’s proposal.

Hu, of course, could not play. They needed one more person to complete the table, so the boat’s master would have to join them. Xiao Jiaji was an expert, but what of A’qiao? He had to ask. “Sister A’qiao, have you played with Ningbo people before?” “Of course.” “Have you ever played aboard a boat like this?” “This is only the second time I have traveled in one. But mahjong is mahjong. What difference does it make whether one plays afloat or ashore?” “For this kind of mahjong, you need a good memory—” “Naturally.” She thought the warning unnecessary. “If you cannot remember what the players before and after you are discarding or taking, how can you play at all?” Hearing this, Xiao could hardly say more. They unfolded a small square table and divided the counters. The boat’s master then lowered a cord fastened to the cabin roof and hung a bamboo basket on the hook at its end. It hung exactly over the center of the table, level with their heads and within easy reach. A’qiao had no idea what it was for.

She was competitive by nature and hated to admit ignorance of anything, so she would not ask. It must have some use; she would watch and see. They determined the dealer and took their seats. A’qiao sat next in turn after Dr Zhang, while the boat’s master, opposite her, dealt first. Having drawn his fourteen tiles, he inspected them from left to right, immediately laid them face down, and displayed a North Wind as his discard. Xiao, who preceded him in the order of play, called “Pung!” Dr Zhang explained to A’qiao, “This is where the Ningbo players calculate so finely. On his first discard the dealer puts out North instead of South. If the player before him claims it for a pung, the dealer gets another draw at once. It may be a South Wind, giving him the pair that would otherwise have gone to the next player. A draw like that makes all the difference.” His repeated use of the word for drawing tiles, with its other sense of touching or feeling, grated on A’qiao. She ignored him. Xiao displayed an East Wind; when no one wanted it, he raised his hand and tossed it into the hanging basket.

So that was the basket’s purpose! A’qiao began to feel alarmed. “The Ningbo way of playing is different,” she blurted out. “Yes—” Dr Zhang immediately explained. Heavy seas made the vessel pitch and roll, so Ningbo players prided themselves on remembering their tiles at a glance and playing with them face down. Space aboard was limited too; there was no room for a large square table. Sometimes four players merely sat in a circle with a board across their knees. Naturally, there was no spare space for discarded tiles, so these went into a basket. “But this table is not so very small,” he added, looking at the boat’s master and Xiao. “Why not play as we do ashore?” The master had no objection. Xiao, however, smiled—and that smile offended A’qiao. She thought he was looking down on her and resented it bitterly.

“Never mind, never mind,” she said. “Let us play by the rules.” In effect, she had refused the doctor’s kindness, but he took no offense. She, however, was discovering the truth of the saying about suffering any misery to save face. Trying to remember the other three players’ discards was a torment. Halfway through the hand, all three seemed ready to win, while her own tiles were still a muddle. The longer they played, the harder it became to remember which tiles had already appeared and which had not. “This is no way to go on,” she told herself. “I may never win a single hand. Losing money is one thing, but I cannot afford to lose face.” She decided to change her tactics and concentrate entirely on her own hand. Provided she did not become liable for another player’s limit hand after three exposed sets, she would risk discarding any tile, however unfamiliar. The doctor worried on her behalf, continually reminding her how many of a tile had appeared and which were exhausted. With this help, she settled comfortably into the game and devoted herself to building her own hands. In less than two rounds she won with a pure one-suit hand, a Three Dragons hand, and a mixed one-suit hand. Her luck was running high.

“Dr Zhang, the player after you is on a tremendous streak,” the boat’s master said. “You must keep a closer watch on her.” The more he urged caution, the more freely the doctor played. Not only did he refuse to withhold tiles she needed; he even broke up promising combinations to feed her, adding reminders such as, “Sister A’qiao, this is the fourth Three of Characters. If you do not take it now, there will be no other.” Xiao was playing sharply too, withholding the tiles the boat’s master wanted and scarcely allowing him a single claim. It was almost three against one. The master lost heavily, but dared not offend his customers. After four rounds he pleaded a stomachache and broke up the game. A’qiao alone had won a great deal, yet she had not found the game particularly enjoyable. Embarrassed to take the boatman’s money, she pushed away the counters. “Let it go! Let it go!” Then she stood up and left.

Her generosity naturally earned the boat’s master’s gratitude and respect, and he attended to them with exceptional care. The voyage to Shanghai went smoothly. Hu took his medicines regularly and developed no new symptoms, giving Dr Zhang little trouble. Nevertheless, he continued to show the doctor great consideration. As soon as they reached the Shanghai wharf, he sent Xiao ahead to announce the distinguished visitor and ask Gu to prepare a welcome. Gu was out, but fortunately the Seventh Lady could decide everything herself. She had already heard the earlier part of the Ningbo story from Li Delong. Though anxious about Hu’s illness, she had been somewhat reassured to know that A’qiao was caring for him. She had expected him to remain too ill for a long journey until after the New Year. His unexpectedly early return therefore filled her with both surprise and delight.

She hurriedly made the arrangements, hired three enclosed winter sedan chairs, and rushed to the wharf with male and female servants. Going aboard, she saw A’qiao first and then Hu. His frightening thinness saddened her, and she shed a couple of tears. “You must not laugh at me, Dr Zhang,” she said, rather embarrassed herself. “Little Uncle has suffered one misfortune after another lately. The story of his troubles would fill a basin with tears. But at least Heaven has sent him good people to help him, turning danger to safety and misfortune to good. That is how he came to meet someone as skillful and kind-hearted as you.” The doctor had heard of this lady, whose frank, outspoken manner commanded both respect and a measure of fear. If he hoped to win A’qiao, her help would be indispensable, so he was especially courteous. “You are too kind, too kind! You yourself are the most warm-hearted person under Heaven.” The Seventh Lady loved nothing better than to be praised for her kindness. She found that he had none of a famous physician’s airs: he was pleasant and agreeable in conversation. She took to him at once. Originally she had meant to put him up at an inn; now she changed her mind. “Dr Zhang, since you have come all this way, stay and enjoy yourself for a few days. You must be our guest at home—though I fear our little house will not do you justice.” A’qiao tugged at her, plainly disapproving. But the invitation had been given and could not be withdrawn. She could only see how the doctor answered and then decide what to do.

“You are too kind! I must get home before the year is out. I fear staying with you would put you to a great deal of inconvenience.” He meant this as a polite demurral, but the Seventh Lady took advantage of it to leave the question open. “Let us get you home first and discuss it there. We should go ashore now.” Hu naturally had to be settled first. A chair was carried aboard, and he was wrapped in a quilt like a swaddled infant, lifted inside, and screened securely behind its curtains. Getting him ashore required particular care. The boat’s master directed the operation himself; the whole crew helped, laying four gangplanks before carrying the chair to the wharf. The second chair was for Dr Zhang. The remaining one should have been A’qiao’s, but she insisted on squeezing into it with the Seventh Lady: she had something on her mind that could not wait. The Seventh Lady had barely heard the opening when she began to laugh. A’qiao was annoyed. “I am speaking seriously, and you laugh at me!” “I am not laughing at you. I am laughing at that doctor—a toad dreaming of swan’s flesh. Never mind. Tell me, and I shall find a way out for you.” “That is more like it!” Her annoyance vanished. She explained everything in detail, finishing just as the chair reached the house.

The first task at home was to settle Hu; the second was to entertain their guest. For that the master of the house must return. Moreover, the Seventh Lady had devised a way of rescuing A’qiao from her predicament, and Gu would have to carry it out. Xiao was eager to go home and see his mother, so she asked him to find his teacher first and explain the situation. He tried two places without success. Finally, at the firm, he learned that Gu had gone to the Zhejiang Maritime Grain Transport Bureau. Zhejiang’s tribute-grain shipments had long been suspended, and the bureau now served merely as the province’s dispatch station in Shanghai, forwarding official communications. Gu had presumably gone there for news of Hangzhou. Xiao was leaving a message and preparing to go when his teacher returned. His face was so gloomy that, if he had been seeking news, it was plainly bad.

The sight of his pupil, however, brought him pleasure. Like his wife, he had expected Xiao to remain away until after the New Year. His first question was therefore, “Where is our patient?” “He has come back too—with the doctor. It was very kind of the man to make the journey so near the year’s end. Madam asks you to come home at once. She wants you to show him a good time for a couple of days.” “That is easily done. But how is Little Uncle?” “Out of danger. A few days’ rest and care should get him on his feet.” Gu sighed deeply. “I fear that once he gets up, he will fall ill again.” Xiao understood at once. “Has Hangzhou fallen?” “On the twenty-eighth of last month.” Gu’s voice seemed drained of strength. “I have just heard at the bureau.” “And Governor Wang?” “They say he died for his duty. We do not yet know the details. Perhaps he escaped; one cannot be sure.” “No, he would not have.” Xiao remembered their recent meeting: the first time he had seen Wang had become their final parting. Hot tears streamed down his cheeks.

“Ah!” Gu stamped his foot and sighed. “If even you feel like this, what will it do to him? I truly do not know how to tell him.” “You must not tell him now. His illness would return at once. You must not merely keep silent, but find some way of preventing him from hearing it.” “I know. Go home and see your family. There is no need to come back tonight. We shall meet tomorrow morning.” Teacher and pupil took the same carriage. Gu dropped Xiao at home before returning to his own house. Naturally, he first greeted Hu with affectionate inquiries, then sat down for a cordial conversation with Dr Zhang. By then it was almost dinnertime.

The Seventh Lady found an opportunity to draw her husband aside and discuss the evening’s entertainment. At home, even with Li Delong, there would be only three men at dinner, making a rather dull party. Better to take the doctor to a restaurant. “Best of all, give him a dinner with courtesans,” she said. “Of course we shall do that. But how do you know he likes such dinners?” “It should not stop at dinner. Better still, find him a good woman whom he can take home. There is a reason for it; I shall explain later.” “I do not know what you are plotting, but I shall do as you say.” Gu continued, “There is something important I must tell you. Be extremely careful not to let Little Uncle hear it, or the consequences will be dreadful—” “You are driving me frantic! What is it? Tell me!” Yet no one knew her better than her husband. If he blurted out the news of Hangzhou, she would make such an outcry that everyone would hear. He warned her once more. “Do not cry out. Hangzhou has fallen. We do not know whether Governor Wang is alive, but almost certainly he died for his duty.” She did not cry out. For a long time she could say nothing. Then, like Xiao, she began to weep. Closing her eyes, she said, “How bitterly I regret it!”

“Regret it?” Gu was bewildered. “Regret what?” “We are his adopted kin, after all. It may have been presumption on our part, and we dared not take it too seriously, but the relationship existed. We watched him trapped in Hangzhou, waiting for death, and did nothing for him. Perhaps even beneath the earth he is reproaching us.” “This was fate. Even a man as capable as Little Uncle could do nothing. What could you and I have done? We can only find out exactly what happened. If he really died for his duty, we shall arrange a Water-and-Land service to deliver his spirit.” She said nothing, but knitted her brows in deep thought. Gu always watched her particularly closely at such moments: this was how she looked when faced with a difficult matter on which she meant to reach a decision. “Take our guest out first. Come back early if you can. And make further inquiries about Governor Wang.” He assented to each instruction, then asked, “Where will Dr Zhang stay?” “Here in our house,” she answered without hesitation. “We shall have considerable need of his help over the next few days.”

Gu did not know what she had in mind, but he understood that he must court the physician’s favor with particular care. He therefore arranged a full dinner at the best Western restaurant and summoned two courtesans to join them. The doctor was much taken with a popular woman, the Fourth Miss of Yanchun House. Gu accordingly arranged a two-table banquet at her establishment, sent invitations flying out to his friends, and installed Dr Zhang in the seat of honor. The guests were all veterans of the pleasure quarters; each summoned at least two courtesans. Surrounded by jewels and beauty, the party became extraordinarily lively. The doctor, new to the foreign settlement, was quite bewildered—and thoroughly enjoying himself. Naturally, their table talk included current events. Gu was anxious for news of Hangzhou and questioned the speakers closely.

According to their accounts, the army’s resolve inside Hangzhou had begun to collapse after the twentieth of the eleventh month. The chief reason was the exhaustion of food. On the twenty-fourth, a quantity of wood-ear fungus had been found in a dried-seafood merchant’s shop, allowing each man one liang by weight. On the twenty-fifth, some salted olives, known locally as “salted green fruit,” had been discovered, yielding half a liang apiece. Troops from other provinces then began searching households, rich and poor, for food. The governor’s own central troops were all Zhejiang men who had spent many years in Hangzhou and knew many people there. Retaining a little shame, they pulled their hat brims low and demanded provisions in assumed Fujian or Henan accents. Nor was looting food their only breach of military discipline. Order collapsed. Wang Youling personally led a small detachment of his own guards, seized more than a dozen offenders, and had them executed in the street. It did nothing, however, to restore either discipline or morale.

Another strange phenomenon had appeared: people were “selling money.” Copper cash was heavy and awkward to carry, so they exchanged it for silver bullion or silver dollars. Strings of coins were openly offered for sale with wisps of straw stuck into them as signs. Naturally, silver commanded a high price and copper fell cheap. These were preparations for flight. With the soldiers in such a state of mind, the civilians were more frightened still. Whenever they met, there was only one subject of conversation: on what day would the Longhairs take the city? On the twenty-seventh of the eleventh month, the defenders decided to seek life in the midst of death. At dawn the next day they would charge out through Genshan Gate, force a passage, and meet whatever relief forces might conceivably be outside. It was a desperate fantasy, yet at least it represented a determined attempt to save themselves, capable of heartening troops and people alike. It could do good and no harm. During the night, however, matters changed. Soldiers began lowering themselves from the wall in twos and threes. The planned assault had become desertion, each man seeking his own escape. It was said that someone had incited this change, and that the instigator was no less a person than Zhejiang’s provincial treasurer, Lin Fuxiang. Lin commanded a force called the Dingwu Army, whose discipline was the worst and whose fighting was the least effective. He was skilled at empty display, forever devising useless flourishes. He also liked ingenious stratagems, which usually cost him both the prize he sought and the men he risked. Many consequently suspected that he was secretly in league with the Longhairs. It was said that he and an expectant prefect surnamed Gan had visited the enemy camp to discuss matters.

Those rumors could not be verified, but one incident did give substantial grounds for suspicion. Wang Youling had captured a spy named Xu Zong’ao, a battalion commander whose appointment to the Dingwu Army Lin Fuxiang had recommended. The messages exchanged between Wang inside the city and Zhang Yuliang outside, fixing dates for coordinated operations, had all passed through the Dingwu Army. Xu had forwarded more than ten of these military dispatches to the Longhairs. After an informer exposed him, he was arrested, interrogated, and proved guilty. His entire family was beheaded, apart from a three-year-old son who was spared. Yet only this principal offender was dealt with. Though Wang had become deeply suspicious of Lin behind the scenes, he feared smashing the vessel while striking at the rat. With a powerful enemy surrounding them, he would not risk an internal conflict that might throw their own ranks into disorder. He could only suppress his anger and remain silent.

Lin, however, had indeed reached a secret understanding with the Longhairs. Unwilling to surrender openly, he had promised to help undermine the defenses from within. His incitement of the Genshan Gate troops to desert that night was meant to bring down Hangzhou’s tottering wall. The Longhairs did not notice the deserters during the darkness. At dawn they discovered the traces and took them as evidence that the garrison was disintegrating. They launched an attack, and Fengshan, Houchao, and Qingbo gates were the first to fall. When the news reached Wang, he knew that all was lost. “I have not failed the court,” he said, “only the hundreds of thousands of loyal and righteous people of Hangzhou.” He had long resolved to die for his duty. The moment had come. Returning to the governor’s yamen, he put on his formal robes and cap, bowed toward the imperial palace in gratitude for the emperor’s favors, and left a final letter. He swallowed gold; fearing that it would not kill him, he also took opium. By then the cries of the household mingled with the uproar outside. The Longhairs were approaching, and, rather than suffer humiliation, Wang hanged himself. Among those who died for their duty at the same time were Education Commissioner Yun Xigeng, Wenrui, brigade commander of Chuzhou, and Wu Baofeng, magistrate of Renhe. Salt Commissioner Zhuang Huanwen led the Quanzhou troops, fierce and experienced fighters recruited in that district of Fujian. They fought desperately to break out but were defeated, and Zhuang drowned himself.

Lin Fuxiang, by contrast, received exceptional favor from the Longhairs. He was lodged in the western reception hall of the provincial treasurer’s yamen, entertained with fine food and wine, and promised an escort to any destination he chose. He chose Shanghai. It was said that he would also escort Wang Youling’s coffin and surviving family there, for onward travel to their native Fujian. At this, Gu had to interrupt with questions. If Wang’s coffin came to Shanghai, Hu would inevitably insist on mourning before it. Quite apart from that, Gu’s own relationship with the dead man obliged him to pay his respects. He remembered his wife’s bitter regret that they had done nothing for their adopted kinsman while he lived. Bowing before the coffin would not be enough; they must also offer comfort and assistance to the surviving family if they were to ease their sense of guilt. No one could tell him when the coffin would reach Shanghai. That did not matter greatly. There would be a formal reception and roadside offerings when it arrived; any yamen would know, and the date would be easy to discover.

The courtesan banquet continued until midnight. Seeing how fascinated the doctor was with the Fourth Miss of Yanchun House, Gu thought of playing matchmaker. He drew aside the proprietor, a man nicknamed Big Jin, and asked whether the doctor might “borrow a bed for the night.” “Young Master Gu!” Big Jin laughed. “You are an old hand at pleasure. Think for yourself: is that the way things are done?” “Rules are made by people. Let me be frank. I owe this doctor a favor. Help me out, and leave the rules aside, will you? Besides, he is from out of town and will not be staying long. He cannot spend weeks observing all your formalities.” Gu was a lavish patron, and Big Jin wanted to cultivate him. He had inwardly consented as soon as the request was made. But agreeing too readily would cheapen his concession and prevent Gu from appreciating the favor. He therefore spoke of the young lady’s reputation, of how people would laugh if a man who had summoned her for the first time that day and observed none of the customary preliminaries were allowed to stay overnight. In the end, naturally, “For Young Master Gu’s sake, even if I were unwilling, I must agree.” Once that side had consented, however, the other refused. In Shanghai’s foreign settlement, Dr Zhang might be a countryman, but in Ningbo he was a person of standing. He would not give people grounds to say that he had spent his very first night in Shanghai in a courtesan house. He insisted on going home.

On returning, he examined Hu again, carrying out all four stages of a physician’s inquiry: inspection, listening and smelling, questioning, and taking the pulse. He then told Gu and his wife, with great satisfaction, that the patient would certainly be able to get out of bed in ten days. “Then, Dr Zhang,” the Seventh Lady said, “will you do me the honor of staying with us for those ten days?” “You are too kind, too kind!” He looked troubled. “Another ten days would take us to the festival for sending off the Kitchen God.” Gu too felt that, with the year drawing so rapidly to its close, forcing a man to remain far from home would be unreasonable and unkind. He proposed a compromise. “Stay five more days, then.” “Very well, five days.” The doctor looked a little bashful. “There is another matter in which I may have to ask a considerable favor of you both.” A’qiao, just about to lift the curtain and enter, heard this and turned away at once. Gu did not understand and was about to ask, but his wife quickly tugged at his clothes to silence him. “Please do not put it that way, Dr Zhang. If there is anything within our power, you have only to tell us. You must be tired today. Have some congee, then go to bed.” “I shall not have any congee. But yes, I am rather tired.” He sounded somewhat disappointed.

The Seventh Lady was always sincerely attentive to guests. She had cooked an excellent pot of congee with fresh sliced fish and insisted that he taste her handiwork. Besides, she said, there were still things to discuss. He could hardly persist in refusing, so they talked over the late supper. The first important subject was Hangzhou. The doctor had already heard the details at Yanchun House, so Gu could not properly repeat them at length. The essential points to tell his wife were that Wang’s death had been confirmed and that Lin Fuxiang was escorting the coffin to Shanghai. “Then I was right. My idea was sound.” She turned to the doctor. “Perhaps you do not quite understand the situation yet. Little Uncle and Governor Wang were friends bound to one another in life and death. To hear that the governor died so tragically must naturally upset him all the more while he is ill. But Little Uncle is exceptionally reasonable and knows how to accept things. He had already foreseen that the governor could hardly escape death, so the news will not be entirely unexpected. Now the coffin is coming to Shanghai and will soon leave for Fujian. If he cannot go and weep before it, he will blame us afterward. I therefore think we should tell him within the next day or two. What do you say, Dr Zhang?”

“It is very difficult to judge,” the doctor replied. “Nothing is worse for a sick man than grief. Yet from what you say, perhaps it would do no harm.” “Yingchun, what do you think?” She turned to her husband. Gu knew that she had already made up her mind. She was asking only to show respect for his position as her husband in front of their guest. He ought to take the hint—and taking it meant playing his part. “Dr Zhang is naturally cautious. But knowing Little Uncle’s temperament, one way might be to tell him plainly and put an end to his hopes.” “Exactly!” The doctor thought this perceptive. “His constant preoccupation with Hangzhou is not good for his recovery either. But you must prepare him gradually. Do not give him the news too abruptly.” “Certainly.” The Seventh Lady now came to her request. “I imagine that once he knows, there must be an outpouring of grief. He may suffer a shock, and we shall need you to take particular care of him. I may be a woman, Dr Zhang, but I do not like to beat about the bush. Let me say it beforehand: if his illness returns, I shall insist on keeping you in Shanghai for the New Year.” What could the doctor say in such circumstances? He could only offer a wry smile and give a vague assent for the moment.

When they had finished the congee, Gu personally escorted the doctor to his room. The Seventh Lady had arranged it herself: a large brass bed, entirely new bedding, and, as a special mark of honor, a canopy of grey squirrel fur. The lavish attention made the doctor quite uneasy. They chatted a little longer about where to go sightseeing the next day. Then, with “Until tomorrow,” Gu returned to the bedroom, where his wife had removed her makeup and was waiting for him. “Did Dr Zhang enjoy himself tonight?” “There was a Fourth Miss at Yanchun House whom he liked very much. I meant to arrange things so that he could stay there. Everything was settled, but he absolutely refused, so I had to let him have his way. What is behind all this eagerness of yours? I have been turning it over in my mind and cannot work it out.” “It is an amusing story. Do you know what brought him on this journey?”

Clearly the question had a meaning behind it. Gu covered his wife’s mouth with his right hand. “Do not tell me. Let me think.” An intelligent man needs only a hint. Once he began to think in terms of a woman, he immediately remembered A’qiao’s fleeting appearance at the curtain a moment earlier. The doctor’s attentive behavior toward her on his arrival also came back to him. Now he understood. “Was that the reason?” He withdrew his hand and bent two fingers to make the sign for seven, hinting at the word qiao, “cleverness.” The Seventh Lady looked rather disappointed. “How tiresome! How did you manage to guess?”

“Guessing that much tells me nothing. Come.” He drew her down to sit beside him. “Tell me the whole story.” She told it from beginning to end, omitting no detail, more clearly than anyone who had been present could have done. Each participant knew only what he or she had witnessed or been told. There were things Xiao was reluctant to say, while A’qiao and Hu had kept much of their thinking to themselves. Only before the Seventh Lady had they emptied their hearts, allowing her to grasp the whole truth. “That young rogue Jiaji!” Gu scolded, a little worried but also rather proud. “He was resourceful enough already. Put him with Little Uncle, and he learns ever more tricks. If he goes on like this, he may become so carried away that he thinks of nothing but crooked schemes.” “He is not that sort of person,” she replied. “Enough of that. There is something else you must know. You know how generous Little Uncle is, and he feels deeply indebted to the doctor. So my plan—” “Wait! You have not told me your plan yet. Do you mean to sacrifice the plum tree to save the peach—to find a substitute?” “Of course. Otherwise, things really will turn ugly. Little Uncle agrees that my plan is the only way. He would like it settled before the New Year so that the doctor can go home happy. He says he will pay a thousand taels or so and let him have the woman.” Even among the expensive changsan courtesans, a thousand taels was a generous redemption price. But to make so celebrated and costly a woman the doctor’s concubine would be to give him a match altogether too grand for him.

“That would not do. Capable as you are, you know too little of affairs outside the home—” “I will not accept that!” Her impatience flared. “Even if I knew nothing, are you saying Little Uncle is wrong too?” “Of course he is. He has just had a serious illness; naturally his mind is not yet working at its best. Besides, even he knows less about the pleasure quarters than I do. A popular courtesan of that sort can be described in a word: she cannot endure a quiet life. Never mind the fine clothes and good food. She has become accustomed to music, company, and revelry every night. How could she be content with Dr Zhang?”

“According to you, then, not one of them can leave the profession and marry?” “Even among ten houses, fragrant herbs may be found. Of course there are women who emerge unstained from the mud. But you encounter them by chance; you cannot summon them to order. Where could we find one at a moment’s notice, even with a lantern? And if we did, we should still have to see whether the two were suited. What use is affection on only one side? For money’s sake, a woman might reluctantly go home with a man, keep his house, and bear him sons. But she would never smile; or, if she did, the smile would be forced. In those circumstances, I would respectfully decline even a fairy descended from Heaven.” There was sense in what he said, though he overstated it. Yet if they did not follow the plan, what were they to do? “Must we simply abandon the whole thing?” she asked, staring at him. “In the end you would have to abandon it anyway, after spending the money and being cursed for it. What a waste that would be!”

She objected strongly. “How strange! Such things happen all the time. Did you not tell me yourself that last month some Mr Zhu who collected likin spent three thousand taels on a ‘living treasure’ and presented her to his superior?” “Presenting a living treasure to curry favor with a superior is another matter.” Gu had further arguments. Finding beauties to offer to men above one had been a practice of official life since ancient times. Its purpose was to please for the moment; consequences need not be considered. Moreover, if a famous courtesan became the concubine of a high official, even a strict household and restrictions on her movements were compensated by rich food, fine clothes, and splendid display. Many women of the pleasure quarters loved vanity. Surrounded by jewels and attendants, showing off to former companions and hearing their admiring exclamations—that moment could be deeply satisfying.

“What could Dr Zhang give the Fourth Miss of Yanchun House?” Gu asked. “Even if he is comfortably off, a business household follows business habits. It values practical things. He is unlikely to keep having clothes and jewelry made for her. Nor will the daily table resemble a great official’s, with chicken, fish, duck, and meat every day. An inland town is not Shanghai. Think how a woman accustomed to all this splendor would feel. She would be finding something to be angry about every few days. That is what it means to be restless at home. How would Dr Zhang enjoy his good fortune then?” The Seventh Lady remembered the saying that love may do precisely the harm it wishes to prevent. She too began to think the plan unsuitable. But why should it bring curses upon them? Before she could ask, Gu supplied the answer. “Doing a favor is an art in itself. In such a case, anyone with sense would accuse Little Uncle of repaying kindness with injury. And that is the least of it. The doctor’s family would curse him to death. Would they not?”

Putting herself in their place, she realized that she would do the same. She would curse not only the person who paid, but the person who proposed the scheme. The thought troubled her deeply. Yet what was to become of A’qiao’s predicament? “There must be some conclusion to this. Naturally, it requires willingness on both sides. If one refuses, the other has nothing to say. But the way it began makes it look as though we deliberately used a beauty to lure him into a trap. If he resents it, what he says afterward will be unpleasant. Quite apart from A’qiao, Little Uncle will certainly be unhappy too.” Gu considered for a while, then answered calmly, “There is no other way. We must give him more money as compensation.” “Then that is what we must do,” she said. “We can discuss it when the time comes. There is no need to rack our brains over it now.”

CHAPTER FIVE

People in the foreign settlement, particularly those who spent their time in pleasure and revelry, were accustomed to late nights and late mornings. With business to attend to, Gu Yingchun rose comparatively early, but even he did not get out of bed until nine. This morning, at eight, a maid knocked at the bedroom door. Someone had come from the firm with a message. “What message?” Gu asked through the window. “A Third Master Liu has arrived from Hangzhou. He is at the firm.” “Which Third Master Liu?” Still sleepy, Gu could not think who it was. The Seventh Lady, in the inner room, remembered at once. Lifting the bed curtains, she said, “Is it not Liu Bucai—Third Master Liu?” “Him? Surely not. He is one of our own people. He would come straight here. Why go to the firm?” “Madam is right,” the clerk answered from outside the window. “We asked him to come to the house, but he said his clothes were too ragged. He was embarrassed to call.” So it really was Liu Bucai. The unexpected news left Gu bewildered and momentarily speechless. The Seventh Lady thought faster. They still knew nothing of the Hu family’s circumstances; some misfortune might have occurred. If Hu learned that Liu was here, he would insist on seeing him immediately. Face to face, nothing could be concealed from him, and that might be disastrous. She therefore answered for her husband, telling the clerk to return to the firm and say that Gu would be there at once. She also warned the servants not to let Hu know that Liu had arrived in Shanghai.

“To think that he has come,” Gu said. “Will you go with me?” “Of course!” Husband and wife hurried to the firm together in a carriage. When they met Liu, all three were silent for a moment. Gu and his wife studied him from head to foot. His clothes were ragged, but his spirits and complexion were quite good. He did not look like a man near starvation. “Third Uncle Liu!” The Seventh Lady was the first to speak. “Are you well?” “Well enough, well enough!” Liu seemed suddenly to awaken. Blinking, he said, “Alive for a second time, and together with you again—of course I am well.” Husband and wife both breathed more easily. “And the Hu family?” she asked. “Are they all well?” “Refugee life has been hard. The old and young took turns falling ill, but now they have all recovered.” “Ah!” She let out a long breath and pressed her palms together in thanksgiving. “Thank Heaven!” Then she added, “But now I do not understand. Countless people have starved to death in Hangzhou—” She swallowed the rest. Her unspoken question was plain: if so many had starved, how had every member of the Hu household survived? Liu understood, but no brief answer could explain it. “It was truly the Bodhisattva’s protection. I could not tell it all in three days and nights.”

He changed the subject abruptly. “I hear Xueyan brought grain to Hangzhou but could not get it into the city and returned to Shanghai. Where is he?” “He has been very ill and is not fully recovered, though he is out of danger now.” The Seventh Lady spoke apologetically. “Forgive us, Third Uncle Liu, but you must not see him yet. Let us find out exactly what happened first. Did Governor Wang really die for his duty?” “A good death! A good death!” Liu, ordinarily so easy-going that nothing could make him take matters seriously, spoke with loud conviction. “A death that counted. To be honest, his reputation as an official was not particularly good. Now there can be nothing but good said of him. Even the rebels admire him.” According to Liu, on the day Hangzhou fell, Li Xiucheng, the Loyal King, rode alone straight to the governor’s yamen. Expecting Wang to take his own life, he had hoped to prevent it. But he was too late. Dressed in his court robes, Wang had already hanged himself from an osmanthus tree to the right of the main hall. Respecting his loyalty, Li had the body taken down and laid to the left of the pavilion by the eastern outer gate. He found a fine coffin for the burial preparations. Naturally, Wang’s household, old and young alike, was placed under protection. “The rebels have some humanity after all,” the Seventh Lady said. “Was there not talk of sending the coffin to Shanghai?” “I heard nothing of that.”

“What of the Manchu quarter?” Gu asked. “General Ruichang must have died for his duty too?” “The Manchu quarter held out for another three days—” During those days, Li suspended his attacks and sent men to offer terms of surrender. The terms were generous. The bannermen would be allowed to leave freely, taking their portable valuables, and would receive travelling money as well. Ruichang was also shown an edict from the Heavenly King granting the Hangzhou bannermen a special pardon. The intention was to allay their fears, but it had the opposite effect. Perhaps the terms were so generous that they were difficult to believe. Besides, defeated officers returning to the banner establishment would certainly be punished. Death would be hard to escape; they would lose the compensation their families might otherwise receive and might even be struck from the banner rolls, leaving their descendants disgraced and without a livelihood. Ruichang and his officers therefore agreed never to surrender. Once the three days were over, Li ordered an assault. Every member of the garrison took up arms and resisted fiercely. The Manchu quarter had a circuit of nine li, five gates, and large hongyi cannon mounted on its walls. More than three thousand rebels were killed by their fire. It was not until the afternoon of the first day of the twelfth month that the quarter fell. Ruichang drowned himself in a lotus pond. The deputy banner commanders Jiechun and Guanfu also took their own lives. Countless men, women, and children died in fires they themselves had set or by throwing themselves into West Lake.

At this point in his account, Liu himself grew frightened and pale. Gu quickly ordered hot tea so that he could recover his breath, then asked about his own experiences. “I happened to be in Hangzhou when the situation became critical. Xueyan discussed it with me. Huzhou was under siege too, so I could not return there for the time being. He asked me to escort his family to the three Tianzhu temples for refuge. We parted then, and I never saw him again. Later I decided that the temples were unsafe as well. We kept retreating farther into the countryside. Truly, the Bodhisattva protected us. We reached Liuxia.” Liuxia was a place west of Hangzhou. Its name meant “keep it.” According to tradition, when Emperor Gaozong of the Song moved his capital to Hangzhou and surveyed possible sites for a palace, this place pleased him, and he ordered it kept for consideration. Hence the name. With its hills, winding paths, streams, and many secluded places, it was well suited to sheltering refugees. “But there were so many refugees that a market sprang up. With all those people, there was no seclusion left. Seeing the danger, I told Xueyan’s wife that we must go farther and deeper into the hills: the more remote and impoverished the place, the better. She had good judgement and agreed. I found a spot deep in the mountains where people scarcely ever went. Best of all, there was a ravine with running water. Once we had water, there was nothing to fear. I hired men to build a thatched shelter, only three chi high, with boards laid underneath. Then I had seven or eight dan of rice, a jar of salted vegetables, and a dozen or so hams carried up. You may find it hard to believe: while untold numbers were starving in Hangzhou, we never went a day without solid rice.” “No wonder you do not look as though you have gone hungry,” said the Seventh Lady. “Did the rebels never find you?”

“They nearly did. One day I went gambling—” “Wait a moment,” she interrupted. “Were there places to gamble even among the refugees?” “Not only gambling. There were singing entertainers too. The market was thriving.” It was the refugees who had created that market. At first someone had put up a thatched hut to sell everyday necessities. It had no business name and was simply called “the little shop.” Then it became a tea shop, where people gathered to hear the news. Refugee life was full of anxiety and boredom; people needed distraction, and the tea shop became a gambling house. At first Liu had walked seven or eight li to this new market each day merely to avoid losing contact with the world and to learn what was happening. Later he went chiefly to satisfy his appetite for gambling. Pai gow, the treasure game, dice—he played them all. If he could hold the bank, he did; otherwise he played against it. He became one of the gambling house’s mainstays.

One afternoon he was banking at the small version of pai gow and enjoying extraordinary luck. Often he turned up a zero-point hand, only to find that the heavily backed opposing hand was also zero, so that he still came out ahead. At the height of the game, someone suddenly shouted, “The rebels are coming!” Liu was reluctant to believe it, having once been deceived by the same cry. On that occasion, the players had fled in panic and nothing had happened. When they returned, however, the gaming table had been stripped bare. They learned afterwards that someone had raised the false alarm to seize the money. Suspecting another attempt to profit from panic, Liu stayed while the others ran, calmly collecting his stakes. “Third Master Liu!” the proprietor warned him. “The rebels really are here.” Only then did Liu take fright. He hurriedly thrust several dozen taels into his waistband, slung five or six strings of copper cash over his shoulder, and bolted through the door. But it was already too late: two rebels pursued him relentlessly. Urgent though the danger was, he kept his head. His clothes were better than other people’s, and he had copper cash over his shoulder; they would never let him go. If he simply kept running, would he not lead the enemy to the family’s hiding place? Once he saw that, he knew what to do. He pulled a string of cash towards him, undid its slipknot, and sent a thousand coins clattering across the ground. After a few more strides he did the same again. When all five or six strings had been scattered, the weight was gone and he could run more lightly. Even so, he dared not take the direct route for fear of revealing their shelter. He made a long circuit and did not get home until evening.

“After that, Xueyan’s mother and wife forbade me to go gambling again. In any case, the market broke up. That first party was only a small detachment of rebels which had stumbled upon the place. There were too few of them to do much. The next day, or perhaps the day after, a large force came. They raped, looted, seized people, and finally set the place on fire. Who knows how many refugees suffered?” Liu’s expression was complicated. Fear and sorrow still lingered, yet he also looked relieved, even pleased with himself. “Luckily I saw what was coming. That was one wager I judged correctly.” Even discussing matters of life and death, he could not shed a gambler’s language. The Seventh Lady felt both admiration and amusement, but concern predominated. “You never met the rebels again after that?” “No. Several times we heard them, though. Only someone who has lived through it knows how dreadful that constant fear can be.”

Even now, the days of fear were not entirely over. Once the rebels entered Hangzhou, Li’s discipline kept the usual burning, killing, rape, and robbery from becoming too severe. Yet the people of Hangzhou were unwilling to submit. Men hanged themselves, women threw themselves into wells, and whole families took their own lives in countless numbers. This was not entirely an expression of loyalty to the dynasty. Many had lost all desire to live, for different reasons. Some feared humiliation and suffering. Some had seen horrors beyond anything they could emotionally endure and sought release. Others had neither clothes nor food and could find no means of prolonging their lives; believing death inevitable, they chose it sooner rather than later. Still others, having survived terrible calamities only to find everything lost in the end, killed themselves in bitter despair. Among a hundred thousand households, perhaps not one had escaped the catastrophe as the Hu family had. Yet now calamity threatened them again. They could no longer remain in their mountain hut, chiefly because their food was exhausted and the whole family had to come down. The city was uninhabitable: the stench of bodies made it impossible to approach. Had it not been the depths of winter, epidemic disease would already have spread. Fortunately, Hu’s mother had always been devout. Since her son’s sudden rise in the world, she had been more convinced than ever of the Bodhisattva’s protection. Monks and nuns who came soliciting donations for temples, large or small, never left her empty-handed. The three Tianzhu temples were an important centre of worship, and she knew them all. Finding a place to stay was therefore easy enough. The difficulty was still food. All Hangzhou depended on twenty thousand shi of rice that Li had brought from Jiaxing. Without the troops’ needs, it might have lasted some time. But the army received its rations first, and only afterwards was grain sold to civilians at controlled prices. The benefit to ordinary people was limited.

“Now the whole household, old and young, has only one meal of gruel a day. I can manage, but something must be done for the old people and the children.” “We shall find a way,” Gu said. “But there is something I do not understand, Third Uncle Liu. You have always had a good appetite. How can you keep going on one meal of gruel a day and still say you can manage?” Liu smiled a little sheepishly. “I find ways to get a meal at the rebels’ residences.” The Seventh Lady laughed too. “You really would snatch food from a tiger’s mouth! How do you manage it?” “That I cannot tell you. Let us leave idle talk aside. There is something serious I must discuss with you. A bastard is making trouble for Xueyan. If he is ignored, something will happen.” The “bastard” was Yuan Zhongqing, acting magistrate of Qiantang County. He had originally been a petty militia commander under Yuan Jiasan. After making a windfall from the war, he worked through one of Yuan’s advisers to have his name added to a list of men recommended for honours. He obtained sixth-rank status and the right to wear a blue feather. Later he violated military orders, and Yuan Jiasan intended to execute him. Terrified, he deserted overnight and fled to his native Jiangxi.

The governor of Jiangxi at that time was Zhang Fu, a fellow graduate of He Guiqing’s examination year and a favourite pupil of Muzhang’a. Yuan falsely represented himself as a sixth-rank county assistant magistrate entitled to the blue feather. Through further connections, he secured a position under Zhang. Before long, the rebels attacked Nanchang, the provincial capital. The people of Nanchang threw themselves into its defence, winning Zhang’s warm regard. That goodwill extended to their fellow native, and the “bastard” benefited: he was appointed to assist with military supplies at the manufacturing bureau. It was an extremely lucrative post, and Yuan found opportunities for profit on every side. Soon afterwards, the victory at Ningguo occasioned a special recommendation for honours. Zhang took particular care of Yuan, telling his advisers to give him an excellent assessment and recommend him for promotion to county magistrate. Ordinarily this would have been wonderful news. Anyone else would have been delighted. Yuan, however, looked miserable and could neither sit nor rest in peace. His colleagues naturally wondered why. “Yuan, promotion is just ahead of you! The authorities have taken special care. This is no routine recommendation with a token appraisal; yours runs to sixteen characters. The capital is sure to approve it. You are about to rule a county. Why look as though you have lost both parents?” “Promotion? It will take all the merit my ancestors accumulated to keep me from a criminal charge.” Then he shook his head. “No, the charge is certain. Even my ancestors’ merit will not help.” His colleague was astonished. “Why?” At first Yuan dared not explain. But the man was so earnest and helpful, solemnly promising to find a way out, that Yuan finally revealed his secret. “To be frank, my sixth rank and blue feather are perfectly genuine. The county assistant magistracy is a fake. What will happen when this recommendation goes up?”

“What? You are not really an assistant magistrate?” A false appointment could not stand scrutiny. Once Jiangxi’s recommendation reached the Ministry of Personnel, the records would naturally be checked. Yuan was being recommended for county magistrate because he supposedly held an assistant magistracy. The first question would be how he had obtained that appointment. Finding no record, the ministry would send an enquiry. Could Yuan produce an official certificate or a receipt for a purchased appointment? Such impostures were not unknown. Almost all the ministry’s clerks were old hands at extortion, knowing every variety of fraud and forever looking for an opening. Once they had discovered his weakness, they would demand whatever they pleased. Even after extracting enough money, they might still charge him with falsely claiming official status and have him sent into military exile. His colleague proved as good as his word. After consulting someone knowledgeable, he found a solution: Yuan could purchase an assistant magistracy now. Since the war began, the government had greatly expanded the sale of appointments to raise military funds. Each province had received large quantities of blank receipts from the Ministry of Personnel. These were called receipts for the actual amount paid. There were many schemes and discounts for purchasing appointments; the receipt stated the silver actually contributed and could later be exchanged for a formal ministry certificate. Paying the silver and obtaining a receipt was easy enough. The difficulty was the date. If the receipt showed that the appointment had been purchased after the recommendation, the deception would be exposed at once. “There is only one way. We must call in a favour.” “And favours cost money. I know,” Yuan replied. “My post yields a little, but I have spent it on cultivating friends. After food and expenses, there is not much left. Everything I have is here.” He opened a small chest kept by his pillow. It contained quite a few banknotes, but all the little sums together came to only a hundred taels or so. Backdating a receipt was a serious risk and would cost at least three hundred. His friend knew that Yuan was putting on a performance. But having taken the matter this far, he could hardly abandon a good deed halfway through merely because Yuan was unreliable. He added fifty taels of his own and, after much pleading with the people handling the business, got it arranged.

But Yuan Zhongqing’s reputation for shabby conduct had spread. He could no longer get on in Jiangxi. In any case, he had to leave: having been recommended for promotion to county magistrate, he could not be appointed to a vacancy in the same province. Through contacts at the ministry, he was assigned to Zhejiang to await a post. He had originally hoped for Guangdong, but the intermediary proved less reliable than expected and had the assignment changed to Zhejiang. With no alternative, Yuan reported there. This was shortly after the provincial capital had fallen for the first time and been recovered. Wang Youling was putting all his efforts into preparing its defences: long trenches were dug, batteries added, and rows of barracks built along the walls. Strong hoisting frames with winding drums worked all day, bringing up firearms and ammunition. Everywhere were bright banners and gleaming weapons. The city certainly looked capable of holding out. Yuan’s spirits revived. Working through Provincial Treasurer Linzhi, he actually secured appointment as acting magistrate of Qiantang. Hangzhou comprised two counties, Qiantang and Renhe; Qiantang was the senior one, and its magistracy carried particular weight. Yuan was calculating and possessed the talents needed behind the scenes. The magistrate of the senior county, however, needed public accomplishments: a fine appearance, engaging conversation, splendid clothes, smooth manners, and, above all, a generous hand and a gift for entertaining. Yuan was not the natural choice. But with military affairs so urgent, few high officials passed through, and there was little ceremonial receiving and seeing off to do. He could put his own abilities to use. His strength lay in extracting money. For that, one needed a pretext; and when defence took precedence over everything, how could pretexts be lacking? Levies, compulsory donations, requisitioned labour and materials—all could be charged to military needs in accounts no one could untangle. So long as he satisfied his superiors and did not drive the people into revolt, no one would ask how much he pocketed in between.

When Hangzhou was besieged in the ninth month, households ran out of food and everyone grew thin. Yuan alone seemed full of vigour. Many suspected that he had hidden grain and was secretly feeding himself, but without evidence nothing could be proved. Naturally, such a man would not sacrifice his life when the city fell. Some even said he had opened the gates to the rebels. That too was unsubstantiated. What was beyond doubt was that, on the second day after Li Xiucheng entered the city, Yuan accepted office under the rebels. His new title was Military Supervisor of Qiantang, but the work was his old occupation: procuring military supplies. What the rebels most urgently needed was boats. On the one hand, their plundered jewels, valuables, antiques, paintings, and calligraphy had to be carried to the Heavenly Capital as offerings to the Heavenly King. On the other, grain had to be brought quickly into Hangzhou from the outlying ports. Yuan discarded his official hat and feather, took off his rank-badged coat, and dressed like a rebel in a red silk padded jacket with yellow silk wrapped around his head. Every day he went down to the river, brandishing Li’s command token, to requisition boats. Countless refugees were outside the city. Any attractive woman who encountered the insatiably lustful Yuan was in danger of assault.

“That bastard!” Liu said angrily. “He actually went to the Hu residence himself and told the people left to look after it: ‘Hu took several tens of thousands of taels in public money to Shanghai to buy rice. Why has he not returned? Send him word to bring the amount of rice he owes to Hangzhou at once. Otherwise he will suffer for it!’ Tell me, is that not a deliberate attempt to make trouble?” It certainly was trouble. With Yuan’s degraded character and vicious methods, unless precautions were taken promptly he might discover where the family was hiding and ill-treat the old people, women, and children. The danger was alarming. “What worries me most is that the bastard can ruin things more easily than he can achieve them. If he arrested the family and could be persuaded through connections to release them all, that would be one thing. But I fear he may tell the rebels about them and have them arrested, then have no authority to get them freed. Once that happened, finding a way out might be harder than climbing to Heaven.” Liu’s words, reinforced by an unusually grave expression, filled the Seventh Lady with anxiety. Her usual cheerful decisiveness deserted her. Gu was worried too, naturally. But he had always been thoughtful and calm, partly through Hu’s influence. He believed that difficulties became less formidable once one ceased to fear them. This was not the only problem before them. In deciding what was urgent, they must remember that Hangzhou was far away. If disaster had already occurred, anxiety would achieve nothing. If there was in fact no danger, frantic attempts to find a remedy might only hasten calamity. Liu might have accepted this reasoning. The Seventh Lady certainly would not. To reassure his wife, Gu therefore took the whole burden on himself.

“It will be all right,” he said, tapping his chest. “I have ways and contacts. I shall attend to it today. First we must decide something else.” That was how to tell Hu the news from Hangzhou. His family at the rebels’ mercy and his closest friend dead—these were two griefs he could scarcely bear. Yet the news that his mother was alive and the whole family safe might counterbalance them. Gu therefore favoured letting Liu speak to him in person. The Seventh Lady agreed, and Liu naturally complied. First, however, he wanted a bath, something he had longed for day after day. “That is easily arranged.” She turned to Gu. “Take Third Uncle Liu to the bathhouse. I shall go home and have a room prepared.” “No need, Sister Seven. I would rather stay at an inn. It gives me more freedom.” “He likes to come and go as he pleases. Let him do so,” Gu agreed. He had another reason: if there were matters that could not be discussed in front of Hu, an inn would be a convenient place to meet Liu privately.

On the newly laid-out Stone Road they bought a complete set of clothes, shoes, and headgear, from the innermost layer to the outermost. The ragged, filthy clothes Liu took off should by rights have gone straight into the rubbish. But he insisted on keeping them. “Before this, I never truly understood how hard it is to earn the necessities of life. I have been down on my luck. There were days when, after eating lunch, I had no idea where supper would come from. But I never worried, and it never occurred to me to save when I had money. This calamity has changed me. I shall keep these clothes as a family treasure. I am not joking. I want my descendants to know that their ancestor suffered like this.” Gu was surprised. “Third Uncle Liu, I never expected you to think in that way.” “Something else shook me too. A man must have the will to prove himself. The sights on the road from Tianzhu into the city were heartbreaking; there is no need to describe them. But nothing is as frightening as the human heart. Xueyan did a great deal for the place. Yet now not one person in ten has a good word for him. I cannot accept it. If he really falls, or can never return to Hangzhou, he will be wronged for the rest of his life and never clear his name. One day, when the city is recovered and he returns as influential as before, I should like to hear what those people have to say.” This complaint struck Gu as unusual. In all the time he had known Liu, he had seldom heard him grumble. On the rare occasions when he did, it was with detached, sardonic humour. This was the first time Gu had heard such earnest indignation. Thinking over his words, he began to sense something behind them. “Third Uncle Liu, you seem to be keeping something back.” Liu looked up sharply and stared at him. After a long pause, he nodded deeply. “You have guessed correctly, Brother Yingchun. There are things I ought to discuss with you. Xueyan’s position is very unfavourable—” As Liu continued, Gu learned that Hu had become the target of attacks from every direction. Some said he had used the purchase of rice as a pretext to abscond with a substantial sum from the provincial treasury. Others alleged that Wang’s savings from office had all been invested through Hu, who, now that Wang was dead and could not testify, had appropriated them. There were further accusations that he had used public business for private profit and that his reputation for benevolence was false. They portrayed him as a thoroughly unscrupulous villain.

“These are people who were always jealous of him, or who suffered at Governor Wang’s hands and are taking their resentment out on him. When mad dogs snap, keep out of their way. He could have ignored them. But now the rebels have taken an interest in him too, and that makes trouble.” Why should the rebels want Hu? Gu was greatly puzzled, though the explanation made it understandable enough. “Xueyan always liked taking the lead in good works. Let us be honest: part of it was enthusiasm and a liking for lively affairs; part was a desire for reputation. Li Xiucheng heard about him and wants him to come forward and help restore order after the fighting. That makes the jealousy worse. There is already a group exploiting the situation. If Xueyan appears, their opportunities will be gone. First they sent a bastard like Yuan to threaten him. That was bad enough. Their next move is vicious. They are said to be planning to stir up officials in the capital to accuse him of taking Zhejiang’s public rice-purchasing funds and thereby obstructing supplies for the army and the people. They want an imperial order for his investigation.” Gu changed colour. “How could they say such a thing? They mean to destroy his family and his life!” He shook his head. “But I do not understand. If an imperial investigation makes it impossible for Little Uncle to remain in Shanghai, he will have no choice but to turn to the rebels. How would that benefit them?” “There is more. They have also spread word that, if he returns to Hangzhou, they will beat gongs, summon a crowd, and settle accounts with him.” “What accounts?” “Who knows? The poison lies in summoning a crowd. Once they create a scene like that, he will be a rat crossing the street, with everyone shouting to strike him.” Gu tapped his temple and frowned. “You have confused me. They forbid him to return, yet they want to make Shanghai uninhabitable for him and force him to join the rebels. What exactly do they want? Surely they cannot expect to drive him to hang himself. That would not be so easy.”

“Of course not. If he let them drive him into a corner, would he still be Hu Xueyan? They know it cannot be done. They want to force one promise out of him: ‘Leave me alone, and I shall never interfere with your affairs.’ Do you think he will say it?” “Whether he wishes to or not, he must. His whole family is at stake.” “True. But there is another difficulty.” That difficulty was a knot that could not be untied. Chen Bingwen, an able subordinate of Li’s who in practice controlled civil affairs throughout Zhejiang, found the work of restoring order so troublesome that he was determined to enlist Hu. He was said to have found a senior clerk at Fukang Bank and told him to carry the message. Liu reckoned that the man would arrive in Shanghai within two or three days. “Then we cannot keep it from Little Uncle,” Gu said, feeling how difficult the situation was. “You have seen it for yourself and must have thought about it these past few days. Is there any way out?” “Of course I have thought about it. There is only one way to protect the whole family. But—” Liu shook his head. “You will not approve.” “Tell me anyway.” “It is plain enough. There is only one word for it: go. Frankly, if Xueyan really returned to Hangzhou, what could those people do to him?”

Gu strongly disagreed. But Liu had already anticipated his disapproval, which made it difficult to reproach him. “Third Uncle Liu,” he said slowly, “we must meet the immediate danger, but we must also think about the years ahead. I believe Little Uncle must decide this himself.” Bringing news that the whole family was safe also meant bringing confirmation of Wang’s death. Liu did not mention Wang; the silence said everything. Tears ran down Hu’s face, but the grief was bearable. This end had long been expected. More than a month earlier, his bow from the boat on the Qiantang River had been a final farewell. The sharpest moment of sorrow was already past. What of Wang’s surviving family? He wanted to ask, but feared hearing something so tragic that he hesitated to speak.
