The Seventh Lady, meanwhile, deliberately steered the conversation towards reassurance. Beginning with Hu’s mother, she asked about every member of the family in turn, giving him still less opportunity to ask. They talked until supper. Dr Zhang returned just then, and after introductions they all sat down to eat and drink. He and Liu were in the same line of business, both having connections with medicine shops, which gave them an immediate sense of fellowship. They talked readily. After the meal, Gu specially asked the doctor to examine Hu again. Perhaps because of the good news, his spirits and vitality were stronger; his condition had improved even since the morning’s examination.

“There is another very troubling matter we must discuss with him,” Gu asked the doctor quietly. “Would that be bad for his condition?” “Nothing troubling is good for a patient. But he is an unusual man. If he can bear the shock, it need not harm him.” Gu therefore decided to conceal it no longer—from Hu, at least. It was his wife who must not hear. Once she had gone to the bedroom to see the child, he and Liu explained the threats from Hangzhou, gently but in full detail. Hu remained composed, though his face was grave. When they had finished, he sighed. “Disorder corrupts men’s hearts. It is hardly surprising. In times like these, a man who insists on reason and loyalty is liable to suffer for it. But interests and consequences can still be discussed.” His tone suggested that he already saw a solution. Gu asked at once, “Little Uncle, there is no time to lose. Whatever you decide, it must be done quickly.” “No hurry. Take it slowly and keep your temper!” That Hu could still make so light a remark at such a moment irritated Gu a little. “You really do not seem concerned,” he said with mild reproach. “Have you some miraculous strategy?” “Nothing miraculous. It is quite plain. Since they have sent a man from my bank with a message, naturally they must wait for a reply before deciding what to do next. The man has not even arrived. Why the panic?” Gu could not help admiring him. The reasoning was simple; his own agitation had prevented him from seeing it. Such composure was easy to praise but difficult to maintain when matters came to a crisis. It was precisely where Hu excelled.

“Very well!” Gu smiled. “Now that you have explained it, I feel easier. We can discuss it calmly.” His sudden tension and release revealed how deeply he shared his friend’s distress. This greatly comforted Hu and gave him fresh confidence. He spoke more calmly still. “As for Yuan Zhongqing, I can see right through him. He is a clay Bodhisattva crossing a river—hard pressed to save himself. He will not dare stir up too much trouble. The others must think carefully too. If they really make difficulties for my family, they know I am not a man to be trifled with. They will not push me too far. It would do them no good. First, I could return to Hangzhou. The rebels want me and would listen to me. Those people must consider whether they could prevail against me. Second, if I stay here, they have relatives and close connections in Shanghai. They must fear retaliation. Third—but there is no need to speak of that. It concerns the future.” Gu nevertheless pressed him. “What about the future?” “We are bound to meet again someday. They should leave themselves room to do so. One must not carry things to extremes. Even now, everyone is saying how bad I am. But if they harm my family, they will put themselves in the wrong. They will turn a seemingly justified position into an unjustifiable one. Anyone with a little sense will avoid that.” Gu could not agree with the last argument. Leaving room for future encounters seemed excessively scrupulous. The first two reasons, however, were quite enough. “Tell us your plan, Little Uncle.”

“My plan is to make a bargain. If they do not want me back in Hangzhou, very well. I shall not compete with them. I shall even do them a favour: when the government troops recover the city, I shall find a way to protect them. In return, they must help me now and get my family out of Hangzhou.” Could such a bargain really be made? Gu was doubtful. He said nothing, merely looking at Liu to hear what he thought.

Liu, however, was greatly interested. “That might work,” he said. “As I see it, they want to eat the mutton without enduring its smell. They are afraid that, once the government troops recover the city, they will be called to account. If you really can protect them, they will be willing to do as we ask. But mere words will not suffice.” “For the moment, words are all we have. If the offer appeals to them and is something we can fulfil, they will believe it.” Hu paused. “Third Uncle, I must trouble you to make another journey. They would find it harder to believe anyone else.” “Of course I shall go. What shall I say to them?” “A little boasting will be necessary.” Hu broke off. “Let me think it over properly.” He thought for a long time—or perhaps put the matter aside for the moment. At any rate, he said no more about it. Instead he asked about Hangzhou, showing concern for every trifling detail. He had a wide acquaintance, but of the people he asked after, most had died in the calamity or vanished without trace. Fewer than one in ten were known to have survived. Even Gu, who had no personal connection with them, listened in deep distress.

After ten o’clock, the strain of the journey by land and water, followed by so much uninterrupted talking, had exhausted even Liu’s vigorous constitution. Gu urged him to forget about returning to the inn and get a good night’s rest first. Liu agreed. A maid of the household attended to him, and he went to bed.

Hu was still full of energy. “Gu,” he said, beckoning his friend to sit by the bed, “I do not use underhand methods against people. This time I must make an exception. My whole family is in the hands of those bastards; I cannot afford to leave myself unprotected. I intend to lay a fuse. If all goes well, well and good. If it does not, I shall light it and blast the devils out of their comfort. Now that I have explained, you know what is in my mind. I need you to take some trouble over an official dispatch for me.” He might consider himself to have explained, but Gu was utterly bewildered. “Little Uncle,” he said, frowning, “I still have no idea what you mean. What fuse? What official dispatch?”

“The dispatch is the fuse, and the fuse is the dispatch,” Hu replied. “It is to be addressed to Governor-General Qing at the office of the governor-general of Fujian and Zhejiang. I shall write in my capacity as a circuit intendant awaiting appointment in Zhejiang and a member of the militia bureau, commissioned by Governor Wang to arrange military supplies, food for the people, and local relief. Begin by explaining that, before his death, Mr Xuexuan feared that reinforcements might not arrive and Hangzhou might prove impossible to hold. He expressly charged me with this duty: he himself was determined to die with the city and repay the court with his life, but the people of Hangzhou must not be abandoned. I held no local administrative post and bore no responsibility for defending the territory. Therefore, should Hangzhou fall, I must remember my native place and find ways of protecting its people. That is the first section.” Gu listened closely and began to understand how Hu meant to proceed. “The second section will presumably describe your attempt to bring rice to Hangzhou and your inability to enter the city?” “Exactly. But there is no need to mention the diversion to Ningbo.” Hu paused briefly. “Now comes the third and most important section. It must say this: since I am in Shanghai and cannot return to Hangzhou, I have sent someone to contact such-and-such persons, asking them to protect the inhabitants and secretly prepare to assist the government troops when a favourable opportunity arises upon their arrival. These men are all upright local gentlemen, loyal at heart. At present they are trapped in the city and cannot act freely. When Hangzhou is recovered, whatever offices they may have held under the rebels must not be counted against them. On the contrary, they should be generously rewarded.” “Ah!” Gu nodded deeply. “I understand. You are leaving them a way out for the future.”

“Precisely. We ought to wait for Governor-General Qing’s endorsement, but he is in Fuzhou and that cannot be obtained quickly. So we must improvise. While submitting the report to him, I shall also ask the governor of Jiangsu to forward an official communication to his office and give me a reply reciting my original report in full. I can then show it to those men.” Gu reflected. “But what did you mean when you said the dispatch was the fuse?” “Do you still not see? Think about the passage concerning assistance to the government troops.” A hint was enough for a man as shrewd as Gu. If those people refused to cooperate, or actually harmed Hu’s family, Hu could retaliate with this very dispatch. He could inform the rebels that the men were in league with the Qing forces, producing the reply from the governor of Jiangsu as conclusive evidence. The consequences were easy to imagine. It was a ruthless move. But it was intended for retaliation, and might serve as protection in itself. If the men understood that the dispatch was a fuse which, once lit, could blow them to pieces, they would not dare act rashly. “Little Uncle,” Gu exclaimed in admiration, “you have found a brilliant move in a position that looked completely lost! How did you think of it?”

“I did not invent it. I have merely adapted a method used before. In fact, I owe Mr Xuexuan my thanks. He once told me a story from his own province. In the Kangxi reign there was a Minister Li, a very famous man in Fujian, apparently. A fellow graduate of his, Hanlin Academician Chen, became his sworn enemy.” This was the story Wang had told him. Minister Li came from Anxi in Fujian; Academician Chen came from Fuzhou. When their period of training at the Hanlin Academy ended, the two took leave and travelled home together. Not long afterwards came the Revolt of the Three Feudatories. Geng Jingzhong responded to Wu Sangui’s call and rebelled in Fuzhou, establishing an administration and appointing officials. Chen was compelled to accept an office under him. Li saw the opportunity with keen interest and also wanted to seek a post in Fuzhou. Chen, however, doubted that Geng’s venture would succeed and advised him against it. The two then closeted themselves together and devised a plan. Li would write a secret memorial setting out a strategy and urging the court to send a large army into Fujian at once. The memorial was sealed in a pellet of wax. A messenger from Li’s household carried it through Jiangxi to the capital, where a fellow provincial was asked to submit it to the emperor.

“They meant to come out well whichever side won,” Hu continued. “Li and Chen agreed that, if the imperial troops entered Fujian and Geng fell, Li would of course have performed a great service. He could then clear Chen’s name, explaining that Chen had joined the rebels solely to discover their secrets and assist the government forces—” “Ah, excellent! And if Geng succeeded, no one would know of Li’s secret memorial. Chen could still recommend him for a fine position under the new regime. Was that the plan?” “Exactly.” “What happened afterwards, then?” Gu asked with interest. “How did they become mortal enemies?” “Because Li was a scoundrel who betrayed his friend. Geng fell, the court recovered Fujian, and the rebels were to be punished. Li, secure in his own success, did nothing to clear Chen, who was sent into military exile beyond the pass.” Hu went on, “I am now borrowing their method. I only hope those people have the sense to cooperate. The means of clearing their names that I am providing will certainly be useful to them one day.” “I understand your intentions completely, Little Uncle. I shall prepare the dispatch for you tonight.”

“There is no hurry. Tomorrow will be soon enough,” Hu said. “There is something else I must discuss with you first.” This concerned provision for Wang after his death: his reputation and his property. His savings from office were not exceptionally large, but neither could it be said that he had taken nothing. As the saying went, even an honest prefect might save a hundred thousand taels in three years. Officials had many sources of income, including surpluses from grain taxation and the customary allowance for wastage when small pieces of silver, collected in commutation of tribute grain, were melted down into official ingots weighing fifty taels each. In the Yongzheng reign, such receipts had been brought into the open and formally designated as allowances for the maintenance of integrity. There were also gifts at the New Year, the Dragon Boat Festival, and the Mid-Autumn Festival, and on the birthdays of the official and his wife. Subordinates invariably sent them, in amounts largely fixed by custom. These were all forms of income permitted by the court.

Wang’s savings had naturally been entrusted to Hu for investment. What Hu now needed to discuss was how to account for the money that had passed through his hands. “They say I have Mr Xuexuan’s money. Of course I have. With the friendship there was between us, I would never dream of keeping it dishonestly. But”—he grew agitated—“in a situation like this, how can I call in loans and recover invested capital at a moment’s notice? I truly do not know how to settle matters.” It was indeed a serious difficulty. Gu’s thoughts went further. Wang had died for his duty and left a sizeable family. They needed money for their immediate living expenses and, later, to marry off the children. Even if they received the money, they could not simply live on their capital until it was exhausted. “You must think beyond settling the account now,” he said. “You ought to make lasting provision for the Wang family.” “There is not much to plan there. So long as the Hu family has rice to eat, I shall never let the Wangs live on gruel. It is the immediate problem that worries me. Mr Xuexuan and I were so close that he was as much myself as I was. His spirit in Heaven will understand my predicament. But Mrs Wang may not know what is in my heart; her relatives and friends will understand even less. All they know is that I hold this money, not that I cannot recover it at once. Since such rumours are already abroad, unless I can produce the shining silver itself, people will say I am taking advantage of a widow and orphans. Think what that would do to my reputation!”

Gu thought this sound reasoning. He too understood human nature thoroughly, and carried the argument a step further. If Hu handed over the entire sum belonging to Wang, the family would naturally trust him and ask him to continue investing it, leaving it at his disposal. Otherwise they might become uneasy and insist on withdrawing it. Given that, he might as well make a handsome job of it. The trouble was that Hangzhou had fallen, while the capital invested in Shanghai could not be withdrawn immediately. A handsome settlement was therefore impossible without help from their friends. “Little Uncle, how much of Mr Xuexuan’s money do you have?” “Mrs Wang has the accounts. About a hundred thousand taels. There are another twenty thousand in Yunnan, though I do not know whether she knows about those.” “How strange! How did twenty thousand taels come to be in Yunnan?”

“This is how it happened,” Hu said. “In the winter of the sixth year of Xianfeng, He Genyun left his post as governor of Zhejiang, and Mr Xuexuan had little prospect of making a career there any longer. Arrangements had already been made for him to become grain intendant in Yunnan. I remitted twenty thousand taels there in advance. Afterwards He was promoted to governor-general of Liangjiang, and Mr Xuexuan naturally followed him to Jiangsu. The twenty thousand in Yunnan were never touched; they remained on deposit with a bank in Kunming, earning interest. Mr Xuexuan never forgot Yunnan. He used to tell me that, given the opportunity, he would very much like to serve a term as governor there. Better still if he could become governor-general of Yunnan and Guizhou. Sooner or later, he said, the money would be useful in Yunnan, so we need not withdraw it. Now, of course, he will never need it again.” At this, emotion overcame Hu once more, and tears rose to his eyes.

Gu waited while he wiped his eyes, blew his nose, and recovered some composure, then resumed the discussion. “Money deposited with a bank must have a passbook. Who has it?” “That is the difficulty. There was a passbook, and I gave it to Mr Xuexuan. I suppose he lost it and forgot about it, for he later asked me where it was. It did not seem to matter at the time. We could simply ask them for a replacement. Then other business intervened, and it was left undone. Now that he is dead, it becomes troublesome. I fear they may refuse to acknowledge the deposit.” “You handled the original transaction,” Gu said. “Whether Mr Xuexuan is alive or dead would seem to make no difference. But I do not know much about banking customs. Where is the difficulty?” “For a bank, credit comes first, business connections second, and personal friendship only third. This Yunnan bank’s credit is not particularly good, and there is no friendship to speak of. The only thing we could count on was influence through our connections. While Mr Xuexuan lived, he was a serving governor whose word carried weight in Yunnan. As for me, Fukang’s Shanghai business may be modest, but in Zhejiang we hold first place. Yunnan has transactions involving interprovincial military subsidies and other public funds, and I could help them with those. Our business connections should have been sufficient. But things have changed. They may no longer choose to oblige us.” The analysis was penetrating. Gu took it to heart, reflecting sadly that success in business nowadays seemed to depend either on official influence or on foreign connections. With things as they were, what more could be said? Unless someone very powerful intervened, Wang’s money might well be lost.

“There is only one course,” Gu said. “We must find an intermediary to ask the governor-general of Yunnan and Guizhou, or the governor of Yunnan, to send someone to speak to the bank. Mr Xuexuan died for his country; his family deserves consideration. Perhaps one of those great men will consent to concern himself with the matter.” “That is all we can do,” Hu agreed. “But whatever negotiations we undertake, I must make up the money myself for the present.” “That makes a hundred and twenty thousand altogether.” Gu considered for a moment, then said resolutely, “We are in business together, and our credit is shared. I shall find a way to raise the full amount for you.” Here was the value of friendship. Hu felt both gratitude and unease. Gu’s willingness to bear the burden did not absolve him of responsibility. He still had to ask how it would be managed.

“Gu, words of gratitude are unnecessary between us. But I cannot let you ruin yourself on my account. A hundred and twenty thousand taels is no small sum. I do not yet know how much I can raise myself—perhaps thirty to fifty thousand at most. That still leaves seventy or eighty thousand. Finding that much ready cash will not be easy.” “To tell you the truth, Little Uncle, I cannot produce seventy or eighty thousand at once either. I can only move money around temporarily, in the hope that Mrs Wang will merely look at it and entrust it to you again to invest at interest.” “Yes,” Hu said. “I think that can be arranged. But we must allow for the possibility that it cannot.” “Then we shall simply have to hold on somehow. We cannot take every contingency into account now.” Hu nodded. He reckoned the plan had an eight-in-ten chance of success and thought no further about it. They turned to another matter.

“This concerns Mr Xuexuan’s reputation through the ages,” Hu said. “I have heard plenty of storytellers and know of a few loyal ministers. Few died as cruelly as he did. We must find some way to honour him and leave a lasting memorial. Only then shall we have done our duty by him.” “Why should you have to trouble yourself over that? The court has its established honours for loyalty.” Hu knew this, of course. In a case such as Wang’s, the posthumous honours must be generous. Besides the customary provision for a deceased governor, there would be a posthumous title, an official biography, and sacrificial offerings. A high provincial official who died for his duty could customarily be honoured in the Shrine of Illustrious Loyalty in the capital. His descendants might receive a hereditary rank such as Yunqiwei, to be transmitted without reduction. As for a special shrine in his own province or in a province where he had performed distinguished service, approval was virtually assured if someone submitted the appropriate memorial.

But Hu was not thinking of these customary honours. “I cannot stop thinking how unjustly Mr Xuexuan died,” he said. “When I remember his words about being unable to close his eyes in peace, I doubt I shall ever sleep soundly. I want to obtain redress for him. At the very least, people must know the wrongs he suffered while alive.” In Hu’s view, there were several. First, Wang Lüqian had obstructed him at every turn. Ningbo and Shaoxing could have been held but were lost, cutting off Hangzhou’s food route and placing it in a hopeless position. Second, Li Yuandu held office in Zhejiang and drew his pay from Zhejiang, yet lingered in Hengzhou without advancing. Had he fought hard in western Zhejiang, he could at least have diverted the rebels there, and Hangzhou would not have been so completely surrounded. Third, Zeng Guofan, governor-general of Liangjiang, had received an imperial command to assist Zhejiang but stood by with folded arms, apparently willing to watch others perish. That was surely heartless. Hu spoke with considerable agitation. His friendship with Wang had been profound, and he himself had lived through the events and shared the suffering. Gu’s view was different. He considered consequences and interests, with less involvement of feeling.

“Little Uncle,” Gu asked calmly, “how do you propose to seek redress?” “I have two plans. First, I shall ask someone to write an epitaph setting out all these wrongs. Second, I shall spend some money in the capital and get a censor to impeach them severely.” “Impeach whom?” “Wang Lüqian, Li Yuandu, and Governor-General Zeng of Liangjiang.” “I think that will be difficult,” Gu replied. “Governor-General Zeng is at the height of his favour. You will not bring him down by impeachment. Besides, in fairness, he is far away in Anqing and lacks even the strength to relieve Jiangsu. Where could he find troops to spare for Zhejiang?”

Hu did not agree, but he had no wish to quarrel. “What about Wang Lüqian and Li Yuandu, then? Surely those two deserve punishment?” “Wang Lüqian is bound to suffer. Li Yuandu is another matter. Besides, with war and disorder everywhere and the roads closed, how could the court conduct a thorough investigation? It must wait until peace is restored.” Hu flared up. “Do you mean we are simply to let those two go unpunished?” he roared. He had never spoken to Gu with such harshness. Gu stared in astonishment, unable to answer for a long while. The embarrassed expression on his face was something Hu had never seen before; it reflected his own loss of control back to him. “I am sorry, Gu,” he said, lowering his head. His voice was much gentler, though it could not conceal his indignation. That indignation was not directed at Gu, of course. Gu felt both pity and respect for him, but would not comfort him merely by saying what he wanted to hear. “Little Uncle, I know how close you were to Mr Xuexuan. But we cannot act on feeling alone. We must consider right and wrong, and the likely consequences.” Hu naturally accepted that principle. At the moment, however, he could not see what those considerations meant in this case. If a man had been wronged, was he not even allowed to complain? What, then, became of the saying that injustice must find a voice? Seeing him silent, Gu went no further. In truth, he too was thinking only of consequences, not of right and wrong. While seeking news of Hangzhou for Hu, he had lately had much contact with official circles. He had heard many opinions on Wang’s death and the fall of the city. Most people sympathized with Wang, but some defended Zeng’s failure to help Zhejiang with great vigour. The arguments plainly reflected factional loyalties and hostilities. Wang had fought alone. His closest patron was He Guiqing, who was himself barely able to keep his head above water. In such circumstances, anyone who championed Wang and disputed the rights and wrongs would inevitably offend powerful sensitivities and invite trouble. Would that not be foolish? Gu knew that his own reasoning was commonplace and ignoble. Yet his concern for Hu was so deep that he could not help calculating the dangers. These considerations could not be fully explained at once; indeed, it was better that Hu should understand them without their being spoken. Gu believed that, in time, he would. For the present, the urgent tasks were to raise the hundred and twenty thousand taels and prepare the dispatch to the governor-general of Fujian and Zhejiang.

From the next day, Gu devoted all his energy to finding the money. He did not need to draft the dispatch himself. His acquaintance was wide, and he enlisted Lei Zihan, a county magistrate awaiting appointment who served as a drafting officer in the Jiangsu governor’s office. Lei took the entire matter in hand. Within two days, they had even obtained Governor Xue Huan’s written reply to Hu. Only then did Hu explain the arrangements to Liu. “Third Uncle,” he concluded, “that is how we shall proceed. But I do not insist on carrying the plan through. Why? Because it will be very difficult.” Liu hated being looked down upon or dismissed as an idle scion incapable of serious work. Hu’s words therefore provoked him. “Xueyan,” he demanded sternly, “who could do it easily, then?” Hu realized that his careless wording had touched a sore point and been misunderstood. “Third Uncle, it would be difficult for anyone. If you cannot succeed, no one can.” This unintentional compliment transformed Liu’s anger into pleasure. “Well, tell me what is to be done,” he said in a milder voice.

“First, you must be careful on the road—” “Look!” Liu interrupted, reaching down to undo the ribbon binding his trouser leg. It was three cun wide and made of black silk, but had been specially designed. He showed Hu the two layers, left unstitched at one end to form a long, narrow pocket. “I had this made on the Main Road two days ago. I knew I should be carrying confidential documents backwards and forwards from now on, so I prepared for it.” “Good. That part presents no great difficulty. But when you reach Hangzhou, how will you broach the matter with those people? Have you thought about that?” “You have only just told me, so I have not.” Liu reflected briefly. “Too soft will not do, and neither will too hard. If I am too soft, they will think I am afraid of them. If I am too hard, they may take fright and refuse—or even turn informer.” “Exactly. That is where the difficulty lies,” Hu said. “Remember these two lines, Third Uncle: tell a man only a third of what you know; never lay your whole heart bare.”

CHAPTER SIX

More than a month later, Liu Bucai returned to Shanghai. He had proved remarkably resourceful: his mission to bring out Hu Xueyan’s family had actually succeeded. Yet with the whole household about to arrive, Hu found himself burdened with a new worry. A’qiao was living with him in a rented “little house”, with no settled status. He did not know what to do about her and could only appeal to the Seventh Lady. “Seventh Sister, you must find a way for me. There is no one else I can consult.” “Naturally! I cannot refuse to concern myself with your affairs, Little Uncle. But first you must decide what you want.” His own attitude towards A’qiao was precisely the difficulty. He could only smile bitterly. “Seventh Sister, is not the whole Romance of the Western Chamber already in your head?” She did indeed know their situation thoroughly. In a word, they appeared devoted lovers, yet a deep gulf lay between them. A’qiao’s heart belonged to Hu, but she would rather remain his mistress outside the household than become a concubine within it. She dreaded the number of people in his family. Besides attending upon his mother, she would have to show deference to his principal wife and even watch Furong’s expressions for signs of favour or displeasure. There was another consideration. She had come from the governor-general of Liangjiang’s official residence. Though not his principal wife, she had virtually acted as mistress of his household. To become any other man’s concubine now seemed a descent. This was also the chief source of Hu’s misgivings. He himself had arranged her union with He Guiqing from beginning to end. To take her over now felt somehow improper, whether he considered his own feelings or other people’s criticism. Renting a “little house” and living as temporary lovers could at least be explained: she had travelled a thousand li to join him in adversity, and gratitude had awakened a love he could not resist. He could offer that excuse to himself and to others. Bringing her into his family would leave him without an answer.

There was another formidable obstacle. Mrs Hu had stated her position with absolute firmness. A successful man might have several wives and concubines; that was nothing unusual. But no one could take away the standing of the principal wife. If Hu chose another woman, he might marry her and bring her home, but he must not establish a separate household outside. At the same time, she had promised to treat every woman he brought home as a sister. Her conduct towards Furong had already proved that she kept her word. This made her position all the stronger; even Hu’s mother had to respect it. But understanding Hu’s predicament and deciding his course were two different things. The Seventh Lady could do the first, not the second. “If you want my help, Little Uncle, you must first make up your own mind. Will you keep her or part from her? Once your purpose is settled, we can find a way. But I must tell you plainly beforehand: whatever happens, I cannot help you conceal it.” Hu was bitterly disappointed. Concealing the affair from his wife was exactly what he had hoped she would help him do. He could find nothing to say.

His silence exposed his thoughts completely. The Seventh Lady advised him, “Little Uncle, harmony at home brings success in everything. Your wife is good and capable, a tremendous help to you. But let me add this: she is formidable too. You must never give her cause to hate you. If you want to bring A’qiao home, I shall wear out my legs and talk till my mouth is dry trying to persuade her. Of course, I cannot promise success. If you decide instead that you and A’qiao must go your separate ways, leave that to me. I can arrange a friendly parting without spoiling the affection between you.” “Then tell me how we could part on such terms.” “I cannot yet. I must give it some thought. But of that I am confident.” Hu thought for a long time without reaching a decision. “We shall talk tomorrow,” he sighed. “Think it over carefully tonight, Little Uncle, and make up your mind. If you mean to bring her home, I must begin preparing the way. I shall ask Third Uncle Liu”—she gestured outside—“to do some work with your mother and your wife beforehand.” Hu started. What sort of work? A moment’s reflection made it clear. Though his wife had said she would not forbid him to take concubines, that did not mean she felt no jealousy. Her sisterly affection for Furong arose partly from her determination to set a virtuous example, partly from Furong’s gentle willingness to accept a subordinate place. Their dispositions and circumstances had combined to produce an unusually happy arrangement. He could not expect every wife and concubine to behave so well. The Seventh Lady meant Liu to sound them out and smooth the way. If Hu truly wanted A’qiao at home, and A’qiao herself would agree to enter the household formally, such preparation was necessary. Yet this also reminded him how formidable A’qiao was—quite unlike Furong. Even if everything went smoothly at first, if she changed her mind and his wife kept her promise, the happy state would not last. Two strong women confronting one another would neither yield. Open quarrels and secret struggles would raise mountainous waves of jealousy. Then he would truly know how hard it was to be a husband caught between two wives.

These reflections brought worry and vexation together, further spoiling his appetite. But he never liked to dampen another person’s pleasure. Liu was in excellent spirits, so Hu roused himself to keep him company, and they talked until midnight. When he returned to the “little house”, A’qiao had tea and refreshments ready as usual. Heavy curtains in the bedroom kept out the sharp chill of early spring. She wore only a lined jacket of soft satin, cut so close that her waist seemed slender enough to clasp in one hand. Taking her hand, Hu found that she really was too lightly dressed. “For the sake of looking pretty, you freeze till you jump! Mind you do not make yourself ill.” She smiled without answering. She poured him hot tea, then cradled a small teapot of Qianlong five-colour porcelain, filled with hot tea, to warm her hands like a hand stove. Her bright eyes watched him, waiting for him to speak. Every evening he would tell her what had happened outside—where he had eaten, what amusing people he had met, what news he had heard. Tonight, contrary to his habit, he sat without a word. “You are tired, are you not?” she said. “Go to bed early.” “Mm. Tired.” His mouth answered, but his eyes remained fixed on the hanging kerosene light beneath the ceiling, called a “safety lamp”. She remembered only one other occasion when he had been so absent-minded, so blind to what lay before him: the night he learnt that Wang Youling had died for his country.

“What is it?” she asked unconsciously in the softest, most coaxing Suzhou dialect. “What is troubling you?” “My mother is coming.” Hu had said little to her about bringing out his family. She knew only that they were all trapped in Jiaxing and that reunion was impossible for the moment; she had thought no further. His sudden announcement threw her into confusion. “That is happy news!” she said, forcing a smile. “Happy, certainly. But it brings worries too. A’qiao, what do you say?” “About what?” She knew perfectly well. After a little thought he said ambiguously, “We cannot go on like this for ever.” Her colour changed and she lowered her head. He could see her lashes moving, but not the expression in her eyes. Now his own thoughts grew confused. He rose, threw himself on the bed and stared vacantly at the canopy. She said nothing, but neither did she weep beneath the lamp. Setting down her teapot, she lifted an earthenware pot from the kerosene stove and poured out some richly simmered chicken broth. From a tin she took seven or eight salted biscuits, arranged them on a porcelain plate and placed both dishes on the dressing table. Then she removed Hu’s boots and slipped embroidered slippers onto his feet. Only after attending to him in proper order did she speak. “Get up and eat.”

Sitting at the dressing table for his bedtime supper had always been the most agreeable moment of Hu’s day. As he ate, he watched A’qiao remove her make-up and listened to her soft Wu speech, now a remark, now a pause, full of entertaining trifles that required no thought. He would not have exchanged that pleasure for a throne. Tonight his mood was different. Yet on second thoughts he would not surrender the pleasure, and sprang from the bed like a tiger, giving her a fright. “You!” She cast him a reproachful glance. “You really are behaving strangely tonight.” “Take pleasure while it lasts.” Suddenly he felt ravenously hungry. “What else is there to eat?” “At this hour, only dry refreshments. The wonton seller and the man with Huzhou rice dumplings and tea eggs have already passed.” She asked, “Did you not eat enough at the Gus’?” “I scarcely ate at all.” “Why? Did the dishes not suit you?” “The Seventh Lady made Luzon shark’s fin, and there was raw fish too. Unfortunately I had no luck with such delicacies. I could not eat.” “Why ever not?” “Ah!” He shook his head. “Let us not speak of it. Bring me some more salted biscuits.” He would say no more, and she asked no more. She did as he requested, then sat to remove her make-up. Gathering her hair in one hand, she combed it repeatedly with a boxwood comb. The room was very quiet. Only the crunch of Hu’s biscuits could be heard.

“When is your mother arriving?” A’qiao suddenly asked. “Soon. In ten days or a fortnight.” “Where will she stay?” “I do not know yet.” “They are nearly here, and you still do not know where they will live? Is that not ridiculous?” “I have been busy these last few days and have had no time to arrange it. I shall see to it after Third Master Liu leaves tomorrow. With money, need one fear being unable to find a house? Only—” “What?” She turned towards him. “Why have you stopped?” “I do not know how large a house it ought to be.” “Stranger still! Surely you can work out how much space you need for the number of people?” “It is the number of people I cannot work out.” Hu glanced at her and deliberately turned away, though he was watching her expression in the mirror. She remained silent and composed, inscrutable. Then she rose and prepared the bed, still without a word. “Let us sleep.” Hu patted his waist. His stomach was full, but his heart felt empty, suspended between heaven and earth with nothing to hold it.

“What is really troubling you? Say it plainly. All this trailing thread and tangled vine makes one itch with impatience.” How could a woman as clever, alert and worldly as she fail to understand? Her deliberate pretence of ignorance was no good sign. Hu did not usually care to play intricate games with women, but with this woman, at this moment, he made an exception. Meeting reserve with reserve, he smiled. “A hidden thought is more enjoyable if you guess it slowly. Why expose it all at once?” Unable to get through to him, A’qiao fell into offended silence. She finished arranging the quilts and helped him undress and get into bed. Then she moved a foreign lamp to a small stand inside the great rosewood bed and turned down the wick, letting the dim yellow light hide her displeasure. In the stillness, Hu’s thoughts became clear. He saw only two courses open to himself and A’qiao: continue as they were, or part. “Do not torment yourself with fancies,” he said without quite intending to speak aloud. “Let me think of a proper solution.”

“What are you talking about, out of the blue like that?” “What else but you? My wife will not allow you to live outside, and you will not live with the family. I must find a way.” A’qiao said nothing. She was reasonable enough to understand his difficulty. But if she yielded out of consideration and agreed to join the household, only to find they could not get on, she would have to leave in disgrace. They would not merely quarrel; she would acquire a bad name: she parted from every man she joined. Better to harden her heart now and leave him to struggle with the problem. She could decide what to do when she saw the result. Yet she must show some tenderness. From beneath the quilt she reached across and clasped his left arm tightly, expressing both her gratitude and her dependence upon him.

Hu had no one else to consult. Once more he went to the Seventh Lady. “Seventh Sister, there is no need to mention living together. I think it would be best to continue as we are. Since you will not help me conceal it, could you help me gain her consent?” “You mean you want me to plead with your wife and persuade her to let you go on living outside together?” “Yes.” “Difficult!” She shook her head vigorously. “A country has its laws, and a family its rules. Your wife runs the household now, and the rule she has made is reasonable. Even your mother could hardly override it, much less those of us who are her juniors.” “Never mind seniority. She listens to you. Plead for me this once: an exception that will never be repeated.” “Little Uncle, how can you talk of not repeating it? You must never say that. It would only make her angrier: ‘Oh, two are not enough! Now he is thinking about a third!’” “You are right. Say whatever you think best, so long as you succeed.” “I fear I shall not.” After a long silence she added, “For your sake, Little Uncle, I shall have to risk this rebuff. But you must not blame me if I fail.” “There is no need to tell me that, Seventh Sister. I leave everything to you. Whatever else may be forgotten, I owe her so much for nursing me through that illness in Ningbo.” He was reminding her to lay special stress on A’qiao’s service: it justified an exception, an unusual indulgence. Yet his reminder too was unnecessary. The Seventh Lady had already considered it. Even if she expected a rebuff, she needed some ground on which to appeal, however slight the chance of success. That ground was A’qiao’s hazardous journey to Ningbo and her tireless nursing, hardly undressing as she tended him and prepared his medicine. She had also decided where to begin: by persuading Liu Bucai.

“Last winter, when Little Uncle took rice to Hangzhou but could not get into the city, he went on to Ningbo and fell gravely ill with fever. When the news reached Shanghai, I was beside myself. Third Uncle Liu, consider the circumstances: Ningbo was in the Longhairs’ hands, and he was a stranger to both the people and the place. How could he survive such an illness? Everything depended on having someone considerate to care for him. There could be no neglect. I discussed it with Old Gu and said I must go myself. He said my going would only make matters worse. Why? First, I am too impatient to nurse a sick man. Second, though our friendship makes us as close as family, and I myself would not mind, Little Uncle might feel constrained. He would be embarrassed to ask me to bring tea or water or attend to the less pleasant necessities. A patient must not lack anything. Without someone close and devoted, someone who understood his needs as her own, he could not recover.” “That is true,” Liu said. “So A’qiao volunteered?” “No. I begged her to go. She and Little Uncle had once had something between them, but that was long over. Everything had to begin afresh, and I was the one who lit the fire. Third Uncle Liu, consider it for me: do I not bear some responsibility for the present situation?” “I understand. If you had not begged her to go, there would be no difficulty now. Yet you begged her solely to save Xueyan’s life—and in fact it is as good as true that she saved it. Is that what you mean?” “Exactly!” she said delightedly. “Third Uncle Liu, you have a worldly man’s quick understanding. One hint is enough!”

“Seventh Sister,” Liu said gravely, “put those two arguments to Xueyan’s wife and she will have nothing to say against them. She is a thoroughly reasonable woman. But she will not be happy at heart. Is it worth forcing this concession from her? Think it over.” “Thank you, Third Uncle Liu. But for Little Uncle’s sake, I have no choice.” “That is not the way to put it. Our friendship is close enough that we need not fear displeasing one another. In doing this, Seventh Sister, you must ask whether it will benefit the whole Hu family. You cannot regard the matter as settled merely because Xueyan gets his way for the moment.” His view went deeper. Reflecting upon it, she suddenly understood. Yet there was a difference between them. Liu was an intimate of the Hu household and lived with them. Whatever private troubles that family had, he naturally knew far more of them than she did. She felt that they must discuss the matter afresh. “Third Uncle Liu, I must listen to that. I ought to do what is best for the whole family. Besides, once we are all in Shanghai, it will mostly be the women who visit one another. If Mrs Hu harbours a grievance against me and we are only outwardly cordial, where will the pleasure be? There is just one thing I still do not understand. If we arrange this, will it really disturb the harmony of the whole household?”

“That is hard to say. From what I know, Xueyan’s wife has her own way of running the household. Break her rule once, and her words will carry less weight thereafter.” “Little Uncle has said it would be this once only, never again. If anything of the sort happens in future, I shall stand up for Mrs Hu before she even needs to speak.” Liu suddenly chuckled, then sighed without answering. His manner suggested that she did not understand what she was doing, which rather annoyed her. “Third Uncle Liu, have I said something wrong?” “Your words are right, and your heart is warm. But that is precisely how you will bring trouble upon yourself. As the saying goes, even an honest magistrate cannot settle a family quarrel. Seventh Sister, even if you were Judge Bao himself and judged the matter perfectly, you would still have trouble.” “How? I do not understand.” “Seventh Sister, you are clever in everything else and confused only here. In a lawsuit, either the plaintiff wins or the defendant does. You mend one thing and damage another. Why put yourself through it?” She understood at once. If she sided with Mrs Hu in future, she must offend Hu himself. Was that not mending one thing at the cost of another? “Enough, enough! Really, Third Uncle Liu, if you have a sound reason, why not state it plainly instead of going round in such circles? Fortunately I have changed and have the patience to question you. Otherwise you would have led me astray!” Her complaint was quite unreasonable, but amusingly so. Liu could only laugh.

“I do not want to be a female Judge Bao. I would rather remain a female Zhang Fei.” There was something behind this, which Liu recognised at once. He had to answer. “What do you intend, Seventh Sister? A female Zhang Fei is all very well, but a rash Zhang Fei would be another matter.” “Zhang Fei could be subtle beneath his roughness too. Naturally I know where to stop. Rest assured: there will be no trouble.” Liu considered. “Then do you still want me to prepare Xueyan’s wife?” “Yes. But the argument will no longer be the same.” That puzzled him. Surely this was a question of yes or no: either forget the matter entirely or raise it. How could there be another way of raising it? “The first half can still be used. Mrs Hu should understand how A’qiao and Little Uncle became attached again. If she knows the whole story, she will not begin with a prejudice against A’qiao.” The Seventh Lady paused. “The latter half must be changed as follows.” Her plan was first to reassure Mrs Hu, which would also reassure Hu himself. Once the family reached Shanghai, his separate mistress could not be concealed. He would also ask the Seventh Lady what she had achieved. She therefore needed his wife’s cooperation to keep the situation calm for the time being.

“I need time to work patiently upon A’qiao. My fear is that, before I have made any progress, husband and wife will already be quarrelling. Once people openly fall out, a deadlock often follows. Mrs Hu had best pretend to know nothing. If Little Uncle stays away from his own camp at night, she need not investigate.” “I understand, and Xueyan’s wife can certainly do that. But he often springs surprises. What if she pretends ignorance and he comes straight out with the truth, face to face?” “I doubt that will happen. If it does, she should neither agree nor object, but put him off as best she can.” “I suppose that is the only course.” Liu nodded. “In a word, overcome firmness with softness.” “Which means tact. Tell Mrs Hu that I undertake to settle the matter within three months. What do I mean by settle? Her rule will remain intact. If A’qiao will not enter the Hu household, she will certainly have joined some other man’s.” “So you mean to transplant the blossom to another branch.” Liu was intrigued. “Have you already found a man for her?” “No, no. I must look slowly.” Suddenly she laughed. “Actually, Third Uncle Liu, you would be quite a match.” “You are joking! How could I graduate in the same class as Xueyan?”

It was already the chou watch, after midnight, when Hu returned home, but he was still in excellent spirits. Sitting by the dressing table as A’qiao removed her make-up, he asked about the women’s outing that evening. “We went for a foreign dinner first. There was nothing very good to eat. Their fried quail was not as good as the eight-piece fried chicken in a Beijing restaurant. And with only the two of us, we looked like—” She shook her head and smiled bitterly, unwilling to finish. Like what? Hu closed his eyes and imagined himself as one of the waiters serving them. Two ladies without male companions, boldly showing themselves in public and handling knives and forks over a foreign dinner—what sort of women would they seem? Judging by their ages and their dress, they would look like two remarkably fine proprietresses of expensive courtesan houses. That must have been A’qiao’s thought, and the reason she refused to finish. Understanding this, he understood her feelings as well. Even if she did not aspire to an aristocratic mansion, she had certainly come to despise the pleasure quarters. Her desire for a respectable life was sincere. This made it harder still to give her up. He blurted out, “What did Seventh Sister say to you?”

“What do you mean, what did she say?” A’qiao’s hands stopped in the act of undoing her hair. “What should she have to say to me? You knew beforehand, did you not? Then tell me: why did she suddenly leave Master You at home today and invite me out to the theatre and a foreign dinner?” This volley of questions threw Hu into confusion. He could not parry it. But he possessed one gift: a smile could conceal any embarrassment, and his embarrassment never lasted long. He soon recovered his composure. “I do not understand what you mean. I was asking whether she told you why she suddenly felt like taking you out. Apparently you do not know. Then I know still less.” “Even a man as clever as you does not know?” She gave a faint, cold smile. “Then there is nothing more to say.” “We are husband and wife chatting together. What harm is there in talking?” She suddenly raised her head, her clear, bright eyes fixed upon him. “Husband and wife? Am I so fortunate?” His careless remark seemed to have given her a weapon, but it did not defeat him. “Here we are husband and wife,” he answered calmly. “Then,” she nodded as though speaking to herself, “there is all the more reason not to listen to the Seventh Lady.” “What did she say?” “She advised me to go back.” “Go back” could mean either returning to her own family or entering Hu’s household as a concubine. Her family home was in Mudu, near Suzhou, which was now in the Longhairs’ hands. There could be no reason to advise her to go there.

Once her meaning was clear, he ought to ask what she intended. But he already knew the answer, so there was no point. After a long silence he burst out, “Tomorrow I really must look for a house.” His manner was rather inscrutable. A’qiao remembered how, when they had discussed finding a house a few days earlier, he had hinted that she should live with his principal wife. Did he still mean that? She must find out. “If you cannot find one in time,” she ventured, “why not let them stay here first?” “There is not room.” Did he mean there was no room in any case, or no room if she remained? She still could not tell and had to try again. “They can squeeze in for the moment. Refugees cannot expect every comfort.” “And you?” “I?” She spoke resolutely. “I shall move elsewhere.” “Why trouble? Better to leave things as they are.” Hu thought for a moment and decided plain speaking would be best. “I feel as you do. Continuing just like this would be best. I have already asked the Seventh Lady to plead with my wife when she arrives, to say a few kind words for us and persuade her to make a special exception.” “That is strange!” A’qiao sounded angry. “She urged me to go back instead—the exact opposite of what you asked. Why?”

Hu deeply regretted his carelessness. Knowing what the Seventh Lady had advised, he should not have told the truth and made her seem disloyal to the task entrusted to her. At the same time, A’qiao’s question alerted him to something. Yes—what did the Seventh Lady mean by acting so? It was puzzling. Whatever the reason, he must defend her now. “Perhaps she was only trying to discover your attitude. What did she say?” “She said, ‘A woman must have a proper settled place. Better to join the Hu family formally and kowtow upon entering the household. Otherwise you should make a firm decision.’” “What did she mean by a firm decision?” “Ask her.” The weariness of A’qiao’s tone showed that this “decision” meant a course she could not possibly accept. Putting her remarks together, Hu understood the Seventh Lady’s intention, but not why she had formed it. “She often takes unexpected measures. Do not be angry yet. Calm yourself and wait to see what happens.”

“How long must I wait?” A’qiao suddenly cried, her voice high and shrill. “Do you know what she said about you? That you are slippery! That you have no constancy and fall in love with every woman you see! Dogs have eaten the conscience of a man like that. She urged me to part from you soon, or I should suffer for it later. Well, I think she was right in every word. Hmph! You could deceive a person to death and never pay for it.” The barrage, striking both directly and by insinuation, left Hu bewildered. He could scarcely believe his ears. Naturally he was angry too—not with A’qiao, but with the Seventh Lady. She had not merely failed to act loyally for him: she had betrayed a friend. With such friendship between them, how could she resort to so underhand a trick? It was more than he could swallow. He had never been so angry. His face turned livid and his lips pale. Just as he was about to erupt, a thought warned him. The Seventh Lady was called a hero among women. She was neither without judgement nor ignorant of loyalty between friends. There must be a reason for what she had said—a very deep reason, too deep even for him to fathom. At that thought his expression softened at once. “What else did she say?” he asked. “What else?” A’qiao’s anger was not merely lingering; it grew as she thought. “Unless you had given her some hint that you meant to abandon me, would she have said such things? You heartless—” Suzhou women were fond of the curse “may you be cut into a thousand pieces”, but some tenderness remained in A’qiao, and she swallowed the words. Hu offered no defence. Until he understood the Seventh Lady’s purpose, he feared that defending himself might spoil it. Yet he could not say nothing at all. He answered vaguely, “Why must you listen to her?”

“Then whom should I listen to? You?” A’qiao pressed him harder. “Speak! Give me one plain, firm assurance.” What assurance did she mean? He was genuinely puzzled. “Tell me what you want me to say.” “There! I knew your heart had changed.” She stamped her foot and spoke bitterly. “Do you really not know what to say? You simply refuse to say it. Enough, enough! At last I know you.” Her angry cries in the quiet night roused the maids and older servants. Shadows gathered outside the window; they seemed to want to enter and mediate but dared not. Hu felt thoroughly uncomfortable. He rose and urged, “It is late. Let us sleep.” Then he quietly made his way into the adjoining room, where there was another bed used occasionally for afternoon naps. It offered a convenient refuge from the lioness’s roar. He turned up a lamp and sat alone, lost in thought. Seeing no further disturbance, the servants withdrew. A’qiao, offended, ignored him and went to bed by herself. Hu did not dare provoke her again and made do with a night apart. The next morning, when he came out of the adjoining room, she was already sitting at the dressing table. Silent and pale, she looked thinner than ever. On closer inspection he thought he saw tear marks. Perhaps she had wept enough in the night to soak her pillow.

“Why do this to yourself?” he said. “You are ruining your health.” “I have thought it over,” she said dully. “This cannot come to a proper end. And I cannot match you. Enough, enough.” She waved her hand and turned her head aside, as though relinquishing everything. Naturally he felt distressed, but could think of nothing suitable to comfort her. “I am giving Chief Yu lunch today,” he said, meaning that he must go out early. “Go, then.” There was coldness in her voice. He hesitated, longing to say another word or two of reassurance. Yet he had no adequate assurance to give and could only abandon the attempt. It was barely ten when he reached Gu’s house. The Seventh Lady was already up and vigorously directing the servants in preparing to receive their guests. The rosewood chairs in the main hall all wore bright red satin covers embroidered in gold. Fresh flowers stood in the vases, and eight tall silver dishes, polished to a brilliant shine, held fresh and dried fruit. The weather was fine, sunlight filled the courtyard and there was no wind. All the doors in the screen and the windows stood open, making the house especially spacious and cheerful.

“You are early, Little Uncle! Have you eaten?” Suddenly she noticed his appearance. “You look very unwell. Are you ill?” “No. I scarcely slept last night.” “Why?” She added, “Even one sleepless night should not leave you looking like this. There must be a reason.” “There is. Where is Old Gu?” “At the firm. He will be back at half past eleven.” “It is still early for the guests. Are you busy, Seventh Sister? If not, I should like a few words.” Her eyes blinked several times. Calmly she answered, “I am free. Let us talk in Yingchun’s study.” Once there, Hu could not begin. He held his tea and stared into space. She already had some idea of his trouble. If she was right, the effect had come rather quickly. She needed time to consider what to say. His silence therefore suited her, and she remained silent too.

At last he spoke. “Seventh Sister, A’qiao had a terrible quarrel with me last night. What exactly did you say to her?” Instead of answering, she asked, “How did she quarrel with you?” “She said I had hinted to you that I meant to abandon her. There is no truth in that, is there?” She smiled. “What else?” With difficulty he answered, “She said you called me slippery, said dogs had eaten my conscience, and said I fell in love with every woman I saw.” She smiled again, this time with a trace of embarrassment. “Little Uncle,” she asked almost teasingly, “were you angry?” “A little at first. Then I thought again and ceased to be angry. You are not a woman without judgement. There must be a reason for speaking that way.”

Relief and gratitude spread over her face. “Little Uncle, it was only because I knew you understood my intentions that I dared act so rashly. In fact, it was not rash. I discussed it with someone beforehand and thought it through carefully. I concluded that this was the best course. But I could not tell you first. Had I done so, it would not have worked.” Setting that aside, she asked again about A’qiao. “What did she say? Why did she quarrel with you? Because she believed me?” “She believes I hinted that I meant to leave her, and that you said those things for that reason. I should have thought the same in her place. With our friendship as it is, why else would you run me down so completely before her?” “Quite right. Entirely right.” She asked with great concern, “And you, Little Uncle—did you defend yourself?” “No. From the way things stood, there seemed little use.” His unwitting cooperation, like drum and drumstick answering one another, fixed her resolve beyond alteration. She had accepted Liu Bucai’s advice and considered the awkward situation between Hu and A’qiao from the standpoint of the whole family’s harmony. A clean cut through the tangle seemed the best solution. Yet she could not control matters single-handed, nor could she explain her intention to Hu in advance. She could only act as opportunity offered, saying as much as each stage allowed.

Her first step had really been a test. If A’qiao had trusted Hu alone, laughed off the Seventh Lady’s remarks behind his back about his inconstancy and apparent heartlessness, and forgotten them, the play would have been difficult to continue. If Hu’s infatuation had been so deep that he strenuously denied every charge and won A’qiao’s understanding, it would have been harder still. Instead, A’qiao suspected that Hu had authorised the remarks, while Hu seemed to accept the accusation in silence. To let such a chance slip would betray the Seventh Lady’s own purpose. She therefore composed her face and spoke with great earnestness. “Little Uncle, you must give A’qiao up. An onlooker sees clearly. I have considered it for you: if you refuse to let go, there will be no end to the trouble.” She advanced four reasons. First, if A’qiao insisted on establishing a separate household, breaking Mrs Hu’s rule would set husband and wife against each other. Second, even if she agreed to join the family, she would do so unwillingly; the grievance in her heart would destroy harmony between wife and concubine. Third, since she had left He Guiqing, and Hu himself had arranged that union, he ought now to avoid suspicion. Otherwise people might say he had originally offered a beauty in exchange for advancement—a profoundly ugly reputation. Fourth, she had come from the pleasure quarters and had seen the great world in a governor-general’s residence. Whether such a woman could settle permanently into respectable life with Hu was very doubtful. “Little Uncle,” she urged finally, “when Hangzhou fell and Governor Wang died, your roots were cut and your support collapsed. You must begin again. It will take real effort to recover your former position. If you were like Fatty Zhang, content to hold what you have and keep the family fed and clothed, I should have nothing to say. But if you mean to build a career, this is the one time when you must not have domestic trouble. More than that, you need your wife’s solid support. Think carefully what you stand to gain or lose.” Hu thought the first four reasons little more than alarming suppositions. But the last warning—that this was no time for domestic trouble—truly startled him. “Seventh Sister, you know I am not like Fatty Zhang. I mean not merely to begin again and build a career, but to build a great one. You have reminded me: my mind cannot be in two places at once. Where have I the time for family quarrels?” “Exactly!” she broke in. “You may not want domestic trouble, but domestic trouble will come looking for you. You cannot push it away or throw it off. That is when you will suffer.” “That is just what I fear. It seems I must listen to you.” “At last! Thank Heaven, Little Uncle, you have come to see it!” she said joyfully. “A’qiao is a fine woman, of course, but she is not the only one in the world. There will be others in the future.” “The future!” Hu stamped his foot. “Let it be for the sake of the future. Seventh Sister, let us discuss this properly.”

They needed to decide what to do for A’qiao. Here the Seventh Lady hesitated. “To be honest, I must still think of a way.” Hu seemed uneasy. “There is one thing you must promise me. Whatever plan you devise, explain it to me first.” This made her a little uncomfortable and a little resentful. “Oh, listen to you! You make it sound as though I might secretly push her into some dreadful fate.” She defended herself with considerable feeling. “A’qiao and I have always got on well. Now, for your sake, I am acting against my conscience, and you seem to blame me for taking too much upon myself—” “Seventh Sister, Seventh Sister!” Hu stopped her with a deep bow. “I must not be the dog that bites Lü Dongbin and fails to recognise a benefactor. I merely feel I have wronged her and want to compensate her properly.” “Do I feel otherwise? Rest assured. I shall never devise anything to harm her.” Suddenly her eyes brightened. A smile broke over her face as she gazed into space. A very good idea had occurred to her. Hu dared not interrupt, though he was impatient to hear it. She, however, seemed determined to take a little revenge. “I have most of it worked out. But forgive me, Little Uncle: I have not begun yet. When I do, I shall explain it clearly, and you will certainly approve.” “Why not tell me first?” he asked with an ingratiating smile. “No. At least let me think it through before I tell you. I am not deliberately keeping you in suspense. I am simply not yet sure of it, so I had better say nothing for the moment.” Her evasions left him entirely uncertain what she was planning. Knowing her temperament, he realised further questions were useless and gave up with a sigh.

The next day Hu called again. Gu was there too. They discussed the approaching arrival of Hu’s family and the need to find a house, furnish it and obtain everyday necessities. Normally A’qiao could have been asked to see to these things, but now it seemed awkward to trouble her. “Never mind!” The Seventh Lady was always most enthusiastic about such matters and enjoyed them greatly. “Leave it all to me.” Listening beside them, Gu was puzzled. “Why can A’qiao not help?” “There is a reason,” his wife interrupted. “I shall tell you later.” “What new device is this?” He warned her, “Do not give Little Uncle ill-considered advice. At this moment, peace is what matters most.” “Peace is precisely the purpose.” She did not want her husband interfering and hurried him along. “Were you not invited to a courtesan banquet? You can go now.” “One waits for a reminder before going to such a banquet. Who hurries off on his own at this hour?” Gu saw what she meant and thought it best to comply. Finding his own way out, he added, “Still, I can visit a friend first.” “Wait!” she said. “I remember Mr Qin once mentioning that a relative of his had a house on Third Road, available either for sale or under a redeemable property pledge. Ask about it for Little Uncle.” Mr Qin was the accountant at their firm. Obedient to his wife’s command, Gu agreed to question him at once and asked Hu to return for news the following day. “Better still,” she said, “ask Mr Qin to come here early tomorrow.” “You probably want him to write another letter,” Gu observed. “If he is free tonight, I shall send him over then.”

Once her husband had gone, the Seventh Lady returned to A’qiao’s affairs. “Little Uncle, is your mind made up? You will not regret it later and complain behind my back that I struck apart a pair of loving mandarin ducks?” “How could I? Do you still not know my temper?” “I know: you do what you say, and do not regret what you have done. Still, I had better ask. Since you are determined, I shall begin tomorrow. You must merely pretend to know nothing. Whatever unusual thing you notice, keep it to yourself.” “I understand. But what if she presses me with questions?” “She will not. Everything will continue as before, without a change. What would she ask?” “Very well. As we say in Hangzhou, I shall watch the fire from City God Hill. I shall wait and enjoy the spectacle.” Between less intimate friends, this might have sounded offensive or sarcastic. Even so, she warned him not to suppose that he had removed himself from the affair. Once the fire began, events might alarm him and draw him in despite himself. He must remain firm, act as though he saw nothing and above all avoid losing his composure and intervening. “Otherwise you will bring the fire upon yourself, and I shall suffer too. In a word, whatever A’qiao says, pay no attention.” Hu’s need to buy a house had given her an idea. She would use it to provoke A’qiao into leaving of her own accord and settle the matter once for all. But there might also be a great storm, hence her special warning.

The purchase went smoothly. The house Mr Qin recommended stood on Third Road near the famous Huajin Lane. Though the neighbourhood was busy, the house was spacious and deep; closing its great door shut out the clamour and created quiet amid the bustle. Hu liked it very much. The price was modest too—only two thousand five hundred eagle silver dollars—so he completed the purchase that day. The Seventh Lady was full of zeal. “Little Uncle, give me another thousand dollars and leave everything to me. Attend to your own affairs for three days. On the fourth, come and see what I have made of it.” “What more could I ask? But it is so much trouble for you, Seventh Sister.” He knew her ways. That polite acknowledgement was enough. If he worried that she was overworking and sent someone to share the burden, he would only displease her. He gave her a thousand silver dollars and neither asked nor interfered. During the three days he examined the accounts at his own bank and enquired into its business, accomplishing some solid work. On the third day he came home from Fukang Bank in Jixian Lane and found A’qiao with her hair dressed, her face carefully powdered and her lips coloured. Yet she wore informal clothes. Was she about to go out or had she just returned? “I have only just come back. I went to see the Seventh Lady,” she said. “The house on Third Road has been made very handsome.”

Her tone was calm, but Hu thought he heard a reproach for keeping it from her. Embarrassed, he hardly knew how to answer. “She asked whether I liked the house. Naturally I said yes. Then she asked if I wanted to live there. Do you know what I told her? That I was not so fortunate.” Hu almost replied, “I fear I am the one without that good fortune,” but checked the words. Casually he said, “What good fortune is there in one of these so-called lane houses in the foreign settlement? When Hangzhou is recovered, I shall build a proper estate beside West Lake. To live in such a paradise would indeed require merit accumulated in a former life.” A’qiao said nothing. She went to the dressing table to remove her ornaments, while a maid helped Hu take off his riding jacket and change into slippers. He sat by the window with his tea. “I think I had better accumulate merit for the next life,” she said suddenly. “In the next life we shall be husband and wife,” he answered without thinking. Her face changed dramatically. He meant that, since she would not become his concubine in this life, he could only hope that in the next they might be one husband and one wife, growing old together. But she misunderstood. “I wondered why the Seventh Lady said those things. So your heart really has changed! If you have something to say, say it yourself. Why go round corners and get someone else to do it?”

Hu knew he had spoken wrongly. Yet he could hardly be blamed. He had asked the Seventh Lady what to say if A’qiao pressed him about her future. She had assured him that, with everything unchanged, no questions would arise. But things were different now. A’qiao had seen the new house; the change had begun. She would keep asking, and he could not have her fly into a rage every time, as she had now. It seemed he must rely on his own ingenuity after all. He began studying her attitude closely. Why did she not consider her own refusal to live with his principal wife? She was defying accepted practice and asking the impossible, yet repeatedly accused him of a change of heart. Could it be that she herself wished to leave but could not say so, and was pressing him and shifting blame in order to make him responsible for the break? The thought seemed almost absurd. Was he judging her with a petty mind? Not necessarily. She had once given her heart to He Guiqing too. Whatever might be said, in the end she had failed him—and when he was in misfortune. Was it not rather naive to regard her as a paragon? In that moment Hu felt his own heart harden. In a quiet voice, almost cold, he said, “I have nothing to say. If you wish to cultivate good fortune for another life, I can only hope that we shall be husband and wife then.” She turned and looked directly at him. “You mean you do not want me in this life?” He avoided her eyes. “I did not say that. Only—” He stopped.

“Say it! A grown man should not mumble and hesitate.” If she had been willing to yield for the sake of peace, she might have wept at his tone, but would not have pursued the question. By pressing him, she showed that she was prepared for an open break. Hu felt it was enough to understand her position. There was no need to quarrel now—better never to quarrel, but to part amicably. He smiled. “Neither of us is a two- or three-year-old child. Why make ourselves ridiculous before the servants?” “Hmph!” She gave a cold laugh and turned back to the mirror to remove her make-up. Hu felt thoroughly weary of the place, a feeling he had never known before. He spent little time at home, but whenever he returned, the mere sight of A’qiao had made it seem the most comfortable place in the world. Only absolute necessity could draw him out again. Now he wanted to go somewhere—anywhere, even for a walk in the street. The impulse grew irresistible. Rising, he said, “I must go out again.” Then, feeling apologetic, he asked, “Is there anything you would like to eat? I shall bring it back.” She merely shook her head, as though even speech were too much trouble. A chill passed repeatedly down his back. He left at once, going straight downstairs in his slippers, without putting on his riding jacket.

Outside the door he felt at a loss. A’fu, one of his sedan-chair bearers, hurried after him. “Where are you going, sir? I shall fetch the men.” There were four bearers. Hu had said on returning that he would not go out again that evening, so the other three, who lodged at Fukang Bank, had already left. Only A’fu remained. “No need!” Hu waved a hand and went out of the lane alone. He wandered without purpose, dispirited. He tried to think of something pleasant, but could not put A’qiao out of his mind. Her lovely, expressive eyes and enchanting smile remained before him; her soft Wu voice, sweet as an oriole’s, sounded clearly in his ears. Suddenly he was seized by overwhelming regret. He turned and walked home at great speed.

The stone-framed outer door was already shut. He knocked several times with the bronze ring. A’fu opened it, and Hu went straight upstairs. One glance chilled his heart. “Where is your mistress?” Pointing to the dark bedroom, he questioned Suxiang, the maid who had come out of another room to meet him. “She has gone out.” “Where?” “She did not say.” “When did she leave?” “As soon as you had gone, she said she was going out. I asked her a question and she scolded me: ‘Mind your own business.’” “How did she go? Why did she not take you?” “She would not have me with her. She said she would be back shortly. I asked if I should hire a chair, and she said she could get one herself at the entrance to the lane.” Hu was deeply disappointed and full of misgivings. He had returned to tell her that everything would remain as before, without a change. Whatever his wife might say, he had resolved to maintain this separate household. Unless A’qiao herself wished to choose another man, he would never change his heart. Now all that warmth sank into an abyss. Her movements seemed deeply suspicious. Suxiang was her personal maid and always accompanied her outside. If she had left the girl behind, she must have gone somewhere the maid could not go—or somewhere she wished to conceal even from Suxiang.

With that thought, an inexpressible bitterness and despondency filled him. He stood in the sitting room without speaking for a long time. Suxiang grew frightened. “Sir,” she asked timidly, “will you dine at home? I shall tell the kitchen.” “I am not hungry. Where is A’xiang?” “A’xiang has gone out.” “Out? Where?” “You would have to—” Suxiang faltered. “You would have to ask A’fu.”

Her manner too seemed suspicious, and Hu nearly lost his temper. Then he steadied himself. “Fetch A’fu.” Once the bearer had been called and questioned, the truth emerged. A’xiang was “amusing himself” at a little grocery nearby. Its elderly owners had only one child, a daughter of seventeen. Hu had seen her: she resembled a cheerful Wuxi clay doll, with a plump, round face and a smile that never left it. A’xiang had set his heart on her. As soon as Hu went out, he would linger in the shop, acting as an unpaid assistant and errand boy, without even receiving his meals. “If you want him, sir, I shall fetch him.” “No need.” The news made Hu feel rather better. He might as well leave A’qiao aside and concern himself with A’xiang’s affairs. “That sounds promising. What is the girl’s name?” “The same as—” A’fu struggled to get the words out. “The same familiar name as our mistress.” So she too was called A’qiao. “A coincidence indeed!” Hu laughed with interest.

“I told A’xiang not to call her directly by her name. It is our mistress’s name too, and a servant ought not to be so disrespectful.” This was the sort of observation one expected from a man versed in books and propriety. Hearing it from a muddy-legged chairbearer surprised and pleased Hu. But his questions remained about A’xiang. “What does he call her, then? Sister? Little Sister? That would be rather sickly.” “Yes, far too sickly. He told me they never use each other’s names. They simply say ‘Hey!’ to one another. When he speaks of her to her parents, he calls her ‘your young lady’.” “Excellent!” Hu thought that when a young man and woman needed only “Hey!” and recognised each other by their voices, theirs was no ordinary acquaintance. But how did her parents feel? “They are very pleased with him.” “How pleased? Do they regard him as a prospective son-in-law?” “Something like that.” “Then you ought to lend a hand and earn a cup of wedding wine.” “A’xiang was bought into your household, sir. Everything must be decided by you. We would not dare interfere. Besides, we could not arrange it.” “Why not?” “A wedding needs—” Hu understood and nodded. “I see. Fetch him for me.”

Before a cup of tea could have been drunk, A’xiang was back. His embarrassed expression showed that A’fu had explained the matter on the way. “How old are you?” “Seventeen.” “Seventeen!” Hu hesitated slightly. “Rather early.” After a pause he asked, “And how old is ‘their young lady’?” He was playfully repeating A’xiang’s own phrase, but it sounded like mockery to the boy, who grew painfully embarrassed. “Two months older than I am. I was born in the ninth month; her birthday is the seventh of the seventh month.” “You even know her birth particulars!” Hu could scarcely keep from laughing, but restrained himself to preserve his dignity. “What is the family’s surname?” “Wei.” “How does Mr Wei treat you? Is he thinking of giving you his daughter? Do not be shy. Tell me the truth.” “Of course I would tell you the truth, sir. Mr Wei has said nothing, but his wife has dropped a hint.
