She says they have only one daughter and cannot bear to part with her. Whoever marries her must join their household.” “What did you say?” “I pretended not to understand.” “Why? Are you unwilling to marry into the Wei family?” “It would be no use if I were willing. I have taken my master’s surname. How could I change it again to Wei?” “You have a conscience, at least.” Hu nodded approvingly. “I shall see what can be done.” That plainly meant the happy outcome was possible. A’xiang was delighted. Yet he was still shy. He knew this was an excellent opportunity, but could not bring himself to ask and settle the matter while the chance was before him.

Saying nothing left him awkward and uncomfortable, so he found an excuse to speak. “You have probably not eaten, sir. I shall tell them.” He called, “Suxiang! Suxiang!” She emerged from the servants’ room without looking him in the face. Passing him, she muttered, “Must you call as though summoning a soul?” Then she asked Hu, “Did you call me, sir?” He saw it all and understood. No wonder she had seemed reluctant to answer when he asked where A’xiang was. She was jealous of his attachment to the Wei girl. The falling blossom was willing, but the flowing water felt nothing: A’xiang had disappointed her. Hu felt sorry for her. Yet there was no reason to interfere blindly and upset what was already taking shape. He could only pretend not to understand and find her something to do.

“I shall not dine at home.” He told A’xiang, “Go straight to Mr Zhang and say I invite him for wine. What is the name of the tavern at the entrance to the lane?” “Wang Baohe.” “I shall wait for him there. Be quick, and ask him to come at once.” “Yes, sir!” The boy answered as loudly as though he had received a general’s command, then hurried downstairs. After he had gone, Hu finished the tea Suxiang had poured and went out too. As he looked into Wang Baohe, the sharp-eyed proprietor hurried forward, bowing repeatedly. “What brings Your Excellency here? Are you looking for someone?” “No. I have come for a drink.” Mr Wang was overwhelmed by the honour. “Please come in! The private table is free. You have chosen just the right moment.” The private space was a small projecting recess, about a square zhang, furnished with a narrow side table and a square dining table. On the side table stood a shrine containing a tablet to the ancestors of the Wang family. The setting deepened Hu’s homesickness. He seemed to be in a little tavern near Salt Bridge in Hangzhou, remembering the days when he and Fatty Zhang would drink together in their leisure. “Your Excellency, I shall open a jar of genuine Shaoxing Huadiao—guaranteed. Try it.” “As you please. What have you to eat with it?” “Razor clams have just come into season. There are tender whip bamboo shoots too, as well as soy-braised duck and chicken in wine lees.” “Bring them all. And two other things: one-legged crabs and fried stinking bean curd.” “One-legged crabs” meant sprouted beans, available in every tavern, large or small. The bean curd was harder. “The vendors have all passed by at this hour,” Mr Wang said. “I do not know whether any can still be found.” “I must have it,” Hu insisted. “Send someone a little farther to look. He must bring some back.” “Certainly, certainly! We shall find it.” Mr Wang sent a young apprentice searching, with particular orders to hurry back.

Hu began drinking alone. Of all the dishes on the table, he touched only the sprouted beans. What he savoured was not the beans, but the little pleasures wrested from hardship in his humble days. An inexpressible mixture of pain, comfort and lingering longing filled him. Suddenly he looked up and saw Fatty Zhang smiling before him. Only then did he realise how far his thoughts had wandered. Collecting himself, he asked, “Have you eaten?” “I was just having a drink when A’xiang arrived.” Zhang sat down. “You must be at leisure today, to think of drinking here.” “Not at leisure. At a loose end.” Zhang had never heard such dispirited words from him. His eyes widened and he was about to ask, but the fragrance of the warmed wine reached him, and drinking took precedence. “Fine wine!” he declared after a sip, smacking his lips. “True Shaoxing Huadiao.” “However fine it is, it cannot taste as good as the spirits we drank at Salt Bridge.” “Oh!” Zhang looked around. “This does resemble the Chunhao tavern we used to frequent.” “Who knows what has become of it?” Hu sighed faintly and drained a bowl. “You must not drink like that. You will be drunk.” Zhang set down his own cup, looking troubled. “What has upset you?” “Nothing. I miss Hangzhou, and the old days. Old Zhang, ‘Never forget friends made in poverty; never cast out the wife who shared your bran and chaff.’ Here, I drink to you.” Zhang did not know what had stirred him and watched uneasily. “Less, less! Take it slowly.” Fortunately, Hu was broad-minded and well behaved in drink. After two bowls he wanted to think only of pleasant things. Remembering A’xiang, he asked, “Old Zhang, do you know the owner of the grocery up ahead? His name is Wei.”

“We are in the same trade. Of course I know him. You must have a reason for asking.” “He has a daughter called A’qiao, with a round face—the sort of face that promises sons. You must know her well too?” Zhang’s interest awakened. He sat up briskly and looked at Hu, nodding as he answered slowly, “Very well. She comes to my shop every week or so.” “Why?” “When her father needs money to restock, he borrows from me. She is always the one he sends.” “Then his little grocery must be rather hard pressed.” “Yes. He has always had very little capital. That is just what you want. If the family were comfortably off and cared little for money, her parents might not agree.” “Agree to what?” Hu did not understand. “You tell me! Did you not say she had a face that promised sons?” Hu stared, then suddenly understood. He sprayed out a mouthful of wine and hastily turned aside, coughing and laughing, while Zhang was left completely bewildered. “Old Zhang, you have spent your whole life thinking yourself clever. Where has your imagination taken you?” “Were you not thinking of taking a concubine who could bear you sons?” Zhang asked hesitantly. “That is why I say you are too clever by half. Nothing of the sort! But there is a marriage to arrange, and I still want you to be the matchmaker.” He then told Zhang the whole story of A’xiang and the Wei girl’s affection.

“Excellent!” Zhang said delightedly. “This match certainly will not become a bowl of ‘spring-plum pulp’.” That was a Hangzhou expression. If a matchmaker brought together an unhappy couple, both families blamed him. Every dispute was referred back to him for negotiation, burdening him without end; such a predicament was called “spring-plum pulp”. Zhang’s words showed that he approved of the match, which made Hu feel still more satisfied with what he was doing. In his good humour, he made the terms more generous.

“Tell Mr Wei that A’xiang may marry into his household, but may not change his surname. With her son-bearing looks, there should be no shortage of sons. Mr Wei can choose one to continue his family line. I shall put A’xiang into the grocery trade too, lending him a thousand silver dollars as capital.” “Then there is no need to discuss a separate betrothal payment. The groom’s side can provide everything—the trousseau, the feast and the rest. Once the marriage ceremony is held, the two households will become one. Mr Wei need spend nothing, and he will have a son-in-law to support them in old age. Such favourable terms should satisfy him completely. You will see: I shall settle it tomorrow, and we can choose a day for the wedding at once.” “Then I entrust it to you. I shall have a red packet ready to thank the matchmaker.” “Thank me? Have you not helped me often enough already?”

Just then the apprentice arrived with a great plate of fried stinking bean curd. Hu had no time for further talk. He ate three pieces in succession, almost wolfing them down, which gave the talkative Zhang another opening. “You leave the other dishes alone, but how you enjoy those sprouted beans and that bean curd!” Hu nodded and set down his chopsticks. “My old sworn brother Ji Heling once told me a story. A poor scholar went to live in a temple and became friends with an old monk. The monk would dig up taro roots and roast them in hot ashes, and the scholar ate them with delight. Later, after ten years of study in obscurity, the scholar won fame at a single stroke, rose rapidly and became a great official. Returning home in glory, he remembered the flavour of those taros and went specially to visit the monk and taste them again. This time he said they were no good. The monk answered, ‘The taros have not changed. You have.’ My wish for beans and stinking bean curd today means something rather like that.” “So that is it! You remember how those were always the two things we ate with a bowl of wine at Chunhao.” Zhang asked, “Now you have tasted them, do they taste as they did before?” “Yes.” “That is remarkable!” His tone suggested he doubted Hu’s sincerity. “Shark’s fin and sea cucumber have not spoiled your palate?” “You misunderstand. I did not say they tasted good. They did not then, and they do not now.” “I do not understand that! Why eat what you do not like? We ate them countless times in the old days, and I never heard you say they were no good.” “There is no need to say it. Find a way to get better things to eat. Empty talk achieves nothing; it only makes others unwilling to endure hardship.” These words left Zhang staring for a long time before he could speak. “Old Hu, we have been friends far longer than three or five years, but only today have I understood your nature. No wonder! You have climbed from apprentice to great proprietor, while I have gone from senior bank clerk to owner of a tiny grocery. There is a reason for both.” The habitually cheerful Zhang was suddenly overcome by feeling, and his face grew gloomy. Hu understood the disappointment beneath his complaint. They had shared poverty and adversity for so many years that he too felt distressed.

He wanted sincerely to comfort his friend. He thought of the plan he had discussed with Liu Bucai and Gu Yingchun. Once they had made contact with Little Zhang of Hangzhou and Sun Xiangtai of Jiaxing, they intended to trade on a large scale in imported and southern goods. Why not give Fatty Zhang a share? Naturally Hu would advance the capital. Zhang need only lend a hand; it would certainly offer more promise than clinging to a little grocery. He was about to speak when he thought better of it. Zhang could not keep his mouth shut, and the trading scheme served a particular purpose. He ought not to know of it. Better to find another way of helping him. After a moment, an idea came. “Old Zhang, I know that your present position is beneath you. But the times are wrong. You must hold on for a while. You know my bank: with its old roots in Hangzhou cut off, its living source of funds is gone. We too are struggling to keep it going. There is certainly hope, but work must be put into it. Would you come and help me?” “Between us, willingness does not enter into it. But forgive me, Old Hu: I have had my heart chilled by banking. Think of my former employer. I worked so hard for him, and in the end he turned against me. Without your great help, I might have faced a lawsuit. From that day I swore never to earn my living in a bank again. However small my own business, at least I am its proprietor.” Realising he had spoken awkwardly, he hurried to qualify it. “With you, naturally, it would be different. But I swore not to work in another man’s bank, and I made that oath kneeling before Lord Guan. It is not a thing to treat lightly. A thousand words come to one: I am sorry, Old Hu.” He raised his cup in apology.

“I cannot drink to that. Let me ask you two questions. Your oath was that you would not work in someone else’s bank?” “Yes.” “That is, you would not be another man’s clerk?” “Exactly!” Zhang answered emphatically. “Then understand this first, Old Zhang: I am not asking you to become a clerk at Fukang.” “What, then?” Zhang asked in surprise. “A shareholder. In effect, your own proprietor. However solemn your oath, that cannot violate it.” “A shareholder!” He was tempted. “But I have no capital.” “I shall lend it to you. I shall assign ten thousand taels as your share. You will manage the business and draw a separate salary. I have thought of a solution for your grocery too: transfer it to A’xiang, and he will naturally combine it with his father-in-law’s. Does that not all follow neatly?”

With such terms and such friendship behind them, Fatty Zhang ought, by all ordinary reasoning, to have accepted without hesitation. Yet he still wavered. For one thing, the blow he had suffered in banking had truly chilled his heart. For another, friendship required feeling to be repaid with feeling: precisely because Hu Xueyan treated him so generously, he must consider whether he could open up new business and put the bank in order. If he could not be sure of doing so, it would be better to decline now and preserve their friendship than to disappoint his friend later. Of course, he could neither bring himself to refuse nor bear to let the opportunity go. After thinking it over and over, he said, “Let me look into it first.” “Look into it? There is no need!” Hu replied. “Fukang is naturally less lively than it was when Governor Wang was alive. Compared with the other Shanghai banks, I can honestly say that we fall short of the best but stand well above the worst. There is absolutely no deficit. Our borrowers are reliable, and the few large depositors are all substantial men. They will not all descend on us to withdraw their money at once. The trouble is that I cannot give it my whole attention. The chief clerk I engaged is a straightforward fellow, but his health is poor; the place has become lifeless and never gets ahead. With you there, things would certainly be different. Let me provide an idea or two and you carry them through. Working together, we shall polish Fukang’s signboard until it shines like gold again.” Put that way, the venture was worth undertaking. Still, Zhang asked, “In exactly what capacity am I to join Fukang? You know the rules of banking.” By those rules, authority rested entirely with the chief clerk; shareholders were not to interfere. Hu had already thought this through. “In relation to me, you are a shareholder,” he answered without hesitation. “In relation to Fukang, you are the chief clerk. You will be working for yourself, not serving another man.” The explanation was complete, and Zhang was satisfied. “Then it is settled,” he said firmly. “You supply the ideas; I carry them out. You represent us outside; I take charge within.” “Excellent! That is exactly what I mean—”

“Wait!” A sudden thought struck Zhang, and he asked urgently, “What about the man already there?” “You need not worry about him. His health is poor, and his son has made his way in the world. He is a comprador with an American trading firm and has long urged his father to retire and enjoy life. The old fellow is grateful for my treatment of him. He has offered his resignation several times, but when I refused to let him go he felt ashamed to insist. He could wish for nothing better than to have you take over.” Zhang was relieved. “I was afraid of breaking another man’s rice bowl.” Once more he grew reflective. “My own employer treated me badly. We must not make him say behind our backs that his employer treated him badly too.” “Do you think I am that sort of man?” Hu asked. “Old Zhang, once a gentleman gives his word, four horses cannot overtake it. From this moment, we are partners. Now let us talk business. How do you think we should proceed?” That stumped Zhang. He had begun as an apprentice and risen step by step to chief clerk. Before advising on internal management he would have to examine the actual position, and before discussing expansion he would have to study the market. Asked to produce an idea out of thin air, he was helpless. At last he said, “The price of silver fluctuates sharply nowadays. With prompt information, we could make a handsome profit simply buying and selling.” “Certainly. You can deal with that yourself; there is no need to consult me. What we must discuss is how to let out a long line and fly our kite far ahead: three years from now, once affairs have settled down, how can we make a sudden leap into prosperity?” “That,” Zhang said with a laugh, “is beyond my abilities.”

Business talk always roused Hu’s enthusiasm. Now, pleasantly warmed by wine, he was more eager than ever. “We seldom have time to ourselves. Let us make some proper plans today. Old Zhang, you must know the practices of the Shanxi remittance houses?” “Different trades are mountains apart. Banks and remittance houses may look like the same business, but their methods differ.” Before Hu, Zhang dared give nothing but an honest answer. “Besides, their influence does not extend south of the Yangtze. I have had no opportunity to see their inner workings.” “For us bankers, the Shanxi houses are the only formidable rivals. Know yourself and your enemy, and you can win a hundred battles. That is why I always pay close attention to them. Let me tell you something about them first.” As Hu understood it, the Shanxi houses had originally dealt chiefly in remittances, with the capital as their centre. In recent years, war and difficult roads had made it inconvenient to transport government funds to Beijing. Almost without a formal arrangement, the houses had taken over some of the duties of the central and provincial treasuries. They paid no interest on government balances, while their remittance charges were substantial: naturally, the profits were great. Moreover, wealthy merchants and high officials in the provinces considered the capital the safest place under heaven. They therefore sent much of their ready cash there, which in practice meant depositing it with the houses. Such deposits were intended to preserve capital, not earn income, so the interest paid was very low.

“Once you have deposits, you must find an outlet for them. Leave funds to rot, and large ingots will never give birth to little ones,” Hu said. “In recent years, the Shanxi houses have commonly lent to officials in the capital. They call it making ‘capital loans’. I hear that against a note for ten thousand taels they actually advance seven thousand—” “What?” Zhang interrupted loudly. “What sort of loan is that? Worse than the usurers who collect their instalments every day!” “You think it worse, but the borrowers agree willingly enough. The wool comes off the sheep’s back in the end. It is the common people who suffer.” “How so?” “Think about it. When an official borrows, what can he repay with? Naturally, the people repay for him. Suppose someone is appointed provincial treasurer of your Zhejiang. He must make payments in the capital and pay for his journey. Once he arrives, he needs a residence, chairs and horses, servants—what does not cost money? So he first takes a capital loan. After assuming office, he finds some public money to divert and pays it off, then gradually makes up the sum. Unless he devises ways to extract it from the people, he will be left with a deficit in his accounts.” “But is that not risky? Suppose he refuses to acknowledge the debt once he takes office?” “He will not. First, there is a guarantor, who must also be an official in the capital. Second, there is a signed note. If he repudiates the debt, a petition can be lodged with the Censorate. One impeachment from a censor, and the debtor loses his post. Third, others will help the houses and prevent defaults. Why? The poor Hanlin scholars live on loans. They hope to be sent out as examiners or education commissioners, collect ceremonial gifts from their pupils and repay their debts. Having repaid, they can borrow again and keep going. If anyone defaults, the houses may act together, declare officials untrustworthy and stop lending. The poor scholars would panic. They will help recover the money.” Hu paused, then went on. “There is only one real risk: something may happen to a newly appointed official on his journey. He may die or lose his appointment. Even then, it depends. A sound guarantor will shoulder the whole obligation.” “No wonder!” Zhang exclaimed. “These past few years the Qi, Tai and Ping groups of remittance houses have been opening branches all over the provinces. So this is the advantage!” Eager to try it himself, he asked, “Why should we not learn from them?” “Exactly!” Hu drained his cup. “That is just what I have in mind.”

Hu intended to follow their example with two kinds of loan. The first would go to officials. With travel between north and south so difficult and circumstances so altered, transfers and promotions in recent years had often departed from normal procedure. The requirement that officials be sent to the appropriate ministry and presented for an imperial audience had not been abolished, but exceptions were commonly made. In particular, among civil and military officials recommended for promotion on the strength of wartime service, there were even provincial treasurers and judicial commissioners—men responsible for the finances or justice of an entire province—who had never been to Beijing. A man appointed from the capital could, of course, take a capital loan. But an official promoted within the lower Yangtze region—a Jiangsu magistrate made prefect in Hubei, for instance—could not move without money for the journey and for establishing his household. Hu proposed to help such men with loans on the same model. “There is risk, of course. But we must reckon the whole account, using the surplus here to cover the shortfall there. Since the likin taxes began, goodness knows how many men have grown rich. Someone given a tax post gains an extra day’s profit for every day earlier he arrives. However high the interest, he will borrow, and we shall not suffer a bad debt. Our method is to earn more from these accounts to make up for losses on others. We can say it plainly from the start: we shall ‘take from the rich to help the poor’.” “Take from the rich to help the poor!” Zhang repeated the phrase once or twice and nodded. “I understand the principle. What is the second kind?”

“The second is for families of the inland gentry who have fled to Shanghai. At home, most lived on rents. In the morning they carried a birdcage to the teahouse; in the afternoon they slept at the bathhouse; in the evening they sat down to a drinking party and went home tipsy. Of the three hundred and sixty days of the year, at least three hundred passed like that. To flatter such men, you call them young gentlemen of leisure. Less politely, they are idle vagabonds. Had their ancestors not earned merit and left them great estates, they would certainly have become generals of the outstretched hand. When they first fled here, they had some cash and valuables, so they did not immediately fall on hard times. But spend without earning, and even a mountain is exhausted—especially in this foreign settlement, with its feasting and pleasure. After these few years, a good many once-famous young gentlemen are close to begging.” This was no exaggeration. Zhang had encountered several himself; the most wretched even depended on their wives and daughters to earn a living entertaining men. He therefore sympathised deeply with Hu’s account, but disapproved of lending to such people. “It is easy to relieve an emergency and hard to rescue poverty. We should be certain to lose the money.” “No,” Hu said. “We must look further ahead. The Longhairs’ allotted days are almost over. Once Jiangsu and Zhejiang are recovered, the refugees will go home. The rebels cannot carry off their great tracts of farmland. After a year or two of hardship, they will be gentlemen of leisure again. Why should the loans be lost?” “Ah!” Zhang drew a deep breath. “That had not occurred to me. On that basis, I have a method to suggest.” “Let us hear it.” “Require their title deeds as security. If they have no deeds with them, have them sign a note stating that, if they fail to repay, specified land shall be valued and surrendered in settlement.” “Quite right! That makes it safer still.”

“One thing more!” Their long conversation had taught Zhang a great deal and sharpened his wits. “Since we are relieving poverty, we must look ahead. These men brought up as young masters spend ten thousand if they have ten thousand, regardless of the consequences. We should not advance the full amount on a property at the first application. Leave something in reserve for an additional advance when they cannot make ends meet.” Now they were thinking along exactly the same lines. Their discussion grew wider and deeper. Yet they spoke only of lending. Where were the funds to come from? The question had been in Zhang’s mind all along, but he had held it back, expecting so meticulous a man as Hu to raise it without prompting. Hu never did, however, and Zhang was obliged to ask. “Old Hu, both these kinds of loan are long-term. Those secured on land, especially, cannot have a definite prospect of repayment until the country is recovered. That may take more than two or three years. It will require a great deal of capital. Have you considered that?” “Of course. How can we do business with loans going out and no deposits coming in? I have a way of attracting deposits, but I fear you may not approve.” “Why should you think so? We are in business. If deposits come our way, surely we shall not turn them down?” Hu did not answer at once. He smiled and sipped his wine with a mysterious air that once more left Zhang unable to fathom him. Zhang’s feelings were mixed: admiration, but also wariness. Hu’s resources were so unpredictable that one could never safely let down one’s guard with him.

At last Hu spoke, asking a wholly unexpected question. “Old Zhang, suppose I were a Longhair and wanted to deposit money with you under an assumed name. Would you dare accept it?” “Well—why should I not?” “What if there were conditions?” “What conditions?” “He asks no interest, and it is not a demand deposit. He will withdraw it after three years or five. There is only one condition: whatever happens, you must repay the full amount.” “Of course we repay the full amount. What else could we do?” “Old Zhang, you have not understood what I mean, nor what is at stake. You know what happens when a household’s property is confiscated. Any private assets entrusted elsewhere are ordinarily pursued as well. What I mean is that, even if the authorities recover this deposit from you, you must still repay the depositor. Would you dare accept that risk?” Only now did Zhang see. “I would not!” he said, shaking his head vigorously. “If the authorities came looking for the money, we would have to disclose it. Otherwise we should be guilty of concealing rebel property—a terrible charge. Once they had taken it away, how could we pay the man when his deposit matured?”

“I knew you would not dare,” Hu said. “I would. Why? Because I am certain they will not pursue it.” “Oh? What makes you so sure? Let me hear your reasoning.” “What reasoning is needed? We have been fighting the Longhairs for years and have taken plenty of their leaders alive. When have you seen the authorities trace their assets?” He lowered his voice. “Look at the imperial troops. Once they catch a Longhair, they strip him of everything and never report it. If anyone traced the property, they would have to begin with the troops who plundered it. Would that not be making trouble for themselves? As I said, the Longhairs’ days are nearly over. Many are laying secret plans. There is still one catastrophe for them to survive; once through it, they can live in comfort for the rest of their days.” “What catastrophe?” “The fall of the Taiping Heavenly Kingdom. Once it collapses, the Longhairs will be like rats crossing the street, with everyone shouting to strike them down. That first confusion will be the most dangerous time. Once order is restored, the court will naturally issue an edict: punish the ringleaders, spare those forced to follow. Still less will it pursue their private property. That is why I say that, if they survive that catastrophe, they can live free of want for the rest of their lives.” Now Zhang understood everything. The Longhairs who had gorged themselves on plunder needed two kinds of protection against the coming disaster: protection for their lives and for their wealth. When it came, they might escape alive but lose their property. In other words, saving their lives was easier than saving their wealth. Hence the need to arrange matters early.

Once he saw the scheme, Zhang could not help exclaiming, “Excellent! Excellent!” Yet he shook his head instead of nodding. “Old Hu,” he said, with something of the man who feared the sky might fall, “such ingenuity may provoke Heaven’s jealousy!” “There is nothing so extraordinary about it. I mean no one harm. Why should Heaven be jealous?” “But is it against the law?” Feeling he had spoken too bluntly, Zhang hurried to explain. “Old Hu, it is because the scheme is so good. As the saying goes, good things meet many obstacles. I am afraid there may be something that makes it unworkable. I cannot feel quite at ease.” “You are right to ask. We cannot break the law. But the court’s laws are definite things: we do what they say, and that is obeying the law. Where they say nothing, we may follow our own wishes. Concealing a criminal’s property is certainly illegal. But if it is a question of law, we have an answer: when a man comes to make a deposit, he does not have ‘I am a Longhair’ written on his forehead. If he deposits under an assumed name, who knows his identity?” “In fact, we do know. Our consciences cannot accept that.”

“Old Zhang, Old Zhang!” Hu took a sip, at once moved and pleased. “I was not mistaken in you. You are good-hearted by nature. That is excellent. But as for conscience, a merchant’s conscience concerns his customers. Fair dealings, cheating neither young nor old—that is our conscience. Officials must have a conscience towards the court. It is really the same principle as a merchant’s duty to his customers. ‘Master the arts of letters and arms, then sell them to the imperial house.’ The court is the customer of civil and military officials; it feeds and clothes them. They cannot disregard their consciences towards it. We need not consider conscience in that way.” “What should we consider, then?” “The law. To obey the court’s laws is to satisfy our conscience towards the court.” Zhang nodded and drank in thought. After a long while, he spoke with pleasure. “Old Hu, I think I understand at last. For years I have wondered: among the four callings—scholar, farmer, craftsman and merchant—why does one hear of dishonest merchants, but not dishonest scholars, farmers or craftsmen? Evidently, a merchant’s conscience needs a different explanation. I could never work out what it was. Now I understand. Obey the court’s laws and treat customers fairly: that is conscience, and that keeps one from being a dishonest merchant.” “Exactly! To speak plainly, if merchants obeyed the court’s laws and officials were conscientious towards it, the world would certainly be at peace. More than that: if officials were conscientious, merchants would not dare disobey the law. When officials have no conscience towards the court, to demand that merchants supply it is mere pedantry.” “Yes, yes. Let me think that through.” Zhang spoke as he reflected. “Suppose a Longhair leader is captured and his household’s property confiscated. An official stifles his conscience and pockets the property. That is acting without conscience. But if we act conscientiously? A Longhair offers a deposit under an assumed name, and we refuse because the money ought to be confiscated. What is the result? We simply benefit a corrupt official, who pockets it all the same. Exactly!” He slapped the table and raised his voice. “It is no use for merchants alone to be conscientious towards the court. Obeying the law is enough!” “Old Zhang!” Hu too raised his cup with pleasure. “Now you have truly understood.”

They thoroughly enjoyed their drinking, and in the end Zhang insisted on paying. Before they parted, Hu gave him a particular reminder: he wanted the bank and A’xiang’s affairs settled during the next few days, before his family reached Shanghai. Once they were reunited after their ordeals, he meant to spend proper time with his aged mother. Even the most pressing business would have to be put aside. Zhang agreed repeatedly. On arriving home, he first consulted his wife about winding up their little grocery. She had some judgement of her own. His account made her both happy and wistful. Going into the bedroom, she opened a chest and took out a packet of soft cotton paper. She unwrapped it to show him a cheap vine bracelet mounted in silver. Her husband was puzzled. What could it have to do with their discussion? But Mrs Zhang wished to tell an old story. “This bracelet is Xueyan’s. It was this that showed me he would prosper. It happened before he met Governor Wang. He had lost his job at the bank and was having a very hard time. One day he told me that a good friend had come from Jinhua to seek work in Hangzhou and fallen ill at an inn. He owed a fortnight’s room and board and still needed a doctor. Without five taels he could not get through. Could I help? Though Xueyan was down in the world, he did not have the air of a beaten man. His cotton gown was faded and patched, but washed and starched as neatly as ever. That showed he had a capable, virtuous wife to support him. For that reason, I lent him five taels without a moment’s hesitation.” “Really!” Zhang was fascinated. “There was such a story, and you never told me! Did he repay you?”

“Do not interrupt. Listen!” Mrs Zhang continued. “Xueyan told me, ‘I am in poor circumstances now. I do not know when I can repay these five taels, but I certainly shall.’ To be honest, since I was willing to lend, I naturally did not expect repayment at once. So I said, ‘Never mind. Repay me when you have it.’ Then he pulled this vine bracelet off his arm and put it in my hand. ‘It is not worth even one tael,’ he said. ‘It cannot count as security. But it belonged to my mother, and I value it greatly. This will remind me not to forget to repay what I owe.’ I refused it, but he absolutely would not take it back, so he left it here.” “That does not sound like Xueyan. He always keeps his word.” “You think that because the bracelet is still here, he never repaid the five taels? Not at all! He paid them back long ago.” “When?” “Soon after his luck turned, when Governor Wang was appointed to Zhejiang.” “Then why do you still have the bracelet?”

“That is what you cannot help admiring about him. He brought a red envelope containing a banknote for five taels, with four kinds of modest gifts besides. When I offered the bracelet, he refused it. Five taels now, he said, were nothing like five taels then. The kindness he owed me had not been repaid. He wanted me to keep the bracelet and come to him if I ever had a difficulty. Only after he had helped me would he accept it back. I thought his mother must be wearing gold and jade by then and would hardly miss a vine bracelet. It seemed of little consequence, so I kept it. When he did you that great favour, I took four gifts to visit him, specially to return it. Again he refused.” “What was his reason?” Zhang was more interested than ever. “I should like to hear what he had to say this time.” “He said he had helped you out of loyalty to a fellow banker. Besides, a man’s business outside the home did not concern his wife. He could not cross off his debt to me against that account. I was to keep the bracelet. One day he would do something to please me, and only then would my kindness be repaid.” “There is sense in that. This is what makes Xueyan such a fine fellow. He plainly does you a favour and makes you feel comfortable about it too. But enough talk. Let us consider how to hand over this grocery.” Zhang frowned. “Small as a sparrow, but with all its organs intact! All the little sums owing to us and owing from us—it will give me days of headaches to sort them out.” “Headaches? Why should it? Everything is in the books. Transfer the premises, the stock and the accounts in one lot. We shall abdicate and surrender our little kingdom, hand everything over and brush ourselves off as we walk away. What could be easier?” Zhang was delighted. “Exactly! You are the decisive one. When Xueyan asks tomorrow how much I want for the shop, I shall say that I started it with sixteen hundred silver dollars and will take sixteen hundred for it.” This accorded perfectly with his wife’s wishes. In those few moments, they decided to part with the business they had built for three or four years, and neither felt any reluctance. Their attachment to the old trade was deeply rooted, after all, and he would be working with Hu Xueyan. Beside that prospect, the little grocery was no chicken rib, too poor to relish but too good to throw away. It was a worn-out shoe, to be discarded without a thought.

CHAPTER SEVEN

On rising early, Fatty Zhang followed the habit of many years and took his birdcage to the teahouse. Sometimes, passing Mr Wei’s shop, he would stop to greet a fellow tradesman. Wei was industrious and frugal and never went to teahouses, but that morning Zhang was determined to take him out for tea: the proposed marriage could hardly be discussed in A’qiao’s presence. Entering the shop, he stated his immediate purpose plainly. Wei was taken by surprise and inclined to decline. Just then A’qiao brought her father breakfast, a bowl of soy milk and a ball of glutinous rice. Seeing their visitor, she greeted him sweetly. “Good morning, Uncle Zhang! Have you had breakfast?”

Zhang did not answer at once. He looked her over from head to foot, very much as though appraising a prospective bride, until she grew embarrassed. Since he had not yet answered her greeting, however, she could hardly turn and leave. She merely averted her face from his fixed gaze. “A’qiao,” he asked, “how old are you this year?” “Seventeen.” “Your birthday is naturally the seventh day of the seventh month. At what hour were you born?” That startled her. What could a man mean by calling first thing in the morning and asking for her birth details except that he had come to arrange a match? Her thoughts flew at once to A’xiang, and she panicked. “Who asked you to come matchmaking and ruin my life?” she thought. Hurrying to the back, she found her mother. “What has Fatty Zhang come for so early?” “Which Fatty Zhang?”

“Who else? The one in the same trade as Father—his rival!” “Has he come? I did not know.” “Mother!” A’qiao clutched her clothes. “He is up to something. First he asks my birthday, then my birth hour. I—” She stamped her foot. “I am not getting married! No one need come meddling.” Her mother, however, became interested at once. What sort of young man had Zhang found for her daughter? “Do not make a fuss yet. Let me ask.”

Her mother’s interest disappointed and alarmed A’qiao. Surely she knew that her daughter’s heart was already given. Her ordinary manner toward A’xiang had long hinted that she regarded him as half a son. She therefore knew whom A’qiao wanted and approved of him herself. Why, then, should she still want to “ask”? Was she growing foolish and unreasonable in her old age? The trouble was that A’qiao could say none of this aloud, nor could she think of any hint that would remind her. In desperation she could only behave like a spoiled child, clinging to her mother’s clothes. “Do not ask! No ivory comes from a dog’s mouth. There is nothing worth asking him.” “What harm can it do? Why are you behaving like this?” Mother and daughter tugged at each other, delaying and reaching no conclusion. Meanwhile, Wei had found it impossible to refuse Zhang’s invitation. From the front he raised his voice. “A’qiao’s mother! Come and mind the shop. I am going out for tea with Mr Zhang.” Now A’qiao was more anxious than ever. She had meant her mother to call her father in and warn him not to listen if Zhang proposed a match. Instead, they had wasted precious time without making the important point clear. Naturally, if the men went out for tea, they would discuss the marriage. Marriage was said to be decided by both parents, but seven-tenths of the authority lay with her father. If he carelessly swallowed Zhang’s honeyed words at the teahouse, she would have cause for resentment all her life. Her thoughts raced like lightning. “Father, come inside!” she blurted out. “Mother has something important to say.” Wei returned, and his wife asked, “Has Fatty Zhang come to arrange a match for A’qiao?”

Before he could answer, A’qiao broke in. “Who asked him to arrange any match? I am not getting married.” “Eh?” Wei looked from his wife to his daughter in genuine bewilderment. “How did you come to think of that?” Quick-eared and quick-witted, A’qiao brightened at once. “Then what has he come for?” “He says he wants to discuss a piece of business.” “Business?” his wife asked. “Can he not discuss it in the shop?” “That is what I said. But he says he must have his morning tea or he has no energy. There is no harm in my joining him for once.” “Very well!” A’qiao gave her father a little push. “Off you go, then. But you may discuss business only—nothing else.”

Two hours passed, and he did not return. Uneasy, A’qiao sent the little apprentice to make a discreet inquiry at the teahouse Zhang visited every day. The boy soon came back: neither man was there. This looked suspicious. By noon there was still no sign of Wei. Mother and daughter waited a long time, then decided to eat without him. They had just picked up their chopsticks when he appeared, his face flushed and wreathed in smiles. A’qiao was relieved but reproachful. “Where have you been? You did not even come home for lunch.” “Fatty Zhang invited me for a drink. A thoroughly enjoyable drink it was too.” “What was so enjoyable? Did you settle the business? What business was it?” “We discussed more than business. There was something else—something important.” Wei sat down, smiling. “You guessed correctly. Zhang did come to arrange our daughter’s marriage.” A’qiao’s hands and feet turned cold. She threw herself against her mother’s shoulder and shook from head to foot.

Both parents were startled and alarmed. They asked what was wrong, but she seemed unwilling to explain. With an effort she sat down again and stared at her father. No one knew a daughter better than her mother, who at last guessed what was troubling her. “You must not agree carelessly to a match Fatty Zhang proposes,” she told her husband urgently. “Why should I not agree?” “You have agreed already? What sort of family is it? What is the bridegroom like?” “Why should I tell you what he is like? You see him every day.”

The first person A’qiao thought of was Runsheng, the young assistant at the fruit shop next door. He worked hard and was quite presentable. He also had a remarkable skill: if a customer bought a pear and wanted it peeled, Runsheng would set two thin-backed, flat-tipped fruit knives whirling in his hands. In the blink of an eye the pear was bare, its skin removed in one long strip. A’qiao loved to watch him do it, and he loved to see her gazing at him with a smile. Once he had become so absorbed in looking at her that his hand slipped and he sliced off a small piece of his finger. The whole street had made a joke of it. To avoid further gossip, she had kept out of his way ever since. But the shops were neighbors, so they could not help seeing each other daily, and his employer thought highly of him. It was quite possible that he had asked for her. The second person she thought of was the son of the incense-and-candle shop owner opposite. He was handsome, and their families were well matched, but he could never remove his cap: his scalp was diseased and patchy. The thought repelled her, and she hurriedly cast him from her mind.

Then she thought of A’xiang. Her face flushed and her heart beat wildly. She could not bring herself to ask, but fortunately her mother could. “Who is it?” she asked her husband. “Who else? A’xiang, naturally.” The name had scarcely left his lips when A’qiao sprang up and fled into the back, her feet light as air. Wei stared for a moment, then burst into laughter. “What are you laughing at? Tell us! How did A’xiang come to ask Fatty Zhang to act as matchmaker? What did he say, and what did you answer? Begin at the beginning.” As the story unfolded, everyone—including A’qiao, listening from the other side of the wall—was entirely satisfied, so happy that tears seemed near. All shared the same thought: A’xiang had found a benefactor in an employer like Circuit Intendant Hu. Hu himself, however, could not attend to A’xiang’s affairs just then. Another A’qiao was giving him a headache.

Sister A’qiao had been out all night and had not returned until nine that morning. Asked where she had been, she said she had felt depressed and spent the night talking at the home of an old friend. These “young companions” of hers were all over thirty now. They had either left the profession to marry or become proprietresses—in other words, madams. If she had visited a woman who had married into a respectable household, the family would hardly have kept an unaccompanied visitor overnight; someone would have escorted her home the evening before. Her movements therefore looked suspicious. Without betraying his thoughts, Hu sent A’xiang to investigate. She had not used the household sedan chair, but he did not imagine she had walked. A’xiang accordingly questioned the bearers waiting for hire at the mouth of the lane. He found what he wanted. She had gone to Zhaorong Lane, north of Baoshan Street. The bearer remembered setting her down at a stone-framed doorway, the second house from the far end. As the saying went, “Zhaorong and Zhaofu, with Gongxing close beside—all are roads to the pleasure quarters.” Those lanes were famous haunts of luxurious dissipation. Why had A’qiao dismissed her attendants and visited them secretly? It was deeply suspicious.

Only the Seventh Lady could solve the mystery. Hu counted the days: this was the fourth day since she had undertaken to furnish his new home, the day agreed upon for him to inspect it. He therefore took his chair directly to Huajin Lane instead of Gu’s house. Sure enough, the rooms had been repainted and looked entirely new. The Seventh Lady herself was directing the servants as they arranged brand-new rosewood furniture. It was late in the third month; brilliant sunshine filled the courtyard, and the weather was quite warm. Her face was flushed as if she had been drinking, her forehead beaded with sweat, and her hair coming loose. Plainly she had worked hard. Hu felt deeply indebted and made a profound bow of thanks. Her answer was graceful. “There is no need to thank me, Little Uncle. Your honored mother and my aunt—your wife—are coming. We of the younger generation ought to show our filial respect.” She led him through the house, room by room. Not only were the family apartments arranged in perfect order; even the servants’ quarters had been supplied with everything necessary. Such care left the owner nothing to do but praise it without reservation. After making the circuit, they returned to the drawing room to rest over tea. Only then did Hu explain A’qiao’s movements the previous night and ask for advice.

The matter was so unexpected that the Seventh Lady had no ready answer. She considered his account from beginning to end and decided that a few points must first be clarified. “Little Uncle, how did she behave toward you when she came back? Did she complain?” “No. She was very cold.” “Did she pack any clothes or valuables, as though she meant to move out?” “No. She just sat there picking at her nails and thinking, as if I were not in the room.” Those two questions were enough. The Seventh Lady nodded slowly, almost talking to herself. “That fits. That must be her plan.” The exchange had revived Hu’s own recollections, and he too had begun to understand, though he would rather have been wrong. “Seventh Sister,” he asked painfully, “can she have arranged with her old companions to appear in public again—to open a courtesan establishment of her own?” A curse escaped him. “The shameless woman!”

“Little Uncle, there I must take her side.” The Seventh Lady’s nature asserted itself; righteous indignation showed in her face. “A person must find some settled place in life. She is willing to accept a subordinate position, but she feels that, having come from outside respectable society and been accustomed to freedom, she could not bear the restrictions of a great household. She wants to live separately. That is not an excessive request. If it cannot be granted, she must find another way. Is it not natural to return to the place she came from? Even if she wishes to marry, she can hardly summon a matchmaker and say, ‘I want a husband; find me one.’ She could open an establishment without taking clients herself. If she meets a suitable man who understands her, they can decide their own future. That would be a proper course.” After this scolding, Hu grew calmer. He smiled. “If that is what she means to do, she ought to have consulted you first.” “She has not consulted me. In my heart I might not oppose it, but I could hardly say aloud that I approved of her returning to that pit of fire. She is a clever woman. Why should she give me a hint? What worries me now is that she may resent you and deliberately damage your reputation afterward.” “How?” Hu smiled bitterly. “If she goes back into that life at all, my reputation will be damaged enough.”

“Not as badly as it could be. Suppose tomorrow she puts up a sign saying ‘The Hu Residence of Hangzhou.’ Then you will have something to look at!” Hu was stunned. He too had heard how, during the past year or two, the flourishing pleasure trade in the foreign settlement had developed a new device for fleecing wealthy fools. A provincial rich man, newly ventured among the flowers, would find his eyes dazzled by jewels and beauty, his nose intoxicated by powder and perfume, and his ears drowned in music. At the banquet and beneath the quilts, the woman would lavish tender, clinging affection upon him. When their feelings had ripened and marriage was mentioned, she would explain that she was lonely and without family, had long been weary of her life, and was held back only by debts incurred in the profession. If he paid those debts, she would belong to his household. She asked for nothing else.

The gullible patron would pay, have her signboard taken down, and bring her out of the profession. Once installed as a respectable wife, however, she would gradually become restless at home, making endless demands. Nothing could fill the gulf of her greed. Any slight disappointment brought a change of face; minor quarrels every three days and major ones every five left the entire household distraught. Only then would the victim realize how thoroughly he had been duped. But regret came too late. He would have to pay another sum to be rid of her. The trick was known as “taking a cleansing bath.” If, having washed away her debts, she left his household and broke off all dealings, she still had some conscience. Other hardened foxes were more ruthless. Returning to the profession after marriage, they refused to release their victim. They received new and familiar patrons alike under his surname, even recounting over dinner what married life in his house had been like, or exposing its private secrets and scandals. More money would then be needed to purchase peace.

Surely A’qiao would not be so heartless. “What good would it do her?” Hu asked. “She is an exceptionally reasonable woman. Why would she harm another person without benefiting herself?” “That is a very fair point. She ought not to be that sort of person. Now that matters have reached this stage, they are easier to settle. Leave it to me, Little Uncle. I guarantee that I shall arrange everything properly. And do not go back for the next couple of days. Stay at my house or at the bank, whichever you please. Only do not see A’qiao.” Hu could not fathom her plan. He knew that questions would be useless; the only course was to put the matter aside and let her manage it. “I shall stay at the bank. I have engaged Fatty Zhang as a senior clerk. These two days will give me time to sit with him at the shop and discuss future business.” “Zhang is a dependable man. Very well. Attend to your business. If anything arises, I shall find you at Fukang.”

That afternoon the Seventh Lady went to see an old intimate of Brother You’s, the Second Miss of Yiqing House. Years earlier, when the Songjiang Grain-Boat Guild had fallen on hard times and its members could scarcely make a living, how could its leader establish a gilded nest for a favorite? However often the Second Miss urged him, Brother You had refused to redeem her from the profession. Angry, she had chosen another man, a sickly son of an impoverished distinguished family. He died within two years. She felt no obligation to remain a faithful widow; indeed, his principal wife would not tolerate her. She therefore returned to the pleasure quarters, first as a courtesan and later as a proprietress. Her ties with Brother You had never quite been severed and continued to this day.

A’qiao had once worked in the Second Miss’s establishment. The Seventh Lady sought her out partly to learn the truth about A’qiao’s supposed return to the trade and partly to enlist her old friendship as a means of mediation. But as a respectable married woman, she could not enter a courtesan house without compromising Gu’s reputation. She went instead to a familiar Western restaurant and asked a waiter to invite the Second Miss to meet her there. They had not seen each other for more than half a year. Their affection had always been strong, and they clasped hands warmly, with endless inquiries and greetings. After asking about the Seventh Lady’s family and Brother You, the Second Miss also asked after Hu, giving her visitor just the opening she needed. “It is on Little Uncle’s account that I want to consult you today. A’qiao and Mr Hu are going to part.” “Why?” the Second Miss asked in surprise. “Why can they not get along?” “There is really no quarrel between them—” The Seventh Lady explained that Hu’s family had escaped and was coming to Shanghai, then described A’qiao’s wishes. Throughout, she emphasized that the separation was settled and the relationship could not be saved. The Second Miss listened attentively, looking puzzled. “A’qiao and I meet every month or two. Why has she not discussed something so important with me? What will she do after leaving Mr Hu? She cannot return to Suzhou, and she is not used to country life.” “Exactly,” the Seventh Lady agreed. “Whatever she does, our friendship remains. Even Mr Hu is concerned for her. A woman alone must have somewhere suitable to settle. She seems to have made up her mind, but I do not think her plan is wise. Second Sister, do you know whether she has any close friends in Zhaofu Lane?”

The Second Miss thought for a moment. “Yes. Before she came to my establishment, she worked for the Sixth Miss of Xinxianghong House. She was very friendly with A’jin, who worked there too. I know A’jin as well. She lives in Zhaofu Lane now and keeps a handsome young lover.” “What does she do nowadays?” “She runs an establishment herself.” “Then I may have guessed correctly.” The Seventh Lady described A’qiao’s secret visit to Zhaofu Lane and explained her inference that she had gone to discuss following the same path. “How strange! Why did she not consult me?” “That is a fair question, Second Sister. But let me ask you: if A’qiao wanted to do such a thing, would you approve?” “How could I? Anyone who can avoid earning a living that way should do so.” “There you have it. She knew you would not be enthusiastic. Why should she consult you?” “That is true.” The Second Miss was still puzzled. “I simply cannot understand why she should return to that wretched livelihood.”

That, the Seventh Lady felt, was precisely what they must discuss. If A’qiao intended to return to the pleasure quarters, there could be only three motives: the need to support herself, revenge upon Hu, or a wish to use the place to meet men and find a dependable partner for life. Having analyzed the possibilities, she gave her own view. “First, she need not worry about money. She told me herself that she has twenty or thirty thousand taels put away. Besides, Mr Hu will certainly give her another sum when they part. As for revenge, there is no deep hatred between them. To disgrace him, she would first have to disgrace herself. She is not so foolish. The more I think of it, the more I can see only one explanation: she wants to choose a suitable patron to marry.” “Go back into that life in order to find a husband? I have never heard of such a thing.” The Second Miss shook her head vigorously. “Unless she means to follow A’jin’s example and keep some handsome young man in a little house. She will never find a good patron to marry that way.” Her words could be understood in two senses: she still did not believe that A’qiao would adopt such a poor plan, and she certainly disapproved of it. Either way, the Seventh Lady found them reassuring.

“I too can hardly believe that A’qiao would take that road. But let us speak plainly, Second Sister. I am trying both to help Little Uncle and to do what is best for her. For Brother Five’s sake, you must help me with this.” The Second Miss smiled in embarrassment. “You deserve a penalty for saying that! Would I ever refuse something you asked? Why must you bring Fifth Master into it?” “I spoke wrongly. Do not be angry. Let us discuss the real business. I already had a plan—” The Seventh Lady intended to transplant the blossom onto another branch. She had specially asked Mr Qin at the firm to write to Dr Zhang in Ningbo, hoping to arrange a marriage between him and A’qiao. There was a long story behind it. The Second Miss listened from the beginning, learning how they had met and how the doctor, whose feelings had found no response, had returned home disappointed. She felt considerable sympathy for him.

“I approve of your plan. But whether it can succeed is another matter. Between a man and a woman, everything depends on affinity, and A’qiao does not seem to have any with him.” “That is not it. Little Uncle stood between them. Her whole heart was fixed on him, so however good the doctor might be, she would never consider him. The doctor, for his part, saw that she already belonged to someone and withdrew before the obstacle. As I see it, if she is unwilling to become a concubine, she could do no better than marry him. First, his wife has recently died. Given his devotion to A’qiao, he would be willing to take her as his new principal wife. Second, he is not very old. How old is A’qiao now?” “She was born in the year of the Sheep. This year—” The Second Miss counted on her fingers, then exclaimed in surprise. “Exactly forty!” “She looks young; one would never guess forty. But forty she is.” The Seventh Lady paused, then added apologetically, “Do not be offended, Second Sister, but a woman of forty, with her background, cannot easily become a man’s principal wife.” “Not easily? One could hardly find such a match with a lantern. Tell me seriously: how certain are you of the doctor?” “Six or seven chances in ten, at least.” “That is fairly certain. I shall see A’qiao today and ask what she means to do. If she has no intention of returning to the trade, so much the better. If she has, I must stop her. Whatever she intends, I shall act as matchmaker.” “You will represent the bride, and I the groom. We must make it succeed. It will be a good deed.”

“A good deed, certainly. Though it seems rather unfair to the doctor.” At that, the Seventh Lady remembered how, before she married Gu, Hu had arranged for Wang Youling’s mother to adopt her as a daughter. Inspiration came at once. “Since you feel that way, Second Sister, let us consider how to raise A’qiao’s standing.” Her plan was to ask Hu’s mother to adopt A’qiao. Hu would then take the part of a brother marrying off his sister, receiving the honors due to the bride’s brother. Such arrangements had often been made in former times. In their present circumstances, it seemed a particularly considerate and reasonable solution. The Second Miss naturally approved and rejoiced for A’qiao: through this, she thought, her long journey would reach a proper fulfillment.

The Seventh Lady herself was delighted with the idea. Impatient by nature and now carried away by enthusiasm, she wanted to take the Second Miss straight to A’qiao and settle the whole matter face to face. The Second Miss was more cautious. She thought they should first see A’jin, establish the facts, and invite her to join them; A’qiao would then be more easily persuaded. “Very well. Let us go and see A’jin.” “Well—” The Second Miss knew that A’jin disliked unfamiliar visitors because she kept a young lover, but she could hardly say so outright. She changed tack. “A woman of your standing should not go there. I shall send for her.” She called the young maid who had accompanied her and sent her hurrying to Zhaofu Lane, with particular instructions to hire an ordinary street carriage and bring A’jin back in it without delay. While they waited, the two women naturally talked of their own affairs. The Second Miss sounded weary of life in the pleasure quarters, yet said nothing of choosing a man to marry. Quick in both thought and speech, the Seventh Lady heard what lay beneath her words and could not resist offering candid advice.

“Second Sister, do not fix your mind on a single course and refuse to turn aside. That is too foolishly devoted. If you go on waiting for Brother Five, you may wait until your hair is white and still achieve nothing. He must have told you why. He leads a whole guild and must act in ways that earn its members’ respect. While they are too poor to feed themselves, how could he justify setting up another household? Give up that hope.” The Second Miss had no answer. Silent and tearful, she turned away to wipe her eyes. The Seventh Lady too felt an ache of unhappiness and regretted having spoken so plainly. “Truly,” she said, searching for something to ease their mutual embarrassment, “that doctor is a good man, and comfortably off. Why did I not think of it? I ought to have arranged a match for you long ago.” “Thank you. But I was born to ill fortune and took up this wretched livelihood. I cannot even become a concubine. What more is there to say?” Her words still held resentment, and the usually eloquent Seventh Lady could find nothing to add.

Fortunately, Zhaofu Lane was nearby. The carriage soon returned with A’jin. The maid had explained matters on the way, so A’jin needed no introduction before greeting the Seventh Lady courteously. “You are the Seventh Lady of the You family? What a fine-looking woman you are!” “You are too kind.” The Seventh Lady turned to her companion. “Then this must be Sister A’jin?” “Yes.” Acting as hostess, the Second Miss ordered food and drink for the newcomer, then came straight to the point. “The Seventh Lady is concerned about A’qiao and has specially asked you here. Has A’qiao been to see you these past few days?” “She comes to see me often.” “Sister A’jin,” the Seventh Lady said, “we have not met before, but Second Sister knows me: I am frank and speak my mind. If I say anything clumsy, please do not take offense.” A’jin and the Second Miss had seemed at odds as soon as A’qiao was mentioned. The Seventh Lady feared that a collision would accomplish nothing and only damage their friendship, so she made this precautionary apology. A’jin too had long experience of the world and understood its ways. She knew that her stiff answer had carried a hint of hostility and had been ill judged. “Please do not say that. I am clumsy with words. Second Sister and I are old friends, so I do not stand on ceremony with her. You must not misunderstand me.” With both sides showing humility, further explanations would only suggest that there really was a quarrel. Still, as a new acquaintance, the Seventh Lady could hardly press certain questions herself. The Second Miss would be better placed to ask them. She smiled and gave her a meaningful glance.

The Second Miss nodded and explained calmly to A’jin. “A’qiao and Mr Hu have fallen out. Even a wise magistrate cannot settle family troubles, so there is no need to decide who is right or wrong. In short, they will probably separate. The Seventh Lady has always been close to A’qiao and helped bring them together in the first place. Naturally, she cannot be indifferent to what happens now. She came specially to ask me, but I know nothing. I have not seen A’qiao for some time. Hearing that she had visited you in the last few days, I thought she must have told you something. What does she intend to do?” “Oh.” A’jin did not answer immediately. Instead, she turned to the Seventh Lady. “How are things really between A’qiao and Mr Hu?” “Not bad.”

“Then it is strange.” A’jin looked puzzled. “Whenever she comes, she complains of her unhappy fate. I ask whether Mr Hu treats her well, but she only shakes her head and refuses to say. From the look of things—” She left the next words unspoken, though their meaning was easy to guess. Now the Seventh Lady was puzzled in turn. “Why should she give that impression? This talk of parting is very recent. Mr Hu’s family is coming to Shanghai, and his principal wife will not allow him to keep a separate household. A’qiao refuses to enter the family home, so they are at an impasse. Before this, I could see no sign of disagreement between them.” A’jin nodded. “Let us leave that aside.” Her face grew gloomy. “Perhaps I am to blame. I have a great-aunt on my father’s side, from a collateral branch of the family. She is now abbess of White-Robed Convent in Fahua Town. Whenever she comes to Shanghai, she visits me. Sometimes she has met A’qiao here, and they have talked with great enthusiasm. The old abbess is always saying that suffering in this life comes from a failure to cultivate virtue in the last, and urging her to prepare for a better life to come. It was only the usual talk of a religious person. Who could have thought A’qiao would become so absorbed in it?” Only then did the Seventh Lady realize that her guesses had been entirely wrong. A’qiao’s visit might have concerned the abbess, not the opening of a courtesan establishment. “What do you mean, absorbed?” she asked urgently. “It is quite unexpected. The other day she asked me for the address of White-Robed Convent. I told her, then asked why she wanted it. At first she would not say. When I pressed her, she finally admitted that she wanted to enter the convent and leave the world.” The Seventh Lady’s face changed with shock. “Become a nun?”

“Who can tell?” A’jin answered unhappily. “I spent the whole night trying to persuade her out of it, but she never gave me a definite answer. No one can guess whether she changed her mind.” “I do not think she will do it,” the Second Miss said calmly. “For all these years she has been accustomed to comfort and has never lived a hard life. She could not endure the austerity of a convent for a single day. As I see it—” She stopped. To go on would have been unkind. The Seventh Lady too thought A’qiao might not take that road. Yet even as she comforted herself with this thought, another worry arose. It was a relief that A’qiao did not intend to return to the pleasure quarters. But such a drastic gesture also showed that she would not release her hold upon Hu. There seemed to be plenty of trouble still ahead. “What are we to do now?” she sighed. “I have no more ideas.” In all their years of friendship, the Second Miss had never seen her so helpless. Partly for her sake and partly for A’qiao’s, she felt bound to make an effort.

“A’qiao will not shave her head and become a nun. She is only disheartened. We all want what is best for her, and we must find her a way forward.” The Second Miss turned to A’jin. “She is exactly forty this year. At that age, what future has she in this world of ours? The Seventh Lady is proposing a marriage—” When A’jin had heard the story of Dr Zhang, she too grew excited. “With such a proper ending in prospect, why become a nun? How good of the Seventh Lady to take such trouble! We are A’qiao’s old companions and ought to work even harder. Bringing this match about would be a true deed of merit. There is no use sitting here talking. Let us go and see her now. I refuse to believe that the three of us cannot persuade one woman.”

Their enthusiasm restored the Seventh Lady’s confidence. After a little thought she asked, “Sister A’jin, Second Sister, are you determined to help A’qiao?” “Naturally,” the Second Miss replied, “if we can.” “Good. Then listen to me. Matters settle more smoothly when one takes time. And with sufficient effort, an iron pestle can be ground into a needle. From today, let Mr Hu stop seeing her altogether. We shall first divert her thoughts and make her forget that such a person exists. That cannot be done in two or three days, which is why I had to ask you first. If we truly mean to help, we must devote time to it. Beginning today, the three of us will keep her company constantly. Plays, storytelling, Western dinners, carriage rides, foreign circuses—wherever there is something enjoyable, we shall take her. If she refuses, we shall say that we want to go. People care about their friends’ feelings. She will be too embarrassed to refuse and too considerate to spoil everyone’s pleasure with a gloomy face. At night we shall take turns staying with her, both to watch over her and to talk and ease her mind. After fifteen or twenty days of that, her mood will change. Then we can mention Dr Zhang, and success will be much easier. As for the cost of these outings, let me boast a little: I can still afford it. I shall pay for everything.” A’jin drew a deep breath. “Second Sister, with such generosity, the Seventh Lady has said all there is to say. We can only follow her lead. But we cannot let her bear all the expense.” “Of course not.” The Second Miss turned to the Seventh Lady. “We shall do everything as you say, except on that point. Do not argue. We shall take turns as hostess, and today is mine. Come, let us invite her out to see the foreign circus.” She signed for the bill, putting a personal flourish on the account the waiter brought, a mark intelligible only to herself. This was a Western custom called “signing,” an acknowledgment of the debt. Ordinarily one wrote one’s name; an illiterate person could make any distinctive mark, provided the proper form was observed.

Each woman had brought a young maid. They crowded into two hired street carriages and drove straight to A’qiao’s lodgings. On getting out, they immediately sensed something wrong. The front door stood half open, but no one answered. The Seventh Lady raised her voice. “A’xiang! A’fu!” Neither appeared. Instead, someone hurried down the stairs, her long braid swinging, her face full of alarm. It was Suxiang, A’qiao’s maid.

The three women exchanged glances. Each guessed what had happened. In such a situation the Seventh Lady remained calm and even comforted the maid. “Suxiang, do not be frightened. Tell us slowly.” “Madam has disappeared!” Suxiang said, almost in tears. “No one knows where she has gone.” Asked to speak slowly, she still began in the middle of the story. The Seventh Lady had to question her. “How do you know she has disappeared? When did she leave?” “Not long after Master went out, she sent me to buy silk thread in Xiangfen Lane and sent A’xiang to order rice and firewood. When we came back, she had already gone. No one knew when she had left, not even the man at the front door. Then we looked at the back door and found it half open. By three this afternoon she still had not returned. I went into her room: a small jewelry box was missing, and quite a few changes of clothes. That—that—” Too anxious to explain, Suxiang faltered. There was no need to say it: she must have gone to the elderly abbess. The Seventh Lady drew in a sharp breath and stared at her companions. The Second Miss asked, “Suxiang, does your master know?” “No. A’xiang and the chairmen have gone to look for him.” “Your master is at the bank,” the Seventh Lady said. “See whether any of the chairmen are still here. Send someone to tell him at once. Ask him to come here; I have something important to say.”

At that moment Hu arrived, accompanied by Xiao Jiaji. He knew the Second Miss well but had never met A’jin. There was no time for inquiries; indeed, the presence of a stranger made him all the more determined to remain composed and keep his domestic troubles from becoming public. He merely nodded and asked quietly, “How do you two come to be here as well?” “We happened to meet,” the Seventh Lady replied. “Let us talk inside.” In the sitting room she introduced A’jin. They were approaching matters that Suxiang and A’xiang ought not to hear, so she asked to speak with Hu privately. “I know where A’qiao has gone—to a convent in Fahua Town. We must lose no time; someone must go for her at once. I think—” She hesitated. “It had better be just Sister A’jin and me. You ought not to meet her.” Hu was utterly bewildered. “What has happened? How do you know where she is? And who is this Sister A’jin?” “There is no time to explain everything now. Little Uncle, just arrange for us to get to Fahua.”

“The whole Fahua district is occupied by Anhui Army troops from Anqing. I do not even know whether we can get through.” “That does not matter,” Xiao said. “I shall go.” “Excellent! You are the best person.” The Seventh Lady was delighted. Xiao knew the Anhui Army commanders well, and his presence would make many things easier. “Seventh Sister, I still think I ought to go,” Hu said. “I need not see her, but at least let her know that I am not indifferent. What do you think?” “I was afraid you would quarrel if you met and make matters impossible to resolve. Since you put it that way, Little Uncle, there is no harm in your going.” It was dusk when they reached Fahua Town. Xiao sought out a battalion commander named Zhu, who served under the Anhui Army general Cheng Xueqi. A frank and helpful man, Zhu heard their errand, gave them a meal, then ordered a subordinate officer, a bazong, to fetch the local constable, Old Hu, and explain what was needed.

“Certainly, certainly. I shall show the way,” Old Hu said. “The three of you, please follow me.” They headed east in the moonlight and soon turned into a lane. At its far end stood a residence behind a whitewashed wall, with luxuriant flowers and trees inside. In the faint light of the new moon, the shapes were indistinct. A warm breeze carried out the rich scent of night-blooming flowers. Everyone felt suddenly refreshed, yet for no reason their hearts grew restless, filled with a swelling emotion they could not name. Xiao felt it most strongly. “What is this place?” he could not help asking. “Here? White-Robed Convent. At night we use the side entrance.” The side entrance had a solid wooden door. Above it, within reach, was an inconspicuous flattened iron ring. Old Hu reached up and pulled it twice. Clang, clang. After a little while came footsteps; the door opened a crack, and someone asked, “Who is it?” “Xiaoyin, it is me.” “Oh. Old Hu, what brings you here at this hour?” “Can you not see the visitors?” He pointed behind him. “Tell Sister Liaochen that I have brought them.”

The door swung open with a creak. By the light they saw Xiaoyin, a girl in ordinary secular dress with her hair hanging loose. Once the visitors were inside, she shut the door and led them forward without a word, along a flower-lined path and through two covered passages to a monastic room apparently used for receiving guests. There she stopped and looked at Old Hu. He hesitated a little, then bowed to the officer. “Sir, shall I keep you company here for a while?” There was scarcely any need to ask; the officer naturally agreed. Old Hu whispered a few words to Xiaoyin, then motioned Hu to follow her. Beyond the room lay a large courtyard. As they turned into the western covered walk, their shadows mingled with those of the flowers upon the whitewashed wall. There was also the shadow of a cat, arching its back and yowling for a mate on the roof to the east. Xiao nudged Hu gently with his elbow and murmured a couplet: “A winding path leads into seclusion; the monks’ quarters lie deep among flowers and trees.” Hu too could see that something about this convent was unusual. But Xiao’s behavior verged on frivolity and was scarcely respectful in a Buddhist sanctuary. He coughed to warn him to restrain himself. They followed Xiaoyin silently into another courtyard, planted with trees and facing an elegant three-bay retreat. Sandalwood incense mingled with the scent of flowers. Xiao exclaimed involuntarily, “What a refined place!” “Please sit inside,” Xiaoyin said, lifting the curtain to admit them. “I shall fetch Sister Liaochen.” Then she went off on her own.

Entering, the two men saw a white porcelain Guanyin enshrined at the upper end of the room. To the east stood a row of tables and chairs of unpainted cypress; to the west, an enormous wooden couch covered with padded Sichuan brocade. Flowers glowed in vases, incense curled from a burner, and an ancient qin completed an exceptionally tasteful arrangement. Yet none of these things caught Xiao’s attention as much as the horizontal scroll hanging above the couch.

“Mr Hu!” Xiao sounded excited. “Look!” The scroll contained three poems. Hu could at least make out where the lines began and ended: “At leisure I knock at the Buddhist gate to visit a moonlike beauty; / Pine and creepers cover the ritual altar and the garden of herbs. / A courtyard of osmanthus flowers falls to greet the visitor; / Books and paintings fill the walls, rich offerings to the Buddha. / In writing rhapsodies I must blush before Song Yu; / With a flower in your hand, you should be Vimalakirti’s companion. / Coming here, my worldly thoughts all melt away; / Yet savoring our former ties, I find them all temptation.”

“Old legend tells of Chang’e’s flight up to the moon; / Now I knock at a cloudlike cell enclosed in clinging vines. / Your gifts need yield nothing to Xuanji’s; / Your romantic ardor seems greater even than Sun Chuo’s. / Who has attained even half a lay disciple’s practice? / We wait to grasp the Three Vehicles and the Agamas. / When shall we share a Buddhist vegetarian feast, / And in this cool, pure world dispel the demon of verse?”
