“On the register of flowers are many traces of smiles; / Today I visit this cloudlike cell in our native place. / Cherishing talent, you have left fine lines behind; / I once beat time in praise as I heard you sing aloud. / Cool shade finds distant shelter in the holy mountain’s bamboo; / Lush beauty trails low among a thatched hut’s vines. / Chrysanthemums beneath the hedge adorn the autumn light; / All wandering thoughts I give to the demon of meditation.”

In literary matters Hu knew even less than Xiao. He had no idea who Song Yu and Sun Chuo were, nor that Xuanji meant Yu Xuanji, the Tang dynasty Taoist nun. The allusions to Buddhist scriptures were wholly beyond him. Yet he could tell that the tone of the poems was hardly one in which to address a nun who kept strict vows. Astonished, he asked, “What is all this?” “Wait and see,” Xiao answered softly. “This Sister Liaochen must come from Jiaxing or Kunshan. If neither, then Zhenze or Shengze.” Hu knew nothing of what distinguished the nuns of Kunshan, but he had personal experience of the convents of Jiaxing. When staying at Tongli in Wujiang, he had also heard about the customs of Zhenze and Shengze. Both were prosperous silk-producing towns, luxurious in their habits. In Shengze it was fashionable to entertain guests at convents, where a vegetarian banquet cost more than a feast featuring bird’s nest. The dishes were said to be flavored with stock made from plump chickens and the finest ham, which accounted for their extraordinary savor. The convents of Zhenze were equally famous for their cooking: meat and vegetarian dishes alike rivaled the celebrated boat cuisine of Wuxi. Besides the delicacies on the table, of course, there were beauties to feast one’s eyes upon. Remembering all this, Hu thought back to Old Hu’s assurance when they first met that there was “no inconvenience” in visiting White-Robed Convent at night. Everything from that moment to the scene before him seemed to confirm Xiao’s view. His curiosity rose, and he longed to see what Liaochen looked like. Xiao, however, had grown more sober than he. “Mr Hu, I am afraid there has been a mistake. A’qiao cannot be here.”

“Why do you say that?” “Is this a place for shaving one’s head and practicing religion?” Hu was about to reply when he glimpsed a white gauze lantern floating toward them beyond the glass window. He fell silent and rose to wait. The curtain lifted; first came Xiaoyin, then Liaochen. Had they not known beforehand, they would never have believed her to be a nun. Her appearance was not entirely secular, certainly. She wore neither an ordinary woman’s three-part coiffure nor separate upper and lower garments. Her hair reached her shoulders, and she had on a long, round-necked robe. One might have called it a nun’s robe, but a nun’s robe would hardly be made of such glossy soft black satin, still less be cut so closely to the body, with a narrow waist and slender sleeves. Her face looked less monastic still. Though she wore no powder, she had applied rouge; her naturally fair skin needed no powder. Above all there were her eyes: at first glance clear as water, but at a second revealing an undertone of amorous feeling.

With such a person, excessive solemnity and reserve seemed unnecessary. Smiling, Hu brought his palms together in greeting. “Are you Sister Liaochen?” “I am Liaochen. May I ask your surname, benefactor?” “Mine is Hu. This gentleman is Mr Xiao.” Liaochen greeted them in turn and invited her “benefactors” to sit. She herself sat cross-legged on the wooden couch and asked what had brought them. “We originally came to see the elderly abbess,” Hu said. “But Old Hu, the constable, tells us that you are really in charge here. There is a small matter we wish to ask about. Perhaps my young friend Xiao had better explain.” Xiao nodded. Instead of coming to the point, however, he first asked, “Judging by your accent, Sister Liaochen, are you from Zhenze?” She blushed. “Yes.”

“These three poems,” Xiao said, pointing above her, “are very fine.” “They were written by three benefactors in moments of poetic inspiration. Such wild nonsense! What can one do with them?” Liaochen smiled faintly. “Have you lived in Zhenze, Mr Xiao?” “Yes, for more than a year. But I was only a child then and understood nothing.” “Meaning that you understand everything now?” So direct a question had something challenging about it. Xiao was no weak opponent and had no difficulty answering with composure. “Not everything, even now. I shall return another day for instruction. Today there is something in which I must ask your help.” “You put it too strongly. Please tell me what you wish. I only fear there may be little I can do.” “If you are willing, a word is all it will take. Has a lady come to White-Robed Convent today to ask the elderly abbess for refuge? She has had domestic difficulties and, in a moment of distress, could see no way out. She may have asked the abbess to conceal her whereabouts. If so, she has done your convent a disservice.” Liaochen’s expression changed; she looked frightened. She glanced from one man to the other, then finally nodded. “Yes, someone has come. Is she perhaps Mr Hu’s lady?”

So she was here after all. Once the news they had sought so eagerly was confirmed, the two men found themselves at a loss. Xiao and Hu looked at one another in silence, their glances asking the same question: what had A’qiao meant by coming to this convent? If she sought religious discipline, Xiao’s question was justified: “Is this a place for shaving one’s head and practicing religion?” If she did not seek that, she must be choosing the other path and becoming another Liaochen. Until they understood which it was, they could decide nothing; yet they did not know how to begin finding out. At last Hu made a decision. “Sister Liaochen, I should like my young friend Xiao to see my lady first. Would that be possible?” “Why not? That would be best. But I must ask her first.” Since Liaochen approved of Xiao’s seeing A’qiao, they could guess that “asking her” really meant persuading her. Provided Liaochen would help, the meeting was assured. Hu and Xiao said no more and were content to wait. As soon as she had gone, Xiao asked, “Mr Hu, what shall I say when I see A’qiao?”

“What puzzles me,” Hu said, without answering, “is whether that woman A’jin knows nothing of the kind of place this is.” “There is no time to investigate that now. Just tell me what I ought to do when I see A’qiao.” “You need say nothing else. Only ask what she means to do. Once you know, we can go back and discuss it with your master’s wife.” Xiao met A’qiao in the courtyard where the elderly abbess lived in quiet retirement. Its furnishings were less fine than those in Liaochen’s rooms, but still more elegant than those of other convents. Plainly White-Robed Convent was prosperous. Either it owned extensive property from which it received regular rents, or its income from devotees’ offerings was substantial.

A’qiao looked worn and haggard, and her eyes reddened at the sight of Xiao. After greeting her, he came straight to the point. “A’qiao, whatever made you come to this place?” “I have wanted to come for a long time. Life has no savor. I might as well cultivate merit for the next.” So she had intended to become a nun. Xiao asked, “Have you been here before?” “No.” Afraid of listeners beyond the wall, he could not speak openly. After a moment’s thought he remembered Hu’s question. “What about A’jin? Has she been here?” He meant that, if A’jin had visited, she must know what went on here: had she not told A’qiao? A’qiao shook her head. “No, she has not either.” “That explains it.” He could say no more. A’qiao seemed to understand. She gave a long, soundless sigh, as though she too had much that she could not freely express.

“What now?” Xiao asked. “You must have some plan.” “I—” A’qiao paused. “I shall stay here for the present and decide in time.” “Very well. My master’s wife will come to see you tomorrow.” “No!” A’qiao answered decisively. “Please tell her not to come.” That was extraordinary. She would receive a male visitor with whom she had no particularly close connection, yet refused to see the Seventh Lady, who had long been her friend. Such an emphatic refusal must have a reason. Xiao thought quickly, widely, and deeply; in matters of this kind he was especially cautious. After considering, he said, “A’qiao, I know you have always been very close to my master’s wife. Am I to repeat exactly what you have said?” “Why should you not?”

“Then if she asks why you do not want her to come, what am I to tell her?” At this question a look of resentment came over A’qiao’s face. “I have broken with all my worldly relations and friends. That is why I do not want her here. If she comes, I shall refuse to see her.” Her voice was more determined than ever; coupled with her expression, it suggested an irreconcilable hatred of the Seventh Lady. Xiao was deeply alarmed, but spoke all the more cautiously. “A’qiao,” he asked indirectly, probing for the truth, “am I not a person of the world too?” This exposed a flaw in her argument that she could not explain away. Her expression changed back and forth for a long time before she answered. “Young Master Xiao, to tell the truth, I am afraid of your master’s wife. Her methods are formidable; I am no match for her. And to tell you another truth, this is precisely what has made life seem so empty and disheartening. I thought she was a friend who gave her heart for mine. Who could have known she was two-faced, helping others scheme against me? My heart has turned completely cold. You must find this strange; you must refuse to believe it. But think: I am a woman in my thirties and have seen every sort of thing in the world. Surely I am not so foolish that I cannot tell a good person from a bad one, or would accuse your master’s wife without reason. She is really—” A’qiao shook her head and would say no more. Xiao was shaken to the core. He could neither understand nor accept so harsh a judgment of the Seventh Lady. For a long while he sat stunned. At last he said, “A’qiao, what is this all about? I truly cannot understand. Please tell me. If my master’s wife is in the wrong, those of us of the younger generation would naturally not presume to criticize her. But we know right from wrong in our hearts.” “Young Master Xiao, if you will swear before the Bodhisattva to keep everything I say to yourself, I shall tell you.”

“You mean I must not tell my master or his wife what you say?” “Exactly.” “Very well, I swear it: if I tell either of them what A’qiao tells me, may Heaven strike me dead with lightning.” A’qiao nodded, satisfied. “Your master’s wife really does play both the spirit-medium and the ghost—” Beginning with this harsh judgment, she recounted in detail the several occasions on which the Seventh Lady had advised her, interweaving the conversations with comments of her own. In her view, the Seventh Lady had deliberately set out to break her bond with Hu. Her advice that A’qiao swallow her pride, enter the household, make the proper obeisances, and formally become Hu’s concubine only appeared well meant. It was false kindness. Knowing perfectly well that A’qiao would never agree, she could safely urge it upon her and thereby force her to ask to leave.

As for Hu, the Seventh Lady had repeatedly called him “slippery,” a man with “no constancy, who falls in love with every woman he sees.” It sounded like abuse of Hu, but in fact it helped him. “Think, Young Master Xiao. Your master’s wife is forever saying ‘Little Uncle’ this and ‘Little Uncle’ that. She reveres him as though he were a bodhisattva. Even if he really were slippery and inconstant, how could she speak of him that way?” A’qiao had grown agitated. “At first I thought she was naturally outspoken and was truly indignant on my behalf, so she said whatever came into her head. Later, the more I thought, the less it seemed right. I went over everything again and again until I understood. All she meant was that Hu was such a scoundrel that he was not worth staying with!” Her accusations against the Seventh Lady were not without reason. Yet the more reasonable they seemed, the more painful Xiao found them. He regarded his master’s wife as a young brother regards an elder sister, with both respect and affection. For years he had cherished a perfect image of her as forthright, honest, warmhearted, and generous. Now he seemed to see a crack in it. How could that not hurt? He could not utter a word. Neither comforting A’qiao nor defending the Seventh Lady seemed quite appropriate; besides, he was too dejected to feel like saying anything at all.

The sight of Xiao’s face gave Hu a shock. He looked as though he had been ill. What could one meeting with A’qiao have done to leave him so visibly shaken? Hu was troubled and bewildered, yet could hardly question him closely here. He merely asked, “Have you seen her?” “Yes. Let us thank Sister Liaochen and take our leave.” Liaochen had recovered her composure. She too said nothing of A’qiao, only warmly invited the two men to return and “share in the religious joys” of the place. Why should men be invited to enjoy religious visits to a convent? The words had an odd sound, but there was no time to consider what lay beneath them.

Hu, however, never neglected the niceties of social dealings. Remembering something he ought to do, he asked at once, “Where is the book of donations?” “There is no need for such ceremony.” He had already spotted the yellow-covered book hanging on the wall. Taking it down, he handed it to Xiao. “Please enter a hundred taels for me.” “That is too much!” Liaochen interposed. “If you mean to give so much because your lady is staying here, there is no need. Those who have left the world receive support from all quarters and offer support to all quarters. You need not concern yourself.” “Those are separate matters,” Xiao replied on Hu’s behalf, going beyond his own role. “Each of us does what is in his heart.” He used the brush and ink already laid out to make the entry. Hu put a banknote for a hundred taels inside the book, placed it on the table, and took his leave. They returned to the camp to thank Commander Zhu, then rode back to Shanghai under the same escort. They traveled too fast for conversation. Only when they reached the busy streets did Xiao rein in his horse. “Mr Hu, let us go to your house and talk at length.”

They dismissed the mounted orderly and went together to the house Hu had shared with A’qiao. Her powder box still held its fragrance; the bright mirror stood as before. Yet the rooms had taken on a desolate air. Hu chose another room, shutting himself in his study with Xiao for a private conversation. When Xiao repeated A’qiao’s indignant words, Hu understood why he had looked so distressed. He too was deeply troubled. In all the time he had known the Seventh Lady, he had never heard anyone criticize her so harshly. Now, because of him, A’qiao had branded her a treacherous schemer. He felt he had done the Seventh Lady a great wrong. “Mr Hu,” Xiao asked, voicing a question that had occupied him all the way back, “has my master’s wife really been deliberately maneuvering things as A’qiao says?” “Yes.” Hu nodded. “She is too eager to help. Most of what A’qiao says is true. She has misunderstood only one point: your master’s wife is doing it entirely for her sake.” He went on to explain. The Seventh Lady was sincerely considering A’qiao’s lifelong welfare. Since she would not become a concubine, she would do better to part from him and choose another man. Hu did not know that the Seventh Lady intended to match her with Dr Zhang, but he believed that, with her warm concern for others, she would certainly find A’qiao a suitable home.

Xiao could accept this explanation completely. Indeed, it was precisely what he had hoped for: an explanation that would clear the Seventh Lady of the charge against her. His manner changed at once. He became cheerful and relieved, as though a heavy burden had slipped from his shoulders. “That is what I thought! How could my master’s wife do such a thing? If she heard how deeply A’qiao misunderstood her, there is no knowing how angry she would be.” “Exactly!” Hu suddenly took alarm. “You must on no account tell her what A’qiao said.” “But if we say nothing, how are we to explain?” They discussed how to put the Seventh Lady off. If they said they had not found A’qiao, she would ask A’jin to look again. If they said A’qiao had shaved her head and absolutely refused to return, she would still persist and go to White-Robed Convent herself, only to be rebuffed. However much they thought, they could find no satisfactory answer.

Leaving that difficulty aside, Xiao asked, “Mr Hu, what do you mean to do about A’qiao?” That too was hard for Hu to answer. He turned it over in his mind for a long time, then sighed. “I can only resign myself to being cursed.” “You mean you have decided to give her up?” “What else can I do?” “Then leave the matter entirely alone,” Xiao said. “If you are to harden your heart, harden it all the way.” “It is a matter of my own conscience,” Hu replied. “Ignoring her altogether hardly seems right.” “What would be right, then? There is no way to make A’qiao agree willingly to the separation.”

“I do not ask for willing agreement. I only hope she can swallow the bitterness.” Hu made his decision. “This is what I propose—” He would first gain time and keep the Seventh Lady quiet by saying that the abbess of White-Robed Convent had sent A’qiao to another place far away, and that someone was already following to persuade her to return. At the same time, he would ask the Second Miss to enlist A’jin’s help. First, A’jin must support the story and never reveal the truth to the Seventh Lady. Second, she must see A’qiao and deliver a message: whatever A’qiao wished to do—take the tonsure, marry respectably, or even follow in Liaochen’s footsteps—Hu would not interfere. He was also ready to give her a large sum of money. Once he had explained the plan, Hu too felt relieved. After so many days of entanglement, he had at last found a way to cut through it. Xiao did not consider it a good solution, but could think of no better one. Besides, it was not his own affair; however much he wished to help, there was little he could do. He could only nod his agreement. “We must act at once,” Hu said. “Your master’s wife is still waiting for news. Let us do what needs doing. I must trouble you with more work tonight.” “Your business is as much my concern as my master’s.” Xiao thought for a moment. “Let us see the Second Miss first.”

At the Second Miss’s establishment, lamps glowed red, wine flowed, and the night was still young. But she was the proprietress and had a separate little house of her own. Fortunately it was nearby; an attendant led them there in a few moments. As they entered, they heard the clock in the sitting room strike twelve. The Second Miss had not gone to sleep, though she had retired upstairs to her bedroom. When her maid announced the visitors, she invited them up. With the Dragon Boat Festival approaching, the weather had grown close and hot. She received them in an informal lined silk jacket whose broad, short sleeves exposed her white forearms. On one arm she wore a gold bracelet, on the other a jade bangle. Her full face and abundant, carefully dressed hair gave her a look of prosperity and good fortune. The sight stirred Xiao again: by comparison, A’qiao seemed all the more worn and pitiable. Since this was the two men’s first visit to her house, the Second Miss had to observe the usual courtesies. She poured tea, set out dishes of fruit, and even offered to light the opium lamp and let them recline for a while. Before such hospitality, the guests too had to make a show of being at leisure. They began with irrelevant conversation before turning to their purpose.

Xiao had barely said, “A’qiao was at White-Robed Convent after all,” when the maid came in with a tray. Over a little wine and a late supper, he continued his account of the visit. When he finished, Hu asked the Second Miss to act as intermediary. She had listened without a word and did not answer at once. The whole affair was so strange that she could scarcely make sense of it. For a while she blinked in thought, then shook her head. “Mr Hu, I do not think that is the way to handle matters. We cannot do without the Seventh Lady in this.” She told him about Dr Zhang and argued that the Seventh Lady’s course was the right one. A’qiao’s misunderstanding could surely be explained away. What they must do now was persuade her to return, clear up the misunderstanding, dispel her resentment, and arrange her marriage into the Zhang household. If everyone worked together patiently, there was every prospect of a happy conclusion. “There is no need to involve A’jin, and you certainly must not send a message that closes every door. A’qiao’s thoughts have taken a wrong turning; we must patiently guide them back. The Seventh Lady may be hasty, but no one is more considerate or more willing to think of everyone’s interests. She will forgive A’qiao’s misunderstanding and put up with some unfairness. We can explain matters to her openly; there is no reason to let her go to the convent and be rebuffed. As I see it, Mr Hu—” She deliberately stopped, hoping that Hu would understand and volunteer the necessary offer. But his mind was in turmoil and he had no patience for subtlety. “What do you mean, Second Sister?”

“I mean that you should put your pride aside a little. Go to White-Robed Convent again yourself tomorrow, smile, say a few kind words, and first persuade A’qiao to return. We can consider the rest afterward.” Hu could not agree. This repeated and endless entanglement had forced him to neglect a great deal of important business; he was thoroughly weary of it. Now that he had finally found a way to cut himself free, he was to be prevented from using it. Instead, he must smile at A’qiao and speak ingratiatingly. He resented that; worse, he feared she would think he had changed his mind and could not do without her. She would cling to him still more tightly. Would that not merely invite further trouble? Seeing his reluctance, the Second Miss grew anxious. “Mr Hu, in every other kind of judgment I am far below gentlemen in official life such as yourself. But on this matter I am confident. Why? Because only a woman understands a woman’s thoughts. Besides, A’qiao and I lived together for more than a year. Naturally I know her disposition. I am giving you good advice. If you refuse it, you will regret it.” Hu already suspected the Second Miss of being partial to A’qiao, and her words only strengthened his prejudice. He made no commitment, merely adopting a thoughtful expression to indicate his disagreement.

As an onlooker, Xiao could see more clearly. The Second Miss had spoken rather bluntly, but her approach was sound. Yet he also understood Hu’s state of mind: pressing him now would only make things worse. Fortunately, they knew where A’qiao was, and there was no harm in her remaining at the convent a little longer. To avoid a deadlock, they must follow the maxim that matters settle more smoothly when given time. “Mr Hu has his own difficulties. But your intention is right.” Xiao emphasized the words and gave the Second Miss a meaningful look. “Let us take things slowly. Sooner or later we shall find a way through.” “Very well. Please try to persuade him, Young Master Xiao.” “I will, I will,” Xiao promised repeatedly. “I shall bring you an answer tomorrow. It is late now. We should be going.”

They took their leave. Hu walked unsteadily, utterly exhausted in body and mind. Xiao could hardly add anything; he only asked, “Where will you sleep tonight, Mr Hu? I shall see you there.” “At the bank. Will you still go to your master’s wife tonight?” “There is no need now. It is so late.” “Very well.” Hu pondered a moment, then frowned and shook his head, looking unbearably harassed. “Let us leave it for a day or two. My mind has truly gone dull. I have never known such a tangle of trouble, with everyone pulling at everyone else and nothing ever settled.” “Then,” Xiao asked in a subdued voice, almost as though he himself had caused the trouble and was begging for advice, “what should I say when I see my master’s wife tomorrow?” This time Hu answered promptly and decisively. “Say anything you like, provided you do not hurt her.” Back at the bank, his vexation kept him tossing in bed. Only as the sounds of the city began to rise did he drift into an uneasy sleep.

He was deep in a dream when someone suddenly shook him awake. Opening his heavy, sore eyes, he saw Xiao standing beside the bed with a broad smile. “Mr Hu, your family has arrived!” All sleep vanished. Hu sprang up, throwing back the quilt. “Where are they?” “They went first to my master’s wife. She checked the almanac and found that today is an auspicious day for moving into a house, so they decided to go straight to the new home. She sent me to tell you.” Mother and son, husband and wife, father and daughter were reunited as though after a lifetime apart. Everyone in the Hu household sobbed, and the Seventh Lady wept beside them. At last, one by one, they stopped crying and began to describe what had happened since their separation. At every painful recollection the tears started again. Thus they talked and wept, wept and talked; not until the third day did Hu and his mother begin to regain their composure. During those three days, the busiest person was naturally the Seventh Lady. Newly arrived in Shanghai, the Hu family found everything unfamiliar and depended upon her guidance and care at every turn.

Yet whenever there was a quiet moment, she thought of A’qiao: a woman forsaken in middle age, taking refuge in a convent. Putting herself in A’qiao’s place, she could see little joy left in life. From time to time an alarming thought struck her: what if A’qiao took her own life? Her anxiety increased each day until she had to consult Liu Bucai. “Do not worry,” he replied. “I shall go there with Xiao Jiaji, and we shall see how matters stand.” He found Xiao, and they set out for White-Robed Convent along the now familiar route. When they knocked at its gate, Xiaoyin again answered. “Oh, Mr Xiao!” She still recognized him. “A’qiao has gone to Ningbo.” The news was entirely unexpected. “What has she gone to Ningbo for?” Xiao asked. “My teacher will tell you. She said you would certainly come again, and she was right. Please come in.” She conducted them to the elegant retreat Xiao had visited before. They had scarcely sat long enough to drink a cup of tea when Liaochen appeared with a graceful step. Liu’s eyes brightened, and he rose involuntarily with a smile.

“Sister Liaochen,” Xiao said by way of introduction, “this is Mr Liu, a senior relation of the Hu family.” “Ah, please sit. I suppose you have come to persuade A’qiao to return?” “Yes. The young sister tells us she has gone to Ningbo. Is that true?” “She left the day before yesterday—to seek a settled home.” Xiao was delighted and surprised. “Then I presume you know all about A’qiao’s life?” “I do. It was precisely because I knew her story that I urged her to go to Ningbo.” “So it was your boundless spiritual power that persuaded her to turn back!” Liu brought his palms together, closed his eyes, and murmured, “A great deed of merit, a great deed of merit.” He looked rather comical. Liaochen could not help smiling behind pursed lips and seemed to find him distinctly interesting.

“It is indeed a great deed of merit,” Xiao said. “May we hear the instruction with which you persuaded her?” “I moved her simply by speaking of causes and affinities. I told her that her bond with Mr Hu had run its course and must not be forced. Having sown that cause in the beginning, she must now reap this result. With Dr Zhang, however, she has sown a new cause, and that too must bear fruit. If it does not ripen in this life, it will do so in the next. Our passage through worldly lives is nothing but this endless chain of cause and effect. Better to complete this bond in the present life; with no old karmic debt carried into the next, she need not suffer. That is the course of a person of great wisdom and complete understanding.” Liaochen continued, “We talked from facing couches in my quiet retreat. For three whole days I reasoned with her, until at last she woke to the truth.” “Wonderful! Wonderful!” Liu exclaimed. “The sea of suffering is boundless, but turn your head and there is the shore. Only when one person of great wisdom meets another can such perfect merit be achieved.” “Mr Liu truly has the gift of unhindered eloquence.” Liaochen smiled, cast him a glance, then lowered her head and smiled faintly again for no apparent reason.

“You praise me too highly, Sister Liaochen. Still, I have dipped into the Buddhist scriptures myself. One day I must seek proper instruction from you.” “If you truly desire goodness and are sincere in your devotion, come whenever you wish.” “I certainly shall, certainly!” Liu looked about him with open admiration. “To discuss meditation with you on facing couches in such a blessed retreat—who knows how many lifetimes of merit it would take to earn such pure enjoyment?” Liaochen answered with another reserved smile. Afraid Liu would go on indefinitely, Xiao quickly intervened. “May I ask, Sister Liaochen: will A’qiao return?” “No.” “Then what of her luggage? Has she taken it all to Ningbo?” “No. She went ahead alone. Dr Zhang will send someone for it later.” “When his man comes, could you ask him to be sure to call at Fukang Bank?” “There is no need,” Liaochen replied. “Once it is ended, let everything end. Please tell Mr Hu when you return that the bond has run its course. He need not go on seeking troubles for himself.” “Excellent! Excellent!” Liu intoned loudly. “‘To rid yourself of trouble, let the self be gone; each has his own affinities—envy no one else.’”

Xiao could not help growing angry. Liu was simply making mischief. In a fit of annoyance he abandoned his remaining questions and stood. “Thank you, Sister Liaochen. We shall take our leave.” Liu still seemed reluctant to go, but Xiao allowed no discussion and pulled him away. Back at home, he gave a full account of their visit. Gu Yingchun and his wife were delighted beyond expectation, though the Seventh Lady remained somewhat dissatisfied. “You ought to have asked for more details,” she complained mildly. This touched Xiao’s own grievance exactly. “How could I get a word in?” he asked, pointing at Liu. “Third Master Liu was busy making eyes and flirting with Liaochen!” He proceeded to imitate Liu’s words and gestures. Gu and the Seventh Lady burst out laughing; she laughed until tears came. Liu waited for them to stop. “May I speak now?” “Speak, speak!” the Seventh Lady said, still laughing. “Go ahead, Third Uncle Liu.” “Jiaji cannot keep his patience. What is there to be so anxious about? Tomorrow I shall go and ‘discuss meditation’ with Liaochen. What is there that I cannot ask her then?”

“Exactly! Third Uncle Liu, please do ask her—the more detail the better.” Gu said nothing at the time, but afterward spoke privately to Liu. “Your words were right: ‘To rid yourself of trouble, let the self be gone; each has his own affinities—envy no one else.’ For Little Uncle’s unhappy bond with A’qiao to end in this way is an excellent outcome. There is no need to stir matters up again. And let me give you a word of advice, Third Uncle: do not go there to discuss any meditation.” “I was only joking.”

CHAPTER EIGHT

Entering Zhejiang from Anhui, Zuo Zongtang advanced cautiously, securing each position and making safety from defeat his first concern. His initial move was to clear Quzhou and establish a base within the province from which he could exercise his authority as governor. This was early in the sixth month of the first year of Tongzhi. Once firmly established there, he moved against Longyou, Lanxi, Shouchang, Chun’an, and the surrounding districts, driving the rebels out of the country south of the Xin’an River and west of the Qu River, also called Xin’an. Late in the eleventh month, he captured Yanzhou at the confluence of the two rivers. From there his forces passed Yan Ziling’s Fishing Terrace, with its lofty hills and long reaches of water, and proceeded north along the river through Qililong. In the second month of the following year they laid siege to Fuyang, south of Hangzhou and less than a hundred li from the provincial capital. South of the Qiantang River, the Changjie Army under the foreign commander d’Aiguebelle and the Chang’an Army under Diuledeke had recently captured Shaoxing; the Taiping forces then withdrew from Xiaoshan. The eastern, western, and southern regions of Zhejiang had all been cleared. But the rich northern tract, between Hangzhou and Lake Tai and including Haining, Jiaxing, and Huzhou, remained in Taiping hands. At this point Zuo was promoted to governor-general of Fujian and Zhejiang. Zeng Guoquan was appointed governor of Zhejiang, but, being outside the walls of Jinling, he could not take up the post; Zuo continued to act in his place. To repay the court’s favour, Zuo threw all his strength into the counter-offensive. Everyone could see that the recovery of Hangzhou was only a matter of time.

The commander attacking Fuyang with an eye to Hangzhou was Jiang Yili, Zhejiang’s provincial treasurer. Zuo himself remained at Quzhou, where he established workshops and supervised the building of war vessels. The flotilla proved of considerable assistance in the fighting at Fuyang. But the Taiping defender was Wang Haiyang, famous for his courage, and after five months Jiang had made no progress. Zuo was obliged to call upon the foreign commander. He ordered d’Aiguebelle to bring twenty-five hundred men of the Changjie Army across the river from Xiaoshan and Shaoxing for a combined attack. Fuyang was finally recovered on the eighth day of the eighth month. At about the same time, Li Hongzhang, Liu Mingchuan, and Guo Songlin joined forces to capture Jiangyin, while Li Xiucheng and Li Shixian travelled from the Heavenly Capital through Liyang to Suzhou, hoping to find a way to relieve the siege. In Zhejiang, Jiang and d’Aiguebelle advanced north from Fuyang towards Hangzhou, sending a separate force against Yuhang to the west. The Taiping commanders Wang Haiyang, the Court General, Deng Guangming, the Gui King, and Chen Bingwen, the Ting King, resisted repeatedly, but lost battle after battle. Early in the eleventh month, Zuo went in person to Yuhang to direct operations. Hangzhou, however, was still being stubbornly defended.

By then Li Hongzhang had taken Suzhou and Wuxi. In accordance with his earlier plan, he would not move east to seize the easily obtainable prize of Changzhou, lest he encroach upon Zeng Guoquan. Instead, he moved into northern Zhejiang to encroach upon Zuo. On one side he sent Liu Bingzhang, a Hanlin lecturer whom he had personally requested from the emperor for service in his camp, south along the coast from Jinshanwei. Liu recovered Pinghu, Zhapu, and Haiyan. On the other, he sent Cheng Xueqi from Wujiang through Pingwang to attack Jiaxing from the north. Recovery of these districts naturally brought possession of Taiping baggage and stores, as well as the right to collect grain levies and taxes. Li also invoked the precedent of Hu Linyi, governor of Hubei, who had recovered districts on the Anhui border. Since Zuo was far to the south of Hangzhou, with communications obstructed and these places beyond his effective reach, Li considered it proper to exercise authority temporarily on his behalf and appoint acting magistrates to the recovered districts of western Zhejiang. Zuo was furious. Li had not merely seized territory; he was carrying his office as governor of Jiangsu into Zhejiang. It was an intolerable affront. For the moment, however, Zuo could do nothing about it. He could only concentrate on recovering Hangzhou first.

Hu now began planning his return to Hangzhou. Liu was to go ahead, his task being to win over a certain Licentiate Zhang, turn an enemy into a friend, and secure help from within the city. Zhang belonged to the shabby fraternity of petty scholars who exploited their status. Considering himself a gentleman entitled to enter official offices, he habitually took charge of lawsuits and meddled in disputes, bullying the weak and cringing before the strong. He was an utter scoundrel. When Wang Youling had been prefect of Hangzhou, he had detested the man and long wanted to ask the educational authorities to strip him of his degree. Lacking a suitable opportunity, he had kept his anger to himself. Zhang was in league with the runners at the various government offices. These men enjoyed a customary illicit source of income: anyone opening a shop had to pay a fee to the runners attached to the office with jurisdiction over the premises. The amount varied with the size of the shop, and the fee had to be settled before business could begin. This was called “drinking salt water.” Eager to discharge his duties, Wang posted a proclamation forbidding it for good. The runners of Qiantang and Renhe, fearful of the consequences, temporarily restrained themselves. Those at the governor’s and provincial treasurer’s offices, confident in their powerful superiors, paid no heed to the prefect and continued collecting. Since they could not conveniently appear in person, they sent Zhang, agreeing to divide the proceeds in a ratio of three to seven.

Unfortunately for him, Wang happened to pass in his sedan chair while Zhang was haggling over the sum demanded from a second-hand clothing shop about to open on Yanqiao Street. Wang noticed the disturbance, stopped, and made inquiries. The proprietor told him exactly what had happened. In a rage, Wang decided to make an example of Zhang. He summoned him before the chair, reprimanded him, and sternly declared that his degree would be revoked. Zhang panicked. Once stripped of his licentiate’s status, he would be a commoner. He would have to kneel before the local magistrate and could be thrown to the ground and beaten on the buttocks, or fastened in a cangue beside the screen wall of the yamen for public humiliation. After much thought, he could see only one course: appeal to Hu, whose advice Wang ordinarily followed. He brought his wife and children to Fukang Bank, fell to his knees before Hu, and pleaded desperately. Hu carelessly assumed that this was a small matter and Wang would oblige him. He promised without reservation that Zhang would come to no harm. But Wang refused to yield. His authority was at stake, he said. He had recently become acting grain intendant and had also been ordered to organize militia. In raising troops and funds, obedience to his commands was essential. If he could not abolish an abuse that harmed the people, or enforce his own proclamation, how could he command respect? After repeated persuasion, he finally made one concession. He had intended to revoke Zhang’s degree, give him two hundred strokes with the small paddle, and expose him in the cangue for three months. For Hu’s sake, he would spare the physical suffering and public disgrace. But the degree must go. In truth, Hu had done Zhang an enormous service. Zhang, however, thought he had merely made excuses instead of exerting himself. He bore a grudge thereafter, obstructing Hu wherever he could, and had not relented even now.

Fortunately, everything has its master, and one rogue can be made to curb another. Apart from officials, the only person Zhang feared was his son. Young Zhang was a wastrel, devoted to whoring, gambling, and every luxury. The ill-gotten money his father scraped together all went to maintain this treasured son. Liu had himself been a rich young idler and possessed far more experience than the younger Zhang. Hu therefore devised a plan to reach the father through the son. Once the son was won over, the father could be brought to terms. On his second day back in Hangzhou, Liu went into the city to look for the young man. Business remained desperately depressed; nine houses in ten stood empty. Only Qinghefang in the upper city, Jianqiao in the middle, and Yanqiao Street in the lower city retained something of their former appearance. Even there, shops put up their shutters before dusk. After nightfall everything fell silent. Apart from patrolling rebels, scarcely a civilian was to be seen. A few lanes, however, belonged to another world. One was in Jianqiao, where the middle city’s reconstruction bureau had been established. Under Zhang’s direction, a band of local ruffians levied contributions and taxes in the name of preserving order and supplying the rebels, acting as though they were the government itself. Their usual meeting places naturally offered opium, gambling, and unlicensed prostitutes. Liu reasoned that a young man of Zhang’s character would surely exploit his father’s influence to play the grand young master in such places. There he would find an opportunity to approach him.

It was just growing dark when he arrived. Two large lanterns hung at the entrance, where a crowd of swaggering idlers were laughing and talking loudly. Liu strolled over and looked inside. The doors stood open through to the second hall. Vendors had set up their snack stalls in the courtyard, and bright lights in the hall illuminated a dense crowd. It was plainly a gambling establishment. Since it was open to the public, anyone could enter. Liu stepped over the threshold. “Hey!” someone called. He stopped with a smile that was neither servile nor arrogant. “Were you calling me, friend?” “What have you come for?” “To see young Zhang.” “Young Zhang? Which young Zhang?” “Licentiate Zhang’s son,” Liu replied unhurriedly. “An old friend of mine.” His pretence was well chosen. With his father’s rise to power, the son had grown so important that only an old friend would dare call him merely “young Zhang.” Finding the answer plausible, the man waved him in.

In the second hall stood two gambling tables, one for pai gow and one for bao. The pai gow banker appeared to be losing, and his table was much livelier. Liu knew that pushing through a crowd was particularly unwelcome in a gambling house. He stood quietly behind the others and rose on tiptoe to watch. The banker was a middle-aged man with a fleshy, belligerent face shining with oil. He wore a satin-covered robe of heavy fur, with sleeves so broad and large that the expensive garment plainly did not belong to him. Whether from the crowd or the strain of his losses, he had unfastened the buttons across his chest, exposing a broad fold of white fox fur that hung diagonally down the front. Still complaining of the heat, he turned to glare at those behind him, as though they were to blame for crowding so close that he could hardly breathe. “Wu Bigmouth!” said a young man at the upper hand. “I think you should rest. Argue with a man if you must, but never with the tiles!” A losing gambler could bear no advice less easily. Yet Wu seemed afraid to answer. He kept his lips tightly shut and puffed out his cheeks in such silent resentment that he looked comical.

“If you will not listen to good advice, there is nothing I can do,” the young man continued. “You said you would bank for a long run, but you must stop sometime. Do you mean to sit there until dawn?” “Do you want to be banker?” Wu was losing patience. He pulled out a bundle of banknotes. “There are at least two hundred taels here. I shall stop when I have lost them.” “Banknotes!” The young man looked from side to side. “Banknotes at a time like this? Which bank is open to redeem them?” “Most are Fukang notes,” Wu replied. “Fukang has a branch in Shanghai. Why should they not be redeemable?” “You certainly have faith in Fukang. But you must ask whether everyone else does.” The young man looked around. “Well?” “We agreed at the start: no banknotes,” someone said. “Fukang or any other bank, it makes no difference. Gambling means silver in hand.” “Did you hear?” the young man said to Wu. “You have only twenty or thirty taels in silver left. I shall stake against it on the upper hand. If you win, keep banking. If you lose, give up your place. Agreed?”

Wu thought, gritted his teeth, and agreed. The dice were thrown: a five at the head. Wu snatched up his tiles and turned them over at once. He had a Heaven-gong combination, and triumph appeared on his face. The young man slowly exposed one tile, a three-six, while continuing to feel the other. Unable to contain himself, Wu turned over all the remaining tiles with a clatter. Examining them, he said, “There is no Heaven-nine King in small pai gow. Even if you have a Heaven tile, it will not help.” Liu had a remarkably sharp eye for the tiles. He saw the position at once and involuntarily exclaimed, “The upper hand wins. It is a red nine!” The young man glanced at him, ran his fingers over his tile, and smiled. “You called it exactly!” He turned it over. It was indeed a red nine, completing a pair. Furious, Wu shoved his silver and tiles forwards, stood up, and began to leave.

“Wu Bigmouth!” the young man called. “Why leave now that I am banking?” “How can I gamble without money?” “Are your banknotes not money? I do not want those of other banks, but I am not afraid that Hu Xueyan will fail to honour Fukang’s. Give them to me. I shall exchange them for you.” Wu seemed a little abashed and sat down again. While the young man shuffled, someone asked, “Young Master Zhang, will it be big pai gow or small?” The form of address was unusual, but it delighted Liu: this was the licentiate’s treasured son. People in the streets, afraid of the father, called him Master Zhang. The son evidently borrowed his dignity to become Young Master Zhang. Liu concentrated all his attention on him. Zhang proved worthy of his reputation as an accomplished idler; he was perfectly at ease as banker. Only after arranging the tiles did he answer. “Big pai gow produces more ties. The play lasts longer.” They began a quiet game of big pai gow. Hoping for an opportunity to strike up a conversation, Liu also placed bets. Gambling was not his object, and he kept his stakes modest, betting on the upper hand nearest him.

Zhang remained banker for a long time. Bettors came and went, wave following wave, gradually pushing Liu from the back to the front until he exchanged his standing place for a seat. He consequently continued to bet only on his own hand. Slowly, the bank began to lose. Wu recovered his spirits and won back his losses with a substantial profit. The lower hand kept winning, until almost all the stakes were concentrated on that side. The sole exception was Liu’s lonely ten taels on the upper hand, conspicuous in its isolation. His persistence seemed almost an act of opposition. The other bettors disliked it, but the banker felt a corresponding warmth towards him. Zhang gave him a long look, an involuntary expression of gratitude in his eyes.

Now we are getting somewhere, Liu thought. He grew more composed still, watching silently. “Count the upper-hand stake with the lower!” Wu shouted. “Sorry,” Zhang replied. “We agreed beforehand that no one would move the stakes.” Wu had to appeal to Liu. “Hey, friend at the upper hand! Why not move your own stake over here? If it wins, I shall add another half. Your ten taels will win fifteen.” “And if it loses?” Liu asked coldly. “Bah!” Wu spat savagely on the floor. “The devil take it!” Liu said nothing, but Zhang took offence on his behalf. “Wu Bigmouth!” he said, his expression darkening. “A gambler ought to have some manners. If you cannot bear to play, stay away. He likes betting on the upper hand. What bloody business is it of yours? What do you think you are doing?” “Enough, enough!” someone interposed. “Hands off the table. The banker is about to throw the dice.”

When the dice had been cast, Wu snatched up his tiles. Holding them in his hairy hands, he narrowed his eyes and peeped at them repeatedly, then swiftly arranged them in two neat pairs, one before the other. Someone reached out for a look and received a sharp slap on the hand. Plainly Wu held something good. When the tiles were exposed, the Heaven hand ranked first, the lower hand second, the banker third, and the upper hand last. The lower hand certainly seemed charmed. After paying it, the banker collected Liu’s ten taels. “I would rather have paid you,” he said apologetically. “Yes,” Liu replied calmly. “I keep hoping that fortune will turn and the upper hand will have its day. But now—” He hesitated, then drew out his gold watch, detached its pendant, and asked, “Could I pledge this for a loan of fifty taels?”

The pendant was a piece of vivid green jadeite carved in the shape of an ancient coin, worth at least two hundred taels. But its value was not what moved Zhang to lend. “Why not? I shall lend you fifty taels. What need have I of a pledge?” “No. It affects the banker’s luck,” Liu insisted. “If you will not take a pledge, I need not borrow.” In fact, he had Fukang banknotes on him—the very notes Zhang trusted. He staged the transaction to create an opening for further acquaintance. By the time Zhang stopped banking, Liu had lost almost all the fifty taels. He stood up and asked where Zhang lived, saying he would bring the money and redeem the pendant the next day. “What is your surname?” Zhang asked. “Liu.” “Then I shall call you Old Liu. I like you. Take your property back. We are bound to meet again. Bring the money whenever you please.” He held out the jadeite. “Since you put it that way, I am all the less able to accept it,” Liu replied. “Where do you live? I shall fetch the silver and redeem it tomorrow.”

“Why keep talking of redemption?” Zhang hesitated. Out of three hundred and sixty-five days in a year, he spent three hundred away from home. What use would there be in Liu calling on an absent host? He might go home specially to wait, but he could not be sure of keeping to that intention. Liu was quick to understand. He did not know precisely what was in Zhang’s mind, but his reluctance to have a visitor at home was plain. Leaving the pendant had merely been a means of arranging a private meeting. That need not be at Zhang’s house; there was a better place. “Young Master Zhang,” he said, “if you have no objection, why do we not arrange to meet somewhere tomorrow?” “Gladly. Name the place.” “You know Madam A’gou at Huapailou, surely?”

How could Zhang fail to know her? Madam A’gou was a celebrated procuress who ran a very large private brothel. The boatwomen who offered their favours aboard the Jiangshan boats on the Fuchun River all lodged with her when they came ashore. Zhang knew her well. But the siege of Hangzhou had ruined the pleasure trade, and he had not seen her since the upheaval. “So Madam A’gou did not starve to death!” he exclaimed, surprised and delighted. “Her place is lively again. But I am staying in the rear, where it is very quiet.” “Good! I shall certainly come tomorrow afternoon.”

Madam A’gou had prepared the rooms specially for Liu. They occupied a separate courtyard at the back, with a dividing gate that could be bolted to shut them off from the front and an entrance of their own. When Zhang arrived, Liu changed his form of address. “Brother Zhang, will you accept Fukang notes?” “Oh, I nearly forgot.” Zhang took a small tissue-paper parcel from his pocket and handed it over. “Here is your property. Have a look.” “No need.” Liu gave him a fifty-tael banknote. With the transaction settled, he opened a cupboard. “Brother Zhang, I have a few little things for you. Let us be friends.” The little things varied in size and shape: a long walking stick, a small gold watch, a large box of Manila cigars, and an angular rectangular parcel less than five cun long that was harder to identify. It looked like books, but Zhang could not believe that Liu would give him books. Besides, to give books to a gambler was to invite bad luck—the word sounded like “lose.”

“Look at this stick. An excellent thing for protecting yourself.” Liu raised it. “Watch out!” Then he brought it down towards Zhang’s head. Zhang naturally threw up a hand to block it and caught the far end. With some movement of Liu’s, the stick parted in two. What remained in his hand was a gleaming short sword. “How does it work?” Zhang asked eagerly. “Let me see!” The blade was quite unlike a Chinese sword: triangular, with a needle-sharp point and a groove along each of its three faces. It was unquestionably a deadly weapon. “There is a mechanism in the middle,” Liu explained. The join in the stick fitted with remarkable precision. If someone attacked, its owner could strike with it. An opponent who did not catch it would merely suffer a blow; one who tried to seize it would fall into the trap. A twist, using the other man’s grip, released the short sword for an unexpected thrust. Once Zhang understood its ingenuity, he liked it all the more. It would protect him, certainly; better still, he could display it and boast of it to others when nothing threatened him.

He was still grinning with delight when Liu said, “Here are some foreign books.” So they were books after all! That gift was wide of the mark. Zhang clasped his hands. “Old Liu, between friends, let me be frank. I can barely understand Chinese books. Foreign ones would leave me completely at sea.” “You will understand these.” Liu put them in his hands. “Take them home and look through them slowly, by yourself.” The suggestion was too intriguing: Zhang had to open the parcel immediately. A glance made his blood race. It was a foreign erotic picture book. Nothing else could hold his attention now. Liu quietly brought a chair and helped him sit, then took a seat some distance away. He watched coolly as Zhang stared and swallowed, every expression betraying his absorption. Liu’s confidence grew. At last Zhang finished. “What a treat!” he said with a somewhat embarrassed smile. “Old Liu, where did you find this?”

“In the foreign settlement at Shanghai, of course.” “Plenty of people have been to Shanghai, but I have never seen them bring back anything like these.” Zhang was full of admiration. “Old Liu, you certainly know how to manage!” “Not at all. I would not know where to find them myself. I picked them up from a relative. But we can discuss that later. Look at these other two things first.” These were the large box and the small one. Zhang examined both carefully. As he did so, he felt ashamed to receive such presents for nothing. Yet he could not bear to give up the stick and the foreign books. After prolonged indecision, he could only speak honestly. “Old Liu, we have only just met. You are so generous that I do not know what to say. But I really feel rather embarrassed.” “Then you are treating me as a stranger, my dear fellow. Friendship is not for a single day. If you insist on drawing such distinctions between us, I shall scarcely dare presume upon your acquaintance again.” “I make no distinctions!” Zhang protested earnestly. “Only you must let me do something in return.”

He appeared won over. There was no need to labour the point; Liu would strike while the iron was hot. “I shall be frank too. These things do not belong to me. A relative asked me to bring them.” He added, “Your father has misunderstood this relative of mine. More than misunderstood him—he has really done him an injustice.” “Who is your relative?” “Hu Xueyan of Fukang Bank.” “Him!” Zhang exclaimed. “Yes. Why do I say your father has wronged him through this misunderstanding? Such things must be explained. I happen to know something about it.” Zhang waited attentively, but Liu seemed reluctant to proceed. This was puzzling. “Old Liu, did you not say that you knew the inside story?”

“Yes, I know it thoroughly. When Governor Wang was transferred from Huzhou to Hangzhou, I came with him and managed miscellaneous affairs in his office. I know exactly what happened. But if we discuss the rights and wrongs, your father is my elder too. I ought not to criticize him.” “What does that matter? We are among friends. The old man at home is famous enough. Heaven knows how many people have criticized him. I have heard plenty; why should I mind hearing you?” “It is not criticism, really. I only think he was too careless and impatient. A newly appointed official is eager to make his authority felt; he ought to have kept out of the way for a while. Instead, he was caught collecting ‘salt-water’ fees. The proclamation was still posted there—the paste was scarcely dry—yet someone was already treating the prefect’s words as nothing. It was like a slap in the face. After all, he was the head of Hangzhou prefecture, responsible for several counties and more than a million people. How could he hold his authority securely after that? My dear fellow, spend the first half of the night considering the other man, and the second half considering yourself. If you had been Governor Wang, would you not have been furious?” Zhang said nothing. Liu’s reasoning was clear enough, but it was their new friendship that principally disposed him to listen. “True, Xueyan failed to save your father’s degree. But outsiders know only half the story. Governor Wang not only wrote to the educational authorities to have it revoked; he intended to punish your father afterwards to satisfy his anger. Xueyan would not consent to that. He tried entreaty first and then defiance, until the governor reluctantly yielded.” “Oh?” Zhang blinked repeatedly. “I did not know that. What do you mean by entreaty and defiance?”

“Entreaty meant kneeling; defiance meant a quarrel. Xueyan was ready to break with Governor Wang for your father’s sake. Afterwards they said he had failed as a friend and would not help. Was that not unjust?” “As you explain it, he certainly seems to have been wronged. But why send me these things? It would be extraordinary to be so kind.” “Nothing extraordinary. Naturally, he has a favour to ask.” “Then ask!” Zhang replied generously. “I do not know Mr Hu well, but you have treated me as a friend. If it is something I can do, I shall certainly help.” “I am not praising my own relative without cause. Hu Xueyan really does stand by his friends. Although your father has misunderstood him, he has already made provision for your father’s future and left him room to retreat.” This was too obscure for Zhang to follow, though he could tell it meant something favourable. “Then I must thank him. What sort of provision?” “Let me show you something.”

Liu dragged a leather case from beneath the bed, unlocked it, took out a document folder, and handed Zhang an official communication. Zhang’s learning was limited. He could recognize the great purple seal of the governor of Jiangsu and his father’s name, but the elaborate official phrasing left him unable to make out the meaning. “You must never speak of this dispatch outside. If word reaches the rebels, the consequences will be terrible.” Liu assumed an air of solemn warning, then lightened his manner. “Take it home and ask your father to keep it secretly. When the government troops recover Hangzhou, he can produce it. Far from being guilty of aiding rebellion, he will be credited with preserving order. Tell me, has Hu Xueyan done your father a great service or not?” Zhang began to understand. One point still puzzled him. “What about now? What are we to do now?”

“For the present, do whatever needs doing. Are you not preserving order? Continue as before.” “Ah!” Zhang finally saw. “A foot in each boat.” “Exactly. But the boat you are standing in now will capsize sooner or later. The other boat matters more.” “I understand.” “And your father?” “I shall tell him. He will be delighted. You shall have an answer tomorrow. It is getting late; I must go.” Early the next morning, Zhang came to invite Liu to the family home. The licentiate was already waiting with wine warming.

To cultivate their intimacy, Liu addressed him as “Uncle” and performed a full ceremonial bow. Zhang was almost overwhelmed by the honour. “You are too kind, much too kind! Brother Liu,” he said, pointing to his son, “this wretch never makes respectable friends. Who would have thought he would meet a man like you? A blessing for our family indeed!” “Uncle, you will make my face red before I have even drunk any wine.” “Quite right! Let us drink. Drinking makes friendship stronger; gambling makes it weaker.” He scolded his son again. “This wretch loves gambling. Nine times out of ten, when I go to a gambling house, I find him there.” “You need not complain about others,” the son retorted. “If you go ten times and find me there nine, I still go once less than you!” “Listen to him!” Zhang’s two wisps of yellow moustache quivered with anger. “The wretch twists every argument!”

The sight of a father and son each so far from the proper dignity of his role amused Liu greatly. “I suppose my young friend is your only son,” he said. “Spoiled from childhood!” “His mother spoiled him. A misfortune for the family, Brother Liu. We make ourselves ridiculous before you.” “Not at all. I find him most capable and personable—admirably suited to dealing with the world.” This touched the father’s pride. Composing his face, he replied, “Brother Liu, jade must be polished before it becomes useful. The wretch has a sort of cleverness, but he needs to follow someone and gain experience. We can discuss that later. First, let us drink.” The three sat around the brazier, eating and drinking, with the usual preliminary talk of inconsequential matters.

When enough had been said, the father signalled with his mouth. The son rose, went out of the hall, looked around, and called loudly to a servant. “Guisheng, tell the gatekeeper that the master is unwell today and will receive no visitors. If anyone asks for me, say I am out. Official business can be brought to the bureau this afternoon.” This exclusion of outsiders announced that a confidential discussion was about to begin. Liu prepared himself and waited. “Brother Liu, are you a close relative of Xueyan?” The question itself was ordinary, but the form of address was significant. Instead of calling him Intendant Hu, Zhang used his personal name. This suggested both familiarity and equality. Thinking further, Liu felt that Zhang might be implying that old resentments were forgotten and important matters could now be discussed freely. If that reading was correct, it was a good sign. Yet one could know a man’s face without knowing his heart. Remembering Hu’s warning to reveal only a third of what he knew, Liu answered cautiously. “Yes, we are related.”

“How do you address one another?” “Xueyan belongs to the generation below mine.” “Ah! Then, as his elder, you ought to be my honoured uncle. Yet you and my son call each other friends. We shall never straighten out the generations. Brother Liu, it is best if we all meet as equals.” “You are too kind. I shall call you Master Zhang.” Liu had no wish to spend more time on ceremonial niceties. He went straight on. “Xueyan mentioned you too, as a friend from difficult times. He particularly asked me to call on you when I returned to Hangzhou and convey his good wishes.” “Friends from difficult times—that is perfectly true. Before Xueyan prospered, we saw each other in the teahouse every day. Then came his fortunate connection with Governor Wang, and he rose straight into the clouds. His eyes rose with him. He scarcely noticed his poor friends any more, and we could no longer presume on his acquaintance. A friendship forged in hardship became a mere nodding acquaintance.” Liu listened quietly to this complaint, then naturally offered an explanation. “He became busy, and some lapses in courtesy were inevitable. But as to friendship, I am not shielding my own relative when I say that Xueyan has always managed at least to keep faith with others.” “Yes, he knows how to conduct himself in the world,” Zhang conceded. “Since he is good enough to remember me, and times are what they are, whatever was unpleasant in the past should be wiped away. Let us be friends afresh.” “That is exactly his wish,” Liu replied. “After all, we belong to the same place. Leaves return to their roots; one day we shall all be together again. Xueyan is taking care everywhere to leave room for future meetings.” It was beautifully put. Without openly admonishing Zhang, Liu had urged him to leave just such room himself. Already anxious about his service to the rebels, Zhang felt all the more strongly that he ought to renew his friendship with Hu.

“I think the same. None of us is young any longer, and with times like these—truly, we have all lived a second life. Looking back on the past and ahead to the future, we cannot afford to remain foolish. There are some things,” Zhang said resolutely, “on which I should like your advice.” Liu drew his chair nearer, fixed his eyes on Zhang, and assumed a manner of grave, sincere attention. “Let us speak plainly. Xueyan’s patron was Governor Wang, who is now dead. His other support was Governor-General He, who, they say, is hard put to save himself. In that case, Brother Liu, what has Xueyan left to rely on?” The question went to the heart of the matter. Liu dared not answer carelessly. Better slow than wrong, he thought. He nodded while considering. He could invent a plausible tale about Hu’s relations with some great man in the capital or some provincial governor, but Zhang would never believe it. That would be a foolish course. A more independent answer would be more persuasive. “Patrons are unreliable. Ability and friends are what count. With ability and friends, a man will naturally find a patron.” He added, “Master Zhang, those are bold words. Please do not take offence.” “Good!” Zhang was plainly impressed. “Those two sentences show that you are a man of substance yourself.” “You flatter me. I am merely talking.”

“Let me ask you something else, Brother Liu.” Zhang lowered his voice almost to a whisper. “What do you think of the general situation?” This could not be answered lightly. Liu glanced at the son, who understood and nodded emphatically: he could speak freely. “I used to be like you, Master Zhang, shut in a jar, with everything dark around me. This time I spent a few days in Shanghai. People from every quarter gather in the foreign settlement, and news travels quickly. I heard a little. Everyone is saying that this will not last.” As he spoke, he pointed to his hair, indicating the Long-haired Rebels. Zhang said nothing. He took up his water pipe and smoked, bubbling away for some time before asking, “Tell me why it will not last.” “That cannot be explained in a few words—”

Liu was an excellent speaker, and the government troops were in fact fighting very well. With both advantages on his side, he analysed the situation so persuasively that Zhang became entirely convinced. Zhang also knew that he and his son had bad reputations. They needed some extraordinary deed, a conspicuous service to their native place, to cover their many misdeeds and make people see them anew. With this opportunity through Hu before him, how could he let it pass? “Brother Liu, I have made up my mind. Tell Xueyan that, when the government troops arrive to drive out the rebels and recover Hangzhou, I shall assist them from within. When that day comes, he must help clear my name.” “Clear it? Much more than that!” Liu replied. “The court will reward you and appoint you to office. An office earned through military service is more honourable than one obtained by purchase.” And so it proved. When Hangzhou was recovered, father and son were credited with opening the gates to welcome Provincial Treasurer Jiang Yili. The son received a certificate granting him the seventh rank and was appointed to the reconstruction bureau. The father took the opportunity to insist that Hu must be invited back to direct Hangzhou’s reconstruction.

Jiang Yili thoroughly agreed. The special mission to bring Hu back therefore fell to young Zhang. On reaching Shanghai, he settled at the Changfa Inn, a lodging favoured by travelling officials. He then found the address Liu had given him and sent a junior attendant from the inn to fetch him. “I reckoned you must be due, and here you are.” Without wasting words, Liu said exactly what Zhang wanted to hear. “I shall take you to see my relative first. Once your business is settled, you can enjoy yourself to your heart’s content.” “Old Liu, I am now a member of the Zhejiang Reconstruction Bureau, with the seventh rank. Provincial Treasurer Jiang has specially sent me to invite His Honour Hu back to Hangzhou. That is all I have to say.” “Good. I understand. Let us go at once.” Zhang took out his seventh-rank official robes and changed in front of his visitor. Once dressed, he looked embarrassed, feeling rather like a monkey in an official hat. Liu did not laugh. “Have your servant bring a change of clothes,” he said. “That will save us returning here when you want to dress informally.” Zhang’s attendant, Zhang Sheng, was accustomed to serving officials. He already had the visiting cards and clothes bundle ready. The three took a carriage straight to Fukang Bank.

Hu was at a crucial stage in negotiations with several rice merchants. But Zhang had come from a distance and was calling in official dress. Hu had to apologize to his earlier visitors and leave them to greet the newcomer. Zhang had seen him before. As soon as Hu entered the small reception room, without waiting for an introduction, he cried, “Uncle Hu!” and respectfully knelt to kowtow. “You are too kind! My young friend, you are much too ceremonious.” Hu hastily knelt as well. After exchanging bows and helping one another up, they made the usual inquiries after the family, then turned to business. When Zhang had explained his mission, Hu replied, “It is a duty I cannot refuse. I am already making preparations. Enjoy a few days in Shanghai, and we shall travel together.” “Yes, sir.” “Good!” Liu interposed. “The business is settled. Change your clothes, and we can go.”

Liu took Zhang sightseeing in the dazzling foreign settlement. In the evening they ate a Western dinner and watched Peking opera. Zhang’s eyes were opened to an entirely new world. Though tired late at night, he remained excited. Liu stayed with him at the Changfa Inn, and they talked beside their beds until nearly dawn before falling asleep. Hu was still awake. He needed to ship ten thousand shi of rice to Hangzhou. Several merchants had agreed to supply it, only to change their minds at the last moment. He had no choice but to seek out You Wu. Finding him only in the middle of the night, he stated his need directly. You knew the rice trade, but the rice firm collectively run by the Grain-Boat Guild in Songjiang had long since closed. He had been out of touch for some time, and such a vast quantity could not be produced at a word. Over the past few years he had kept out of sight, guarded his speech, and grown increasingly cautious. He dared not promise what he could not guarantee. “Little Uncle, naturally I would not refuse an instruction from you. But you know my circumstances. To buy rice now, I should first have to find the people. Without my old team ready to hand, I cannot be certain of the time. When you could not get your rice into Hangzhou and diverted it to Ningbo, did they not promise to replace the full quantity whenever you needed it?”

Yang Fang had indeed made that agreement with Hu to obtain relief for his native place. But Yang’s position had changed. Implicated in the affair in which Burgevine seized military funds and assaulted an official, he had fallen badly from Li Hongzhang’s favour. His suspension and investigation had not yet been lifted. He had neither the inclination nor the means to fulfil his promise. Hu would not take advantage of a man in distress and had decided to find his own solution. After hearing the explanation, You said admiringly, “Little Uncle, you truly stand by your friends. But Yang is not like you. He made a considerable fortune out of the Ever-Victorious Army, which is also why Governor Li will not let him off. Even if you do not require replacement rice, he should repay the money. It cost a little over two taels a shi then. Now it is nearly six, and hard to buy even at that price. Surely you do not mean to settle with him at the old rate?” “Certainly not. He must pay me at the current market price. Otherwise I shall proceed against him through official channels and see whether he can bear it.”

“Then the money need not worry us,” You said, nodding. “But, Little Uncle, finding ten thousand shi will be extremely difficult. Jiangsu has been short of grain these past two years and has depended on shipments from Huguang and Jiangxi. Last year, when Governor Li bought rice to send to the capital, he had to purchase foreign rice as well. Thirty thousand men worked for two months before the quantity was complete. I fear you cannot obtain ten thousand shi within a month now.” “It is not a month. The month must include delivery to Hangzhou. The purchase must be complete in twenty days at most.” “That is harder still. I doubt even the authorities could manage it.” “If we can do what the authorities cannot, that will show our ability.” The remark put You’s capabilities to the test. Having spoken of difficulty twice, he could hardly do so a third time. He remained silent, considering every possibility at length, before sighing. “We can only all put our heads to it.”

They went off in different directions to seek help. In the end it was the Seventh Lady who found the proprietress of Dafeng Rice Firm, known as the Fair-faced Tigress. She combined rice due for delivery to the capital with two thousand shi of foreign rice held at Jardine Matheson, making eighty-five hundred shi for Hu. You found the remainder, quickly bringing the total to ten thousand. Negotiations with Yang yielded fifty thousand taels towards the purchase price. Hu temporarily made up the shortfall from the hundred and twenty thousand he was raising to return to Wang’s surviving family. Everything went smoothly. Within thirteen days, the sea junks had spread their sails. Following the same route as the first expedition, they sailed by way of Haining and the Qiantang River to the waters outside Hangzhou’s Wangjiang Gate.

Zhang went ahead to Hangzhou. At Hu’s suggestion, he said only that a few hundred shi of rice were to be donated to the authorities. With a handsome gift he then cultivated Battalion Officer Zhang, who commanded at Wangjiang Gate, and arranged how they were to be received. He came out in a small boat to meet them and report. His detailed account of conditions in Hangzhou was far from encouraging. Hu listened, depressed, and remained silent for a long time. As a close relative and an elder, Liu could speak to him with comparative freedom. “Xueyan, are you afraid someone is plotting against you?” “Do you mean someone might accuse me to Governor-General Zuo? That is nothing. They cannot harm me that way.” “What troubles you, then?” “How can I be easy? Great difficulties lie ahead, and danger is before our eyes.” The polished phrasing was unlike Hu’s usual speech, making both men more curious. Liu understood him better than Zhang did. He knew what the future difficulties meant: Hu had often spoken of the countless tasks of reconstruction after recovery—caring for the dead, relieving the living, and restoring commerce. But what danger threatened them now? “I never imagined the government troops’ discipline would be little better than the rebels’,” Hu said. “What young Zhang has just told us about the city gives real cause for anxiety. Daylight may be safe enough. But once night falls, I fear there will be robbery, arson, rape, and looting.” No wonder he was so troubled. Whether or not he exaggerated, precautions were plainly necessary. Zhang, whose home was in the city, was especially alarmed. “Mr Hu! What can we do?”

“There is one way. But it will be of use only if I can see the man in charge.” Jiang Yili now controlled the whole city. Zhang thought for a moment. “May I ask your wishes, Mr Hu? When we enter, shall we see my father first, or go directly to the man in charge?” “The man in charge, naturally.” “Very well.” Zhang could be decisive too. “Old Liu, let us divide the work. Once we land, please help Battalion Officer Zhang with the unloading. Everything is disordered just now. By helping, I mean directing the labourers. There is no need for you to concern yourself with anything else.” Liu understood the implication. He was not to assume responsibility for guarding the grain. If stragglers or wandering soldiers tried to extort it by force or persuasion, he could leave the matter to the officer. “I understand. Let us agree where to meet afterwards.” “At my house,” Zhang replied. “I shall ask the officer to have you escorted there.” Hu opened a small chest containing a serviceable suit of official robes and the hat insignia of the third rank. By the time he had changed, they had reached the shore.

Reached it, that is, as nearly as they could. Laden with rice, the sea junks drew considerable water. The landing outside Wangjiang Gate had fallen into disrepair, and silt had made the shallows dangerous. Coming too close would risk running aground. Fortunately, a generous reward inspired willing service. Battalion Officer Zhang had taken considerable pains. He had found a large abandoned house, posted soldiers to clean and guard it as a warehouse, and held small boats ready to transfer the cargo. Now, on examining the conditions, he changed his plan and ordered the soldiers to wade through the shallows with the loads, which would be quicker and simpler. Only one small boat was used, carrying Hu, young Zhang, Liu, and Hu’s attendant Changgui ashore. Once the arrangements had been explained, Hu and Zhang entered the city, accompanied by Changgui with the visiting-card case under his arm. At the sight of banners fluttering above the walls, emotion overcame Hu and tears streamed down his face. He was thinking of Wang. How wonderful it would have been if the victorious commander entering the city today were Wang, not Jiang! People naturally judged heroes by success or failure. But would the victorious men remember how Wang had clung to the doomed city, exhausting not merely his strength and spirit but his very blood and tears? His suffering and exertions had far exceeded theirs. Hu longed to go first to the place where Wang had died and weep aloud. But the people were still in dire distress. There was no time to release his own grief and indignation. He wiped his eyes, straightened his shoulders, and walked on.

The guard had changed, and a fourth-rank military officer now commanded the gate. Seeing Hu’s third-rank civil robes—the same rank as Jiang’s—he dared not be discourteous. He came forward, saluted, and asked his official title. “This is His Honour Hu,” young Zhang explained. “He has come urgently from Shanghai to discuss important official business with Provincial Treasurer Jiang.” Changgui had already taken a visiting card from the case and handed it over. The officer could not read. He accepted the card, looked at it upside down, and said with an air of comprehension, “So His Honour Hu wishes to see His Honour Jiang!
