May I offer an escort?” “Nothing could be better,” Zhang replied. “Most importantly, could you find two horses?” “There are no horses. But His Honour Hu could take a chair.” A sedan-chair firm stood beside the gate, with two empty chairs still available. There were naturally no bearers. The officer obligingly offered to press some townspeople into service, but Hu firmly declined. To ride in a chair at a time like this would be utterly heartless.

Without horses, and unwilling to use a chair, they had to depend on their own legs. The officer’s soldiers escorted them, however, and they passed everywhere without obstruction, reaching the Sun residence at Sanyuanfang, where Jiang had established his headquarters. When Hu’s card went in, the central gate was opened wide and Jiang’s staff officer came out to conduct the guest inside. Approaching the hall, Hu saw a commander in a yellow riding jacket standing beneath the eaves. Taking him for Jiang, he bowed deeply. “Congratulations! Congratulations!” He was saluting the victory. Jiang returned the bow with clasped hands. “A joy we share!” he replied repeatedly. Young Zhang stepped forwards to make a formal introduction. They entered the hall and sat opposite one another as equals, host and guest, beginning with the customary civilities. Speaking as a member of Zhejiang’s gentry, Hu thanked Jiang. He then observed that, among the southeastern provinces devastated by war, Hangzhou had suffered most. From there he turned to Jiang’s own interests, beginning with a compliment.

Jiang’s courtesy name was Xiangquan, so Hu addressed him respectfully as “Mr Xiang.” “With such a great achievement to your credit, your further promotion to governor of Zhejiang must be close at hand.” “Not necessarily. I would not dare entertain such a vain hope,” Jiang replied. “Ninth Marshal Zeng has a fine elder brother. Once Jinling falls, he will come to take office, and I shall still have to present myself as his subordinate.” Zeng Guoquan held the appointment as governor but had never assumed his duties; Zuo was acting in his place. Jiang understood his own position. In military distinction and in connections alike, replacing Zeng would be no easy matter. Hu saw things differently. “Ninth Marshal Zeng is a great general. Once Jinling is taken, the court will surely have important work for him elsewhere. I believe his governorship of Zhejiang is merely an appointment held from a distance for the present. He will not come to take it up. Do not lose heart, Mr Xiang.” “Oh?” Jiang leaned forwards involuntarily. “May I ask what makes you think he will not come?”

“The reasons are plain. First, Ninth Marshal Zeng has no previous connection with Zhejiang. He knows neither the place nor its people, which makes the appointment unsuitable. Second, his elder brother is modest and most considerate of others. Governor-General Zuo has secured Zhejiang; Commander Zeng will never allow his younger brother to share out Zuo’s territory.” “Ah!” Jiang’s spirits rose. “You see the matter very clearly, Mr Xueyan.” “In my view, the reconstruction of Zhejiang, and especially of the provincial capital, will depend entirely upon you.” Hu paused. Seeing Jiang listen intently, he knew the moment for his real advice had come. Reinforcing his words with a gesture, he spoke earnestly. “The welfare or misery of Hangzhou’s people lies in your hands. Every measure of strength preserved now will save a measure of effort later.” “Quite so!” Jiang rubbed his hands, showing a little anxiety. “Please advise me. I shall spare no effort in preserving that strength.” “With such words, you truly are the saviour of Hangzhou’s people.” Hu rose and bowed. “On their behalf, I thank you.” Jiang hastily returned the courtesy. “You honour me too much!” Touching his chest, he added, “Tell me, Mr Xueyan: where should we begin in preserving what remains after this calamity?”

“Forgive my frankness. You may not be fully aware that men from the various camps are still disturbing the inhabitants.” “Well—” Hu knew that Jiang was in a difficult position. To win battles, commanders inevitably recalled the old saying that generous rewards produced brave men, and promised rewards beforehand. Yet when even military pay and provisions were hard to obtain, where could they find another great sum for bonuses? They were consequently generous at other people’s expense. Whether by hint or tacit permission, soldiers might be allowed, for three days after taking a city, to disregard the two military prohibitions against robbery and rape. Jiang might have made such a promise. To issue a proclamation forbidding these acts now would make him a commander who broke his word and would hamper his ability to lead the troops in future. Hu therefore interrupted quickly. “Mr Xiang, there is more I must explain.”

“You speak too gravely,” Jiang said. Fearing further unwelcome words that might embarrass them both, he tried to forestall them. “Your reproach is entirely just. I can only feel ashamed.” He spoke of shame, not of enforcing discipline. Courteous on the surface, the answer quietly rejected the criticism. Hearing his tone, Hu felt still more certain that his own plan was the wiser course. He could also speak more directly. “As you know, I am a merchant. In business, we speak of fair exchange. The customary courtesy of giving and receiving rests upon the same principle. Your men risked their lives to save Hangzhou’s people. Their exertions and achievements were great. The court may reward them, but if the locality offers no token of appreciation, it is unfair and does them an injustice.” Jiang was puzzled. The opening sounded very worldly, the conclusion exceedingly polite. What was the purpose? He needed to think before answering. This was an impressive man, but a formidable one. Handle him badly and a friend might become an enemy, with considerable consequences. After reflection, Jiang understood. He replied modestly, “You praise us too much. Clearing out bandits for the people is simply our duty. What merit can we claim?”

“Now it is you who are too modest. Since we meet as though we were old friends, I shall speak plainly.” Hu continued with composure. “Our people are famous for their stubborn Hangzhou heads, but they know how to distinguish kindness from injury. The government troops have served us well and deserve a reward. At present, however, nine homes in ten stand empty, and the rebels have stripped the place bare over the past two years. There is really nothing with which to honour the army. Fortunately, a number of Hangzhou gentlemen took refuge elsewhere, and I can reach most of them. I have ventured to take the responsibility of raising a hundred thousand taels and placing it in your hands, so that you may thank the men on our behalf.” Jiang found it difficult to answer. Refusal seemed discourteous, acceptance impossible. Hu’s intention was unmistakable: a hundred thousand taels to purchase absolute freedom from molestation. That was his “fair exchange,” his “giving and receiving.” But although a hundred thousand sounded enormous, divided among several tens of thousands of men it would give each only a modest amount. Whether it could settle the matter remained very doubtful.

Hu could guess the reason for Jiang’s hesitation. To ask, “Is it too little?” would be discourteous; to increase the amount unprompted would make him seem petty, as though haggling over a price. After consideration, he could see only one course: offer a further benefit indirectly. “As for a token of respect to yourself, Mr Xiang, separate arrangements will naturally be made—” “No, no!” Jiang interrupted. “Do not include me. When matters have settled a little and your province’s gentlemen write to their friends in the capital, if they can say that Jiang Yili has done his duty by Zhejiang, I shall be more than grateful.” “That need hardly be said. Mr Xiang, if you do your duty by Zhejiang, Zhejiang will certainly do its duty by you.” “Good! That is well spoken.” Jiang decided. “Then let me thank you for your generosity on behalf of all the men.” He called for an attendant. “Ask Mr Liu to come.” Mr Liu held the provincial examination degree and had purchased entry to the ranks eligible for prefectural and county appointments. Jiang relied upon him as an adviser and employed him as a drafting officer. He was summoned to discuss a proclamation enforcing military discipline and strictly prohibiting interference with the inhabitants.

That was Jiang’s business; Hu need not concern himself with it. His own problem was how to fulfil his promise: how to deliver a hundred thousand taels of shining silver to the provincial treasury for distribution among Jiang’s men. At the thought of the treasury, an inspiration flashed through his mind, like a light seen by someone lost at night. Though it vanished at once, he was certain it had not been an illusion. There must be a way forward. He composed himself and thought quietly for a while. A plan began to take shape. When Jiang’s discussion with his adviser reached a pause, Hu asked, “Where is your military supply office, Mr Xiang?” “Zhejiang’s main supply office is at Yuhang with Commander Zuo. I have a smaller one at Pingyao. He”—Jiang pointed to young Zhang—“is also an officer concerned with supply.” “And the provincial treasury?” “The treasury?” Jiang laughed. “I do not even know whether the provincial treasurer’s office still stands. How can we speak of a treasury?” “The treasury controls the revenue and expenditure of the entire province. It is of the greatest importance and should be restored as quickly as possible. Once its sign is up, people with money to remit will find their way there. Otherwise it is like the saying: carrying a pig’s head for an offering, but unable to find the temple gate. Will that not delay receipts?”

Jiang did not know who might have money to remit at such a time. Still, Hu’s advice sounded entirely reasonable: the treasury ought to be restored promptly. How to restore it, and whom to put in charge, he had no idea. Hu therefore explained the established practice and advantages of a private bank acting for a public treasury. Fukang had formerly handled Zhejiang’s provincial treasury and was willing to serve again. But its old debts and credits still required settlement. To keep the old and new business distinct, he proposed to establish another bank, called Fufeng. “Fufeng is Fukang, with an additional signboard. They will be distinct to the outside world, but internally the same. Either name will do. Mr Xiang, I am doing this entirely for the public service. When Fukang collects outstanding debts and transfers the proceeds to Fufeng, that means transferring them to you. As for what the old treasury owed others, we can judge each case: pay what must be paid and postpone what may wait, giving urgent public needs priority over private claims. Will that not leave us considerable room to manoeuvre?” “Excellent!” Jiang was delighted. “I shall certainly entrust it to you. Fufeng or Fukang, it makes no difference. You are the man I rely on.” “Since you honour me with the commission, I shall do my utmost.” Hu paused briefly. “I shall arrange the hundred thousand taels I have promised, seeing how much ready silver can be raised in Hangzhou. I shall advance the shortfall myself. For convenience, I propose an adjustment between the accounts. That will suit both the supply office and the treasury.” “How would the accounts be adjusted?”

“Suppose we can raise half the amount in silver here. I shall deliver that first. For the other half, I shall give the supply office a note redeemable at any time at my Fufeng bank in Shanghai. If communications are difficult and the money cannot immediately be collected, that need not matter. As Fufeng will be acting for the provincial treasury, any expenditure it advances on the treasury’s behalf will count as cash paid against the note. The treasury and the supply office need only make the corresponding adjustment. Each advance will be deducted, with the accounts settled monthly.” Hu made it sound entirely orderly. Jiang found the argument persuasive, but needed to think carefully before he understood how the entries would work. When he did, a question remained. “What about the treasury’s receipts? Will those first repay your advances?” “How could I do that?” Hu suddenly leaned back against his chair, shaking his head repeatedly, as though the very suggestion were beyond ordinary reason. That gesture alone won Jiang’s complete confidence. He felt that Hu was not merely honest but kind-hearted, genuinely able to treat another man’s interests as his own. Yet the offer seemed almost too generous to believe. Jiang was sure he must have misunderstood and thought it best to clarify. “Mr Xueyan,” he asked cautiously, “do you mean that, up to the amount of the note you give the supply office, your advances for the treasury will count as successive payments redeeming it? Meanwhile, you will still remit the treasury’s receipts in full?”

“Yes, exactly.” Hu continued at once. “Even after that account has been cleared, Fufeng, as agent for Zhejiang’s treasury, must uphold the credit of Zhejiang’s provincial treasurer. We shall continue to make advances.” Jiang was now not merely convinced but grateful. Clasping his hands, he said, “We shall depend upon you in everything. Your bank shall act for the treasury. Would you like an official letter of appointment?” “You are a high provincial official appointed by the court. Your word is sufficient. To me, the presence or absence of a document makes no difference. But to establish confidence with others, I should like a proclamation announcing Fufeng’s appointment.” “Easily done. I shall have it prepared at once.” “I too shall have preparations made overnight, and tomorrow we can post the notices. But”—Hu lowered his voice—“as to which payments should be made, which should wait, and which really must not be made, Fufeng will obey your instructions. Please give us a private sign to guide us.” “A private sign?” Jiang scratched his head, looking perplexed. Young Zhang understood this better than he did. “Your Honour!” he said.

Now that he held office, Zhang performed its courtesies, and “Your Honour” came very naturally. When Jiang turned towards him, he continued. “It is difficult to be the man in charge. Sometimes men ask for provisions or pay, and although you know it cannot be given, you cannot conveniently refuse. You have to endorse the request for payment, and the supply office must of course comply. Yet if there is truly neither money nor food, it must find tactful ways of discussing the matter. Intendant Hu means to spare you embarrassment. With a signal agreed beforehand, he will know your wishes and can find a way to put the applicants off.” “Ah!” Jiang understood. “This has troubled me continually. These are old comrades who have faced death with me, and their pay is already in arrears. When they bring sealed claims for my endorsement, how can I refuse to approve them? But whether the supply office can bear the expenditure is another matter. Often those who should be paid receive nothing, while those who should not receive anything get their money. The supply office has complained to me countless times, sometimes stamping in exasperation, and I have been helpless. A method that lets me play the good man while someone else plays the bad one—that is excellent. Tell me what signal to use.” “You are too kind,” Hu replied. “The signal must change frequently to prevent people from discovering it. For the moment, let us begin simply. If you sign with the single character ‘Li,’ Fufeng will pay in full. If you write the two characters of your given name, ‘Yili,’ it will pay half. If you sign your full name, surname included, that will mean refusal, and Fufeng will find an excuse.” “Excellent!” Jiang clapped his hands. “One conversation with you is worth ten years in office!”

Host and guest looked at one another and laughed, feeling a complete understanding between them. With relations so warm, Hu thought there were matters he need no longer withhold. He glanced at young Zhang, whispered the single word “Rice,” and made a slight motion with his lips. Zhang was quick to read both words and expression. Leaning forwards, he addressed Jiang. “There is something I must report. Battalion Officer Zhang and Intendant Hu’s relative are unloading the few hundred shi of rice. It will be stored temporarily pending instructions. I did not previously explain where it came from. I should now say that Intendant Hu brought it, and the quantity is far greater.” “How much?” Jiang asked with intense interest. “Ten thousand shi or more,” Hu replied. “I brought it expressly to relieve the government troops and the people of Hangzhou. Strictly speaking, the proper course would be to deliver it to you. But I have a difficulty of my own. Could you do me the favour of leaving it temporarily in my custody? After I have seen Governor-General Zuo, it will in any case be placed at your disposal for public distribution. The delay will be only a day or two.” Jiang was astonished. Since the war began, especially in Zhejiang, death by starvation had become commonplace. The sudden appearance of ten thousand shi of rice was like a gift from Heaven. How could he fail to be amazed and delighted?

“Mr Xueyan, your rice is more than charcoal brought through the snow. It is rain in a great drought! I shall send soldiers to guard it and have Officer Zhang assist with the arrangements. At the same time, I shall send a reliable man to escort you to Yuhang to see Commander Zuo. But I hope you will return quickly. There are so many important matters here on which we need your help.” “I shall come back as quickly as I can.” “When do you intend to leave? It must be too late tonight.” “Yes. Early tomorrow morning.” Jiang nodded, summoned his staff and drafting officers, and assigned every task required for Hu. The escort to Yuhang was an officer named He, a nephew through Jiang’s cousin and a trusted subordinate. Hu thanked Officer He warmly, cultivated his goodwill, and arranged to meet him early the next morning at Zhang’s house for their departure.

At the Zhang house, the father naturally spoke to Hu with complete openness, declaring all former grievances forgotten. He honoured him as a distinguished guest, though circumstances allowed courtesy rather than elaborate food and wine. Hu had no appetite for either. After a hasty meal sufficient to fill his stomach, he had many important instructions to give during the one night available. Bitterly short of help, he had to treat young Zhang as a trusted assistant too. There was no time for leisurely discussion; he stated his requirements directly. “First, young Zhang, please consult your father and find a few respected local men. Talk to them and see how much silver can be raised to deliver to Provincial Treasurer Jiang as a temporary loan to Fufeng. Make them understand that this is to save the locality and themselves. Responsibility for the hundred thousand taels rests entirely upon me. If they are willing to share it later, so much the better. If not, I shall bear it alone. But I cannot bring the funds from Shanghai at this moment, and must ask for their help.” Zhang nodded repeatedly. “Leave that to my father and me. You have done everything a man could do for us, Mr Hu. Everyone is grateful. Anyone who has ready silver will certainly be willing to lend it.” “Next, Fukang must reopen at once, and Fufeng’s sign must go up. I should like Third Master Liu to handle the internal arrangements and you to deal with matters outside.” Hu turned to Liu. “First, inspect Fukang’s old premises. If they can be used, have them cleaned immediately. Write two notices on plum-red paper: ‘Fukang will reopen shortly,’ and ‘Fufeng acts for the provincial treasury.’ Post them at once.” “What about the treasurer’s proclamation?”

“Post that on the day we reopen. Next, prepare one or two thousand taels in cash. Most importantly, get several dozen sacks of rice into the premises. Then put up a red notice: ‘All former Fukang staff are requested to return immediately.’ As each man arrives, give him ten taels and five dou of rice. Then our performance can begin.” Young Zhang hurried to speak. “Mr Hu, I must make one thing clear. Since you think well of me, I would go through boiling water and fire at your command. But I must judge my own abilities. I know nothing of banking, and I fear I may not have enough time. I must not delay your important business. Even if you were too kind to reproach me, I could not forgive myself.” “Never mind. I know you are busy. Do what you can.” Hu lowered his voice. “Why do I want to act for the treasury? To establish the name. Fukang has an excellent reputation, certainly, but only old residents of Hangzhou know it. I now want to attract new depositors, and I need another way of appealing to them. Acting for the treasury is the best possible recommendation. If all Zhejiang’s public funds can be entrusted to me, what doubt can remain about my reliability? Once the new depositors think that way, money will flow steadily into Fufeng. Then we shall have both the reward money promised to Jiang and the funds needed for advances on the treasury’s behalf.” With the instructions apparently settled, Liu had something private to say. He signalled to Hu, drew him aside, and spoke quietly. “Getting on well with Jiang Xiangquan will not help. I heard something today from a fairly reliable source. Governor-General Zuo intends to call you to account. He has already spoken of it. If matters go badly, he may name you in a severe memorial of impeachment.” “Do not worry.” Hu remained wholly untroubled. “There is no account of mine that cannot be explained. You cannot believe all the talk outside.”

Seeing his confidence, Liu felt reassured. Young Zhang still had a question. “Your calculation is excellent, Mr Hu. But after all this, who exactly are these new depositors?” “The rebels,” Hu answered. “Once they surrender, they cannot carry away all the silver they have squeezed out of people these two years. They must find somewhere to deposit it.” The new customers Hu meant to attract were the rebels themselves! Neither Zhang nor Liu could have dreamed of such an idea. Both thought his conception remarkable, but both also foresaw considerable trouble. Their astonished and puzzled expressions told Hu what questions lay behind them. He understood perfectly. But there was no time to debate at leisure, and he could only adopt a somewhat peremptory tone. “The plan is sound. Both of you should think along the lines I have laid down. Think about what? How to make them fully confident in entrusting their private savings to Fufeng. Do you understand?” “I understand. But—” Liu stopped. “So do I,” Zhang said. “I know conditions in Hangzhou better. If I find a few people to solicit these deposits, they certainly will not return empty-handed. But before depositing, the men will ask whether their money will be lost at Fufeng. How should I answer?”

“Tell them it will not be lost. But the court’s law must also be respected. If a man himself thinks his deposit may one day be seized by the authorities, he must consider that for himself.” Hu paused, then continued, “In a word, we can offer convenience and accommodation; we cannot break the law. There is no need to force anyone to open an account. We must establish our standing and our credit. Once we have credit, people will come even without a passbook, trusting our word alone.” Neither Liu nor Zhang could immediately fathom this. It seemed somewhat inconsistent. But they could not question him closely now, and the matter was not so urgent that its every implication must be settled that night. They exchanged a glance, reached a silent understanding, and decided to discuss it later.

“Doing the work is easy; conducting oneself is hard.” After a brief silence, Hu unexpectedly began with this complaint, leading into an important explanation—and a warning. “From today, we have much hard work ahead, but it will be worthwhile. Whether it proceeds smoothly depends entirely on how we conduct ourselves. Young Zhang, tell me: how should we behave now?” Zhang considered, then smiled slightly. “Conduct comes down to good faith and loyalty. Since we are helping Governor-General Zuo, we should clench our teeth and help him to the end.” “If we intend to help him through to the end, we must stand openly on his side,” Liu added. “But that will offend a great many people.” “Try to please everyone and you please no one,” Hu replied. “We need only examine our hearts. If we can say we have done our duty by the court and the people, with a clear conscience, there is nothing to fear. It is late. Let us go to bed.” None of them slept well that night. There was too much to think about, and still more emotional excitement. Shanghai and Hangzhou had both been secured; the conclusion of the siege of Jinling could not be far away. What would the situation be then? How were the scattered and unattached soldiers to be dealt with? Should they be discharged or retained? Everywhere were questions, perplexing beyond measure.

Suddenly Hu Xueyan heard someone outside the wall striking a gong and wooden clapper. A watchman was making his rounds. The long-besieged city had only just been recovered; everywhere still bore the confusion of war, yet a night watchman was abroad. As the “tok, tok, clang; tok, tok, clang” drew nearer, Hu felt the joy and gratitude of a man hearing footsteps in an empty valley. His mood changed. Everything before him vanished from his mind, and he remembered his boyhood: snug beneath his quilt on a cold night, hearing a distant cry borne on the northwest wind—“In this bitter winter weather, take care with fire and candles!” How incomparably peaceful it had felt. That was the sound of settled times, and now he heard it again. Suddenly invigorated, he could no longer remain in bed. All three men slept in the same room. He rose quietly, unwilling to disturb Liu Bucai and young Zhang, but Zhang had already noticed. “What are you doing, Mr Hu?” “Have you not fallen asleep?” “No. Have you?” “Neither have I.” “We are all alike,” Liu said from behind his bed curtains. “I have been listening all along. There is quite a stir outside. Treasurer Jiang has kept his word; his efforts to restrain the troops are already taking effect.” “Mr Hu has earned merit in Heaven’s hidden account.” Young Zhang, suddenly inspired too, sprang from bed. “There is more to do these next two days than we can finish. Who has time to sleep?” As soon as they rose, a light appeared in the Zhang family’s kitchen. They washed and drank tea. Zhang expected Hu to return to their unfinished business, but he asked instead, quite at his ease, “Did you hear the watchman’s clapper just now?” “Yes. Everything in Hangzhou has changed except Old Zhou the watchman. He has made his rounds every night, without missing a single one.” Hu’s expression grew solemn. “Remarkable! Truly remarkable! How old is he?”

“Over sixty. He was still strong, though I do not know how he is now.” “He has escaped starvation and still makes his rounds every day. He must have an unusually blessed constitution,” Liu observed. “A pity he is only a watchman.” “Third Master, that is not the way to look at it. There are many tasks in the world that require no special talent. Anyone can do them. The question is whether you are willing to do them, and whether you take them seriously. A man who does that is remarkable.” Hu turned to Zhang. “Do something for me: ask Old Zhou whether he would like to change his occupation.” “Change his occupation? Do you mean to advance him, Mr Hu?” “Yes. Or you might say I mean to make use of his strengths. We are short of hands. We cannot let a man so faithful to his duty pass us by. I should like him to help us.” “I am sure he would be willing. I only fear there is little he can do.” “I shall put him in charge of a warehouse. If he cannot manage, I shall give him someone to help. He need only inspect it at the proper hours, as he makes his rounds.” At this point, the Zhangs’ manservant laid the table for breakfast. It was only leftover rice boiled into a pot of thin porridge, with a single dish of salted greens. But the hungry are easily fed, and after their half-night’s rest the men’s appetites were keen. Everything tasted unusually good. “I have not eaten anything so delicious in years!” Hu declared contentedly. “Third Master Liu was right: ‘If it serves the purpose, it is good.’ At a time like this, rice in hot water and salted greens are more precious than every delicacy of land and sea.” Young Zhang gained another deep insight. The art of employing people was not bound by a single pattern. Its secret was to adapt to time and place. He nodded thoughtfully, knowing now where to look for people Hu could use.

Officer He arrived at dawn with two mounted orderlies and an extra horse. The animal had a distinguished history: a gift from the Mongol prince Sengge Rinchen, it had been carefully tended on Jiang Yili’s orders as a mount reserved for Zuo Zongtang. Now it was being lent specially to Hu. He also brought news. The Longhairs in Yuhang had abandoned that city the previous day and fled towards Huzhou. Zuo was pursuing them in person and was now encamped before Anxi Pass, north of Pingyao. To visit him meant risking arrows and blades. What did Hu wish to do? “Life and death are decreed. If the Commander-in-Chief can go there, so can I. There is no need for fear.” “But it is a long journey. We cannot arrive in a day, and there is nowhere to lodge along the way. That is another difficulty.” “We shall go as fast as we can. If we cannot arrive, there is nothing to be done. With you beside me, I feel quite at ease.” It was merely a casual reply, but He heard it as a deeply sincere expression of trust. He therefore took particular care over the arrangements. After some thought, he asked, “Your Excellency, can you ride at speed?” “I can manage.” “And your steward?” “I am afraid he cannot.” “Then there is no need to bring him. We have four men here; let them attend to anything you require.” He added, “We can use the relay system, changing horses. That will be faster.” An urgent dispatch travelled by changing horses at each post. Since each animal covered only one stage, it could be driven at full speed; this was much faster than riding the same horse all the way. Fine as the prince’s mount was, it could serve for only one stage, and if it were lost in an exchange it would be troublesome to recover. Hu therefore suggested finding another. “Easy enough. We shall exchange it at the stables before we leave.”

Hu took leave of the Zhang household, saying that he would certainly return on the morning of the third day. He and Officer He first exchanged the horse at the provincial treasurer’s headquarters, then rode out through Wulin Gate and galloped to Gongchen Bridge. There He found a military camp he knew, obtained fresh, good horses, and set off northwest. There were naturally checkpoints, delays and moments of danger, but they came safely through them all. At three in the afternoon, they reached Pingyao and at last stopped for food and rest. Enquiries placed Zuo at Anxi Pass, eighteen li farther north. “It is a mountain road, and a difficult one,” He urged earnestly. “Your Excellency, to speak frankly, you are a southerner. As the saying goes, southerners sail boats; northerners ride horses. You do not ride very well. For safety’s sake, we should rest a night before continuing. What do you think?” Hu reflected that he knew neither the place nor its people and could not insist on having his way. Even if they reached Anxi that night, he could not see Zuo. He agreed, with one condition: “We must leave at first light tomorrow.” “Of course. I shall not delay your business.” With that settled, they could rest at leisure. Imperial troops had long been stationed at Pingyao, and its market was fairly prosperous, with many food stalls. Hu never put on official airs and personally invited the four orderlies to drink with him. They were so ill at ease, however, that he finally let them sit at another table. He drank with Officer He and listened to everything he had to say about Zuo.

“Our Commander-in-Chief is excellent in every respect but his temper. When he flies into a rage, though, you must not be afraid. The more frightened you are, the worse it gets.” “He responds to firmness rather than submission,” Hu said. “That sort of man is easier to deal with.” “Yes. But you must not try to outdo him in firmness. The best thing is to take no notice, let him finish his scolding and say all he has to say, then present your own reasons. He will regard you differently.” Hu found this advice valuable and raised his cup to He. Then he asked, “How many years have you been with Treasurer Jiang?” “We are close relations. I have always been with him.” “Forgive a presumptuous question: how does the Commander-in-Chief regard him? Does he consider him a possible replacement for himself?” “Hardly!” He replied. “What sort of man is our commander? He thinks himself another Zhuge Liang. Who could replace him?” To another listener, those casual words might have meant little. To Hu, they led to a most important decision.

He had continually pondered his future course and the powerful support he would need to rebuild his fortunes and begin anew. Jiang Yili had seemed an honest, kindly man. If their friendship grew close, he might become a second Wang Youling, trusting Hu’s advice and treating him like a brother. That would be far pleasanter than serving Zuo, whose temper was notorious. Now He’s words brought sudden understanding. Zuo would never spare no effort to advance Jiang as He Guiqing had advanced Wang. A provincial governorship was no small appointment. Unless another suitable post could be found for Zeng Guoquan, Jiang himself proved fully qualified, and Zuo gave him exceptional support, the seal of the governor of Zhejiang was unlikely to fall into his hands. Hu therefore had only one course: devote himself wholeheartedly to Zuo. They rose in the middle of the night and set off at dawn, taking three hours to cover the eighteen li of mountain road. Zuo’s headquarters occupied a temple to Lord Guan. Even amid the haste of campaigning, he maintained considerable gubernatorial ceremony. A great sedan chair covered in green wool stood before the temple. Against the screen wall were several vermilion boards on tall poles, their inscriptions in imitation Song lettering picked out in gold. One read, “Imperially Appointed Superintendent of Military Affairs in Zhejiang”; another, “Concurrently Acting Governor of Zhejiang”; a third, “First-Rank Insignia, Minister of War, Concurrent Right Censor-in-Chief, Governor-General of Fujian and Zhejiang”; and a fourth, “Granted the Right to Wear the Peacock Feather”. The last was less glorious, and a lifelong grievance to Zuo: his examination distinction was merely “Successful Candidate in the Hunan Provincial Examination of the Renchen Year, the Twelfth Year of Daoguang”. He held only the provincial degree.

Looking inside, Hu saw two lines of armed guards stretching from the main entrance to the statues of Guan Ping and Zhou Cang before the great hall. Several military officers with blue hat-buttons were there too. Unwilling to affront Zuo’s imposing dignity, Hu led his horse aside, took out his formal visiting paper and asked He to present it. A long time passed before an officer of the headquarters guard emerged with it in his hand. Though he had plainly seen Hu, he called out in an official tone, “Which gentleman is Intendant Hu from Hangzhou?” Hu nodded, assumed an official manner himself, and advanced with measured steps. “I am.” “The Commander-in-Chief will receive you.” “Very well. Please lead the way.” They entered the gate but passed to the west of the hall through a side entrance into a small courtyard, also crowded with guards. An attendant in a grey cloth gown was more courteous: he lifted the door curtain and motioned Hu inside. A short, stout old man sat at a square table by the window, holding a long-stemmed pipe in his left hand while the brush in his right flew across the paper. He seemed unaware of Hu’s footsteps. Hu could only wait. When the brush was laid down, he gathered up his gown, made his formal obeisance and announced himself. “Hu Guangyong, expectant circuit intendant of Zhejiang, pays his respects to Your Excellency.” “Oh, so you are Hu Guangyong!” Zuo’s authoritative eyes seemed to flash as they surveyed him from head to foot. “I have heard of you for a long time.” This was no compliment, and Hu saw no reason for modest disclaimers. “Your Excellency has achieved an extraordinary victory. I have come expressly to congratulate you.” “Indeed? You are quick to anticipate the prevailing wind. No wonder you were known as a capable officer in Governor Wang’s day.”

Hu naturally caught the sarcasm, but there was no need to dispute it yet. The first necessity was to secure a seat. Zuo could not be ignorant of official etiquette: a civil officer visiting a governor or governor-general, however low his rank, was entitled to sit. His deliberate omission of the invitation was meant to humiliate Hu, who must first find a way to meet it. The moment he considered it, a solution came. Gathering his gown again, he made another obeisance. “I have come not only to congratulate Your Excellency, but to thank you. The people of Zhejiang have been hanging upside down in their distress, and you have rescued them.” Everyone spoke of Zuo’s “Hunan mule” temper, but this repeated courtesy at last turned the mule. “I do not deserve it.” His voice remained cool, suggesting that flattery would not move him, but he finally answered courtesy with courtesy. “Please be seated, Intendant.” An attendant had long been waiting with a tray, unable to offer tea while the guest remained standing. Now he placed a covered cup on the small table at Hu’s side. Hu inclined himself and breathed more freely. So long as appearances were not humiliating, he thought, they could talk.

“During my two years in Zhejiang, I have heard a great deal about you,” Zuo said without a smile. “I hear you are very grand.” “I would not presume to say so.” Hu leaned forward slightly. “May I ask what Your Excellency means by ‘grand’?” “They say you live and enjoy yourself like a prince. It may be exaggerated, but one can imagine something of the truth.” “Yes. I shall not conceal it. Compared with expectant officials living in hardship, I am very comfortable.” He admitted it frankly without explaining the source of his comfort, which seemed to leave Zuo with nothing to say. After a pause, Zuo spoke directly. “I have received a number of complaints describing your conduct. Such reports may not all be invention. I intend to investigate. If they prove true, my duty to reform the administration will oblige me to impeach you by name in the strongest terms.” “Certainly. If I have acted unlawfully, I shall willingly accept the punishment Your Excellency seeks. But I believe I have not dared commit evil or practise corruption for private gain. Governor Wang recognised and favoured me, and I swore to share life and death with him. In carrying out my duties, I shrank neither from toil nor from resentment. That may indeed have earned me enemies.” “Whether you committed evil or practised corruption remains to be established. As for your oath to share life and death with Governor Wang, that is difficult to believe. He has died at his post, while you remain perfectly well, do you not?”

“If Your Excellency reproaches me for failing to follow Governor Wang beneath the earth, I have nothing to say. But if you believe that dying for loyalty or honour has a rightful purpose, whereas dying merely to accompany a friend is lighter than a feather and no course for a gentleman, then I have a few words in my defence.” “Speak.” “You mean that I shared Governor Wang’s hardship in the besieged city but left him at the crucial moment, making my oath to share his life and death a deception?” “Yes!” Zuo stared hard at him. “How do you explain yourself? I should like to hear.” “First, may I ask Your Excellency this? When Hangzhou was surrounded and Governor Wang struggled to hold it, shedding blood rather than tears from his eyes, what was he hoping for?” “Reinforcements, naturally.” “Yes,” Hu said in a low voice. “Li Yuandu’s army was at Quzhou. We tried every means to hasten its arrival, but it never came. We therefore had no choice but to prepare for a prolonged defence. What does a beleaguered city need in order to hold out?” “Food, naturally. ‘To the people, food is Heaven.’” “True enough. When every last resource is exhausted, civilians may exchange their children to eat. But what happens when soldiers have no food? I need not say; Your Excellency knows far better than I do. Governor Wang discussed it with me and asked me to buy rice in Shanghai.” Hu suddenly raised his voice. “Though he had entered official life by purchase, Governor Wang had read books. He told me the story of the orphan of Zhao in the Records of the Historian. If the city could not be held, he said, its defender could only die, and that was easy. To buy rice in Shanghai was harder, like preserving the orphan’s life. He wanted me to be Cheng Ying, who saved the orphan of Zhao. That was, of course, his generous opinion of me. But consider, Your Excellency: as governor, he was responsible for defending his territory. Even if he could have procured rice, he could not leave Hangzhou. I was the only one who could go to Shanghai for it. I had no right to refuse.” “Mm. And afterwards?” Zuo asked. “Did you obtain the rice?” “Of course. But—” Hu’s voice sank sorrowfully. “It was of no avail.”

He then explained how he had brought the rice to the Qiantang River outside Hangzhou, how every effort to open a supply route had failed, how he had bowed towards the city and withdrawn in tears, and how he had used the rice to relieve Ningbo. He said nothing of the grave illness there that had nearly killed him; that would sound like a claim to merit and would only weaken his account with unnecessary detail. Zuo listened closely, then tilted his face upwards in thought for a long while. At last he said something Hu had never expected: “You seem to have read quite a few books yourself!” Hu was startled, then understood. Throughout their exchange his language had been rather polished, and he had referred to the story in the Records of the Historian. Zuo must suspect that he had consulted some learned adviser and prepared a set speech. There was a measure of truth in it: Hu had repeatedly rehearsed what he would say when they met. But he could not admit to having deliberately concocted pleasing falsehoods. “How could I claim to be a learned man? I merely know enough to respect those who are.” “That too is commendable,” Zuo said. “I shall investigate those accusations. If matters are indeed as you describe, that is naturally another question.” “It is more than that. Having received public money, I must account for its use in public business. The money did not come from Your Excellency’s hands, but you are now the head of this province. It is to you alone that I must render my account.” “Oh, you have come to account for official business? For that sum of public money?” Zuo asked. “How much did you receive?”

“Twenty thousand taels. I now return them to Your Excellency in person.” Hu took a red envelope from his clothing and offered it. Zuo refused to accept it. “This is public money. It cannot be handed over privately. Please deal with the military supply office.” He summoned the officer responsible for receipts and payments. The officer took Hu’s note, issued a receipt and affixed the great seal. One official matter seemed settled, but Hu had more to say. “Your Excellency, there is still my commission to account for. I was ordered to procure rice. Until the rice has arrived, I cannot consider the task discharged.” “But—?” Zuo was puzzled, uncertain what Hu meant or how to respond. “To speak frankly, until I delivered that rice, I could not face Governor Wang’s spirit in Heaven. Now, at last, I can truly render an account.” Hu spoke calmly. “I have ten thousand shi of rice on the river outside Hangzhou. Please send an officer to inspect and receive it.” Zuo grew still more bewildered. “What are you saying? There are ten thousand shi of rice?” “Yes.” “On the river outside Hangzhou?” “Yes. Several hundred shi have already been assigned to Treasurer Jiang as military provisions.”

Zuo turned to those beside him. “Who escorted Mr Hu here?” “Officer He.” He was summoned, and Zuo’s first question was, “Do you know whether several hundred shi of military grain have been brought into the city from the Qiantang River?” “They have, Commander-in-Chief.” He pointed towards Hu. “Mr Hu brought them from Shanghai.” “Good. You may withdraw.” Zuo instructed the attendant, “Invite Mr Hu to the kang.” The courtesies changed at once. From being denied a seat to sitting opposite Zuo on the raised platform—how completely fortune could turn in a moment! Hu felt both reflective and triumphant, and naturally more confident of handling him. The attendant moved the covered teacup to the small table on the kang. Hu thanked his host and sat down. Zuo spoke slowly. “With these ten thousand shi of rice, not only can the people of Hangzhou be saved; I can be confident of clearing all Zhejiang. What you have done is beyond expectation, a service of immeasurable merit. I shall not be the only man grateful to you.” “Your Excellency is too generous.”

“I speak the truth. But there is something else I must say frankly. At current prices, ten thousand shi of rice must be worth fifty or sixty thousand taels. The supply office cannot pay so much at once. We have just won a great victory, and the troops must be rewarded in ready silver. I suggest that you first take back the money you have just returned. Then we can discuss how to pay the balance.” “Your Excellency need not trouble yourself. I am donating all ten thousand shi.” “Donating them?” Zuo feared he had misheard. “Yes. They are my contribution.” “That is a great expense. What do you hope to obtain, my friend? You may speak plainly.” “Nothing for myself. First, I do it for Governor Wang; second, for the people of Hangzhou; third, for Your Excellency.” “I am deeply obliged.” Zuo clasped his hands. “I shall memorialise the throne at once and request a commendation.” “I am naturally grateful for your favour. But there is something I must say, though it may seem unappreciative. It is like a bone in my throat. Please do not be angry if I speak.” “Not at all, not at all!” Zuo answered repeatedly. “Speak freely.” “I am certainly not giving this rice to obtain an imperial commendation. I am a businessman. I know how to get things done, not how to make a career in office.” “How well said! ‘Get things done, not make a career in office’!” The words went straight to Zuo’s heart. He slapped the kang table and spoke loudly, his admiration unmistakable. “I believe Your Excellency too thinks only of getting things done, never of honours and wealth,” Hu continued. “In my view, your temperament is exactly the opposite of that of a certain great man today.” The first part was perfectly judged flattery. The last naturally aroused Zuo’s particular interest. He leaned forward. “Whom do you mean?” “Governor Li of Jiangsu. He knows how to make a career; Your Excellency knows how to do the work.” Hu added, “It is not that you do not know how to make a career. You merely disdain it.” “Ah, how refreshing! How refreshing!” Zuo tilted back his face and shook his head, looking like a man who had found someone who understood him.

Hu stopped while his flattery was well received and offered no more extravagant compliments. Instead he said modestly, “I have been speaking at random, taking liberties before Your Excellency.” “My friend,” Zuo said seriously, “you must not undervalue yourself. Among all the grandees in purple and scarlet at court, few possess your insight. What are the characters of your courtesy name?” “Xueyan: xue for snow, yan for rocky crags.” “Brother Xueyan, you have presumably spent these past few years in Shanghai. You must know Li Shaoquan’s conduct well. Compare him with me.” “In what respect?” Hu asked. “Our achievements.” “Very well.” Hu thought for a moment. “Governor Li’s recovery of Suzhou was naturally a great achievement. But he succeeded through the efforts of others. It cannot compare with your struggle alone, which was far more difficult and therefore more admirable.” “That, at least, is a fair statement,” Zuo said. “I have suffered two disadvantages: my territory is less favourable, and I have fewer able men.” “Yes.” Hu nodded gravely. “Governor Li does know how to employ people.” “Then may I ask a rather presumptuous question? With your great abilities and your record under Governor Wang, did he never think of recruiting you?”

“He did. I could not go.” “Why?” “First, Governor Li was prejudiced against Governor Wang. To serve him despite that would show too little spirit.” “Good! And second?” “Second, I am a Zhejiang man and wish to work for Zhejiang. Besides, Governor Wang left me unfinished public business: this money for buying rice. I had to account for it.” “Admirable, admirable! Brother Xueyan, you are truly trustworthy.” Zuo called out, “Attend me! Mr Hu will stay for a simple meal.” According to official custom, when a superior made such an invitation, the attendants had two duties: to ask the guest to change into informal clothing, and to arrange his move to a garden reception room or even the private apartments. Ordinarily Hu would bring a servant on a visit, carrying a bundle of clothes and ready to help him change. Today he had come alone and naturally had nothing informal to wear. Zuo therefore ordered the attendant to bring one of his own lightly padded gowns for “Mr Hu”. Zuo was short and stout, Hu tall and lean. The sleeves hung loosely, while the hem stopped far too high, exposing a long stretch of boots caked with yellow mud. The effect was not merely inelegant but rather comical. Yet the kindness behind it was touching, and Hu felt thoroughly comfortable in it. Moving to a garden reception room was equally impossible. In this little temple, where could spare rooms be found? The governor-general’s audience room, office and bedroom were all confined here. Behind the temple, however, was a small earthen hill with a thatched pavilion, a modest place from which to enjoy a view. To escape interruptions from officers seeking audiences, Zuo ordered the meal served there.

The wine was naturally excellent. Shaoxing had long since been recovered, and the province’s highest official was supplied with aged wine that had survived the war undamaged in its cellars. The food was Hunan style. Though only two men were dining, there were great dishes and long chopsticks. Finally the cook, wearing a hat with a red tassel, personally brought a food box. He opened it to reveal nothing more than a small plate of Hunan cured pork. Why such ceremony? “My wife prepared this herself,” Zuo explained. “It came all the way from Hunan through countless difficulties. It is no longer good to eat. I keep it simply to remind myself of all the pains she once took to manage our affairs in trying circumstances.” Hu had heard something of Madam Zhou. She came from a wealthy family, but when she married Zuo he was a poor scholar. She understood that this man, who “owned not half a mu yet worried for all under heaven”, possessed extraordinary talent. Though he repeatedly failed the metropolitan examination, one day he would burst through the wall and soar. She encouraged and consoled him with unceasing care. Later he moved into her family’s home. The large Zhou clan could hardly admire a poor son-in-law with such a temper. Afraid that her husband might storm away in anger, yet anxious to uphold his family’s dignity, she mediated on every side until mind and strength were exhausted. Now, at last, her day of vindication had come. Hu drew another lesson from this. Gratitude, more than anything else, lay behind Zuo’s present respect for his wife. Clearly he was a man of deep feeling, unwilling to fail those who had helped him. Compared with Li Hongzhang, who controlled his subordinates through rewards, advancement and political manoeuvring, this was the man Hu would rather befriend wholeheartedly. Accordingly, when Zuo asked questions, he held back still less. From Hangzhou’s reconstruction to raising military funds, he had a method for everything and spoke without reserve. Host and guest soon approached the point where all formality could be discarded and nothing was beyond discussion.

Their meal lasted two double-hours. At last Zuo sighed. “Brother Xueyan, now I am troubled. In what capacity should I ask your help? Reconstruction is the immediate need, yet the monthly military payroll of two hundred and fifty or sixty thousand taels still has no assured source, and there too I must rely on you. If only you could be in two places at once! Tell me yourself: what would you like to do?” “Raising funds is important, but once a method is established, anyone whose integrity can be trusted can carry it out.” Hu sounded apologetic. “I have a small selfish wish: to do something for my native place.” “How is that selfish? It shows your generous and honourable heart. Since the war began, Hangzhou has suffered most grievously. Its reconstruction has countless threads to draw together. As acting governor, I cannot evade responsibility. To have a man of your ability, willing to labour and bear complaints, helping me serve his homeland—that is excellent indeed.” Zuo asked, “You have met Jiang Xiangquan, I suppose?” “Yes.” “What do you think of him?” “He is very straightforward. We got on extremely well.” “Excellent, excellent!” Zuo asked gladly, “Then you must have discussed the work of reconstruction?” “Not in depth. But Treasurer Jiang has honoured my little bank by entrusting it with the business of the provincial treasury. I shall do my utmost to meet the immediate expenses.” “Better still. Nothing is more urgent than relief. With ten thousand shi of rice, the soldiers and people can be fed for a month or two, until further supplies arrive. And with your bank meeting the treasury’s expenses, there will be advances available for helping the wounded and caring for the dead. Relief in Hangzhou should therefore begin at once. I propose establishing a reconstruction bureau. Brother Xueyan, will you serve as its director?” “Yes.” Hu answered solemnly. “Both public duty and private feeling forbid me to decline.” “Then I thank you on behalf of Hangzhou’s people.” Zuo clasped his hands. “I shall have the papers prepared at once and send them through Jiang Xiangquan.”

This arrangement was precisely what Hu wanted. He had always taken “arouse no jealousy” as his guiding rule. His status was much the same as Jiang Yili’s, but under Zuo he was, after all, only an invited adviser. If he appeared too intimate with Zuo, or even bypassed Jiang to take orders directly, Jiang could hardly feel comfortable. Hu had only to put himself in the other man’s place to see that. Though Hu was present, the appointment would be sent through Jiang. This respected the provincial treasurer’s authority and quietly helped Hu draw closer to him. Such a small official procedure, yet so much lay behind it! Holding office and employing men were no simple matters. His admiration for Zuo increased, and he wanted to do a little more for him—or at least devise a few more useful ideas. Just then Zuo returned to military finance, and Hu decided to offer an ingenious scheme. He had long thought it through. Originally he had meant to sell his wares to a buyer who knew their worth, waiting for the proper price: without sufficient reward, he would not readily have disclosed it. Now, feeling that Zuo had recognised his worth, he emptied his store of ideas before him.

“There are many ways of raising military funds, but broadly they fall into two kinds. One is the likin tax. That depends on a thriving market and cannot be forced. The other is soliciting contributions. In recent years, everyone able to give has already given, and persuasion is difficult. But I have thought of a class of people who can afford to contribute and will certainly be willing. We might turn our attention to them.” “They can afford it and are willing? What could be better?” Zuo asked eagerly. “Who are they?” “The Longhairs. They have held the southeast for more than ten years and amassed a great deal by plunder. Is it not only right that they should now contribute something?” Understanding dawned on Zuo. He nodded repeatedly. “Yes, yes. Go on.” Hu explained that during those years, quite a number of rebels had recognised the right moment, made their fortunes and retired into hiding. Once the Hong-Yang rebellion was suppressed, those who had joined it would naturally be punished by law. Yet though rebellion was an unpardonable crime, many had been forced into it—too many to prosecute. To implicate large numbers and keep society in turmoil would not allow a country exhausted by great disorder to recover. The best course was to leave an opening in the net, a way for people to begin anew. Still, to overlook everything would let them off too cheaply. There should be a mild punishment, with each person free to choose between a beating and a fine. “Your Excellency knows human nature,” Hu said. “People prefer a fine to a beating, unless they cannot afford it. I know there are many who can. Most hide in the foreign settlements, relying on foreign protection, and for the moment the authorities can do little about them. Yet they remain men without a lawful identity, unable to emerge openly. Explain the advantages and risks, appeal to their sense of right: their money was ill-gotten anyway. Why should they not part with some of it in exchange for a fresh start?” “Quite so,” Zuo said with a smile. “Such men will not be content with obscurity. They may want not only to become respectable again, but to take office.” “Exactly.” Hu raised two fingers. “Men of that kind must contribute twice.”

“How so?” “Once to become respectable, and once to become officials. Does entering office not require a contribution?” Zuo laughed. “I had confused myself. In that case, I had better request several thousand blank contribution certificates from the ministry at once.” “Yes. Please send the official request.” “Suppose I obtain them, Brother Xueyan,” Zuo asked deliberately. “Whom shall I entrust with them?” Hu was silent for a while. “Allow me time to find a suitable man and recommend him.” “I do not think you need look. You must undertake the difficulty yourself.” “I fear that—” “No, no!” Zuo waved aside his objection. “Do not decline. Once you have met me, Brother Xueyan, you can no longer insist on your own wishes. That may sound unreasonable, but it is not. I know now that your abilities are as vast as the sea. Never mind these two tasks: you could handle several more with ease. You must have capable men under you. Give me their names, and I shall instruct Jiang Xiangquan to appoint them all. You need only travel between Shanghai and Hangzhou and provide the ideas.” Such confidence reminded Hu of two lines Wang Youling had often repeated during the siege: “Bend every effort to the task; cease only in death.” He answered with spirit. “If Your Excellency believes I can do it, I shall try.” “No need to try. I guarantee you will succeed,” Zuo said. “I want you to attend to both matters together. Military affairs in Zhejiang are at a crucial stage. We must on no account have a disturbance over unpaid wages.” “I shall do my utmost. What I need your instructions on now is the name of this contribution. I suggest ‘penalty contribution’.” “That describes it accurately. But—” He pondered for a long time without finishing.

He plainly had a misgiving, and Hu could imagine it. A scheme of “penalty contributions” might provoke criticism and the charge of “sheltering rebels”, a very serious offence. Yet whether it amounted to sheltering them depended on the circumstances. That and allowing people to reform were superficially similar but different things. Hu kept these thoughts to himself, watching Zuo’s expression closely to see whether he would take responsibility. Zuo naturally would; indeed, that was one of the qualities on which he prided himself. What troubled him was finding a different name. He thought for some time without discovering a suitable term and finally decided to use this one for the present.

Another question followed. “Should this penalty contribution be reported to the throne? What do you think?” “If it is reported first, opposition may prevent the scheme from starting. If it is not, the ministry may later raise procedural objections, or a censor may impeach us. There are advantages and disadvantages on both sides. Your Excellency must decide.” “It must be done. But though I am not afraid of trouble, there is no reason to take the blame unnecessarily. Think again: is there a way to prevent interference while leaving us a defensible position?” “Act impartially in the public interest, and there will always be a way to defend ourselves. I suggest not reporting before we begin. Once the scheme has produced results, memorialise the throne with the amounts collected. Thereafter, notify the ministry directly for its records, providing the basis for the eventual accounting.” “Good! We shall do exactly that.” Zuo was delighted. “‘Act impartially in the public interest, and there will always be a way to defend ourselves’—very well said. But the results you mention are important too. The state once kept more than ten million taels sealed in the inner treasury for major campaigns. That great sum was squandered by Saishang’a and his fellow Banner grandees. So the saying ‘The emperor does not send hungry soldiers on his errands’ no longer applies. Now the court not only sends hungry soldiers and requires each province to find its own military funds; it also demands remittances for the capital’s military expenses. If we establish a productive tax or contribution and are forbidden to continue it, very well: let the court assign us a reliable source for the same amount.” That brought his argument to its conclusion. Hu now understood more deeply Zuo’s position, his thinking and his response to circumstances. Any method of raising military funds that was not utterly wicked could gain his approval.

Hu remained at Zuo’s headquarters for two days before returning to Hangzhou. His homecoming was far more splendid than his departure. An escort of Zuo’s personal guards was only to be expected. What surprised him most, outside Wulin Gate, was a welcoming party of distinguished-looking men. More than half were active members of Hangzhou’s gentry, including Licentiate Zhang; the rest wore official robes, though Hu knew none of them. A great green-covered sedan chair stood in the gateway. What was that for? Puzzled, he asked Officer He, “Have they come to meet me?” Before He could answer, the sight of Liu Bucai and young Zhang settled the question. Zhang ran up beaming, caught the ring of his horse’s bridle and cried, “The news reached us the night before last! We have awaited you like clouds and rain in a scorching drought.” What news? Why such eagerness for his return? Before Hu could ask, a guard officer bowed before his horse. “Please dismount, Your Excellency, and take the sedan chair.” Young Zhang hurried to explain. “Treasurer Jiang sent this officer. The green-covered chair is his own, brought specially for you.” “Yes.” The officer opened a visiting-card box and presented Jiang’s card and invitation. “My superior sent me to attend you. He expressly instructed me to say that he intended to come himself, but several urgent matters require immediate decisions, and he cannot get away. He asks to see you as soon as you arrive. There is much awaiting discussion.” Hu understood. The “news” was his appointment as director of the reconstruction bureau. Everyone’s eagerness, and Jiang’s insistence on seeing him at once, arose naturally from the urgency of relief. The work of reconstruction awaited his decisions before it could begin.

Precisely for that reason, he thought it unwise to see Jiang first. Yet Jiang was now effectively the head of the province. To reject such attentive hospitality would be discourteous; only an excellent excuse would do. Hu thought quickly. By the time he dismounted, his answer was ready. “Please tell your superior that I too am anxious to pay my respects and seek his instructions on many matters. But after these days of travelling back and forth, I am disgracefully dirty. To visit a superior in this state would be disrespectful. Let me wash and change into clean clothes, and I shall come at once. His green-covered chair is not one I am entitled to ride in, but it would be rude to refuse his kindness. Please ask the bearers to follow me with it empty.” He went first to the Zhang house for a short rest and wrote an outline of the principal reconstruction measures and the support he needed from Jiang. Only then did he attend the banquet. They met warmly. Jiang handed him the letter of appointment, and Hu took out a sheet of paper in return. It was headed “Urgent Reconstruction Measures” and contained seven proposals. First: bury the dead within half a month. Great epidemics followed great wars; this would both give the departed rest and prevent disease.

Second: provide free porridge for six months. The government should supply the grain, while impartial local gentlemen supervised its distribution. Third: invite merchants to bring in food, clothing, bricks, tiles, timber and other necessities, exempting these goods from likin to encourage trade. Fourth: investigate those who had died loyally in the disaster, collect accounts of their deeds, and petition the throne for a shrine commemorating the loyal dead. Fifth: trace the families of women rescued from the rebel camps and send them home with trustworthy escorts. Sixth: make every effort to arrange spring cultivation, on which livelihoods in the coming autumn and winter depended. Seventh: reopen the academies and give favourable treatment to scholars.

“Quite right, quite right!” Jiang said. “I agree to everything. If you need men, appointments or official papers, tell me, Mr Xueyan. Whatever lies within my power shall be done.” “Thank you, Mr Xiang, for helping the people of Zhejiang. But there is one matter on which I must ask your exceptional support.” Hu spoke plainly. “The troops must be kept under discipline.” Jiang reddened. He knew his men’s discipline was poor, but offered a defence. “To be honest, the men have gone hungry for so long—” “Mr Xiang,” Hu interrupted, “I shall take responsibility for their pay. Please take responsibility for their discipline.” Jiang reflected that Hu could now see Zuo directly and was said to have all his advice accepted. If he raised the matter with their superior and an order came back down, Jiang would lose face. Better to resolve it himself. He therefore decided on two measures: a proclamation reaffirming military discipline, with offenders to be executed on the spot; and, from the next day, his own presence throughout the day at Guanxiangkou in the city centre to enforce military law personally. Discipline did indeed improve considerably, and reconstruction became easier to undertake. The burden fell on Hu, who slept fewer than three double-hours a day and lost several jin in weight. Yet he remained vigorous and showed no fatigue.

Zuo reached the provincial capital on the second day of the third month. The first man he summoned after stepping from his chair was Hu. “Terrible!” There was an unusually dejected look on his face. “Since the war began, I have visited many places, but never seen one devastated like Hangzhou. What was its former population?” “Eight hundred and ten thousand.” “And now?” “A little over seventy thousand.” “A little over seventy thousand!” Zuo sighed. Then he looked up. “Mr Xueyan, you do not say eighty thousand or sixty thousand, but a little over seventy thousand. On what evidence?” “It is an estimate, but not an idle guess. I have calculated it from the tokens issued at the porridge kitchens and the places selling grain at reduced prices.” “Excellent!” Zuo was full of praise. “You truly combine great ability with care for detail. What do you consider the most urgent measures now?”

“Naturally, reviving trade. A market can flourish only if people are willing to come and do business. Merchants are timid. If Your Excellency can make them confident enough to come to Hangzhou, trade will revive, the people will have a livelihood, and public revenue from likin will rise. It will benefit both the government and the people enormously.” “That simply means taking still greater pains to enforce discipline. Tell the merchants not to be afraid. Let them come and trade in Hangzhou. If they suffer any wrong, they may complain directly at my office. I shall punish the offenders severely.” “With that assurance, they will dare come.” Hu added, “Reconstruction covers a vast range of matters. At present, relief and reassurance are the chief tasks. Might the reconstruction bureau be renamed the relief bureau?” “Yes. An excellent suggestion.” Zuo immediately issued a written instruction. Everything else remained as before; only the name changed. The bureau’s troublesome, minute affairs consumed so much of Hu’s time that he could never find a suitable opportunity for another meeting with Zuo.

Half a month passed in this way. Then the courier who had carried the special memorial announcing Hangzhou’s recovery returned from Beijing with good news for Zuo personally. “The Grand Secretariat has received an oral decree: Since taking charge of military affairs in Zhejiang, Zuo Zongtang, Governor-General of Fujian and Zhejiang, has successively recovered prefectural, subprefectural and county seats. He has now taken the provincial capital of Hangzhou and the county seat of Yuhang, demonstrating skilful direction. As a special favour, he is granted the title of Junior Guardian of the Heir Apparent and permission to wear the yellow riding jacket.” Jiang Yili too received the yellow riding jacket. “Zuo Zongtang shall investigate the services of all officers and men involved and recommend the most deserving for reward.” When the news spread, every civil and military official in the city entitled to see the governor-general dressed in formal robes and went to headquarters to offer congratulations. Wearing his new yellow jacket, Zuo received them in groups with generous words of encouragement. When he saw Hu kowtowing with the expectant circuit intendants, he specially instructed a guard to wait at the second hall and detain him. After the guests and staff had dispersed, the two met in informal dress in the garden reception room. Hu naturally congratulated him again. Zuo spoke of the weight of imperial favour and how difficult it was to repay. Then he apologised: the memorial recommending rewards for the recovery of Hangzhou was already drafted and about to be sent, but Hu’s name was not in it. This first list was confined to those who had stormed the city. When the reconstruction staff were recommended later, Hu would certainly head the list.

Hu thanked him and briefly reported progress. His guiding principles were “relief through work; revival through trade”, so that assistance to the needy also accomplished the work of restoration. Zuo nodded repeatedly in approval and asked what difficulties he faced. “There are naturally too many to recount. I would not trouble Your Excellency with them. Whatever I can manage, I shall attend to myself. But without foresight, trouble soon comes close. It is already late in the third month, and the hungry fifth and sixth months will be upon us before we know it. Every household must meet its immediate needs. In the gap between the old crop and the new, people endure because they have something to hope for: the autumn harvest, which will let them repay their debts and pass the New Year. Your Excellency, that may be difficult this year.” The words alerted Zuo. Startled, he rubbed his hands. “Yes! The autumn harvest depends on spring cultivation. This is the time to transplant rice seedlings. If we miss it, the consequences will be dreadful.” “With Your Excellency recognising that, the people of Zhejiang can be saved.” “Do not think it so easy. We must discuss it carefully. Mr Xueyan, how should we encourage cultivation?”

“Your Excellency has read the ancient books widely. Every dynasty has known great disorder. You must remember clearly how they helped country people return to their fields afterwards.” “Ah, yes. A sound observation. I do. The early Han handled this well. Empress Dowager Bo followed the teachings of the Yellow Emperor and Laozi: quietness and non-interference. That truly allowed the people to recover. I only wonder whether our present two empress dowagers can do as she did.” Hu did not understand how those teachings became government through non-action, nor did he know that Empress Dowager Bo was Emperor Wen’s mother. But he understood the familiar phrases “quietness and non-interference” and “allow the people to recover”, and took up Zuo’s point. “No one sees more clearly than Your Excellency. To bring life back to abandoned fields, the method is simple: do not disturb them. Your authority is as firm as a mountain; your orders are obeyed. One command will bring the people boundless benefit.” “Certainly. That command must be issued. Tell me what it should forbid.” “First,” Hu said after a moment’s thought, “military funds come from likin and contributions from wealthy households. They have nothing to do with ordinary farmers. I presume Your Excellency intends to seek remission of this year’s land taxes. My fear is that county revenue clerks may demand supposed arrears under false pretexts. That will frighten the people away from their fields.” “Impossible!” Zuo’s expression hardened. “Anyone who did that would be utterly heartless. He would be executed without pardon.”

“Second, I fear the troops may seize people for forced labour.” “They will not. I prohibited it long ago. Requisitioned service must be paid for. I can reaffirm the order now: during the busy farming season there must be no disturbance at all, and the farmers must be protected. What else?” “The soldiers may slaughter plough oxen.” “They will not do that either. Whoever kills a plough ox, I shall kill him.” “With such protection, there is hope for this year’s harvest. As for seed and implements, I shall procure them. Whether they are to be lent by the government or sold for cash at fair prices, Your Excellency can settle the rules later. Whatever the decision, it cannot be wrong to have them ready.” “Quite right. Please obtain them—and advance the money too. Apart from money, there is nothing here that cannot be discussed.” “Yes,” Hu replied. “And apart from money, there is nothing I need not discuss with Your Excellency. I ask you to stand behind me.” “Of course. If you need men or official authorisation, you have only to speak.”

“There is one matter to discuss. Silk from the counties of Huzhou is the lifeblood of northern Zhejiang. Raising silkworms is extremely troublesome. They are called ‘silkworm babies’ because they are so delicate. Families shut their doors, take turns watching at night and feed them at fixed hours. They will not even receive unfamiliar visitors. Treasurer Jiang is now leading his forces against Huzhou. Wherever a great army passes, the people may have to prepare tea and food, and then the silkworms cannot be tended. Moreover, silkworms depend entirely on mulberry leaves. If the soldiers cut down the trees for firewood, the creatures will starve.” “Oh!” Zuo listened intently. “I have always taken an interest in practical statecraft, but I did not know that raising silkworms required so much care. As you explain it, the matter is vital. I must tell Jiang Xiangquan at once to provide special protection. Besides preventing interference by our troops, we must above all guard against Longhairs breaking out of Huzhou and scattering through the countryside, harming the silkworm households.” “With such an order, all will be well.” Hu was relieved. “In the lower Yangtze region, the fourth month is the crucial one. As the country song says, ‘It is hard to be Heaven in the fourth month.’ Rice transplanting and silkworm raising both take place then: one needs rain, the other clear skies. With the court’s blessings and Your Excellency’s authority, may next month bring favourable weather and success in the campaign. If we can pass that month in peace, Zhejiang’s suffering can end.” “I understand. I shall do what I can to fulfil everyone’s hopes.” Then Zuo’s brow tightened. “But there is a matter on which I must consult you, Mr Xueyan. Have you a clever way of dealing with those verminous clerks?” Hu did not understand the expression and stared, unable to answer. Only after Zuo explained further did he realise that he meant the clerks in the Ministries of Revenue and War in Beijing.

“The clerks in those two ministries long for the Longhairs’ defeat more eagerly than anyone. To them, the end of the Hong-Yang rebellion means the arrival of their chance to grow rich. On the twenty-first of the first month, Zeng the Ninth took Tianbao Fort. Jinling is encircled, and Hong Xiuquan is like a doomed spirit beneath the cauldron. Li Shaoquan’s Huai Army will take Changzhou before long. Once it falls, that army can sweep west, join Feng Zicai, who has held on so stubbornly at Zhenjiang, and go through Danyang to support Zeng. It seems they may be eating Dragon Boat Festival dumplings in Jiangning.” “Not so soon!” Hu answered immediately. Zuo was startled and displeased. “Why do you say that?” Hu knew he had spoken too bluntly. Zuo had left one question unspoken: “Am I less qualified than you to judge military affairs?” Hu therefore changed his approach at once. “Your Excellency’s conduct of war is inspired. How could I presume to offer a careless opinion? But after two years of seeing and hearing things in Shanghai, I believe I understand Governor Li’s character thoroughly. Even if Changzhou is taken, he may not lead his men west. He is not foolish enough to claim a share of the great victory that Marshal Zeng the Ninth is determined to win alone.” “Ah!” Zuo struck his thigh hard. “You think so too?”

“Perhaps I am mistaken.” “You are not!” Zuo sighed. “I have thought the same all along, but refused to admit it to myself. Li Shaoquan is, after all, a Hanlin scholar. His learning may amount only to a well-rehearsed Book of Songs, but he must understand loyalty to his sovereign and love of country. Having received such great favours, how could he disappoint his sovereign’s earnest wish to destroy this great bandit and bring peace to the empire? Now you have reached the same conclusion independently, which shows that we are both right. Shaoquan is not the sort to flatter Zeng the Ninth merely for his own sake.
