But his teacher commands military affairs in five provinces and stands high in imperial favour. His own eagerness for advancement makes him yield and accommodate others. As for Zeng the Ninth, though he cannot help openly, he will do all he can in secret, supplying money and arms. The recovery of Jinling cannot be far away.” “Yes. That is plain to see. Jiangning is surrounded and outside help cut off. Once the food in the city is exhausted, the Longhairs are finished. In my view, success must come around the turn of summer and autumn.” “Then the trouble will begin. Look at this first.”

Zuo took a thick letter from inside his robe, perhaps ten or more sheets, examined it and selected two to hand to Hu. The two sheets concerned a single piece of news. The clerks in the Ministries of War and Revenue saw that the Hong-Yang rebellion would soon be suppressed. When military operations ended, the war accounts would have to be submitted for audit, and they were already congratulating themselves. The Xiang and Huai armies had risen from the countryside; their commanders knew little of official regulations and could hardly know how military expenditure should be accounted for. Someone had therefore organised clerks from both ministries to undertake the work as a package. They would hire extra copyists, prepare brushes, ink and paper, and travel south specially to compile the armies’ accounts on the spot. The commanders need trouble themselves with nothing, merely paying the customary “ministry fees”. The clerks considered this mutually beneficial and were certain both armies would welcome it. They had not only agreed on a plan but raised twenty thousand taels as capital. Their purchases of fine lianshi paper for the accounts alone had emptied the stocks of several paper shops in Liuli. “An ingenious arrangement,” Hu said, deliberately keeping his manner light. “But the ministry fees will be substantial. I dealt with them for the late Governor Wang. They amount to at least two per cent.” “Exactly!” Zuo said. “That is what I wish to discuss. I have spent seventy million taels in all. At the customary rate, I should have to pay two hundred thousand. Leave aside where I could find such spare money: even if I had it, I would not stuff it down a dog’s hole. Think of a way to put an end to this.” “Putting an end to it is easy. Send word declining their services. But afterwards? The trouble may be endless. Auditing military expenditure is the most tedious business. A single case can remain unsettled for years. Consider carefully, Your Excellency: is dealing with these men worth your precious time and energy?”

“No!” Zuo disagreed emphatically. “I mean that there should be no audit at all. Military accounts were scrutinised most strictly in the Qianlong reign, when the ministry clerks had the most tricks. But those were different times. Then, under another man’s eaves, one had to bow one’s head. Why should I bow mine now? The Ministry of Revenue has no right to demand my accounts.” That was rather too high-handed. The Xiang and Huai armies had indeed raised their own funds locally; the ministry had paid none of their expenses. Yet whether the money came from likin or contributions, it was still public money. How could the ministry have no right even to ask for an account? Hu disagreed and remained silent. “Mr Xueyan,” Zuo pressed, “if you have an opinion, please enlighten me.” Hu had to answer. After a moment, he said, “This does not concern Your Excellency alone.” “True. But when the trouble comes, I cannot escape it.” “Even so, it will hardly be greater than the trouble facing the governor-general at Liangjiang.” Zuo understood. “You mean we should prompt Grand Secretary Zeng to resist?” He meant Zeng Guofan, whose concurrent appointments as assistant grand secretary and governor-general of Liangjiang entitled him to be addressed as a chief minister. Hu nodded. “An argument presented by him seems more likely to succeed.” “I have thought of that. It is useless. Zeng fears slander and criticism; he is as timid as a mouse. He recently sent a confidential memorial asking the court to appoint another trusted minister to share his responsibilities. How could he disregard suspicion and request exemption from an audit? Besides, the time is not yet ripe for such a memorial.” “That is the difficulty,” Hu said. “The campaign is not over. A premature request could only create needless complications.” “But we must remove the hidden danger now. Suppose the clerks win over the ministry’s departmental officers, then persuade its senior officials, and finally have the Grand Council present the matter to the two empress dowagers. Once a decision is made, it will be hard to undo.” Hu could not dismiss that concern. The words about clerks conspiring with departmental officers gave him an idea, and he answered without hesitation, “Then we might block the clerks at the first stage.” “Ah, ah!” Zuo pondered. “There is something in that. But how?”

“The clerks cannot decide so great a matter themselves. As you say, they must secure departmental support. An officer could throw cold water on the scheme from the outset, or seem to acquiesce and then speak out at the crucial moment. If his argument is sound, the ministry’s senior officials cannot ignore it.” “True. But where shall we find a Revenue officer with the breadth of view and courage to do it?” “He need not possess either. He need only see an advantage to himself. Then he will step forward.” “Exactly!” Zuo again struck his thigh. “Your insight is a cut above others’, Mr Xueyan. It clears away confusion.” After a pause, he added, “You sound as though you have someone in mind already.” “I do. A man from Hangzhou.” “A Hangzhou man?” Zuo tilted his head. “Who is serving as a departmental officer in Revenue? I cannot recall.” “He gained his metropolitan degree in the second year of Xianfeng and was assigned to Revenue. He began as a secretary and is now a director in charge of a departmental seal. His name is Wang Wenshao. Have you heard of him?” Zuo concentrated for a while, then remembered. “I seem to have heard him mentioned. Is his courtesy name Kuishi?” “Yes. Wang Kuishi.” “What sort of man is he? Capable?” “Very capable, and very smooth. He is well liked. Moreover, Shen Guifen, the ministry’s left vice-minister, was his examiner in the provincial examination and looks after this pupil. Wang is consequently well placed at Revenue.” “If he is so smooth, he may not be willing to lead an argument. Only a rather upright, outspoken man would do that.” “You are right. But I do not propose urging him to argue openly. I would ask him to work quietly behind the scenes. For the sake of his own prospects, I think he would be willing.” “Why? Explain in detail, Mr Xueyan.”

In Hu’s view, a capital official who relied on himself rather than connections had two resources: learning or practical ability. Hanlin scholars depended on learning, departmental officers on ability. That meant the capacity to get things done, not some grand theory of government: when a practical difficulty arose, a man must produce a concrete solution, or at least find a way to get past it. This was precisely Wang Wenshao’s strength. Yet in the capital, ability was a less useful resource than learning. A learned official advancing through the usual stages found constant opportunities to display his gifts. A Hanlin scholar could serve as a daily lecturer and recorder of the emperor’s activities, then enter the offices of the heir apparent and rise to senior metropolitan rank. Such officials could submit memorials directly—another opportunity to show their learning. One memorial might catch the emperor’s attention and bring rapid advancement, even a red hat-button within three or four years. Practical ability offered no such convenient path. “Why?” Hu asked. “Because a hero needs a field on which to use his weapons. Departmental officers handle official matters daily and wield considerable power to approve or reject. There is a reason for approval and a reason for rejection. Put the case correctly, and your ability shows. But you cannot remain a departmental officer all your life. Wang Kuishi has been a director for years. If he rises to a fourth-rank metropolitan post, he may go to the Court of State Ceremonial or the Office of Transmission. Those offices are like a deaf man’s ears: without them, the appearance is wrong; with them, they serve no purpose. However great Wang’s abilities, he cannot use them in an idle office with nothing to do.” Hu paused. “There is another route for departmental officers: examination for a censor’s post. But a censor’s work requires literary composition above all, and Wang cannot manage it. Nor is he a stern, impartial man. He always tries to avoid making enemies. How could he be a censor?” “I understand!” Zuo said. “He does not want to remain in the capital. He wants a provincial appointment?” “Yes, as a prefect. If he does well, he can become a circuit intendant in two or three years.” Hu smiled. “A provincial official must rely on governors and governors-general.” Zuo now understood completely. No one knew better than he how dependent provincial officials were on their superiors. Under the Qing, governors and governors-general held great power. If they disliked a capital official appointed as a prefect or intendant, they had formerly been able to declare him unequal to his duties and request his return to the capital—effectively overturning the imperial appointment. Early in the Qianlong reign, an edict had sharply rebuked this practice and forbidden its repetition. Even so, provincial chiefs still had ways to prevent a man from taking up his post or to request his transfer. For officials sent to a province merely to await vacancies, with no specific appointment, the timing and the richness or poverty of the eventual post lay still more completely in their hands.

If Wang Wenshao wished to serve outside the capital, he must therefore cultivate them. Securing exemption from the audit of expenditure against the Hong-Yang rebellion was an opportunity that might never come again. Every Xiang and Huai commander would be grateful. Consider the provincial chiefs then in office: Zeng Guofan at Liangjiang, Zuo himself in Fujian and Zhejiang, Li Hongzhang in Jiangsu, Liu Changyou in Zhili, Luo Bingzhang in Sichuan, Guanwen in Huguang, Zhang Zhiwan in Henan, Shen Baozhen in Jiangxi, Yan Shusen in Hubei, and Guo Songtao in Guangdong. Every one had spent great sums on the war. Exemption from an audit would leave each personally obliged to Wang. If he were assigned to their province, how could they fail to favour him? Half the knot in Zuo’s mind loosened. “If Wang is truly capable, he should seize this chance to make friends with governors throughout the empire.” Then he recalled something surprising in Hu’s account. “Have you ever been to Beijing, Mr Xueyan?”

“Not yet.” “That is strange. You have never visited the capital and are half official, half merchant. How do you know so much about promotion through the metropolitan service?” “Originally I knew nothing. I met Wang Kuishi in Shanghai the year before last, and we talked for several nights. I heard it all from him.” “So that is it. Still, to explain the whole course of it so clearly is no small achievement. Do you know him well?” “Yes, though we are not close friends.” “Your conversations seem to have gone deeply into things.” “Common interests can make a discussion profound. Friendship is another matter. Wang has no great brilliance or ambition. He is so smooth in dealing with people that he lacks sincerity. I do not like him.” These words suited Zuo’s taste and gave him a clearer understanding of Hu’s nature. Here was a man with whom he could discuss great matters and share adversity. He nodded repeatedly in silent approval.

“Then Your Excellency would like me to write to Wang and ask him to do what he can?” “Yes. But I fear he cannot accomplish it alone. If he goes knocking at every door and stirs up a public commotion, he will do harm rather than good.” “His own strength is indeed insufficient, but he knows what is at stake. He is cautious by nature, not a flighty man who will talk recklessly everywhere. Since you mention it, I shall particularly stress secrecy.” “That would be best.” Zuo turned to his attendants. “Bring the Official Register.” This was a directory printed by Beijing booksellers, its full title the Complete Register of Officials of the Great Qing. Beginning with the Imperial Clan Court and continuing down to the subordinate and miscellaneous officials in every province, it gave brief records of every holder of an official title, from princes to men below the ranked service. Zuo wanted to examine the Ministry of Revenue. At the head of that section he found: “Woren, Grand Secretary of the Wenyuan Pavilion, supervising the affairs of the Ministry of Revenue.” His face brightened immediately. “Good! There is hope of success.”

“Oh?” Hu asked. “What have you discovered?” “Grand Secretary Woren is a Mongol, but his family has long served in the Kaifeng garrison, so he is much like a Henan man. Henan is a home of Neo-Confucian learning. He follows every rule and convention; a little pedantic, perhaps, but an orthodox moralist through and through. The late emperor valued him highly, and both empress dowagers respect him. If we can get the old gentleman to speak, the matter cannot fail.” “May I tell Wang Kuishi this?” “He knows these circumstances far better than we do. You may tell him or leave it unsaid.” Zuo added, “Woren and Zeng passed the metropolitan examination in the same year. Presumably he would help an old examination colleague.” “Then why not write to Grand Secretary Zeng and ask him to make a firm appeal to Woren?” “That is one method. But I fear Zeng may be too full of moral propriety to write such a letter.” “Yes,” Hu said, while thinking differently. The affair was important, but did not concern him personally. Even Zuo need not be so anxious. If the sky fell, the tallest men would hold it up. The Zeng brothers had spent several times as much on the war as Zuo. An audit would strike them first, and they would naturally seek a way to resolve the difficulty. In advising Zuo, Hu need not be overzealous. Better to avoid mistakes before trying to win credit.

This reflection made him more cautious. “For safety’s sake, we might first write to Wang, tell him the idea and ask his opinion. Can it be done? If so, how should we begin? Let him send a brief outline.” “Nothing could be better. But will he agree?” “Certainly. I have a favour on account with him.” “Did you not say you were not close friends?” Having a “favour on account” was an expression from the world outside official circles, and did not mean close friendship. Zuo did not understand it, so Hu explained. “I mean that Wang owes me a favour. If I ask him to do something, he will not begrudge the trouble.” “Then that settles it. Success will benefit you as well. Zeng and Li Shaoquan will both be obliged to you.” Zuo laughed heartily. There was something unfathomable in the laugh. People said this man liked to assert his own heroic superiority; naturally, he liked political manoeuvring too. Was he testing Hu with that remark and inexplicable laughter? On reflection, whether it was a test or not, Hu could use the opportunity to declare his intentions. He answered seriously. “Your Excellency, I am different from Wang Kuishi. He seeks rapid advancement in office. I want to do business on a grand scale. When I look at myself in the mirror, I do not see the makings of an official. I therefore care nothing whether Grand Secretary Zeng or Governor Li feels obliged to me. Even if they do, I shall not seek their favour. There is only one man whose favour I seek.” He paused deliberately to see Zuo’s response. Zuo naturally asked, with great interest, “Whose?” “Whose else but Your Excellency’s? I seek your favour not to obtain office, but out of gratitude. First, you have saved Zhejiang, above all by recovering Hangzhou. When drinking water, one remembers its source. I can return home today, and Governor Wang’s spirit can rest in peace. How could I not be grateful? Second, you have thought well of me and recognised my worth at our first meeting. ‘A man will die for one who understands him.’ Whose favour should I seek if not yours?” “Too much, too much! My dear fellow, you flatter me excessively,” Zuo replied, his face wreathed in smiles.

“Those are my true feelings. I believe Your Excellency can see that,” Hu continued. “Beyond that, naturally, I have ambitions of my own. I want to build something substantial. If a man is to achieve anything, half depends on ability and half on opportunity. Meeting you is an opportunity for me. Naturally I am unwilling to let it pass lightly.” “You speak honestly. It is precisely your kind of nature that I find congenial. Tell me what sort of enterprise you wish to build.” That was an easy question—so easy that it might seem unnecessary. Yet a plain answer would be flat and tasteless. Hu was no master of rhetoric, but his friendship with Ji Heling had taught him a roundabout manner of expression that sometimes lent elegance to ordinary thoughts. Fortunately, his quick mind could make up for his limited store of learning. An anecdote occurred to him that he could put to good use.

“Your Excellency must know the story of the Qianlong Emperor at Jinshan Temple in Zhenjiang during his southern tours?” Zuo smiled. His reasons were complicated, and even he could not have defined everything in that smile. To call the ruler “the Qianlong Emperor” instead of “Gaozong” or “Emperor Chun” was one cause for amusement. Qianlong had made six southern tours and, as Zuo remembered it, stayed at Jinshan Temple each time. There were many stories; which did Hu mean? That was a second cause. Third, here was a man who spent his life turning somersaults through the hole in a copper coin, proposing to discuss imperial tours with him. Amusing as it was, Zuo remained indulgent. It was like hearing a small child utter a solemn grown-up remark: one could only smile and hear him out. “Go on.” His encouraging glance invited Hu to tell his tale. Hu naturally did not pretend to expertise. “I do not know in which year of the southern tours it happened. An older sworn brother told me, and I found it interesting enough to remember. They say—” They said that once Qianlong and the abbot were standing before the temple, gazing idly at the sails scattered over the Yangtze. The emperor asked how many boats were on the river. “Only two,” the abbot replied. “One for fame, one for gain.” The story arose from the Yangzhou salt merchants’ knowledge of Qianlong’s temperament: to receive the imperial party, they had specially engaged monks skilled in witty exchanges. Long repetition, however, had made the anecdote commonplace and stale. Zuo naturally supposed he knew Hu’s point. His enterprise was merely business on a grand scale, pursued for profit.

But Hu unexpectedly gave it a new meaning. “In my view, the old monk was not necessarily right.” He lifted a hand and pointed into the distance with animation. “There is really only one boat on the Yangtze: it sails for both fame and gain.” “Oh!” Zuo looked at him with fresh respect. “How do you make that out?” “Fame and gain are really the same thing.” Hu seemed a little uneasy. “Your Excellency, I am only talking at random.” This went deeper than saying a boat pursued both fame and gain, hoping to obtain the two together. Zuo was increasingly interested and about to press him when an attendant slipped quietly to his side. “Will Mr Hu stay for a meal?” “Of course. What time is it?” “The middle of the wei hour.” That meant two in the afternoon. Zuo was surprised. “We have talked until I forgot the time.” He asked apologetically, “You must have been hungry for a while, Mr Xueyan?” “Until you mentioned it, I did not notice.” “Nor did I. Our conversation was so congenial that I forgot to eat. Come, we can talk over the meal.”

Lunch was served in the garden reception room. Zuo ate and drank heartily, but his dishes were abundant rather than refined. Part of the reason was the general shortage after the catastrophe; there was little scope for indulging the palate. The finest dish was a great platter of young chicken stir-fried with red chillies. There was also a small plate of cured pork. Hu knew it had been sent all the way from Hunan by Madam Zhou. A guest must eat it, and praise it lavishly, so it was the first thing he took with his chopsticks. Before he could properly commend her virtue, Zuo hurried back to their interrupted discussion. “Mr Xueyan, I do not think I have heard anyone say before that fame and gain are the same thing. You must have a reasoned explanation.” “I was only speaking casually,” Hu answered calmly. “I often wondered whether a man should seek fame first or profit. One day I put the question to a friend. ‘I know nothing of other matters,’ he said, ‘but in business reputation must come first. Otherwise, why do we speak of a golden signboard?’ That made excellent sense. Once you establish such a reputation, business naturally flourishes far and wide. Fame comes, and substance follows. Does that not make fame and gain the same thing?” “An interesting reversal of the saying ‘Substance comes, and fame follows’,” Zuo remarked. “But apart from trade, can your argument be applied elsewhere?”

“In some cases. Take a scholar: once he becomes famous, the great men in the capital all want him as a pupil. Before he even sits the metropolitan examination, his selection for the Hanlin Academy seems assured.” At this point Hu remembered Zuo’s repeated failures in that examination. He still held only the provincial degree, and talk of metropolitan success and Hanlin appointments left him with a sour feeling. The example was ill chosen. Fortunately, Hu’s wits were quick, and an excellent alternative lay immediately before him. “Take Your Excellency yourself. Years ago, far away in Zhejiang, we heard of an extraordinary ‘Adviser Zuo’ in Hunan. Your name was known throughout the empire, even to the emperor. He told a Hanlin scholar from your province, ‘Tell that fellow Zuo to come and work for me.’ And so it proved: either you held no office, or you became governor at once. No one begins official life as a provincial governor. The favour shown you was without precedent and will never be repeated. That too is fame bringing substance in its wake.” The compliment lifted Zuo’s spirits to the heavens. He seemed to float among the clouds, light and exhilarated. Unconsciously stroking his short, greying moustache, he raised his cup with a smile. “You praise me too highly, but it is true. To begin with only a provincial degree and enter office as a provincial chief—mine alone has been that exceptional fortune. It should hearten every poor scholar in the empire. Come, Mr Xueyan, I drink to you!” With that friendly cup, their intimacy deepened almost to the point of complete confidence. Their conversation accordingly grew freer still. Returning to the Hunan scholar whom the Xianfeng Emperor had told to summon Zuo, Hu asked whether he was Guo Songtao, now governor of Guangdong. “The very man!” Zuo’s voice rose involuntarily, as though stirred by strong feeling.

Hu was puzzled. He had heard that Guo had once saved Zuo. Such an unusual tone towards an old friend and benefactor sounded distinctly jarring. Zuo too was adept at reading expressions, and had grievances he wished to air. He quickly asked, “You presumably know the connection between us?” “Not much of it.” “Then let me tell you. It happened in the eighth year of Xianfeng—” In the spring of that year, Fan Xie, brigade-general of Yongzhou in Hunan, had been greedy, lawless and unrestrained. He had also offended the immensely powerful “Adviser Zuo”. Zuo accordingly drafted a severe impeachment. When the memorial was dispatched, a cannon was fired according to custom. Luo Bingzhang heard it from his office and wondered: it was not the time for the noon gun, so what was happening? An attendant told him, “Adviser Zuo is sending a military memorial.” Zuo had always acted with such independence in Luo’s secretariat, earning another nickname, “Left Censor-in-Chief”. Governors routinely carried two additional titles: Right Vice-Minister of War, to give them authority over military officers, and Right Deputy Censor-in-Chief, to allow them to discipline and impeach officials. Since Adviser Zuo’s power exceeded Luo’s, calling him “Left Censor-in-Chief”—a play on his surname—implied that the official on the right had to obey him. Luo had heard nothing beforehand of the impeachment of Fan. Now he asked to see the memorial. Its language was severe, but the charges were not groundless. The claim that Fan could not read a single character, for example, was true. Since the memorial had already been sent, there was no recalling it. Luo let the matter rest.

At the time, the court relied heavily on provincial chiefs commanding armies, and almost every impeachment they submitted was accepted. Fan therefore lost his post. Convinced that Zuo had acted from a private grudge, he indignantly lodged a complaint with the governor-general of Huguang and sent someone to Beijing to petition the Censorate. His charge was against Zuo, but implicated Luo as well: the Hunan governor’s office, he said, had “one post and two seals”. So grave a case naturally required investigation. The commissioners appointed were Guanwen, governor-general of Huguang, and Qian Dingqing, chief examiner of the Hubei provincial examination. Guanwen’s attendants had already accepted Fan’s bribes, and matters looked unfavourable for Zuo. Fortunately, Hu Linyi, governor of Hubei, had established a special family connection with Guanwen: the latter’s favourite concubine was the adopted daughter of Hu’s mother. Even Guanwen therefore addressed Hu as “Brother Hu”. The adopted daughter often told Guanwen, “You understand nothing. Just hold your office quietly and enjoy yourself. Leave everything to Brother Hu, and you cannot go wrong.” Guanwen genuinely listened to her. Hu Linyi could therefore mediate and work hard to settle the affair, giving Zuo great assistance.

“In short, Guo Yunxian has soared into the clouds. Within two years, a fortunate conjunction of circumstances has made him a provincial chief. His rise has been abrupt. He should be modest and self-disciplined, striving to make his qualities match his reputation. Otherwise he will become a laughing-stock, perhaps even ruin himself utterly. I am very anxious for him.” At this point, Zuo suddenly could not contain his amusement. “Grand Secretary Zeng looks so solemnly moral, but sometimes his remarks have a wonderfully dry edge. Someone came to camp a few days ago and reported Guo’s complaint: ‘Zeng Disheng has recommended many men in his life. Unfortunately, one of them was the ill-chosen Mao Jiyun.’ When Zeng heard it, what do you think he did?” “With his self-command, surely he merely smiled?” “Not at all. He said, ‘Mao Jiyun has also recommended many men in his life. Unfortunately, one of them was the ill-chosen Guo Yunxian.’ Point for point—exquisite!” Zuo burst out laughing. Hu laughed too, reflecting that Guo was assailed on all sides in his governorship. Remarkable that he could hold on! He seemed a tough man, worth getting to know if the opportunity arose. But for some reason Zuo’s smile vanished, and anxiety appeared. “Mr Xueyan, sometimes the thought frightens me. One hand cannot clap. For wealth, Jiangsu and Guangdong stand together, each drawing riches from Chinese and foreign trade through its maritime customs. Now Zeng and Li have Jiangsu and Shanghai, while Guangdong supplies funds to the Zeng brothers. Guo’s official reputation may be poor, but once Jinling falls, Zeng the Ninth will naturally be pleased with himself. Remembering where his resources came from, he can be expected to support Guo vigorously for his fundraising. In that situation, I shall have very little room to move. What advice can you give me?”

Zuo spoke gravely and with profound intent. Hu listened just as carefully. There was meaning beneath the words, purpose beneath the thought. This was a great matter touching Zuo’s future and perhaps Hu’s own interests. There was neither need nor reason to answer hastily. With equal gravity, he replied, “Your Excellency looks far ahead. I must think carefully before answering.” “Good. Please give it careful thought for me.” Zuo added, “This is not for other ears.” “Of course!” Hu’s expression was solemn. “Surely I understand that much.” “Yes, yes,” Zuo said apologetically. “I misspoke.” “You are too kind.” Hu inclined himself. “There must be many others waiting to see you. I shall take my leave.” “Very well. In future, you need not restrict your visits to fixed hours, nor must you always wear official robes. And there is no hurry about the matter we have just discussed. Let us watch how the situation develops.”

CHAPTER NINE

Events developed in unexpected ways. First, on the sixth day of the fourth lunar month, Changzhou was recovered after a concerted assault by Li Hongzhang, his subordinate commanders Guo Songlin, Liu Mingchuan, Zhou Shengbo, and Zhang Shusheng, and Gordon’s Ever-Victorious Army. Feng Zicai, who had long held Zhenjiang, then advanced and took Danyang. Everyone assumed that once the two armies joined they would follow up their victories, turn west, and march straight on Jinling to assist Zeng Guoquan. Yet despite repeated orders from the court, Li Hongzhang remained where he was, pleading heavy casualties and an urgent need to replenish and reorganize his forces. Zuo Zongtang and Hu had foreseen this. Li did not wish to share the great achievement that Zeng Guoquan was determined to claim alone; his posture was deliberate. Second, the “Heavenly King,” Hong Xiuquan, suddenly issued a dreamlike decree: he would “ascend forthwith to Heaven, obtain heavenly troops from the Heavenly Father and Heavenly Elder Brother, and secure the Heavenly Capital.” Two days later he poisoned himself, fulfilling his promise to “ascend to Heaven.” His successor was his sixteen-year-old son, Hong Tianguifu, styled the “Young Heavenly King.”

As the news spread, everyone knew that Zeng Guoquan’s great triumph was near. Some recited Du Fu’s line, “With spring for company, how gladly I shall journey home,” and began planning to rebuild their homes after the war. Conscientious and industrious ministers in the capital and governors in the provinces were also quietly making calculations. Once Jinling fell, the accumulated wealth of more than a decade of the Taiping Heavenly Kingdom, concentrated in the Heavenly King’s Palace, should suffice to discharge soldiers and restore the devastated country. Admittedly, the spoils of Jinling would be used for the southern Yangtze region and the Hunan Army; nevertheless, if other provinces no longer needed to send their military contributions, the savings would be equivalent to increased local revenue. Zuo, for example, had an agreeable calculation of his own: once Zeng Guoquan took Jinling, Guangdong would again become a source of military funds for Zhejiang. He had told Hu that when the time came he would submit a special memorial requesting Hu’s appointment to assist in administering the likin levy there. Hu had agreed without demur, but privately attached little importance to the scheme. To him the prospect was extremely uncertain. Unwilling to spoil Zuo’s pleasure, he merely assented politely.

With supporting fire from artillery lent by Li Hongzhang, Zeng Guoquan’s troops captured Longbozi at the end of the fifth month. It stood on the summit of Zhongshan, northeast of Jiangning, commanding a view over the whole city. With this position lost, the Loyal King, Li Xiucheng, was at his wits’ end. Zeng Guoquan’s art of war rested upon a single quality: persistence. Having maintained the bitter siege until persistence was about to triumph, he was less willing than ever to relax. He ordered every battalion to tighten the ring on all sides, press up to the walls, and attack furiously day and night. The true purpose, however, was to use the incessant cannon fire to conceal the sound of tunneling. During the two-year siege of Jinling, his men had dug more than thirty tunnels between Chaoyang Gate and Zhongfu Gate. Sometimes the roofs collapsed; the diggers died and were buried where they fell, together in the tunnels. Sometimes the rebels discovered them and sent in smoke or water, killing men by the hundred. Once, when a tunnel was nearly complete, the men inside suddenly saw a spear thrust down from above. In fact, a rebel had merely planted it in the ground while resting on the march. But the imperial soldiers, rash and thoughtless, seized it and pulled hard. First alarmed, then delighted, the rebels dug down and attacked. The enterprise failed at the very brink of success, and more than four hundred men died, all from Zhu Hongzhang’s command.

Zhu Hongzhang came from Guizhou, the only senior commander under Zeng Guoquan who was not a Hunan man. Isolated among them, he had to exert himself all the more to avoid being pushed aside. Zeng valued him highly too, repeatedly recommending his promotion until he held the honorary rank of provincial military commander and was registered for appointment as a brigade commander. He had also put him in charge of the military administration office. The new tunneling operation was jointly entrusted to Zhu and Li Chendian, brigade commander of Guide in Henan and a registered candidate for provincial military command. Beginning on the eighth day of the sixth month, the work continued night and day. After seven days the tunnel was finished and packed with explosives; all was ready for the final assault. Zeng asked which battalion would form the first attacking wave and which the second. The generals said nothing. He then questioned them individually in order of rank. The most senior was Xiao Fusi, who had already been appointed commander of Fujian’s land forces. He too remained silent, leaving Zeng to ask Li Chendian. Li was willing to lead, provided Zhu gave him one or two thousand picked men. “In that case,” Zhu said, “I might as well take the lead myself.” The matter was settled accordingly. A written military pledge was drawn up: anyone who shrank back would be executed.

At noon on the sixteenth day of the sixth month, Zhu ordered the charge fired. More than thirty thousand jin of explosives filled the tunnel, whose entrance had been sealed with enormous stones. Thick bamboo poles extended into it, packed with explosives wrapped in coarse cloth to serve as a fuse. Once the fuse was lit, a low rumbling like distant thunder came from underground, but no explosion followed. The sun blazed overhead with a heat that seemed to melt metal; surely the charge could not fail to burn. Thousands upon thousands of officers and men waited, sweating and holding their breath, until their anxiety became unbearable. An hour passed. Even the faint underground rumbling ceased. Charges had often failed before, and this attempt too seemed to have come to nothing. Throughout the camps the men were bitterly disappointed. They were about to withdraw some of the troops for a rest in shifts when a tremendous crash suddenly burst forth, as though heaven and earth were breaking apart. A section of wall at Taiping Gate, more than twenty zhang long, rose high into the air with the smoke: an unheard-of spectacle.

There was an explanation for it. The founder of the Ming dynasty had made Nanjing his capital and begun building its walls in the second year of Hongwu. Vast numbers of laborers had been conscripted, and the work took four years. The circuit measured sixty-one li, greater not only than Beiping’s forty-odd li and Xi’an’s twenty-four, but than that of any other city in the world. Nanjing’s walls were tall as well as extensive, averaging more than forty chi in height. Their most remarkable quality, however, was their strength. Granite formed the foundations, and specially fired giant bricks made up the walls, bonded with mortar of lime steeped in glutinous-rice paste. When the whole circuit was finished, the same mixture was spread over it. Strike the outer wall at any point and a white mark would show. For five hundred years it had resisted weapons, water, and fire; yet in the end even this wall could not withstand Western explosives. Its bricks were bonded so firmly, however, that the blast hurled an entire section more than twenty zhang long into the air. It was later learned that the section had traveled more than a li before breaking into several pieces and falling to earth, killing several hundred people.

At the moment of the breach, Zhu rushed forward with a flag in his left hand and a sword in his right, shouting for the men to mount the wall. All nine gates were taken, giving rise to the name “the Nine Generals Who First Mounted the Walls.” Besides Zhu, Li Chendian, and Xiao Fusi, they were the registered brigade commanders Wu Mingliang, Xiong Deng, and Wu Weishou; Provincial Military Commander Zhang Shiri; Liu Lianjie, registered for appointment as a provincial judicial commissioner; and Peng Yuju, a registered circuit intendant. When the news reached Beijing, great rewards naturally followed. The late Xianfeng Emperor was said to have promised a princely title to whoever suppressed Hong Xiuquan and Yang Xiuqing. Yet since the rebellion of the Three Feudatories, the Qing had made no one outside the imperial clan a prince, or even a duke. Many imperial nobles therefore opposed fulfilling the late emperor’s promise, creating a difficult problem. At last Empress Dowager Ci’an proposed dividing one princely honor into four lesser titles. Zeng Guofan, whose merit was greatest, became a marquis; Zeng Guoquan came next and became an earl; the remaining titles, a viscountcy and a barony, went to Li Chendian and Xiao Fusi respectively.

When the decree appeared, Zhu was deeply dissatisfied. Judged by their achievements on the day of the breach, he should surely have ranked first. He had been the first over the wall, captured the rebel King Yong, Hong Renda, alive, and occupied the Heavenly King’s Palace. Yet Zeng Guoquan’s report placed Li Chendian first. It was said that Zhu had held the palace until dusk, when Li arrived with troops, announcing that he had come “on the Ninth Marshal’s orders to take over the defense.” The palace thus passed into Li’s control. He guarded it until eight the following morning, when, in broad daylight and for no apparent reason, it caught fire and burned to the ground. Zeng subsequently reported that a search had yielded nothing but a rebel imperial seal. Here, then, lay the secret of Li’s place at the head of the list! Among the nine first over the walls, Zhu did not fare even as well as Xiao Fusi, who at least received a first-class barony, the lowest of the five grades of nobility. Zhu’s rewards were an order that he be given priority for recommendation whenever a provincial military command or brigade command fell vacant, permission to wear the yellow riding jacket, and the hereditary dignity of cavalry commandant. These were by no means insignificant honors, but to rank third was a bitter injustice.

Unable to swallow it, Zhu went to argue with the Ninth Marshal. Zeng had evidently prepared his response, and it was masterly. “I too believe you should have received first credit. But the memorial listing the achievements was submitted by my elder brother. I hear that a secretary of his, a fellow named Li, has been manipulating things.” As he spoke, he drew a gleaming knife from his boot and held it out hilt first. “Go and kill the man.” Zhu did not know whether to laugh or cry. Li Chendian’s glory, meanwhile, proved as fleeting as a dream. He never lived to receive the decree granting his title, yellow jacket, and two-eyed peacock feather. By the time it arrived, he was dead. According to Zeng’s memorial, Li had suffered an injury to an acupuncture point at the waist during the assault, obstructing the flow of his vital energy; he died on the second day of the seventh month. Others, however, said that he had died “beneath the peony blossoms.” When the city fell, treasure, women, and children had been there for the taking. In a single night Li was said to have slept with more than ten women; careless in the oppressive summer heat, he contracted a fever associated with sexual indulgence and died. This, of course, was private rumor. If the cause of death lay in the bedchamber, the truth would never be established. There was a story behind Xiao Fusi’s barony too.

When the city fell and all hope was gone, Li Xiucheng took a trusted page through the disordered troops and escaped southeast through Tongji Gate. He intended to cross Maoshan and travel by way of Liyang and Changxing to Huzhou, joining the rebels who had fled from Hangzhou. At a place called Fangshan he encountered eight woodcutters. One thought he recognized him but was not certain, so ventured to call, “Loyal King!” Seeing that his identity had been discovered, Li sank to his knees and begged, “If any of you will guide me to Huzhou, I shall give him thirty thousand taels.” He and his page pulled back their sleeves. All four arms were covered with gold bracelets. A horse also carried a chest that looked quite small but bent the animal’s back beneath its weight. One could readily imagine it filled with gold, silver, and jewels. The eight woodcutters grew greedy. They wanted both to seize Li’s wealth and to earn a reward for reporting him. They therefore tricked master and servant into Jianxi Village at the foot of the hill and chose a man named Tao to inform the imperial troops. He was to go to Li Chendian’s camp outside Taiping Gate, because a kinsman of his served there and could safely pass on the information.

Passing Zhongshan, Tao grew hungry and thirsty. Remembering that this was Xiao Fusi’s sector, he thought of a cook in the camp whom he knew through supplying firewood. He might as well stop there to rest and get a meal. Carried away by his good fortune, Tao let slip during their conversation how they had captured Li Xiucheng. The cook told a bodyguard, who informed Xiao; from that moment Tao was doomed. After giving secret instructions that Tao be entertained with good meat and wine, Xiao took more than twenty bodyguards and rode hard through the blazing heat to Jianxi Village. There he seized Li with ease and took possession of gold, silver, and jewels worth more than a hundred thousand taels. Tao was killed with a single sword stroke to silence him. Xiao retained enough conscience to spare the cook: he gave him five fine pearls and a good horse, hinting that he should desert that very night and go as far away as possible. It was for capturing Li Xiucheng that Xiao received his barony. Only later did Zeng Guoquan learn the truth and order a reward of a hundred taels for each of the eight woodcutters’ families. His bodyguards pocketed most of it. All that reached the eight households was one large ingot—fifty taels—to divide among them.

Had Xiao truly captured Li Xiucheng by his own efforts, the achievement would indeed have deserved a barony. For although the Heavenly Capital had fallen, the Young Heavenly King had not been captured. Merely claiming that he had died in the fighting would scarcely satisfy the court. Fortunately there was Li Xiucheng, whose practical importance exceeded that of the young ruler. His capture could compensate for the failure to establish the principal rebel’s whereabouts. Zeng Guofan had by then come from Anqing to Jiangning in a specially provided vessel. Besides reassuring the troops and relieving the people, he had another important task: deciding Li Xiucheng’s fate. He appointed Circuit Intendant Pang Jiyun and Prefect Li Hongyi to interrogate him jointly. Li Hongyi was the very “fellow named Li” whom Zeng Guoquan had accused of manipulating the rewards when speaking to Zhu.

During the ten days from the twenty-seventh of the sixth month to the sixth of the seventh, comparatively little time was spent questioning Li. Most of it he spent in his prison cage writing a personal confession, producing some seven thousand characters a day and seventy or eighty thousand in all. Zeng Guofan then cut and altered it extensively, retaining no more than a third before forwarding it to the throne. In discussing what had become of Hong Xiuquan’s two sons after the city fell, the confession stated: “I took only the young lord with me. As he had no good horse, I gave him my own warhorse to ride. After the third watch I led a desperate charge, bringing him out through the breach made in the wall by the Ninth Marshal. Several hundred of us, sovereign and followers together, fought our way beyond the city. The camps we passed stood layer upon layer, with deep ditches and strong defenses. As the young lord came out, cannon fired from every camp of the Ninth Marshal, and shouts rose everywhere without cease. He and I became separated, while the Ninth Marshal’s mounted and foot soldiers pursued us. Though he had escaped the city, whether he lived or died was unknown. A child of sixteen, he had never ridden a horse in all his life and had never endured such terror. With the Ninth Marshal’s troops advancing from every side, he must surely have been killed. If the cavalry or infantry killed him on the road, they would not have known he was the young lord. Who would recognize a little boy?”

This passage chimed perfectly with Zeng Guofan’s report that “the young rebel has already died amid the fighting.” But the attempt to be clever defeated itself and exposed the alterations: the Young Heavenly King was alive and had escaped to Huzhou. At the same time that Zeng became a marquis, further decrees rewarded the principal commanders and provincial authorities of the southeast. Prince Sengge Rinchen received an additional grant of a beile title. Guanwen, Governor-General of Huguang, became a first-class earl, with his title perpetually hereditary without reduction; Li Hongzhang, Governor of Jiangsu, also became a first-class earl. Yang Yuebin, Governor-General of Shaanxi and Gansu, and Peng Yulin, Right Vice-Minister of War, received the hereditary dignity of first-class light-chariot commandant and the honorary title of Junior Guardian of the Heir Apparent. Luo Bingzhang, Governor-General of Sichuan, and Bao Chao, Zhejiang’s provincial military commander, likewise received first-class light-chariot commandancies. Duxing’a, the Xi’an general; Fuming’a, the Jiangning general; and Feng Zicai, Guangxi’s provincial military commander, received hereditary cavalry commandancies. Among the senior officials of the southeast, only Zuo and Shen Baozhen, Governor of Jiangxi, were left out. The decree expressly stated that further favor would be granted once Zhejiang and Jiangxi had been cleared of rebels. Intended as encouragement though this was, the contrast was humiliating. Li Hongzhang’s ennoblement especially angered Zuo. More fundamentally, Zeng Guofan directed military affairs in five provinces, including Zhejiang and Jiangxi. If those two were not pacified, his own task was unfinished. Why should he receive such outstanding rewards? There was another cause for anger. The defeated rebels from Jiangning had only one escape route, southeastward. Operations around Huzhou had been progressing well; suddenly they faced a crushing new pressure. Had Zeng secretly consulted Zuo beforehand about the date of the assault and the plans to encircle and destroy the enemy, Zuo could have stationed troops to block the gaps and ambush the fugitives. How else could the defeated rebels have come pouring in like a collapsing mountain or a bursting dike? Zeng’s army, seeking glory only for itself, was simply diverting disaster onto its neighbor.

Prince Gong, who directed affairs at court, and Grand Councilor Wenxiang—a worthy bannerman, trusted above and respected below, genuinely loyal in his service to the state—knew nothing of the depth of this antagonism among the southeastern commanders. Close upon the decrees rewarding merit came another edict, sternly pressing Zuo to act. Reading it made him more uncomfortable still. It began: “Jiangning has been recovered, the entire band of villains destroyed, and no rebel has escaped.” Those words made Zuo wonder how extravagantly the Zeng brothers had embellished their reports of victory. He therefore resented all the more the ensuing instructions: “Li Hongzhang shall transfer the forces of Wang Yongsheng and others to Changxing to assist in defending Huzhou. Zuo Zongtang shall direct his armies, in concert with the Jiangsu forces, speedily to annihilate all rebels in Huzhou and Anji, recover their fortified cities, and allow not a single rebel to escape farther inland.”

“Look,” he said to Hu. “The Zeng brothers have not merely claimed glory for themselves; they have blocked everyone else’s path to earning it. Judging by the language here, the great work is finished and Zhejiang can be pacified within days. Yet Huzhou is swarming with rebels and will be harder than ever to take. Even if we fight to the death and capture it, we shall get no credit. The Zeng brothers have spoken first: at Jiangning ‘the entire band of villains was destroyed, and no rebel escaped.’ The court will certainly think we are inventing victories. Is that not infuriating?” Hu could only try to soothe him, but vague consolations had no effect. Intelligence reports arrived one after another, all describing this or that rebel “king” moving from Guangde in southern Anhui into Zhejiang, crossing the Tianmu Mountains, and making straight for Huzhou. At last came a report originating among the refugees. It was rushed to Hangzhou. When Zuo read it, he became extraordinarily excited.

The report stated that after the fall of Jiangning, the Young Heavenly King, Hong Tianguifu, had been escorted by King Gan, Hong Rengan; King Yang, Ji Qingyuan; King Yu, Li Ruisheng; and King Yang, Li Mingcheng. They reached Guangde on the twenty-first day of the sixth month. Five days later Huang Wenjin, King Du, who held Huzhou, personally welcomed him into the city. Learning that the Loyal King, Li Xiucheng, had been captured by the imperial forces, the young ruler had appointed Hong Rengan chief military counselor. Zuo thought he could now put the Zeng brothers to shame. He immediately ordered a secretary to draft a memorial, intending to send it by express rider and expose Zeng Guofan’s false claim that the young rebel had died in the fighting. Just as he was exulting and laughing openly, Hu arrived at his temporary headquarters. On hearing the news, Hu felt obliged to dampen his pleasure with a word of caution.

“My lord, might this memorial wait a little?” “What is there to wait for? Now that the principal rebel’s whereabouts are known, how should I dare conceal them?” Zuo answered with righteous confidence. Hu knew that an appeal against quarrels among commanders as harmful to the state would go unheard. He therefore appealed to practical consequences. “We have a saying in Hangzhou: lift a stone yourself, and crush your own foot. My lord, this is a stone you must not lift. Handle it badly, and it may break your head.” “What do you mean?” “Consider what will happen when the memorial arrives. The court will naturally be delighted. ‘Excellent! You must capture the young rebel. On no account may he escape. Take him, and we shall make you a marquis.’ But suppose you fail?” “Ah! Ah!” Zuo suddenly understood. “Then I shall be the man who let the principal rebel escape!”

Hu deliberately said no more. A man as intelligent as Zuo needed only a hint, certainly; but Hu’s chief reason was another consideration. On his recent visit to Shanghai he had heard a great deal about the Zeng brothers and Li Hongzhang from their secretaries and trusted associates. Even remarks in private correspondence, which by rights no third person should ever have heard, had leaked out. Evidently Zeng and Li had spoken freely to those about them. The discreet passed the information only to people who could appreciate it; the shallow boasted of their intimacy with great officials and their access to inside news, adding flourishes until every story came vividly to life. Thus they created endless needless disputes and damaged relationships for no reason. This had made Hu wary of Zuo. His own lord was notorious for speaking without restraint. If Hu devised a scheme to sow discord between Zeng and Li, Zuo might one day tell someone exactly how he had done it. That would be curing one ailment by causing another. To offend both Zeng and Li for no good reason was hardly worthwhile. Zuo, however, had heard enough. Delighted, he kept saying, “I understand! I understand!” Hu found this less than satisfactory. He wanted a definite plan, so that he could grasp Zuo’s intention and help carry it out. He therefore asked, “Then does Your Excellency find something useful in my humble opinion?” “Humble opinion? Your judgment is excellent!” Zuo paused thoughtfully. “Still, I am not a man to change my face at a turn of the hand. Besides, Li Shaoquan has always been, to me—” He too left the sentence unfinished. Anyone familiar with his usual remarks could supply the rest: he had always despised Li Hongzhang. To seek friendship now seemed like asking a favor, and he resented it. Hu decided that open advice toward a proper end was different from privately criticizing others. Even if such words leaked out, they could stand in public without injuring his reputation. He resolved to persuade Zuo to cooperate with Li.

“My lord,” he asked deliberately, “what is the one urgent task before us?” “Do you mean public business, or my own affairs?” “It is the same in both. Two matters in one, and one in two: what is the great task that concerns both?” “Naturally, to clear all Zhejiang of rebels.” “Exactly. And there is only one obstacle left: Huzhou. Once it falls, you can report the province pacified. Then you too will be made a marquis and a grand minister.” “A grand minister is far in the future, and a marquisate is nothing remarkable. If such a grant of nobility is offered, I shall insist upon declining it.” Hu could not tell whether Zuo spoke from real feeling or affected indifference. There was no point in arguing. Only by following the direction of his words could Hu move him.

“A masterly move, my lord!” Hu raised his thumb. “To think nothing of a marquisate shows that wealth and rank are no more to you than passing clouds. That places you above both Grand Secretary Zeng and Governor Li. But unless the court first offers the honor, how can you display such superior character?” “There is truth in that.” Zuo nodded deeply. At last he had yielded a little, and Hu could breathe freely. How cooperation with Li was to be arranged no longer required Hu’s attention. Zuo understood the whole situation, and military strategy was not a subject on which Hu could presume to speak. He merely reminded Zuo of one point: Li had disobeyed the court’s orders to assist at Jiangning and now urgently wished to make amends. Even if Zuo refused cooperation, Li would send troops toward Huzhou on his own, demonstrating obedience to the repeatedly stated principle that provincial authorities suppressing rebels must not let territorial boundaries divide their efforts. Zuo, moreover, truly needed his support.

The first reason was manpower. Huzhou had become the refuge of the rebels of the southeast. Their scattered armies and defeated commanders had gathered there in numbers several times greater than Zuo’s forces. Pressed too hard, they would fight like trapped beasts and must not be underestimated. The second reason was terrain. Around Huzhou, waterways branched in every direction, offering ambush positions everywhere. The place was naturally easy to defend and difficult to attack. When Zhao Jingxian had held the isolated city, he had adapted his defense to the ground and used it to the utmost. Its present rebel commander, Huang Wenjin, was no weakling either. Acting in the Young Heavenly King’s name, he could issue orders readily. Reports also said that Huzhou held enough grain for a year, placing him in a much better position than Zhao had enjoyed. Zuo could overcome neither obstacle alone; only Li could help him surmount both. In manpower, the assistance of the Jiangsu forces was needed to complete the investment of Huzhou—not, of course, a four-sided encirclement like Zeng Guoquan’s siege of Jinling. Even if enough troops could be deployed for that, it would be unwise. Since ancient times, besiegers had often left one side open. Give the enemy no way to live, and he must fight to the death. Even if annihilated, he would inflict terrible losses. Leave an outlet, by contrast, and the hope of survival would weaken his will to resist. Moreover, ambushes could be laid along a carefully prepared escape route. That was the better calculation.

As for terrain, the first vital position outside Huzhou was Daqiankou, opening northward onto Lake Tai. Zhao Jingxian’s loss of Daqian on a snowy night had led to the fall of the city. The circumstances were much the same now: Huzhou could not be taken until Daqian was seized. It was far easier for the Jiangsu troops to cross Lake Tai from the north than for Zuo’s forces to march around to it. Most importantly, an attack on Daqian required a naval force—the Jiangsu army’s strength and Zuo’s weakness. Li would naturally use that strength to the full, both to atone for remaining idle at Changzhou and to justify his newly bestowed title. After his long talk with Hu, Zuo considered matters quietly. In local advantages and connections, his position was inferior to Li’s. Unless he could undertake the whole task himself and promise to capture Huzhou unaided within a fixed time, he could not prevent Li from advancing against it from the north. Without cooperation, the two armies would compete for glory. If Li lost the contest, little harm was done to him. If Zuo lost and allowed his guest to displace him as host, the reputation of a lifetime would be ruined. After much thought, Zuo decided that the best course was to achieve his purpose with Li’s assistance. If he fulfilled his duties as host, the visiting army would have to show him courtesy at every turn. Even if the Jiangsu troops entered Huzhou first, Li could scarcely send his own independent memorial. Provided the report of victory went through Zuo’s hands, he could control the account of the achievements.

His mind made up, Zuo would for the moment be a great man capable of bending as well as standing firm. He personally wrote Li a most earnest letter, discussing how to sweep away the remaining rebels of the southeast and hinting at his need for assistance. Li responded handsomely, promising to commit all four of his “Guo, Liu, Pan, and Yang armies” to Huzhou. These commands—Guo Songlin’s, Liu Mingchuan’s, Pan Dingxin’s, and Yang Dingxun’s—formed the backbone of the Anhui Army. In fact, Li sent more than those four. Liu Bingzhang, who had joined the army from the Hanlin Academy, was also ordered to assist, as was the naval force of Huang Yisheng, Jiangnan’s provincial military commander and a close fellow townsman of Zeng Guofan. They would advance by different routes. Li’s intentions broadly resembled Zuo’s. He meant to make a great display and treat the Huzhou campaign as scarcely less important than the recovery of Jinling. Like a gambler placing a wager, he hoped to capture the young rebel and uncover a hoard of treasure; at the same time, he wished to dampen the Hunan Army’s elation.

The rebels at Huzhou claimed two hundred thousand men and probably had at least sixty percent of that number. Zuo’s and Li’s regular forces together numbered no fewer than eighty thousand; with civilian officials, laborers, and other followers, the total exceeded a hundred thousand. The sides were roughly matched, and fierce fighting was expected. Yet the outcome was virtually predetermined, and even the general course of the rebel retreat could be foreseen. The ground constrained the directions of advance. Zuo’s troops would press from the southeast and southwest, while the Jiangsu forces attacked from northeast and northwest and moved directly south to block Daqiankou, preventing escape onto Lake Tai. East of Huzhou lay the richest country in the southeast, heavily defended; beyond it was the coast, offering no way out. The rebels’ only escape lay westward. If they broke through Guangde, they might join Li Shixian and Wang Haiyang in Jiangxi, both famous fighting commanders, and perhaps prolong their existence a little. A battlefield was like a chessboard. Deception flourished not only between enemies, but among allies competing against one another. Li did, after all, make a move for his own advantage. He sent Liu Mingchuan’s twenty battalions, one after another, toward the Zhejiang–Anhui border, ostensibly to attack Guangde and cut the rebels’ retreat. His real aim was to intercept Huang Wenjin, capture the young ruler, and seize the baggage train.

Huzhou was finally recovered on the twenty-sixth day of the seventh month. As predicted, Huang opened the western gate and fled. The main rebel forces escaped west in three columns. At Guangde they divided again into two routes: one led into southern Anhui; the other, taken by Huang with the young ruler, went through Ningguo, over the western Tianmu Mountains, and by Kaihua and Yushan into Jiangxi. Liu Mingchuan pursued relentlessly. The other armies, eager for credit, also pressed forward with all their strength. After five days and nights of pursuit, the rebels were scattered beyond cohesion, and Huang died amid the fighting. Hong Tianguifu, however, was still missing. The more credible reports said that he was moving south through Jiangxi to join Li Shixian and Wang Haiyang on the Guangdong–Fujian border. From there they intended to turn west toward Hubei, link up with King Fu, Chen Decai, and pass from the Jingzhou–Xiangyang region into Shaanxi, establishing themselves anew in Guanzhong. This was certainly a hopeful calculation. Yet even if it failed, such ceaseless movement was dangerous: a junction with the Nian rebels of Anhui and Henan would give grave cause for concern. The court was therefore deeply angered that repeated, relentless pursuit had still failed to capture the young rebel and bring him to justice. Zuo was angrier still. His memorial announcing “all Zhejiang cleared of rebels” had already gone forward, but with Hong Tianguifu uncaptured, the achievement remained incomplete. For the time being, he could hardly expect his grant of nobility.

Having disposed of the claim of “a hundred thousand,” Zuo turned to responsibility for impeaching negligent subordinates, and his language grew still sharper: “As for the statement that the entire enemy force escaped from Hangzhou and no impeachments were heard of, this is particularly incomprehensible. Jinling had long been completely surrounded; Hangzhou had not been surrounded at all. From Jinling came the report, ‘All the rebels have been killed.’ From Hangzhou came the report, ‘The principal rebels have indeed escaped.’” Those two sentences alone had the force of an experienced magistrate’s judgment: Zeng Guoquan had been responsible for preventing escape, and Zeng Guofan had falsely reported the military situation. At the close, however, the pen turned again with calculated subtlety. “In military affairs, truth and substance are of the first importance; I have therefore had no choice but to explain. In all future public business I shall continue to consult and cooperate harmoniously. I would not dare harbor the slightest prejudice and thereby increase my own fault.” These were needles wrapped in silk. Cautious and modest on the surface, the words were sharp beneath; every sentence contained a concealed accusation against Zeng Guofan.

Within half a month of the memorial’s dispatch, a reply came. Issued through Prince Gong, the confidential court dispatch was extraordinarily tactful. Instead of saying that Zuo could not yet be ennobled, it declared: “Since entering Zhejiang, Zuo Zongtang has recovered dozens of cities and fortified positions and cleared the whole province. His achievement is very great. We had intended at once to bestow a substantial reward, but feared that, with the young rebel Hong still undestroyed, the governor-general would insist upon declining it. As soon as the remaining rebels are eliminated, a decree of favor will be issued.” This clearly implied that Zuo’s ennoblement was only a matter of time. There was gentle reassurance concerning his dispute with Zeng as well. “The court does not wish to press meritorious servants over minor details. When the young rebel Hong entered Zhejiang, the governor-general reported the facts; when he left the province, troops were sent in pursuit. Both were proper actions. His declaration that he will continue to consult harmoniously with Zeng Guofan in public affairs, harboring no prejudice, particularly accords with the bearing of a great minister and deserves high praise. The court’s expectations of him are great and far-reaching. Let him exert himself still further and become an eminent minister of his age, fulfilling our hopes.” The edict did not reproach Zeng, but its judgment of right and wrong was clear. The promise that Zuo might be “an eminent minister of his age” was especially rare language in an imperial decree. In this dispute of memorials, submitted to the sovereign’s judgment, Zuo had gained the advantage on every point. Naturally, his enmity with Zeng deepened as well.

On the surface, the quarrel between Zeng and Zuo was a war of words; beneath it lay a fierce struggle for territory. Caught between them, and at the same time bearing the first impact, was Zeng Guoquan. His substantive appointment was Governor of Zhejiang. In wartime, to encourage commanders, appointments had not followed ordinary administrative boundaries. A senior official of one province leading troops in another was nothing unusual. Once a phase of the war ended, however, the situation changed. By ordinary reasoning, whatever faults Zeng Guoquan might have committed after taking Jiangning, his distinction in a hundred battles was undeniable. The proper course was to let him assume his governorship. But Zuo, Governor-General of Fujian and Zhejiang and acting Governor of Zhejiang, had no intention of giving way. Zeng Guofan requested sick leave for his brother partly out of anxiety over slander and criticism, seeking prudent retirement at the height of success. More fundamentally, he had perceived the hidden uncertainty of his brother’s position. Judging that Guoquan could never take up the post, he thought it better to recognize the situation and withdraw voluntarily.

The court was in a dilemma. On one hand, military operations in the southeast had culminated in the recovery of Huzhou and the pacification of Zhejiang, so Zuo’s dignity had to be considered. On the other, to let a newly created earl lay down his arms and retire to the countryside seemed an improper way to treat a meritorious servant. The request for Zeng Guoquan’s retirement on grounds of illness was therefore left unresolved. The court hoped that Zeng and Zuo might put aside their resentment and be reconciled, with Zuo himself requesting permission to relinquish the acting governorship and an order for Guoquan to take office. That hope was impossible to fulfill. At last the court saw that indefinite delay would not do and agreed to Guofan’s wish, allowing Guoquan to resign. Who should fill the vacancy, however, required careful thought. The court knew what Zuo wanted: Jiang Yili promoted from provincial treasurer to governor, with the treasury vacancy filled on Zuo’s recommendation. But Jiang lacked sufficient seniority and standing; such an arrangement would also humiliate Zeng Guofan. In balancing its commanders, the court would not lightly give the impression of favoring one man. Zuo’s wishes were therefore ruled out.

Three considerations remained. First, the new Governor of Zhejiang must be honest, experienced, and capable. Of all the provinces ravaged by Hong Xiuquan and Yang Xiuqing, Zhejiang had suffered most, and its reconstruction would be the hardest. Nothing less than integrity and practical ability would suffice. Second, he should have neither obligations nor grievances toward Zuo, while enjoying the support of Zeng Guofan and even Li Hongzhang, so that Zhejiang could obtain its neighbors’ help. Third, with the great rebellion suppressed, arms were to give way to civil government. This would be the first provincial governorship filled since the defeat of the Taipings, marking the restoration of civil rule. The appointee should preferably have risen through the examinations. Military achievements as well would make him ideal. An exceptionally suitable man was eventually chosen: Ma Xinyi, courtesy name Gushan. His ancestors were Hui Muslims who had helped the founding Ming emperor win the empire. Appointed military officers in a Shandong garrison, they settled in Heze County, Caozhou Prefecture. More than four hundred years had passed. Apart from his adherence to Islam, Ma was thoroughly a native son of Shandong.

As news of Ma Xinyi’s appointment reached Zhejiang, an important and intriguing report arrived from Jiangxi: the young rebel Hong Tianguifu had at last been captured. The campaign against the Taipings was drawing to a close, but formidable work remained before disorder could be put down. In the judgment of Prince Gong and Wenxiang, three rebellions still stood between the empire and lasting peace: the Taiping remnants who had fled south, the Nian bands disturbing the central provinces, and the Muslim rebellions devastating the western frontier. Fortunately, men of talent abounded in numbers surpassing earlier reigns. Prince Gong and Wenxiang decided to entrust the suppression of these three troubles to four men. The first was still Zeng Guofan. On the first day of the tenth month, as Zeng Guoquan retired in triumph and led the disbanded Hunan troops home, a confidential dispatch reached Jiangning: “Jiangning is now entirely pacified, and military operations there are concluded. Zeng Guofan shall select a portion of his forces and proceed to the Anhui–Hubei border to direct the suppression of the rebels. He must advance speedily and permit no delay.” These “rebels” were the Nian.

The Nian had originally made northern Anhui their base. Under Prince Sengge Rinchen’s vigorous attacks, they had moved into Hubei and Henan. Though Zhang Luoxing was dead, his nephew Zhang Zongyu was no weak successor. Lai Wenguang, formerly one of Chen Yucheng’s commanders, had also returned from Guanzhong. With the Heavenly Capital fallen, he was a man without a home; naturally he joined the Nian, greatly increasing their strength and boldness. The court knew that the prince’s cavalry tactics—pursuing and driving the Nian from place to place—were unsound. Once his troops exhausted themselves, a rebel counterattack must bring disaster. Yet his exalted position made him extremely difficult to handle. Even the Western Empress Dowager had to show him special indulgence. Offer strategic instructions, and he could invoke the maxim that a commander in the field need not obey every order of his sovereign. Press him a little, and he might take offense and act entirely on his own. It would be better, for public and private reasons alike, to find a way for him to relinquish his command and return to enjoy life in Beijing. Only Zeng Guofan could replace him in directing operations across several provinces. His prestige was sufficient, and his practice of “seeking security first and flexibility afterward”—meeting movement with stillness, advancing steadily from firm positions—would correct the prince’s mistakes. For the financing of the campaign, the court had thought of another indispensable man.

That man was Li Hongzhang. An edict appointed him to act in Zeng’s place as Governor-General of Liangjiang, while Wu Tang, Director-General of Grain Transport and a benefactor of Empress Dowager Cixi, was transferred to act as Governor of Jiangsu. The decree did not expressly state that Li would bear full responsibility for the rear supply bureau supporting Zeng on the Anhui–Hubei frontier, but the intention was plain. First, Zeng and Li were teacher and pupil; it was only proper that “when there is work to be done, the disciple undertakes the labor.” Second, Zeng had raised funds when Li commanded in the field; now their roles should be reversed. Third, Liangjiang was the richest region and a principal source of military revenue, so whoever governed it was bound to provide funds. Viewed in terms of the general situation, the arrangement was sound. It merely disadvantaged Zeng and benefited Li and Wu—but not every consideration could be accommodated.

Another man selected was Yang Yuebin, a naval commander whose fame equaled Peng Yulin’s. His original name was Yang Zaifu. Since the first character of the Tongzhi Emperor’s generation in the imperial genealogy was Zai, however, the name risked offending the naming taboo, and he changed it to Yuebin. Before Jiangning fell he had already been appointed Governor-General of Shaanxi and Gansu, with the intention of entrusting him with the suppression of the Muslim rebellion. Such rebellions had broken out not only there but in Yunnan and Xinjiang as well. Yunnan was approaching pacification, while Xinjiang’s troubles were gathering force. For the latter, the court placed its hopes in the newly created viscount Bao Chao. A warmly worded decree declared that without “a man of exceptional courage and strategy such as Bao Chao” it would be difficult to transform the military situation in Xinjiang. Zeng was therefore ordered to transmit instructions that, upon the expiry of Bao’s two-month leave to bury his parent, he should “depart immediately from Sichuan, pass beyond the frontier, and suppress the Muslim rebellion.” Knowing Bao’s love of renown, Prince Gong and Wenxiang flattered him by comparing him with Yang Yuchun, the celebrated general of the Qianlong and Jiaqing reigns. “In former campaigns in the Muslim territories, Yang Yuchun, himself a native of Sichuan, won frontier victories whose glory shone upon the military banners. Bao Chao must lead his forces to pacify the western marches, carrying his fame ten thousand li and matching the splendor of his predecessor. Loyal and brave by nature, he will surely obey upon receiving this decree and fulfill the court’s trust.” The language was most earnest. By requiring Zeng to transmit it, the court also hinted that he should help urge Bao forward. Zeng, however, had long resolved that the Hunan Army should withdraw prudently at the height of its success. Bao was a favorite commander whom he particularly wished to protect. He simply conveyed the edict in the usual way and offered no encouragement to depart.

The other man in whom the court placed great hopes was Zuo. As Governor-General of Fujian and Zhejiang, he was naturally responsible for suppressing the two large forces of Li Shixian and Wang Haiyang, defeated by Bao at Ruijin in Jiangxi and driven into Fujian. Zuo was not a man to shrink from trouble. He regarded the duty as his by right and could not honorably refuse. Yet a succession of court decisions made him angry, anxious, and deeply resentful. His first disappointment was Ma Xinyi’s appointment to Zhejiang. Jiang Yili had been passed over, which meant that Zuo had lost control of the province. Next came Li’s transfer to act as Governor-General of Liangjiang, placing him above Zuo in standing and precedence—a most unpleasant thought. Then Yang Yuebin, still in Jiangxi, was ordered to proceed promptly to his post in Shaanxi and Gansu. Zhejiang was required to contribute a hundred thousand taels a month toward his military expenses and first to raise eighty thousand for his army’s departure. Zuo’s temper flared. Every day, when receiving his staff, he roundly abused Zeng Guofan, Li Hongzhang, and Guo Songtao. After several days his anger abated a little. Since he would not resign and return to farming, he must undertake something substantial and impressive. He was confident of his military ability; the difficulty was an ever-shrinking revenue base. Before Ma arrived, he needed a resourceful man to help arrange matters properly.

That man could only be Hu. “Mr Xueyan,” Zuo said, “they have squeezed me until I have nowhere to turn. Where do you think I can raise funds? It is difficult everywhere.” Together they went through the fifteen provinces one by one. Leaving aside the poor provinces, every wealthy source of revenue had already been claimed. Jiangsu and Anhui lay within Liangjiang, where Zeng and his pupil Li were deeply entrenched. Shen Baozhen’s treatment of the Hunan Army in Jiangxi was warning enough. Shandong and Shanxi supplied the capital, and their governors, Yan Jingming and Shen Guifen, were upright, shrewd, and difficult men to deal with. Hubei consumed Sichuan salt, collecting substantial likin revenue through an office at Shashi. But Guanwen was the only bannerman among the governors and governors-general; right or wrong, he enjoyed the court’s protection, and gaining access there would be hard. As for prosperous Sichuan, Luo Bingzhang governed it. Remembering their old relationship as patron and secretary, Zuo could hardly bring himself to play out a “seizure of Chengdu.” “Fujian is desperately poor. The only places left to me are your province and Guangdong. Guangdong withholds the contributions due to me. That hateful Guo Yunxian recognizes no one but Zeng Disheng and Li Shaoquan! I cannot forget it.” Zuo paused. “As for Ma Gushan, I hear he is reasonable enough. But Zeng recommended him, and he passed the examinations in the same year as Li. He may not be willing to help me. Mr Xueyan, what am I to do?”

Hu remained silent. His position was difficult. He must repay Zuo’s recognition and favor, yet Zhejiang was his home province. He could not devise schemes for Zuo that would earn the curses of its people. Hu usually spoke decisively and accepted even the greatest difficulties without hesitation; such a prolonged silence was almost unprecedented. Mildly surprised, Zuo pressed him for an explanation. “To be frank, my lord, I am in a difficult position. Zhejiang is now the only province you can draw upon, so naturally your supplies and pay must come from it. Raise too little, and it will not suffice; raise too much, and the people suffer. Everyone will say it was my idea. Among my own countrymen, that would be an uncomfortable position. If you continued to act as Governor of Zhejiang, I could still suggest ways to balance one source against another and adapt to circumstances, taking both interests into account. But Governor Ma is coming. I am only a probationary circuit intendant assigned to Jiangxi, whom you had transferred here for service. Once you leave Zhejiang, I shall have no right to concern myself with its affairs. I must even hand over my post at the Reconstruction Bureau, let alone anything else.”

Zuo nodded throughout. When Hu finished, he gestured for patience. “I understand all the circumstances you have described. We must discuss them carefully.
