Through every change, one principle remains: you must continue to help me. How you are to help we shall consider presently. First, let us deal with your difficulty. As you say, Zhejiang is my only base, and Zhejiang must provide my army’s funds. Moreover, there must be a fixed sum, remitted each month on a fixed date, not one day late. Mr Xueyan, in everything we first establish what is reasonable, then appeal to personal feeling. If both reason and feeling support us and we still cannot prevail, I naturally have methods of my own.” Hu did not know what the last words implied. Keeping to the immediate issue, he asked, “What sum do you intend to fix, my lord?” “What would you suggest?” Zuo lowered his voice. “Between ourselves, I shall tell you the truth. I actually have eighteen thousand men, but funding must allow a margin. We should calculate for twenty-three thousand.” Hu reckoned rapidly. A soldier’s monthly pay, grain, weapons, ammunition, horse fodder, and miscellaneous needs—tents, cooking pots, bowls, and the rest—averaged five taels. Twenty-three thousand men meant a hundred and fifteen thousand taels. Add the commander’s own expenses, the salaries and board of secretaries and commissioned officials, and the costs of hospitality, and no less than a hundred and fifty thousand taels a month would do.

For Zhejiang to bear that enormous sum alone was too much. Hu therefore spoke tactfully. “Fujian and Zhejiang are one family. Over the years, Fujian has contributed at least three million taels toward Zhejiang’s military expenses. Now that Fujian has trouble, we should naturally help. Besides, the troops you command are Zhejiang troops; Zhejiang ought to pay them. But you understand the province’s condition better than anyone. If we could determine our income by our expenditure, matters would be easier.” The usual maxim was to determine expenditure by income; Hu had reversed it, which was something new. Stroking his drooping mustache, Zuo asked with a smile, “What do you mean by determining income by expenditure? I should like to hear.” “Suppose there is a bowl of soup. One man takes a ladleful, another takes a ladleful; very little reaches anyone’s mouth—” “Ah!” Zuo interrupted. “I understand. I had the same thought. First, we absolutely cannot agree to the contribution for Shaanxi and Gansu. Second, Guangdong’s contribution to Zhejiang exists only in name. I shall petition to have it discontinued.” At this his eyes moved from side to side, and a slow, exceedingly secretive smile spread over his face.

There was much behind that smile. Hu felt he must understand it and deliberately probed. “Guangdong’s contribution is a picture of a cake. Though it cannot feed us, it is something to look at.” “No. By asking for its cancellation, I shall show the court that it is only a painted cake. Mr Xueyan—” Zuo suddenly grew excited. “Watch this old man’s methods! I shall turn the painted cake into a great thick barley cake, enough to fill our bellies. Do you believe me?” “Of course. Will you need me as an assistant in this conjuring trick?” “Naturally! Without you I could never perform this trick of producing a cake from bare ground—and sawing a living man in two besides.” Though meant as a joke, the last words were jarring. Half in jest, Hu asked, “Whom do you mean to saw in two, my lord?” “Whom?” Zuo’s smile had grown fierce. “My own in-law.” Hu was appalled. He had long known that Zuo bore a grievance against Guo Songtao, one that time had deepened rather than eased. But how had it reached the point of such language? For a moment he stood speechless. Zuo too ceased smiling. His face grew serious, his eyes wide, his mouth firmly closed; yet his gaze flickered and his lips moved, as though powerful waves were passing through his mind. Hu listened with even closer attention to hear how this “sawing a living man in two” was to be done.

“Xueyan!” For the first time Zuo dropped the formal address and used his courtesy name, treating him as an intimate friend. “I know you have not read many books. But understanding the world is itself learning, and in that respect I admire you.” “You are too kind.” Hu was a little uneasy but remained frank. “If you have something to say, please give me your instructions. Such a tall hat of praise is rather too much for me.” “Why should I need flattery between us? What I mean is that you understand half of my character and conduct. The other half you not only fail to understand, but may strongly disapprove of. That is because you have read too little. If you had studied more, you would understand that other half as well, and see that I have no choice: circumstances compel it.” So that was what he meant. Hu felt almost overwhelmed by the confidence shown him. “My lord, if you speak to me of ‘the Way of Great Learning lies in manifesting luminous virtue,’ I shall understand nothing.” “I shall speak of history, not the Classics. First, tell me what these two sayings mean: ‘For a greater righteousness, sacrifice even one’s kin,’ and ‘For public duty, forget private interests.’”

Hu had no answer, nor felt one was needed. He replied honestly, “Please do not examine me, my lord. Begin with those sayings and tell me your plans.” “I am not examining you. My point is that ordinary people may condemn my actions bitterly. Yet though I fail my kin, I do not fail what is right; though I may be ashamed in private relationships, I have nothing to blush for in public duty. Common opinion cannot understand this. Only a learned man will say in his heart: ‘Zuo is a hero born for his age. He could do no otherwise.’” The speech was rather bookish, but Hu understood it well enough and grasped its implication: Zuo meant to deal Guo Songtao a severe blow. What he could not see was what blow lay within Zuo’s power. His question was answered at once. Zuo rose, sat at his desk, took a brush, and spread out paper. He rapidly drew a map, adding place-names among the curved lines and dots. Hu recognized the Fujian–Guangdong frontier. “Li Shixian is at Zhangzhou, which fell on the fourteenth day of the ninth month. Brigade Commander Lukui was killed in action; Circuit Intendant Xu Xiaofeng of Tingzhou, Zhangzhou, and Longyan died at his post. Li has probably more than eight thousand men and must not be underestimated.” Pointing to the triangle formed by Changting, Liancheng, and Shanghang, Zuo continued, “Wang Haiyang is here. In my judgment he is fiercer than Li. Still, there is no cause for alarm about putting these rebels down. Xueyan, you must have learned a little strategy in recent years. Where do you think Li and Wang can escape?”

For a moment Hu seemed stumped. He studied the map closely before answering. “They came from Jiangxi in the west. To the east lies the sea, a long way off—and without ships they could not put to sea anyway. In the north, your troops are pressing down. Ah!” Understanding came suddenly, and he spoke with confidence. “Their only way out is south into Guangdong. Jiaying Prefecture will bear the first attack.” Zuo nodded deeply, stroking his mustache with a smile. “Exactly. Jiaying will bear the first attack. Then we shall no longer be feeding ourselves on painted cakes.” There was a hidden meaning in his words. He did not continue, and Hu dared not ask, fearing that a mistaken guess might lead him into a grave indiscretion. Zuo, however, had no such reservations. He truly regarded Hu as a confidant fit to hear the deepest secrets. “Guo Yunxian has always feared that Zeng Disheng would drive the rebels into Guangdong. He never imagined it would be his own in-law who did so. When that happens, Xueyan, the situation will be entirely different.” Hu shuddered involuntarily. Zuo’s methods seemed altogether too ruthless. He had guessed this very thing, certainly; but a conjecture was one matter, and hearing it confirmed in the man’s own words was another.

“Xueyan, tell me what the situation will be then.” “Yes.” Hu considered. “The court will naturally turn to your prestige and ability, appoint you imperial commissioner, and place military affairs in Fujian, Zhejiang, and Guangdong under your authority. Governor Guo—” He stopped. His meaning was clear: under Zuo’s handling, Guo would suffer severely. “Precisely. Circumstances must lead to that. Then, Xueyan, I shall no longer burden Zhejiang. Guo Yunxian will have to raise funds obediently for me. Unless—” Zuo pondered for a long while. “There is no certainty. Guo is foolish and obstinate. He may still refuse to see reason.” “And if he does, my lord, what will you do?”

“Then he cannot blame me. What a pity!” The two remarks seemed disconnected. Quick-witted as he was, Hu could not guess their meaning. Since the matter concerned a possible reduction of Zhejiang’s burden, he had to ask. “A pity in what respect, my lord?” “A pity I have no suitable man in reserve to become a governor.” In other words, if Guo Songtao failed to raise enough money for him, Zuo would not hesitate to bring him down and put someone else in his place. That would indeed be “forgetting private interests for public duty” and “sacrificing kin for a greater righteousness.” “We shall see when the time comes. It is too early to speak of it.” Zuo stared for some time at his map. Suddenly he struck the desk and sprang up. “Yes! That is how it shall be done!”

He explained his sudden inspiration, pointing to the map. His forces were still insufficient. Enveloping the rebels on three sides and squeezing them toward Guangdong would be difficult. If one gap remained unblocked and the rebels escaped to sea, whether off Fujian or Zhejiang, he would still bear responsibility. Would that not defeat his own purpose? He therefore wished to enlist Li Hongzhang’s Anhui Army. Their cooperation at Huzhou had been satisfactory; surely Li would not refuse another request. “But I cannot make the approach myself,” Zuo said. “If I invite them, I must fund the visiting troops. Suppose he sends ten thousand men: that means at least fifty or sixty thousand taels a month. Add their departure expenses, and the first allocation must be a hundred thousand. That is beyond me. If the court orders the Anhui Army to bring its own pay and provisions and assist in Fujian, we need provide no more than fifty thousand as a reward. That would satisfy appearances. Xueyan, what do you think of my calculation?” “It is a good one. But Governor Li may refuse to provide both pay and food. If he supplies the pay and we the grain, he will have nothing to object to. His men must be paid even if they remain idle. As for obtaining an imperial order, we must ask Fujian officials in Beijing to work for it.” “That may not succeed.” Zuo shook his head. “No Fujian man there currently holds a sufficiently high position; their words carry little weight. Fujian and Zhejiang depend on one another like lips and teeth. Think of the senior Zhejiang men in Beijing. Whom could we approach?”

“Among the senior Zhejiang men, Lord Xu naturally comes first. But he has retired from the Ministry of Personnel. His eldest son, however, is much favored in the Southern Study. He also has a nephew in the Grand Council secretariat, dealing particularly with military affairs—” “Exactly! Exactly!” Zuo interrupted. “There could be no better approach. Please arrange it for me. The Xu family is one of Hangzhou’s great clans. Surely you know someone?” “There are many of them, and I know several. But a matter of this importance cannot be entrusted to just anyone.” Hu thought a moment. “I should like to go to Shanghai and see Seventh Master Xu. I can put your business before him and at the same time try to persuade him to return to Hangzhou and take charge of reconstruction.” After considering, Zuo found this an excellent plan. “Seventh Master Xu” was Xu Naizhao, Governor of Jiangsu at the time of Liu Lichuan’s Small Swords uprising, now living as a refugee in Shanghai. His elder brother, Hu’s “Sixth Master Xu,” was Xu Naipu, retired Minister of Personnel. Unable to return south because of the rebels, he had become a kind of home away from home for Zhejiang men in Beijing. A senior examination graduate who had long presided over literary selection, he was treated with particular respect by the capital’s elders. His eldest son, Xu Pengshou, had graduated in the same cohort as Li Hongzhang and headed the metropolitan examination list in the dingwei year, the twenty-seventh of Daoguang. Xu Naipu also had a nephew, Xu Gengshen, who had rendered important service during the Xinyou coup. Now a highly favored senior secretary of the Grand Council, he understood military affairs and strategy and exercised considerable influence over deployments. This route opened doors in every direction. If necessary, Xu Pengshou could use his fellowship with Li to write him a particularly earnest letter. There was every reason to expect success.

Bringing Xu Naizhao back to direct Hangzhou’s reconstruction was likewise an excellent and necessary move. Once Ma arrived, Hu would have to leave, and Xu was the best possible replacement. First, he had governed a province and risen through the Hanlin Academy, a gentleman of established rank in every sense. Second, Ma was not only his junior but a fellow graduate of his nephew Xu Pengshou. Visiting Ma in the position of an honored uncle of the older generation, Xu would be entitled to the formal courtesy of the central gate being opened for his arrival and departure. His voice on local affairs would carry correspondingly greater weight. Third, he was just, incorruptible, and universally respected, eminently qualified to lead the local gentlemen. Zuo therefore gladly accepted Hu’s proposal and offered to write Xu a most earnest personal letter.

With public business settled, they turned to “private” business, which was itself public: Hu’s future position. Zuo intended to have him transferred to Fujian. He need not travel with the army, however; he would remain in Shanghai and manage all Zuo’s affairs there. Hu agreed without hesitation. From the following day, Zuo energetically began carrying out their agreed steps. Besides military reports, he sent several memorials in succession. The first pleaded that Zhejiang could scarcely meet its own army’s needs and asked the Ministry of Revenue to find another source for the contribution to Shaanxi and Gansu. The second urged Ma Xinyi to take office quickly and requested that Provincial Treasurer Jiang Yili temporarily administer the governorship until his arrival. The third stated that, of the eighty thousand taels ordered for Yang Yuebin’s departure expenses, only half had been raised, and that with the greatest difficulty.

The fourth concerned Fujian. Every year, as summer passed into autumn, the Ministry of Revenue pressed the provinces to remit their contributions to the capital. These paid not merely the Eight Banner guards but the salaries and allowances of princes, ministers, and officials; the expenses of government offices; sacrifices at ancestral temples and imperial tombs; and the silver presented at three annual festivals for the personal use of the emperor and the two empress dowagers. Provincial military contributions might fall into arrears; the capital’s funds could not. Fujian owed a hundred thousand taels in customs revenue and twenty thousand in tea tax. An edict demanded payment in full during the twelfth month and threatened named impeachments under the prescribed rules if further excuses delayed essential expenditure. Despite this stern order, Zuo meant to default once more. Only thus could he demonstrate Fujian’s poverty and its need for Zhejiang’s help. Yet default required method; blunt defiance would not suffice. Besides pleading poverty, he remitted his own ten-thousand-tael governor-general’s integrity allowance through a draft bank to the Ministry of Revenue, to be credited toward the capital’s funds.

The fifth memorial requested an end to Guangdong’s military contributions to Zhejiang. Its chief purpose was to show the court that these contributions had been no more than a painted cake. The stated reason for discontinuance was brief: “Military operations in Zhejiang having now ended, the above contributions should naturally cease.” But the preceding accounts were set out precisely. From the first month of the first Tongzhi year to the eighth month of the current year, including the intercalary month, thirty-three months had elapsed. Guangdong should have remitted three million three hundred thousand taels. Zhejiang had actually received only two hundred and eighty thousand, of which two hundred and twenty thousand came from likin. Zeng Guofan had reported that Guangdong’s likin receipts, from the levy’s introduction to the end of that eighth month, totaled one million two hundred thousand taels. Thus Zhejiang’s army had received “not even two-tenths.”

The sixth memorial concerned personnel after the move to Fujian. Its heading requested rewards for officials who had managed military supplies and funding; it also sought their transfer. Hu was naturally included. He was then a probationary circuit intendant assigned to Jiangxi, holding the honorary rank of salt commissioner. Zuo asked that he be “reassigned to Fujian for substantive appointment as a circuit intendant, and granted the honorary rank of provincial judicial commissioner.” Such wording looked less splendid than military requests for a peacock feather or the title of baturu. In reality, it did Hu a tremendous service. Once approved, the change from probationary status to eligibility for substantive appointment meant that he could immediately fill any vacant circuit intendancy. The judicial commissioner’s honorary rank would also qualify him to act in that higher office, rising above an ordinary circuit post into the senior provincial administration. Zuo’s recommendation was worth at least three years of service.

Hu knew nothing of this memorial when Zuo sent it, for he had already reached Shanghai. Carrying Zuo’s personal letter, he called on Seventh Master Xu, and their conversation went most agreeably. He completed all the business entrusted to him, stayed only two nights, spoke with his aged mother of West Lake after the devastation, spent half a day with Gu Yingchun, and returned to Hangzhou in the same boat. The first man seeking him there was not Zuo but Jiang Yili, the provincial treasurer temporarily administering the governorship. Jiang had already left word at Fukang Bank that he was to be notified the moment Hu arrived, so that they could meet. “Mr Xueyan,” he said with a miserable expression, “you must help me. The commander has ordered Zhejiang to send Fujian two hundred thousand taels every month, remitted on the twelfth without a single day’s delay. Is that not asking the impossible?” Hu too was startled. Zhejiang had suffered especially grievously in the Taiping war; devastation lay everywhere, and everything awaited rebuilding. Where could it find two hundred thousand taels a month to support an army entering Fujian? Hu had reckoned that scraping together a hundred thousand would already strain the province to the utmost. Now Zuo demanded twice that amount and would not even permit a delay in payment. It was excessive.

“On both public and private grounds, you must speak,” Jiang continued. “Privately, the commander listens to you and follows your advice. Publicly, as the saying goes, wool must come from the sheep. If this sum is forced from us, the people must suffer. You could not bear that either. Besides, I am, after all, only the provincial treasurer.” That last sentence revealed his real distress. For the moment he held the governor’s seal and could issue effective orders. Once Ma arrived, however, if the new governor considered the contribution excessive and ordered a reduction, Jiang would have to obey. Yet Zuo was the patron who had raised him, and Jiang had already undertaken to maintain the original sum. Would he not be crushed between the two? After thinking it over, Hu concluded that he must take this difficulty upon himself. He nodded. “Very well. I shall find a way.” “Then there is hope!” Relieved, Jiang clasped his hands and asked, “I imagine you already have a plan. Might I hear it?” “Of course. I wished to consult you. First, I shall ask Lord Zuo to reduce the amount.” “Reduce it by how much?” “At least by thirty percent.”

“That would still be a hundred and forty thousand a month,” Jiang said. “Now that the province is pacified, I need no longer command troops. I ought to accomplish something in my proper office. Consider—” He listed his duties. The most important for the economy and the people’s livelihood was the repair of waterworks. Zhejiang was a land of cultivated fields: those near the hills were poor, those near water rich. Silk production made mulberry cultivation particularly important, and mulberries required much the same irrigation as paddy fields. Since ancient times, officials whose kindness was long remembered in Zhejiang had invariably distinguished themselves in water management.

The chief concern was northern Zhejiang, and there the sea dikes were paramount. The Qianlong Emperor had made inspection of these dikes the stated purpose of all six southern tours—proof enough of their importance. To withstand the tides, they needed to be built of stone. Since the rebellion, the stone dikes around Haining had received no repairs and were steadily crumbling. Restoration would cost more than a million taels. With no alternative, temporary earthen works would have to suffice. “Even earth dikes will cost three hundred thousand,” Jiang said. “The materials are cheap; labor is dear. After the great disorder, there are fewer able-bodied men, which makes labor expensive. We must discuss how to raise money for repairs. If the sea dikes give way, the consequences will be terrible. The thought keeps me awake.” Even if this display was affected, Hu found it worthy of respect. “Since the Three Dynasties, the danger is only that men may not care for a good name.” Jiang’s deepest motives need not be examined. He sounded as though he wanted to be a good official. Hu might as well encourage and support him, pressing him into that path for the province’s benefit. “Do not worry,” he answered at once. “I shall make a plan. Let everyone contribute according to his means; it should not be difficult.” “That would be excellent.” Jiang was pleased. “The dredging of West Lake cannot wait either. Its waters irrigate fields in Hangzhou and Haining. Everything must be put in order before next spring, and that too will cost tens of thousands of taels. Is a treasurer’s task easy? I must ask your advice on new revenue.” “For the present, salt is our only resource. My method could tide us over. In six months or a year, the Fujian campaign should reach a conclusion, and Zhejiang will no longer have to contribute. Then things will be easier.” “I think so too. But I know little of salt administration. The commander is an expert.” “Lord Zuo is an expert?” Hu asked in surprise.

“Nothing surprising about that. Do you not know? The commander’s family is joined by marriage to that of Lord Tao, posthumously honored as Wenyi.” “Ah! So that is it!” Hu suddenly understood. Zuo’s knowledge of salt administration had a history. When Zuo was twenty-six, Tao Shu, Governor-General of Liangjiang, had completed an inspection of troops in Jiangxi and obtained leave to visit the ancestral graves at his native Anhua in Hunan. Passing through Liling, he was received with the customary official arrangements. While preparing his residence, the magistrate asked Zuo, then head of the Lujiang Academy at Liling, to compose a couplet. Tao admired it immensely. Learning who had written it, he immediately sent for Zuo. Sure enough, when Hu raised Zhejiang’s salt administration, Zuo declared at once that reforming it was one of the tasks he must complete before relinquishing the acting governorship. He intended to replace the quota-certificate system with salt permits, beginning in the first month of the fourth Tongzhi year, on a one-year trial.

“My plan has four parts,” Zuo said. “First, suppress smuggling; second, abolish irregular fees; third, reduce prices; fourth, conduct a proper registration of salt-producing households. All salt-tax receipts will go to the army. After expenses, there should be at least a hundred thousand taels a month—enough for half my requirement.” So Zhejiang was indeed expected to provide two hundred thousand a month for his Fujian campaign, exactly as Jiang had said. Hu remained composed and set that aside for the moment, speaking first of salt. “The last three parts can be carried out. Suppressing smuggling will be more difficult. Zhejiang salt is sold in Songjiang, which is Jiangsu territory and beyond our reach. Have you considered that?” “Certainly. I was going to discuss it with you. Did you not once mention a leader of the Grain-Boat Guild in Songjiang, an honest and capable man? Would he help Zhejiang?” “His surname is You. If you give the word, he will gladly serve. But what form would his help take?”

“Naturally, he would lead troops against smugglers.” Hu had asked a question whose answer he knew. Now he deliberately shook his head. “I fear that cannot be done. He is a grain-transport boatman, not an official. By the rules of rank he must address even a bazong respectfully as ‘sir.’ How could he command troops? Even if he agreed, the officers and men assigned to him would not obey.” “True.” Zuo hesitated. “But unless he leads troops against smugglers, how can we make use of him?” “You know the workings of the guild well. His followers all obey his word. Give him an official capacity, and there will certainly be results against smuggling around Songjiang.” “Ah, I understand.” After a moment Zuo said, “There is no objection to that. Let him recruit his own men—not too many, of course; two or three hundred will do. We shall recommend him for an official rank and let him command them. I shall tell the salt commissioner to arrange it. Please make contact with You. Pay and operating expenses will follow the rules of my own army and come from the salt administration’s funds.” Hu was delighted. He had found a livelihood for Brother You and a means of serving public business at the same time. He gladly agreed.

They next turned to the business Jiang Yili had entrusted to Hu: Zhejiang’s monthly contribution to the army in Fujian. “Zhejiang formerly depended on Fujian for more than three million taels,” Hu began. “Now that Zhejiang troops are going to its aid, Zhejiang should naturally support them. What arrangement do you propose, my lord? We should know early enough to prepare.” “I have settled it with Xiangquan: Zhejiang will send me two hundred thousand a month.” “Two hundred thousand is not much. The trouble is that Zhejiang’s strength has been so badly drained.” Hu deliberately paused, then asked without warning, “Once you reach Fujian, do you intend to request the transfer of Jiang and Yang to assist you?” Zuo was baffled. “I have no such intention. Both have worked their way up to senior provincial office. For one thing, Fujian has no suitable positions; for another, the court might not approve. Besides, why should I clear the road for Ma Gushan, vacating two important posts so that he can bring in his own men?” Hu had expected this answer, particularly the last point. Keeping Jiang as provincial treasurer and recommending Yang Changjun as judicial commissioner was a calculated move to restrain Ma and protect Zuo’s revenue in Zhejiang. Why would Zuo surrender that advantage? Hu had asked precisely to lead the conversation toward his real purpose. “Exactly. If Jiang and Yang remain secure in their posts, it will be almost as though you still held the acting governorship. But I fear I must offer some unwelcome advice.” “Go ahead. There is nothing you and I cannot discuss. Besides, I know it will be for my good.”

“Yes, I am thinking of your interests: a small but steady stream, a firm and lasting arrangement,” Hu replied calmly. “Zhejiang’s income is not merely limited; it cannot yet be estimated with certainty. The land-tax payments have been remitted by imperial order. Salt revenue will not improve before late spring or early summer next year. The rice levy depends upon neighboring provinces’ willingness to help. Only likin can be relied upon. As trade revives, receipts should increase, though even that depends upon the honesty of the collectors. On the expenditure side, reconstruction comes first, and the sea dikes second; both require great sums. Government offices and civil and military salaries must also be provided for, and the province must maintain some troops of its own. Suppose two hundred thousand taels is scraped together each month and sent first to your supply bureau, leaving the provincial treasury bare and all other work at a standstill. How could Jiang Xiangquan remain treasurer?” “Surely—” Zuo was silent for a while. “Surely things cannot be as difficult as you suggest?” “Of course. I have overstated them a little. But consider Governor Ma too. Having just risen to provincial authority, he naturally wishes to accomplish something. If he is hampered on every side, what can he do but press Jiang? And to press Jiang is to press you.” Hu paused. “Governor Shen of Jiangxi owed his advancement entirely to Grand Secretary Zeng. Yet he withheld his province’s likin revenues when he chose, and the court did not blame him for delaying the Zeng brothers’ eastern campaign. Governor Ma may not be as fierce as Shen, but neither is he content merely to drift.” The example of Shen Baozhen’s rupture with Zeng put Zuo on his guard. No one paid closer attention to practical interests. Considered calmly, Ma’s position was secure and offered no weakness to attack. If a dispute arose, Zuo might not prevail. Jiang would bear the first blow; once removed from Zhejiang, how could he provide two hundred thousand a month? With this thought, Zuo asked in a more even temper, “Then what do you advise, Xueyan?”

“The amount must be reduced,” Hu answered directly. “By how much?” “I dare not fix a figure. You must ask the officers and men to bear some hardship for the present. Once matters improve, they can be compensated.” “Very well.” Zuo nodded. “I too cannot bear to overburden Zhejiang. As you suggest, I shall have the supply bureau recalculate and reduce the sum as far as possible. But you know my position. I have always been isolated and unsupported. I must open a way forward.” “Yes,” Hu answered without expression. “You must help me greatly. How do you think I should find that way?” “Have you not already decided, my lord?” He meant the plan to gain control of Guangdong. Zuo frowned slightly. “Removing Guo is not difficult. The question is who can replace him. Xiangquan still lacks the standing for a governorship. If my recommendation is rejected, I shall find it hard to speak again. The consequences are serious. I cannot act rashly before I am sure. Yet I cannot tell Xiangquan this.” Hu listened closely, searching beneath the words. If Jiang wanted Guangdong, he would need another source of support. In other words, if influential patrons at court could ensure that the recommendation would succeed, Zuo would gladly submit it.

Hu then asked himself whether he should help Jiang and whether he could. The first question required little thought: Jiang’s promotion to Guangdong would bring great public and private advantages. As for whether help was possible, it was too early to say. Much depended upon effort; if he did his utmost, there was hope. “You are a pillar of the court, my lord, and have always enjoyed great imperial favor,” he said. “In Shanghai I heard from people newly arrived from Beijing that Prince Gong values you highly, and Prince Chun admires you still more. Grand Secretary Zeng was allowed to recommend governors for provinces within his military command. Why should you not have the same right? As for Xiangquan’s standing, promotion from Treasurer of Zhejiang to Governor of Guangdong would be no extraordinary leap. Governor Ma is an example ready to hand. Guangdong is different because its maritime customs revenues are closely connected with the Imperial Household Department. Still, if someone in Beijing looks after the matter, there is hope.” “Exactly—someone in Beijing must look after it. Xiangquan is at a disadvantage there.” “It is not too late to begin tending those neglected fires.” Zuo looked at him intently, pondered for some time, then finally said, “You might discuss it with Xiangquan.” “Yes.”

“He must rely on you,” Zuo continued. “And I can do without you even less. You command no troops for me and run no supply bureau, yet your work is more important than either. Once I leave, you must set out promptly and remain in Shanghai. If the supply bureau falls short—whether of pay, grain, or military equipment—you alone will be my resource.” The responsibility weighed heavily upon Hu, but he could show no hesitation. He braced himself. “Yes, my lord. You may be at ease.” “With that assurance, I truly can.” Zuo breathed out, then asked, “What would you like me to do for you? I intend to leave at the end of the month; there is still half a month. If you want anything, tell me early.” Hu had already considered this. After Zuo left, Jiang would hold the acting governor’s seal and could still be consulted; yet a request to Zuo would be more direct and effective. During their half-year together, Hu had asked no favor. He must not miss the opportunity now, especially when Zuo himself had invited him to speak. Further silence would seem affected and false, and would hardly be wise.

He decided to speak freely, first retreating a little in order to advance. “There are many things I wish to ask, but I fear you may find them tiresome.” “Not at all!” Zuo waved both hands. “I am always asking you for help and owe you many favors. Speak.” “First, the former Governor Wang died so tragically. You upheld justice, investigated what had happened, and reported the facts to the throne. But the case is not finished. I should like you to see it through.” “Oh?” More than two years had passed, and Zuo’s recollection was vague. “In what way is it unfinished?”

“I mean your memorial in the fourth month of the first Tongzhi year, reporting the inquiry into Wang Lüqian’s obstruction and failures—” “Ah!” Reminded, Zuo said, “Wait a moment.” He lifted his riding jacket and took a key from his belt. Keys signified authority. In institutions as great as the Shenji Camp or the Imperial Household Department, to be designated keeper of the keys meant being entrusted with leadership. In a wealthy household, a steward—or Wang Xifeng in Dream of the Red Chamber—likewise displayed practical power by holding the keys. A key was small, however, insufficiently imposing to proclaim such authority. Hence the additions: usually quantity made the impression. Useless keys were tied together, sometimes threaded on a large iron ring that clattered in the hand. The intention resembled a jailer’s: shake the bunch, and every prisoner would submit. Yet the truly weighty key was often a single one. Zuo had only one, threaded on a silk cord at his belt. Pulling it out, he leaned toward a book chest, unlocked it, and removed a thick stack of red booklets. From a distance Hu could read the large characters on their covers: “Copies of Memorials.” Zuo found the booklet for the fourth month of the first Tongzhi year, put on his smoky-crystal reading spectacles, and studied it before asking, “What remains unsettled, Xueyan?”

“If you look at the reply received then, my lord, you will see.” The reply could not readily be found. “You must know what it said. Tell me.” “Yes.” Hu now felt awkward.

When Wang Youling had held Hangzhou through the siege, his principal source of funds had been Shaoxing. Wang Lüqian, the militia commissioner residing there, had been uncooperative. Unscrupulous local gentry, skilled with their pens, surrounded him and portrayed the governor’s demands for military funds as callous extortion for private gain. Officials and people had almost imperceptibly become enemies. This made the position of Prefect Liao Zongyuan extremely difficult. When the rebels advanced from Xiaoshan toward Shaoxing, imperial gunboats actually clashed with the local militia. Badly outnumbered, Liao’s bodyguards lost twelve men, and he himself suffered a head wound. Wang Lüqian should have intervened and restored order, but stood aside. Soon Shaoxing fell and Liao died at his post. Wang Lüqian had already fled to Ningbo and escaped by sea to Fujian. The needless loss of Shaoxing, together with Wang’s constant obstruction and disregard of the larger cause, filled Governor Wang with bitter hatred. In his blood-written letter from the doomed city, he said that he could not “close his eyes in death.” Hu too had never forgotten and wished to avenge him.

Even without Hu’s intervention, of course, the court would have investigated responsibility for Hangzhou’s fall and would not let Wang Lüqian escape punishment. In the spring of the following year, the first Tongzhi year, Qingrui, Governor-General of Fujian and Zhejiang, was ordered to arrest Wang and send him to Quzhou for interrogation by the new Zhejiang governor, Zuo. Zuo recommended penal military exile to Xinjiang; the throne approved, giving some satisfaction to Governor Wang’s memory. But the chief offender in the affair was Zhang Cunhao, a wealthy Shaoxing gentleman. He had led the false accusations that Liao’s gunboats were in league with the rebels, the killing of the guards, and the assault on the prefect. Zuo’s memorial stated that Zhang and the others, enraged by Liao’s urgent demands for contributions, had acted from private resentment and committed unforgivable offenses. Once Shaoxing was recovered, they should be arrested and punished to the full extent of the law. Now not merely Shaoxing but all Zhejiang had been cleared. Yet no arrest had followed. That was what Hu meant by the unfinished case.

His present difficulty arose from a sudden reluctance to be harsh. Amid the disorders of the age, who had failed whom, and who owed what to whom? Such questions were never simple. Years had passed, and everyone had survived a great catastrophe. Perhaps they ought now to be calmer and more forgiving. Zuo could not guess this change of heart. “We have many matters to discuss,” he urged. “Take them one by one, quickly. We must not waste time.” Hu had to tell the truth. As he spoke, another thought occurred to him. After explaining his original intention, he added, “You might show mercy and spare Zhang Cunhao, but he should not get off too easily. Let him make amends through some useful service. Could he be required to contribute to a public undertaking?” “Certainly. What are his means?” “He was wealthy before, and I hear he is still comfortably off.” “Then I shall tell Xiangquan to instruct the Shaoxing prefect to summon him and require a contribution proportionate to his means for some local work of merit.” “That will satisfy both public justice and private feeling. As for those loyal men who died during the two disasters, the Reconstruction Bureau is collecting their histories and reporting them in turn. I hope you will memorialize promptly, to comfort the dead.” “Naturally. I shall attend to that before leaving. Now tell me the second matter.”

The second was important in both public and private affairs. Since Ma’s appointment had begun to change Zhejiang’s political arrangements, Hu had wished to stop acting as banker to the provincial treasury. Jiang would not release him. At the slightest mention, he clasped his hands repeatedly and begged Hu to “continue helping.” Hu valued personal feeling too much to refuse and had reluctantly carried on. “Things are different now,” he said, after explaining. “You have ordered me to remain in Shanghai. If grain, money, or arms are urgently needed, I alone must answer. The responsibility is too great for me to continue serving Zhejiang’s treasury as well.” By “serving,” he meant finding advances whenever receipts failed to cover immediate needs. Zuo understood, but saw it differently. “Zhejiang’s treasury is to remit a hundred and forty thousand taels to me each month through your bank. If you remain its banker, it will be merely a transfer from your left hand to your right. It costs no trouble. Why refuse so firmly? In my view, your continuing in that role can only benefit me. Whenever funds arrive, they can be remitted. If the front urgently needs money, you can arrange it more easily.” “No,” Hu replied. “First, once I have been transferred at your request for service in Fujian, it is improper for me to continue acting for Zhejiang’s treasury. Second, precisely because I am managing your supplies, I must keep my dealings with Zhejiang clearly separate. If someone raises objections later, your reputation will not be involved.” “I am deeply obliged. You think of my interests at every turn. Since you insist, I shall tell Xiangquan.”

The promise lifted a weight from Hu. He alone saw the entire situation clearly. Although the contribution for Fujian had been reduced by sixty thousand taels, the remaining sum was still an immense burden. The new governor would naturally have plans of his own. Even if he could not restore everything at once, the sea dikes alone required a large expenditure. Frost’s Descent had already passed, the season when river officials reported that the waters were safely under control. At the beginning of the new year, however, work must start immediately. From the spring “peach-blossom flood” onward, rising waters at the turn of spring and summer might bring catastrophe. Would it be easy to act as banker to the treasury then? Hu could plead inability to advance the cost of the dikes. But if Ma said, “Then let Intendant Hu’s bank advance the contribution to Fujian,” could he refuse, on either public or private grounds? In truth, even after relinquishing the treasury business he would still have to advance funds for Zuo. The difference lay in how the transaction was understood. As the treasury’s banker, he would be expected by Zhejiang to find the money. Zuo, meanwhile, would regard it as an advance for Zhejiang and owe him no gratitude. Hu would please neither side. If instead Zhejiang, unable to remit, privately asked him for an advance, his action would be a public-spirited favor: Ma would be grateful, and Zuo would acknowledge his friendship. Most importantly, a formal loan from his bank to the provincial treasury would carry security and interest, leaving him no risk to bear. “What else is there?” Zuo asked. “You might as well tell me everything now.”

“I shall trouble you no further,” Hu said with a smile. “The rest are small matters I can manage myself.” Yet he had an enormous amount of public business in hand. Besides the bank, his own interests included silk and tea, and there were innumerable favors undertaken for others. With the political arrangements changing and Zuo’s new responsibilities requiring him to remain in Shanghai, he had to settle his public and private affairs in Zhejiang during the interval between Zuo’s departure and Ma’s arrival. He became so busy that meals and sleep lost all regularity. He wished for another mouth and another pair of hands. Even amid this pressure, Zuo frequently summoned him. One day a personal letter arrived, inviting him to visit West Lake the following morning. Hu scarcely knew whether to laugh or cry, but could not refuse. He stayed awake all night to finish several long-delayed and urgent matters originally scheduled for the next morning. Just before dawn he dozed in his clothes. Soon he woke with a start to find bright sunlight filling the windows. He hurriedly washed, dressed, and took his chair toward the lake. Passing the ruined Banner garrison, he noticed unusually large numbers of people walking north along the shore. He sent an attendant to inquire and learned that they were going to see a “Western steamer.” Then he understood. Zuo had not invited him for a leisurely excursion but to witness the trial of a small steam vessel. Hu knew about the undertaking: as early as summer, Zuo had told him that suitable mechanics had been found to attempt its construction. Too busy to ask further, Hu had not imagined that in three or four months they would actually produce a vessel of their own. This was momentous.

If they could build a small steamer, they could build a large one. Hu’s mind ranged quickly over the many advantages China would gain from possessing large steamships. He became so absorbed that only the chair’s stopping roused him. He stepped out before Zhaoqing Temple, one of West Lake’s four great monasteries. A pavilion of canvas stood by the water. Outside it, rank buttons and feathers glittered, and swords flashed like snow. Most striking were several foreigners among the crowd. One wore Western clothes; another wore the uniform of a third-rank Chinese military officer, with an artificial queue attached behind his hat. Hu knew them well. Both were French: Giquel had left military for civil employment and been appointed commissioner of the Ningbo New Customs, hence his civilian dress; d’Aiguebelle had been recommended for the rank of canjiang for military service and willingly adopted Chinese dress to signify allegiance. Zuo valued him highly. On the lake, a small steamer lay secured by a very thick cable, its fire already lit. Hu joined the inspection. While explanations and pointing fingers occupied the group, Zuo emerged from the tent, passed slowly between the civil and military officials standing at attention, halted at the shore, and called loudly, “Ask Lord Hu to come!”

Hu came forward, saluted, and first apologized for not arriving earlier. Then he smiled. “Grand Secretary Zeng and Governor Li both concern themselves with foreign methods and powerful arms. Now they will have to fall behind you.” The compliment delighted Zuo. Stroking his short, graying beard, he nodded. “That is just what I want them to see! I particularly asked you here because only you understand my intention.” Hu dared not pursue the subject. A passing compliment was harmless, but he did not know what deeper purpose Zuo had in mind, and understood little of the matter himself. Better to conceal his ignorance for the moment. Fortunately, this was a time for watching rather than discussion. At a command, the crudely built little vessel began to chug, churning a great patch of water astern. Its engine faltered and resumed like the coughing of a feeble old man, struggling for breath. At least ten thousand officials, soldiers, and ordinary people watched from the shore in anxious silence. The vessel made noise but did not move; everyone feared it would fail. The four mechanics responsible, drenched in sweat, kept climbing in and out of the engine compartment. At last the sound grew stronger and steadier. The vessel suddenly surged forward. “Thank Heaven, it is moving!” Hu exclaimed involuntarily. Moving it was, but scarcely at any speed: it staggered along, barely making headway. In about half an hour it completed a circuit of the lake and returned. The expectant prefect responsible for the work brought the mechanics, now wearing red-tasseled hats, to report. Zuo’s face was grave, but he nevertheless ordered twenty taels for each man.

Seeing his dissatisfaction, the onlookers lost their enthusiasm. Hu felt the same. After standing in the ranks to see Zuo depart, he hurried back to his business in the city. Yet at dusk another bodyguard arrived with Zuo’s card: “The commander will not dine until Lord Hu arrives.” At headquarters Hu was surprised to find both foreign advisers present, with an interpreter named Cai. He greeted Zuo, exchanged bows with d’Aiguebelle and Giquel, and went in to dinner. Though host, Zuo took the highest seat, the two Frenchmen on either side; Hu sat below them. Interpreter Cai, like a bodyguard, could only stand behind Zuo. “For foreign methods, we must consult foreigners,” Zuo told Hu. “I asked Commander d’Aiguebelle and Commissioner Giquel to inspect the vessel. They say the copied design is broadly sound, and that making the engine work at all is a considerable achievement. But speed requires Western machinery. The commander happens to have a book of shipbuilding drawings. You should look at it.” “Yes.” Hu ventured a question. “Your intention, my lord, is—?” “First hear what they say.” Without answering directly, Zuo turned slightly and instructed Cai, “Ask whether they can hire foreign mechanics for me. I wish to manufacture marine engines.” Cai translated into French. Both men answered eagerly, one after the other, speaking rapidly with rolling tongues. “They say they can do more than hire mechanics,” Cai reported. “They would also procure materials, establish a yard, and supervise construction. Now that fighting in Zhejiang is finished, Commander d’Aiguebelle intends to leave military service and return to France. If you wish, he will devote himself to arranging this.” “Ah.” Zuo nodded and gave Hu a penetrating look.

Hu understood and put a succession of questions to the two guests, concentrating upon expense. They knew only approximate figures and could not answer everything. Yet they followed the old Chinese maxim: acknowledge what you know, and acknowledge what you do not. They offered no evasive guesses. Hu could therefore use the reliable figures they supplied, drawing comparisons and deductions, to arrive at a general estimate. Dinner lasted until the first watch. After seeing the foreigners off, Zuo kept Hu behind and invited him into his private office. Once seated, he said at once, “Xueyan, I want to build steam warships ourselves.” Hu was startled. “That is no easy matter. A shipyard would cost at least five hundred thousand taels. Each warship would cost two or three hundred thousand—and one cannot build a yard for a single ship. Say only ten: that is two or three million. Fujian and Zhejiang, even with Liangjiang added, may not have the means.” “True. But do not be hasty. Hear me out, and you will see that my plan is not only practicable but necessary.” Zuo looked about him with satisfaction. “Li Shaoquan’s knowledge comes from experience gained in these last few years. He entered the Hanlin Academy merely because he knew the Book of Songs well. When I began serious study, he probably could not yet finish an essay. As for great warships upon the open ocean, I began studying them in the nineteenth year of Daoguang—” In that year Lin Zexu destroyed opium in Guangdong, British warships entered Chinese waters, and the Opium War broke out. Tao Shu also died that year while Governor-General of Liangjiang. Zuo moved into the Tao household to look after its affairs and gained access to the books left at Yinxin Stone House. Histories, biographies, miscellaneous records, and tales from the Tang and Song onward, together with Qing chronicles and public and private documents—anything touching upon maritime lands—he read them all. Great ships on the ocean were therefore no wholly unfamiliar subject to him.

“Modern foreign steam warships have no precedent in antiquity,” Zuo continued. “But principles can be extended from one case to another. Ocean-going ships cannot be used in rivers without running aground. The absurd thing is that our distinguished officials fail to understand even so simple a point and let foreigners play them in the palms of their hands. Li Shaoquan’s knowledge of foreign affairs is really limited.” Hu could make nothing of this sudden declaration. They were now familiar enough to dispense with ceremony, so he asked plainly, “Which affair do you mean?” “The purchase of warships from Britain at the end of the Xianfeng reign, of course.” “Oh, I remember. Hangzhou was under siege then. After it fell, I was seriously ill in Ningbo and cut off from everything. I knew there was such a purchase, but nothing of its history.” “I know it well. I have read all the memorials and can give you the outline. The Englishmen Lay and Hart were manipulating matters. Britain was to procure three medium-sized steamers and four small ones, at an agreed price of six hundred thousand taels. Lay took it upon himself to increase the amount again and again, until it reached one million seventy thousand. Once the ships arrived, their officers’ and men’s pay, coal, and miscellaneous expenses would cost a hundred thousand taels every month. Worse still, the crews were all to be commanded by British officers—” “May I interrupt?” Hu asked. “Why was that?” “Here. Read this and you will understand.” Zuo had carefully collected copies of the relevant edicts and memorials in a booklet. He opened it at one entry and handed it to Hu.

It was a joint memorial submitted in the fifth month of the second Tongzhi year by Prince Gong, minister directing foreign affairs, Wenxiang, and their colleagues. It began: “Your servants, faced with the continuing rebellion, sought every means of securing victory and proposed the purchase of foreign gunboats to assist in its suppression. A special memorial was submitted in the fifth month of the eleventh Xianfeng year, and the following decree was received: ‘The rebels’ power is spreading through the southeast. If foreign gunboats can indeed be purchased, they will provide effective aid in suppressing them and benefit the general situation.’ Reverently obeying, we communicated with the governors and governors-general concerned. Thereafter Zeng Guofan, Governor-General of Liangjiang, reported that ‘the purchase of foreign ships and guns is the foremost urgent measure for rescuing the present situation.’” Hu needed to read no further. “If the purpose was suppressing rebels, small gunboats able to navigate the Yangtze would suffice. Why spend a million taels?” “Exactly! It shows how blind our eminent gentlemen are. Read this other memorial.” Zuo pointed to one submitted by Zeng Guoquan late in the eighth month of the second Tongzhi year: “By imperial order, seven steamships have been purchased at great expense. The undertaking has fortunately succeeded, and all are said to be approaching the coast. Yet recent correspondence between the Zongli Yamen and the foreigner Lay shows that, besides the actual purchase price of a million taels, the foreign crews’ monthly maintenance will exceed seventy thousand, amounting in a year to no less than a million, all deducted from customs revenue. The national treasury is empty. To add so vast an annual expense must strain it further.”

“When the purchase was first proposed, it was assumed that Chinese personnel would be employed and could be freely commanded. At that time the Yangtze was obstructed; these powerful vessels were intended to defeat a powerful enemy. Since the capture of Jiufuzhou this summer, under the Emperor’s august protection, the river route has been opened. Of the cities along it, only the provincial capital, Jinling, remains unrecovered. Our river navy, its masts thick as a forest, is cooperating fully with the land forces. Once the encirclement is complete, the enemy can certainly be swept away within a fixed time. I have observed steamships navigating the Yangtze. Wherever sandbanks twist and intersect, a vessel may fail to turn aside in time and risk grounding. The river is narrow, offering none of the sea’s freedom of movement. It is like a strong warrior holding a long spear in a short alley: before, behind, and on either side he must encounter obstacles. The conditions make this inevitable. If even ordinary movement in a straight line is difficult, how can such ships maneuver freely in battle and display their full powers? On the river their effectiveness is not merely reduced; circumstances may leave them helpless. If brought upriver now, their fighting strength is not truly needed. If kept at the river mouth, they will lie idle with nothing to do.”

Again, Hu could close the booklet. Purchasing and building great steamships was not for the purpose of suppressing rebels. When Zeng Guoquan submitted his memorial, Jinling was about to be encircled and Li Hongzhang was vigorously attacking Suzhou. Success was already assured; naturally Guoquan wanted no one to share his glory. His comparison with a long spear in a short alley, moreover, aptly described ocean-going ships operating on a river. The court already regarded Lay as deceitful and difficult to deal with. Such a memorial would naturally encourage it to abandon the proposal decisively. “But circumstances have changed,” Zuo said. “Along Fujian’s coast, only steam warships can secure our frontiers and resist foreign enemies. What do you think?” “Yes. You take the long view, my lord. Governors and governors-general are responsible for their regions’ defense. Internal disorder is nearly ended; foreign threats must not be neglected. If outsiders attack from the seas off Fujian and Zhejiang, responsibility for holding the territory will rest entirely upon you. Without long-term preparation, even if you do not repeat Lord Ye’s absurd failure in Guangdong, your reputation will suffer.” Lord Ye was Ye Mingchen, Governor-General of Guangdong and Guangxi, who “neither fought, negotiated, nor defended; neither died, surrendered, nor fled,” and finally died in exile in India. The comparison was somewhat awkward, yet his fate offered a warning. Zuo grew animated. “Exactly! It makes my duty all the clearer. I am determined to establish a shipyard.” “Provided the funds can be found, it should certainly be done.” “There is no need to worry about funds. The former purchase was apportioned among the customs stations. We shall follow the same precedent. Fujian and Zhejiang’s customs will naturally have to exert themselves especially.” “Certainly. But—” Hu fell thoughtfully silent.

Zuo knew that such hesitation meant Hu saw a serious difficulty and could not readily voice it. He also believed that in the end Hu would solve it. The essential thing was to let him speak without reserve. Adopting a leisurely air, Zuo said, “No hurry. We can discuss it slowly. The undertaking is necessary; its timing need not be fixed.” He smiled mysteriously. “Once I set out on this campaign, the whole situation will be in my hands. I can make it move quickly or slowly, tighten or relax it as I please.” The last words were obscure. Even Hu, alert as he was, had to study Zuo’s expression and weigh their meaning. At the sight of that confident yet enigmatic face, he suddenly understood. Zuo meant the campaign in Fujian. He was confident not merely that the remaining rebels could be eliminated, but that he could determine when: soon if he wished, late if he preferred. The choice would depend entirely upon his purpose. Eager to serve the throne and win distinction, he could pursue vigorously and end the campaign quickly. If the rebels were useful—for instance, as a reason to demand troops and money—he would preserve the enemy to increase his own importance.

Hu must first understand his plan. “How many years do you intend to remain in Fujian, my lord?” “An excellent question!” Zuo was delighted by this meeting of minds. “How many years do you think I should remain?” “If you are determined to establish a shipyard, several years will be necessary.” “I think so too.” “And how will you manage it? Surely you cannot keep fighting the rebels indefinitely?” “Naturally not. They are doomed souls at the bottom of the cauldron, unable to withstand a blow. To prolong the campaign would injure my reputation. But neither need I succeed the moment I arrive.” He stroked his beard in thought, the smile fading. After a long silence he nodded. “As you know, I must gain Guangdong as a base. How long the fighting lasts depends upon whether my in-law understands the situation. If he withdraws in good time, I shall gladly defeat the enemy promptly. Otherwise, more money must be spent. Do you understand?”

“I understand,” Hu replied. “Only when I know your intentions can I make plans for you.” “Then you understand now?” The question reminded him that it was time to do so. He answered with renewed energy. “If Guangdong follows your lead, matters will be manageable. When you petition to establish a shipyard, the court will surely ask the coastal governors and governors-general for their opinions on so important an undertaking. Fujian and Zhejiang are assured. If Guangdong also replies in strong support, your position will be formidable.” “Exactly! That is why I must bring Guangdong to my side. With three southern coastal provinces behind me, how could Liangjiang oppose me?” “It would not oppose you openly. It would make difficulties over sharing the expense. Funding a shipyard from maritime customs revenue is perfectly reasonable. Set aside Tianjin’s northern customs for the moment. In the south, including Guangdong, there are five major customs establishments: Jianghaiguan in Shanghai, Guangdong Customs at Guangzhou, Fujian Customs and Xiamen Customs, and our own Ningbo Customs. When the cost is apportioned, support from Guangdong would give you three of the five, counting Fujian and Xiamen. Zhejiang lies under your jurisdiction, so Governor Ma would have to show you consideration too. Could Liangjiang then insist that Jianghaiguan contribute nothing?”

“Precisely. Your plan is fair and reasonable, and Guangdong is the decisive point. Take this collection of copies home, study the precedent of the former ship purchase, and prepare a detailed memorandum. When to submit it will depend upon the opportunity. I think we must first settle Guangdong.” “I agree. Fortunately, we have ample time. I shall consult d’Aiguebelle and the others and make a rough calculation of the sources of revenue. Some money will be needed for preliminary work; I shall arrange to advance it.” “Excellent. We shall proceed that way. But Jianghaiguan holds the richest resources. We cannot let Li Shaoquan keep them entirely in his grasp. Think of a way to draw more out of him.” “There is a way,” Hu said, shaking his head, “but it would be better not to use it.” “Why?” “We should be roundly criticized.” “Leave that aside for the moment. What is the method?”

“We could borrow from foreigners. A loan requires security. If Jianghaiguan says it cannot contribute now, there must be a time when it can. We could earmark a certain proportion of its receipts in specified future years to repay the foreign loan. That would be the security. But for our great Celestial Empire to borrow from foreigners would certainly provoke opposition. If the censors attacked together, it would be difficult to bear.” Zuo was struck silent. He rose and paced solemnly for some time, then took back the booklet he had handed to Hu, turned to a page, and pointed. “Read this passage.” It came from Prince Gong’s memorial. Lay had told the prince that, should China need money, he could arrange a loan of ten million taels from foreign merchants, repayable with interest over a term of years. The prince, however, had concluded: “As for the proposal to borrow ten million taels, China can under no circumstances adopt such a course.” “There, my lord.” Hu pointed to the sentence. “The court will never permit a foreign loan.”

“That was then; this is now!” Zuo abruptly changed the direction of the discussion. “Xueyan, remember this: in great affairs, the most important thing is to make up one’s mind. Once the decision is made, reasons are not hard to find. Take this memorial. China was buying ships but had to submit to foreign wishes in everything; many at court objected. Prince Gong was already inclined to retreat, which is why he said China could never borrow abroad. Suppose the military situation had been uncertain and foreign armor and guns indispensable. There would have been a different argument. First, foreigners lending to China would be showing admiration for the Celestial Dynasty and voluntarily supporting the rightful cause. Second, their willingness to lend without fearing loss would demonstrate confidence that China could suppress the Taipings, recover the rich southeastern provinces, and repay the debt. Consider how pleasing that would sound! Why would the court not gladly agree?” To Hu, these words were an education. He sincerely acknowledged the lesson, encouraging Zuo to speak still more eagerly. “Let me tell you another secret of great undertakings. There is a saying: ‘Better to use a favorable situation than wait for a favorable time.’ Many apparently difficult enterprises succeed smoothly because someone understands how to use the force of circumstances. What is that force? Let me test you. Tell me what it means.”

“You have defeated me with that question,” Hu said with a smile. “Please instruct me.” “In some matters I must seek your advice; in others I may properly claim the right to teach you. The force of circumstances depends upon the person, the business, and the time. In a person, it is influence—the power that attracts the self-seeker. I once nearly met disaster because Guanwen, Governor-General of Huguang, had more influence than Luo Bingzhang, Governor of Hunan. Naturally the court listened to him. It was easy for him to impeach me.” “Yes. The same matter carries different weight according to who speaks.” “And according to what he speaks about,” Zuo added. “Today, military affairs outweigh everything; among military affairs, eliminating the Taiping remnants comes first. My words on other subjects may carry little weight. Speak of suppressing the rebels in Fujian, however, and they will certainly listen. Do you believe that?” “Of course! Certainly I do.” “I thought you would. My present position gives my words sufficient force. But I must still consider the business and the moment to speak. Choose the moment exactly, and advice is heard and plans followed. Speak too late, and you harm yourself; too early, and it is useless.” Zuo smiled. “For example, the time has not yet come to drive out my in-law.”

“Exactly,” Hu answered at once. “Not until the fighting reaches the Fujian–Guangdong border.” Zuo laughed heartily, then grew serious. “The shipyard must wait until military operations are concluded and coastal defense becomes the subject of discussion. Even then, the moment must be right. But we must prepare in advance or the opportunity will pass. Do you understand?” Hu understood not merely the words but their full implications, carrying the thought farther than Zuo himself. Combining the general situation, Zuo’s future distinction, and his own business and interests, he saw one essential point: Zuo must win a brilliant military victory. Nothing else could take its place.

From this understanding Hu decided the order of his work for Zuo, though he must first obtain agreement. “I understand your ambitions and can broadly judge when each matter should be undertaken. I shall prepare beforehand. What I wish to ask now is this: in the coming campaign, what matters most?” The question halted Zuo. After a moment he answered, “Funds, naturally.” “I can arrange advances. But that is not a service only I can provide. If the provincial contributions arrive regularly, why borrow from me and pay extra interest?” “Ah, I see what you mean. A workman must sharpen his tools before he can work well. Good soldiers with poor weapons can defend but cannot attack. I need fine Western arms, the more the better. That is something I must rely on you for.” “Exactly.” Hu was pleased. “Then procuring Western weapons will be my first concern. You need not remind me; I shall watch for them myself. Shells and ammunition go without saying—I shall never let the front run short. Second comes money. Whatever sum is due under the regular allocation, I shall have it ready, whether Zhejiang’s treasury remits early or late. Small additional sums will naturally be available whenever needed. For very large ones, please give advance notice so that I am not taken unprepared. As for the shipyard and everything else you have entrusted to me, I shall attend to each in turn. You need neither worry nor press me. I shall not miss the proper moment.” “Excellent!” Zuo was delighted. “The founding Han emperor owed his success to Xiao He. Then we are agreed. Go ahead freely. I shall stand behind everything.”

CHAPTER TEN

At the end of the tenth month of the third year of Tongzhi, Zuo Zongtang relinquished his concurrent duties as acting governor of Zhejiang. Amid cannon salutes and the respectful farewell of Hangzhou’s civil and military officials, he set out for Fujian to direct the campaign. The relief armies had already departed in three columns. The western column’s main force comprised eight thousand newly raised troops under Liu Dian, Zhejiang’s judicial commissioner and assistant director of military affairs in Fujian. With twenty-five hundred men under Wang Debang, who held the designation of judicial commissioner pending appointment, they entered Tingzhou from Jianchang in Jiangxi. In the centre, Huang Shaochun, designated for appointment as provincial military commander, and Deputy Brigade Commander Liu Mingdeng led forty-six hundred men from Quzhou through Pucheng and Jianning towards Yanping. In the east, Acting Zhejiang Military Commander Gao Liansheng and Wei Guangbing, a prefect awaiting appointment, led forty-five hundred men across the Qiantang River to Ningbo, where they boarded steamships for Fuzhou. All three columns were directed towards southern Fujian. Li Shixian occupied Zhangzhou, west of Xiamen; Ding Taiyang was at Wuping, where Fujian, Guangdong, and Jiangxi met; Wang Haiyang ranged between the eastern and western districts of southern Fujian. Zuo was determined to prevent them from putting to sea. He would tighten the pressure from north, west, and east, forcing them south. South of Fujian lay Guangdong. Mao Hongbin, governor-general of Liangguang, and Guo Songtao, governor of Guangdong, saw the danger. If Zuo drove the rebels into their province, the relieving army would follow, leaving Guangdong squeezed between rebels and “friendly troops.” They were deeply alarmed. Yet the Guangdong forces under the two deputy brigade commanders Fang Yao and Zhuo Xing amounted to only eight thousand. Kang Guoqi, intendant of the Yanping-Jianning-Shaowu circuit in Fujian, was a Guangdong man and commanded a newly formed force largely recruited there, but it could not be counted as a Guangdong army: he served under Zuo and took no orders from Guangdong’s senior officials. Mao and Guo had little choice. They sent Fang and Zhuo into Fujian to join the campaign, openly blocking the rebels and covertly checking Zuo. Meanwhile, hoping to strengthen their own position, they planned to ask the court to appoint Feng Zicai, the celebrated defender of Zhenjiang, to direct military affairs along the East River.

Before the relieving armies arrived, Lin Wencha, Fujian’s land-force military commander, had already fought Li Shixian. A native of Changhua in Taiwan, Lin had entered military office as a youji commander in the eighth year of Xianfeng, after contributing funds and helping suppress bandits in Tamsui. His Taiwanese volunteers were fierce, adept with firearms, and effective in battle. Their success helped him rise to brigade commander and gain the title Baturu. While Wang Youling was besieged in Hangzhou, Lin had been ordered to assist Zhejiang but was held up at Hengzhou. Later placed under Zuo’s command, he earned further distinction and received substantive appointment as commander of the Funing Brigade in Fujian. Soon promoted to the province’s land-force military command, he returned to Taiwan with troops to suppress disorder in his native district. The rebel leader was Dai Chaochun, whose ancestral home was Longxi in Zhangzhou. He belonged to the surviving adherents of the White Lotus sect, that old source of rebellion which never seemed to disappear from Chinese history. Nominally he organized militia in Changhua; in reality he was in contact with the Taipings. At the transition from the Xianfeng to the Tongzhi reign, Zhejiang fell and many government troops in Fujian were transferred to northern Fujian and southern Zhejiang. Dai judged the moment favourable. In the third month his follower Lin Gangsheng rose at Dadun. Five days later Changhua was occupied and Kong Zhaoci, Taiwan’s military intendant, was killed. Dai called himself the Eastern King and Lin the Southern King; there were also Western and Northern Kings, with a Grand Preceptor, chief ministers of the left and right, and ministers of the six boards beneath them. This rough imitation of the Taiping administration, a collection of pretenders playing ministers and generals as though on a stage, survived for a time because the southeastern war had reached a critical point. In response to Grain Intendant Ding Rijian’s urgent requests, the court had sent only six hundred men against it. Not until the autumn of the second year of Tongzhi, when Zuo was confident of recovering Zhejiang, did he send Lin back to Taiwan to rally his former troops. Fujian’s governor, Xu Zonggan, also sent Ding, who had served in Taiwan for many years, with reinforcements. Ding was appointed Taiwan’s military intendant and given charge of military affairs throughout the island.

Early in the eleventh month, Changhua was recovered, followed by Douliu. By the end of the year Dai had been captured at Zhangcuozhuang and Lin Gangsheng defeated and killed at Sikuai Cuo. The situation could be considered stable. Clearing out the remaining rebels was nevertheless arduous. In particular, after Li Shixian occupied Zhangzhou, Dai’s surviving followers prepared to join forces with him and rise again. This was precisely why Zuo feared that Li and Wang Haiyang might put to sea. Lin Wencha faced a dilemma. On one hand, he could not relax his efforts to prevent the embers of rebellion from flaring up. On the other, as Fujian’s highest military officer on land, he had an inescapable responsibility for recovering Zhangzhou, Tingzhou, and the other occupied districts. After careful thought, he decided that he must return to Fujian. If Li could be destroyed, the rebels in Changhua would lose both support and hope and collapse without a fight. He crossed hurriedly, taking only two hundred personal guards in his ship. Having fought Li more than once, he did not underestimate him. His original intention was to make arrangements after reaching Fujian and only then advance. But Li was already prepared, with an ambush at Wansong Pass awaiting his arrival.

Lin then changed his plan. Feeling his force too small, he gathered many stragglers along the road into an improvised army. Given ample pay and provisions, sufficient time, and capable assistants, such men could gradually be trained into a strong force. Without those advantages, the only course was to exploit their urgent desire for a livelihood or their thirst for revenge, sharpen their fighting spirit, and stake everything on one desperate effort. An experienced commander, Lin studied the circumstances and concluded that he must seek a rapid decision. Delay would merely consume supplies while discipline became impossible, and the army would collapse without fighting. Zuo’s original order had been to hold Quanzhou and Xiamen firmly. That was a difficult task. The country from Zhangzhou eastwards to the two coastal cities was flat, easy to attack and hard to defend, while the opposing forces were grossly unequal in strength. Strategically, attack should serve as defence. In weighing the consequences, although attacking a larger force was arduous, defeat while attacking was preferable to defeat while merely holding a position. Early in the tenth month, therefore, Lin advanced from Quanzhou and Xiamen and camped at Wansong Pass. Also called Wansong Ridge, it stood on Phoenix Mountain, twenty-five li east of Zhangzhou, commanding the route into the city after crossing the river from the coastal towns. His camp was scarcely established when Li sent a force to test its strength. Deputy Brigade Commander Huishou proved incapable, and the rebels actually overran the camp. Lin had to withdraw to a place called Yuzhou. Two days later, he attacked and returned with a minor victory.

At this point another order arrived from Zuo: dig deep trenches and raise high ramparts; avoid rash fighting in pursuit of victory; wait for the Zhejiang troops, then cooperate in recovering Zhangzhou. Lin now had to obey. He encamped on Wansong Ridge and waited. Naval Brigade Commander Zeng Yuming established a river camp at Haikou near Haicheng County on the Jiulong River, so that the two positions could support one another. They held on until the end of the tenth month. Zuo had not yet entered Fujian, but the Zhejiang forces sent ahead were nearing positions from which the three columns could close in. Li had originally laid his ambush west of Wansong Pass, expecting Lin to enter it. Seeing him remain inactive while the Zhejiang troops approached, Li urgently needed to open a way out. On the third day of the eleventh month, he launched a surprise attack by land and water. The rebels striking the river camp blackened their faces with soot, both to appear terrifying and to recognize one another. Zeng’s naval force was caught unprepared. Apart from resistance with the small guns aboard the gunboats, the camps collapsed. To the west, Lin’s force at Wansong Pass consisted of stragglers whose condition had steadily deteriorated with delay. Hearing that their rear had been attacked, they fell into disorder before fighting began. Huishou and Youji Commander Xu Zhongbiao could not hold the ranks and escaped as best they could. Lin refused to flee and was shot dead. The routed troops were barely collected on the east bank of the Jiulong River, forming what protection they could for the approach to Quanzhou.

Three months later, Zuo reached Pucheng and formally entered Fujian, intending to establish his travelling headquarters there. The first battle report received at those headquarters announced Lin’s defeat and death in action. This was no omen of immediate success, and Zuo was greatly displeased. In his view, Lin had blunted the Zhejiang army’s spirit and damaged his own prestige. Although the man had not deserved death, his failure was unforgivable. Zuo therefore would not devote a separate memorial to it. Instead, under the heading “Arrival at Pucheng to Direct the Campaign, and Current Arrangements for Suppression,” he reproached Lin for disregarding orders and thereby suffering defeat. Fortunately, Gao Liansheng’s troops had reached southern Fujian from Fuzhou, so Quanzhou and Xiamen could be secured. As for Lin’s posthumous honours, a separate request would be submitted; it was easy to infer that the provision would not be generous. The situation soon stabilized. Zuo’s greatest concern was that Li might break southeastwards to the sea. If Gao’s force could move south from Fuzhou in time to block him and hold him briefly, the arrival by sea of the Jiangsu armies under Guo Songlin and Yang Dingxun would make the clearing of the interior virtually certain.

Zuo accordingly adopted a strategy of holding in the east, attacking from the north, and applying pressure from the west. First he recovered Longyan, in the northern part of southern Fujian. Then, in cooperation with Fang Yao’s Guangdong troops, he recovered Yongding on the provincial border. These two victories greatly raised morale and gave him more freedom of command.
