He began to drive the rebels into Guangdong, first pushing Wang Haiyang’s men south from Tingzhou and Liancheng, near Jiangxi, towards Wuping and Shanghang on the Guangdong border. The Jiangsu reinforcements were arriving in succession. They divided the defensive sectors with Gao’s and Huang Shaochun’s Zhejiang forces, taking Zhangzhou as their common objective. The Jiangsu armies held the south of the city, the Zhejiang armies the north, completely cutting off Li’s route to the sea. In the middle of the fourth month they attacked simultaneously from north and south. On the twenty-first, Zhangzhou was recovered. Li, however, opened the western gate and escaped to join Wang, leaving the two like cornered beasts. The strongest forces now lay along the southeastern coast around Quanzhou, Xiamen, and Zhangzhou. More than seven thousand guarded Yongding to the northwest; a gap in the northeast had also been closed in time. Only the west remained weak, with almost no dispositions made by Zuo. To the west lay Dabu and Raoping in Guangdong. Fang Yao’s troops defended them, but anyone could see they were no match for Li and Wang. What, then, did Zuo intend? A discerning observer could draw the conclusion.

That observer was Xu Gengshen, chief secretary of the Grand Council, far away in the capital. On the twelfth day of the fifth month, a memorial passed down to him caused considerable surprise. Submitted by Li Hongzhang to record his troops’ achievements, it was headed, “Jiangsu Reinforcements in Fujian Join the Zhejiang Forces in Converging Attacks and Recover the Prefectural City of Zhangzhou on the Twenty-first Day of the Fourth Month.” In describing the attack, it stated that “the rebel Shi King, Li Shixian, secretly opened the western gate and fled.” This did not agree with Zuo’s report received at the same time. Zuo’s heading was “Great Victory in the Advance West of Zhangzhou, and Current Arrangements.” It mentioned neither the recovery of the city nor Li’s escape through its western gate. It said only that Li, repeatedly defeated and increasingly desperate, had tried to take a minor route north of Zhangzhou around to Anxi and strike the government troops’ rear. Failing in that, he had attempted to stir up internal disorder in collusion with bandits in Tong’an. Guo Songlin had then hurried to Tong’an with two battalions and, together with Intendant Zeng Xiande, cleared the bandit strongholds at Xitang, Shangzhai, and Hujing. The memorial was dated the twenty-sixth day of the fourth month and submitted from the provincial capital. Fuzhou was only two or three days from Zhangzhou. If the city had been recovered on the twenty-first, Zuo could hardly have remained ignorant on the twenty-fifth. If he knew, why did he not announce the victory in his memorial on the twenty-sixth? This was a major puzzle, but a little consideration made it plain. Zuo withheld the victory report because Li had escaped. By first describing an intended movement towards Anxi, then collusion with Tong’an’s bandits, and finally Guo’s actions, he meant quietly to shift responsibility for Li’s escape onto Guo Songlin.

The reason for Zuo’s wish to deal with Guo could also be inferred. After Lin Wencha’s death, Guo, commander of the Fushan Brigade, had been appointed acting land-force military commander of Fujian. Acting or not, it was a promotion. Had Zuo not requested the Jiangsu reinforcements, Guo might never have obtained this highest provincial military office. Zuo reasoned that, since the promotion arose from service in Fujian and the post itself belonged to Fujian, both gratitude and the proper relationship between title and duty required Guo to enter his command. Yet despite repeated urgings from Governor Xu Zonggan, Guo refused to assume the appointment. He fought in Fujian while holding a Fujian military office, but continued to regard himself as a visiting commander. Zuo found that hard to tolerate. Before Guo and Yang sailed south, however, Li had expressly stated that Guo would not take up the post and that he would not urge him to do so. Zuo had agreed and could not now conveniently go back on his word. His resentment remained. He therefore planted a suggestion for future use: on one hand, his vague phrasing implied that pursuing Li Shixian was Guo’s responsibility; on the other, he could wait to see what happened. If Tong’an’s bandits proved difficult to suppress, he could formally request that Guo remain in Fujian. A province’s own military commander suppressing its own bandits was an entirely proper arrangement. The court could scarcely refuse, Li would have to release him, and Guo would have to stay.

Once Zuo’s concealed manoeuvring was understood, Li’s memorial on the relief forces’ victory and the joint recovery of Zhangzhou became clear. Besides claiming distinction and rewards, it left the Jiangsu armies a means of disclaiming responsibility in advance. Its account of the campaign was particularly precise about the sectors and dispositions of Guo’s and Yang’s forces. First, it stated that Dongshan, ten li south of Zhangzhou on the main road to Zhangpu, was held by Guo with eight battalions. Another ten li farther lay Zhenmen, centrally placed between Dongshan, Haicheng, and Shima, where Yang held the ground with five battalions. Haicheng County lay behind both armies, with mountain paths leading to Zhangpu; three more battalions had therefore been deployed inside and outside the county town to prevent a surprise attack on their rear. Second, it stated that Brigade Commander Liu Lianjie and Officer Wang Kaibang were in the northwest, while Military Commander Gao Liansheng, Huang Shaochun, and their forces were on the eastern route. Once the Jiangsu troops occupied Dongshan, the southern route was cut. Third, it recorded that the defeated rebels fled towards Nanjing County; the various battalions pursued for several li, then joined Gao, Huang, and the others in turning back southeast to clear all the rebel positions at Fangzi Bridge beyond the eastern gate, the Dongyue Temple, and the New Bridge near the southern gate. Every detail showed that, whether defending or attacking, Guo and Yang were chiefly responsible for Zhangzhou’s southern side, with its eastern side secondary. Who, then, was responsible for Li’s escape through the western gate? The answer was obvious.

After repeated examination, Xu concluded that Zuo was practising a dangerous form of political manipulation. Since the war began, provincial commanders had habitually tried to drive rebels beyond their own borders. Zuo, however, seemed deliberately to be making a neighbour bear the calamity, with some further malicious purpose concealed behind it. The matter must be corrected early. Xu explained the consequences to Prince Gong, Wenxiang, and the others. With the two empress dowagers informed, a court dispatch was drafted. It first noted Li’s report that Zhangzhou had been recovered and the rebel Shi King had secretly escaped through its western gate. It continued that, although the city was recovered, the chief rebel remained alive and would certainly join Wang Haiyang in further plans. The southeastern routes were stable and Liu Mingdeng and others held the northeast in coordination, but Zhangpu, Yunxiao, Zhao’an, and Pinghe in the west remained in rebel hands. There could be no doubt that the fugitives would try to escape that way. Fang Yao’s forces guarded Raoping and Dabu in Guangdong, but might be too few to cover the area. Zuo and his colleagues were therefore to detach strong forces, make a circuit to the western route, join the Guangdong troops, and intercept the rebels, preventing them from crossing the border. At this point, Zuo knew the moment had come to drive Guo Songtao from office. He had already laid groundwork for Jiang Yili, using Li Hongzhang as a favourable comparison. Early in the ninth month, when Ruilin and Guo fell out, Zuo had added a remark to his memorial requesting withdrawal of the order placing the armies of three provinces under his control: fighting in Liangguang might continue indefinitely, but if it had commanders such as Li Hongzhang and Jiang Yili, perhaps the disorder could be brought to an end. The implication was that Li should become governor-general there and Jiang governor of Guangdong. This was a tentative hint, and the court had given no explicit response. Yet the impression that Jiang could govern a province had been planted in the minds of the Western Empress Dowager and the grand councillors.

Zuo still could not openly recommend Jiang for promotion and transfer to Guangdong. Instead, he attacked Guo and supported Jiang in an attached memorandum on Guangdong’s military affairs and finances. It began by declaring that the province’s military condition would not bear examination, and its finances scarcely bore inquiry. The war had lasted a long time, it said; in some provinces military strength had grown from weakness, whereas Guangdong, once fierce, was now feeble. In some provinces funds had gradually recovered from scarcity, whereas Guangdong, once rich, was now exhausted. Those two sentences alone attacked the governors and governors-general of the preceding two years. Naturally, Guo bore more responsibility than Ruilin, since he had been in office longer.

The memorandum then concentrated upon military finance, which was precisely the governor’s responsibility. Although Guo Songtao was never named, most of the attack fell upon him, with particular emphasis on the likin collected in prosperous Chaozhou. Zuo declared that, on reaching Dabu, he had met officials, gentry, and ordinary people from Chaozhou and inquired into its taxes. The levies on miscellaneous merchandise and imported opium, Shantou’s trade levy, and the shipping contribution together yielded only a little over thirty thousand taels annually—not enough to pay six thousand men for a single month. If so rich a prefecture in eastern Guangdong produced so little, the condition elsewhere could be inferred. This deliberately distorted the facts. Ever since Qian Jiang had introduced likin, a tax collected on goods, Guangdong had been the most difficult place in which to impose it. On first taking office, Guo had submitted a joint memorial with Governor-General Mao Hongbin explaining the circumstances.

Guangdong differed from other provinces chiefly because of foreign interference. Macau lay to its west and Hong Kong to its east. The strategic outlets of the provincial waterways were in foreign hands, and Hong Kong in particular had become a gathering place for merchant firms. One or two great establishments had moved there to place themselves under foreign protection and further their private interests. The provincial authorities dared not approach them about contributions or likin. Others wishing to evade payment likewise lodged their goods in Hong Kong. The major trades all used it as a point of transfer. Other provinces merely had to prevent evasion; Guangdong also had a refuge to which evaders could withdraw. Local customs posed another difference. Guangdong’s gentry often undertook the collection of taxes and contributions. When likin began, unscrupulous gentry contracted to collect it and enriched themselves by extortion, harming both the public revenue and private interests. When collection was subsequently returned to officials, the former contractors resented the loss of their privilege and incited disorder. Mobs destroying likin offices were nothing unusual. Timid officials dared not punish the instigators. Instead they dismissed the collecting officers or transferred prefects and magistrates in hopes of compromise. The more they yielded, the harder the problem became.

After taking office, Guo reorganized likin on the principles of eliminating private skimming and making the system reasonable. He achieved considerable results. Offices were established successively in places where none had existed, including Qiongzhou, Lianzhou, and Heyuan in Huizhou. Chaozhou, however, lay in a remote eastern corner of the province and had long remained beyond effective control. Only when Wang Haiyang approached the Guangdong border did Zhang Xian, intendant of the Chaozhou-Jiaying-Huizhou circuit, find a way to begin collection. The amount was small, but it was a beginning. Chaozhou’s people would discuss anything if approached tactfully and in accordance with local circumstances; coercion merely made them refuse. Zhang believed that, once they accepted likin in principle, the amount could gradually increase. Moreover, with the rebels pressing near, a quarrel between the authorities and local society over taxation would be extremely dangerous. Guo fully agreed. Zuo, deliberately disregarding these facts, stressed the mere thirty thousand taels collected each year without mentioning how hard won that sum was, or how collecting thirty thousand now offered hope of three hundred thousand later. Most maliciously, he also exaggerated Guangdong Customs revenue. He said he had heard that its various ports collected no less than two million taels annually, while the amount remitted to the capital could not be ascertained. If the governor-general and governor could arrange to use it, the regular imperial remittances need not suffer and provincial military finance would benefit greatly. But this involved an arrangement two hundred years old, he added, which a provincial official dared not lightly question. He then recommended Jiang. Having entered Guangdong with a visiting army, he could not refrain from reporting what he had observed. Coordinating troops and funds was a great responsibility requiring an administrator of exceptional ability. Jiang Yili, Zhejiang’s provincial treasurer, possessed unrivalled talent and judgment several degrees above his own. If the emperor ordered Jiang to Guangdong to direct military affairs and raise funds, the current situation would surely improve. This was a forceful recommendation indeed. Its force lay in the claim of unrivalled talent and judgment superior to Zuo’s. Praise of that kind from a governor-general commanding military operations in three provinces carried extraordinary weight.

Zuo regarded himself as another Zhuge Liang and habitually looked down upon everyone. Such humble admiration was quite unlike him. A rumour therefore circulated: the secretary responsible for memorials in the Fujian-Zhejiang governor-general’s office had received ten thousand taels from Jiang and insisted upon adding the ten Chinese characters asserting Jiang’s unrivalled talent and superior judgment. If true, the payment amounted to a thousand taels per character. Whether there had been a bribe was unknown. On the merits of the situation, however, those ten characters were indispensable. The grand councillors knew Jiang’s abilities and generally considered him unfit to govern a province. Although Zuo had hinted that he should replace Guo and had used Li as a favourable comparison to raise his standing, the court had consistently pretended not to hear. Now the ten characters thundered too loudly to ignore. It could no longer pretend deafness, but it could pretend stupidity. Deliberately overlooking the implied request, the court granted only what had been asked on the surface. On the eighth day of the first month of the fifth year of Tongzhi, it ordered Jiang, as provincial treasurer of Zhejiang, to proceed swiftly to Guangdong to handle military affairs and arrange provisions and pay.

By the time Zuo submitted the recommendation, he was confident of recovering Jiaying, where Wang Haiyang had entrenched himself. A general assault on the twelfth day of the twelfth month brought a decisive victory, and Wang was killed in the gunfire. Ten days later the operation was complete. At the year’s end, Zuo memorialized that Jiaying had been recovered, the rebel leaders exterminated, and the remaining forces swept away. This amounted to the final suppression of the Taiping remnants. Zuo would return to his own post, and the armies would be sent back to their respective provinces. What military affairs, then, was Jiang to handle in Guangdong, and for what troops was he to raise supplies? If someone influential asked that question, his new appointment might be revoked. Zuo had of course anticipated it. He used another matter to press Guo still harder: Gao Liansheng was to take his troops with him when assuming office. Gao’s substantive post was land-force military commander of Guangdong. With Zuo’s command over the three-province campaign at an end, Gao should naturally report to Guangdong. A military commander taking up office needed no force beyond his own headquarters guard. Zuo nevertheless instructed him to bring his entire command. The stated reason was to maintain order after the upheaval, while public feeling remained unsettled. The real object was to burden Guangdong. Gao had five thousand men, requiring at least thirty thousand taels a month, which Guangdong would have to provide.

Guangdong welcomed Gao but not his army. Zuo consequently complained to the throne in forceful terms. He had done everything possible for Guangdong, he said, yet the province made difficulties and wanted him to order Gao’s men back to Fujian. He could not understand it. If Guangdong maintained that Fujian should still pay them, then let it consider its arrears: more than three hundred thousand taels of promised assistance to Fujian remained unpaid. Another fifty thousand or more was owed for sending refugees back to their provinces, relief and reduced-price grain sales in Jiaying and Zhenping, and the rice he had allocated to Bao Chao’s army. If all these sums were applied to Gao’s pay, Fujian’s own money would sustain the Fujian force for roughly a year. By then disorder would have subsided, and gradual disbandment would still be timely. Interprovincial military subsidies formed accounts that could never be finally settled. Who owed what to whom was perpetually disputed. In the end, being able to fight supplied an argument; winning supplied a stronger one. Zuo understood this perfectly and spoke with assurance, his confidence making his case seem all the more just. His memorials often ended with general reflections. This one did too. In attacking Guo, he extended his censure to Guangdong itself. The coastal troubles had originated there, he declared, and so had the rebellion that afflicted the central plains. Now that the campaign was over, those responsible for soldiers and funds should learn from the past and strengthen their position. If they continued to mistake dullness for generosity and evasion for ability, clever in petty calculations but blind to major strategy, they could scarcely relieve the court’s concern for the south. He requested an order directing Guangdong’s senior officials to deliberate carefully, and an imperial decision permitting Gao to bring his troops. As with the earlier recommendation of Jiang, someone in Zuo’s secretariat who had received a gratuity copied the memorial privately and sent it to Zhejiang. Jiang was a rough man, but he had perceptive advisers. They told him promotion was near. Overjoyed, he had sealing strips printed for the governor of Guangdong and began preparing to take office.

The most effective feature of the memorial was its use of Ruilin to attack Guo. The matter had begun with an official communication from Ruilin, and Zuo’s aggressive language initially named him. The implication was that, if the attack on Guo failed, it would turn upon Ruilin. Everyone at court knew the quality of Ruilin’s administration. Prince Gong and Wenxiang, however much more capable than certain Manchu dignitaries who had wielded power in the Daoguang and Xianfeng reigns, still believed that he must be preserved. First, since the war began, nine-tenths of the governors and governors-general had become Han Chinese, an unprecedented situation for the dynasty. Only Huguang and Liangguang were presently held by bannermen. If Zuo’s relentless impeachment forced Ruilin’s removal, there was no suitable senior bannerman readily available to replace him. Second, Ruilin had Empress Dowager Cixi’s powerful support and could not be touched. Third, though mediocre, he was obedient. The Imperial Household Department’s finances were closely connected with Guangdong Customs; an obedient governor-general in Guangzhou made many things convenient. The court therefore had to placate Zuo, both to preserve Ruilin and because the warning about continued danger in the south had aroused concern. The resulting edict rebuked Ruilin severely. After Zuo’s victorious withdrawal, it said, Guangdong would still have much to do in settling surrendered soldiers, hunting bandits, and restoring order. Strong troops should remain to enforce peace. Since Ruilin had urged Gao to assume his substantive command, why now ask Zuo to send the force back to Fujian? If renewed disorder after its withdrawal required another campaign, could the acting governor-general bear such grave responsibility?

The edict then granted Zuo’s request in full. Gao’s five thousand and more men required only a little over thirty thousand taels a month. Zuo was to order him to proceed with his troops, and Ruilin and Guo were to provide their pay monthly, without the slightest shortage or arrears that might impede operations. Ruilin lost face under this rebuke, but his career was preserved. The man whose career was not preserved was Guo, who had not been rebuked. The court meant to sacrifice Guo in exchange for Zuo’s “loyalty” while keeping Ruilin. Yet its edicts constantly proclaimed complete impartiality in appointments and administration. Both governor and governor-general bore responsibility for delays in Guangdong’s military affairs; removing one and leaving the other untouched would be difficult to justify. An unwritten form of “fairness” therefore came into play: rebuke without punishment for one, punishment without rebuke for the other. Ruilin had received his rebuke; the absence of one for Guo foreshadowed his dismissal. Only seven or eight days later, two edicts concerning Guangdong appeared on the same day. A public decree issued through the Grand Secretariat summoned Guo to the capital and appointed Jiang governor of Guangdong. A court dispatch, sent to Zhejiang, Guangdong, and Fujian with urgency just below the highest six-hundred-li express rate, addressed Ma Xinyi’s reports on inspecting the coast, rebuilding war vessels, and whether Jiang should still proceed to Guangdong now that fighting there had ended. Effective vessels and weapons, it said, were essential against maritime raiders. Ma’s proposal to rebuild thirty red-banner Guangdong boats, combine them with the ten already under Zhang Qiguang, and distribute the forty among Wenzhou and other strategic ports was sound. So was the purchase of one or two foreign steamships for mobile pursuit. Both were approved. Ma was to direct coastal officers to patrol vigorously with the vessels already available and suppress the remaining bandits without negligence. The dispatch then announced Jiang’s appointment as governor, ordered him to hand over his duties and leave promptly, and charged him with energetic reform of military affairs, administration, and finance. He must not disappoint the court through habitual delay. The dispatch was to travel by the five-hundred-li express service.

Zuo had driven Guo from office in order to gain Guangdong as part of his own sphere. On the surface, he had succeeded. In reality, he had not. The court had yielded to pressure in accepting his emphatic recommendation, but the combined judgment of its leading men still had resources for dealing with an imperious commander. Before long, discerning observers could see that the men in power at the centre had made the better move. First, the court already had preliminary plans to employ Zuo in another important undertaking. It could therefore make a special show of favour over Jiang’s appointment to bind him more closely. Second, Guangdong’s wealth was famous, and the province had long supplied the Imperial Household Department’s public and private demands. During more than a decade of Taiping devastation in the southeast, Guangdong had suffered comparatively little. Earlier, the need for military funds had forced the court to defer to Zeng Guofan’s recommendations concerning its senior appointments. Now that the fighting was over, the time had come to reclaim authority. But Zeng’s influence was an obstacle, and Guo’s background and administrative record made it impossible to remove him casually without a serious fault. With Ruilin beside him as a comparison, any honest reform of Guangdong’s administration ought to begin with Ruilin. At the least, both men should go. Otherwise the repeated declarations of impartiality would become mere deception. Zuo’s determined attack on Guo offered a rare opportunity to recover control. Guo was hard to dismiss; Jiang would be easy. His background, standing, and abilities were all unsuitable for provincial government. A future edict could move him with little difficulty and no complaint. There was another advantage: compared with Jiang, Ruilin would look quite qualified to govern Liangguang. In the eighth month, therefore, Ruilin’s acting appointment was made substantive.

On that same day, the seventeenth of the eighth month in the fifth year of Tongzhi, two further edicts appeared. One reported that Yang Yuebin, governor-general of Shaanxi and Gansu, pleaded inadequate ability and worsening illness and requested release from office. Zuo was transferred to replace him. The other explained that Yang had proved ill suited to the people and locality and had achieved no effective results. The court’s concern for the western frontier was profound. Zuo’s prestige, strategic knowledge, and ability to handle both military and civil affairs made him the proper choice to suppress the Hui rebellion swiftly and pacify the borderlands. The edict thus expressly entrusted him with the great task of settling the northwest. Under customary procedure, an intended appointee to a major provincial post would first be summoned to the capital. If his audience satisfied the emperor, a public edict followed, after which he received instructions and departed. If a direct appointment bypassed that procedure, the new official should himself request an audience and instructions. A transfer generally implied intended reform, and he needed to ascertain the court’s wishes. There were exceptions: urgent military circumstances might make delay impossible, in which case the edict could explicitly direct him to proceed immediately without coming to the capital. Zuo’s appointment contained just such a direction.

That was the outward explanation. There was another reason beneath it. Travelling from the southeast to his new northwestern post by way of the capital, then through Shanxi into Shaanxi and Gansu, would not have been especially troublesome. Moreover, the Hui rebellion had persisted for some time and did not demand the immediate suppression required by a newly emerging danger. The real reason for preventing an audience was that the Grand Council, boards, and other offices had already experienced Zuo’s methods. He demanded money and men without end. Once in the capital, he might refuse to leave for his post until every demand was met. Why invite that difficulty? Better simply to keep him away. The transfer edict reached Fuzhou more than twenty days later. Zuo was then preparing extensive lists of recommendations for rewards. Those who had helped suppress the remaining rebels in Fujian and Guangdong naturally expected a place; those who had not exerted themselves sought influence and intercession by every means to have their names added. Fuzhou filled with official hats and carriages and became extraordinarily lively. When news of Zuo’s transfer spread, the men awaiting appointment in Fujian who had already secured the necessary connections for military promotions were deeply disappointed. Their patron had not fallen, but he had moved away, leaving them without support they could use.

Hu was also in Fuzhou. To reward his service in supplying arms, provisions, and military funds from Shanghai, Zuo had prepared a separate memorandum and waited for his arrival before sending it with a memorial. It specially recommended Hu for advancement. A recommendation submitted individually, outside the general list, was called confidential and differed in effect from an open recommendation by list. Its language was naturally emphatic. Hu Guangyong, it said, held the honorary rank of judicial commissioner and was eligible for appointment as a circuit intendant in Fujian. Since Zuo’s entry into Zhejiang, he had handled every commission satisfactorily. After Hangzhou’s recovery, his work in local reconstruction had been highly effective. His devotion to public causes, loyalty, and sincere exertions far exceeded ordinary achievements in relief. As Zuo moved from Zhejiang to Fujian and Guangdong, Hu had repeatedly arranged arms and provisions, sending continuous shipments that invariably arrived when required and met the army’s needs. Zuo therefore requested an exceptional reward to encourage such service: might Hu be granted the honorary rank of provincial treasurer?

Advancement was naturally what Hu desired. Yet what particularly excited him was not the title of provincial treasurer itself, but the change of hat insignia it permitted. His existing honorary rank, judicial commissioner, belonged to the upper third grade and carried a bright blue finial. Provincial treasurer belonged to the lower second grade: he could wear a red one. For a man who had entered officialdom by purchase, attaining a red finial was extremely difficult. For a merchant, it was almost unheard of, apart from one or two salt merchants specially favoured in the Qianlong reign. Remembering the source of his good fortune, Hu reflected that, although Zuo had recommended the honour he was about to receive, without Wang Youling there could have been no present success. He therefore made a special visit to Wang’s ancestral home. Providing for Wang’s surviving family was his first concern at every festival and New Year; this visit was solely to remember the old friendship, weep, and offer sacrifice. He had hoped to visit the grave as well, but an important undertaking kept him desperately busy. That would have to wait until the official business was finished.

The undertaking was the plan to build steamships themselves. Zuo possessed an iron determination: once he had set his mind on something, he could not rest until it was put into practice. His orders to direct the Fujian campaign had prevented personal attention to the project, but he had not abandoned it. He entrusted it to the man he most trusted, Hu. In dealings with foreigners, Hu invariably sought Gu’s help. Gu had wide connections and saw no need to employ Giquel and d’Aiguebelle. In steamships, railways, and firearms, he thought, the United States was catching up with and surpassing the older Western powers. Americans also seemed easier to deal with: in his view, they lacked the cunning of the British, the arrogance of the French, the stubbornness of the Germans, and the deviousness of the Japanese. Hu thought differently. Britain possessed the greatest foreign influence in China, France the next. Only France could be used to check Britain. Americans and British were of the same stock, he reasoned, so cooperation with Americans would in effect help British expansion. Moreover, Giquel and d’Aiguebelle had originated the proposal. To abandon them without cause would be dishonourable.

There was another reason Hu did not mention, though Gu, after their years together, could infer it. Zuo’s rivalry with Li over power and advantage had almost become irreconcilable. Li’s dealings with foreigners relied heavily upon the British Inspector General of Customs, Hart. If Zuo consulted the British too, the matter would probably pass through Hart’s hands. Hart and Li supported one another; Hart might well advise Prince Gong and Wenxiang, who directed foreign affairs, that steamship construction should be entrusted to Liangjiang. Would that not open the way for Li? Gu therefore offered no further proposal and devoted himself to assisting Hu in negotiations with Giquel and d’Aiguebelle. In practice, they dealt only with Giquel, since d’Aiguebelle had left military service and returned to France. He arranged there for the shipyard plans, drawings, and associated material to be prepared and sent to Giquel, who discussed them with Hu and Gu. After more than a year, the scheme had been worked out in considerable detail. When Zuo returned from Guangdong, Hu immediately accompanied Giquel to Fuzhou. The clarity of the drawings and explanations delighted Zuo. He personally inspected the proposed site near Luoxing Pagoda at Mawei, by the approach to Fujian’s coastal waters. Clear water and firm ground made it suitable for slipways and docks, while proximity to the provincial capital made supervision convenient. He approved it as soon as he saw it.

The remaining problem was finance. Building the yard, purchasing machinery, and hiring foreign specialists would require more than three hundred thousand taels in initial expenditure. Once production began, materials and wages would cost fifty or sixty thousand a month, or six to seven hundred thousand a year. The first ship was expected after two years, by which time one and a half million taels would have been spent. Thereafter costs would fall: the total for five years was estimated at a little over three million. Raising that sum would naturally be troublesome. Zuo’s idea was to begin first. A million taels would carry the project through its first year. Once it had gone too far to abandon, the court would have to find a means of continuing it. And with Hu at hand, some way to sustain it could surely be devised. Zuo made a rough calculation. He controlled the allocation of Fujian Customs revenue and provincial likin. Zhejiang lay within his jurisdiction and would not stand aside. Jiang in Guangdong owed his advancement entirely to Zuo and should contribute all the more readily. With those three sources, the undertaking could proceed boldly.

In the middle of the fifth month, Zuo submitted an initial account of the proposed purchase of machinery, recruitment of foreign artisans, and experimental construction of steamships. At the same time, responding to a request for opinions, he recommended wagon warfare against the Nian rebels and military agricultural colonies for the campaign against the Hui, where provisions otherwise had to be transported over immense distances. The court’s reply showed little enthusiasm for the wagons or the agricultural proposal. Steamship construction, however, was declared an urgent necessity of the day. Funds could be drawn in suitable amounts from Fujian Customs, with provincial likin available if those proved insufficient. The proposed measures were approved, and Zuo was instructed to submit detailed further memorials on matters not yet settled. With that authority, he set to work vigorously. He instructed Hu to negotiate the details with d’Aiguebelle, now in Annam, through Giquel, who had been transferred to the Jianghan Customs at Hankou. Giquel travelled through the summer heat and reached Fuzhou early in the seventh month. His first task was to survey the site. He selected ground below Mount Mawei, where the water reached twelve zhang in depth when the tide stood level. They then discussed construction, workmen, and finance. With broad agreement reached, they drew up a preliminary contract. At Hu’s suggestion, the French consul-general in Shanghai, Brenier, was to act as guarantor. Arranging that naturally fell to Hu as well.

Late in the eighth month, d’Aiguebelle came directly from Annam to Fuzhou. On meeting Zuo, he raised no objection to the preliminary contract, but disputed the site. The ground below Mount Mawei, he said, had formed from deposited silt and was not firm enough for foundations. Zuo decided to invite Brenier and Giquel to Fuzhou for a final, comprehensive discussion, after which the plan could be settled and formally submitted. He first wrote asking Hu to come. Just as his enthusiasm was at its height, the edict transferring him to Shaanxi and Gansu arrived. He found it abrupt, but a little reflection showed it to be a natural outcome of events, not wholly unexpected. Recalling the many historical campaigns that had pacified the Western Regions, he felt his ambition rise and was eager for the challenge. Hu, however, was deeply disappointed and anxious, almost at a loss. When he called at the governor-general’s office to offer congratulations, his outward composure seemed unchanged, but those who knew him well could see beneath it. One was his fellow townsman Wu Guanli, courtesy name Ziru and literary name Gui’an. A provincial examination graduate, Wu was brilliantly gifted and wrote excellent poetry. Hu had recommended him for Zuo’s staff, where he had won profound trust and become head of the military affairs office. He was the one secretary in the governor-general’s establishment who participated in military strategy, a counsellor directing events from behind the scenes.

Wu understood aspects of Zuo that Hu could not, a difference arising from the extent of their reading. Seeing dejection in Hu’s face, he readily guessed his thoughts. “Xueyan, are you afraid that, once Lord Zuo goes northwest, you will lose your patron?” The question was direct, and Hu answered honestly. “Yes. Both public and private business will be difficult for me.” Wu shook his head vigorously. “Not at all!” In his view, a western expedition beyond the passes would take three to five years to show results. It was a major campaign, certainly, but differed from the struggle against the Nian, who roamed the central plains and threatened the approaches to the capital. Even if not a disease at the heart, they were a danger close at hand. The court could not rest until they were removed, and commanders had to achieve success within a fixed time. No delay could be tolerated. The western war, by contrast, would be fought on a distant frontier, beyond the court’s anxious attention morning and evening. With less immediate pressure, official business would be easier. As for private matters, these meant Hu’s own enterprises. A patron nearby in the southeast could protect him and smooth his path; one far beyond the passes might be difficult to reach in an emergency. But Wu thought that fear excessive too. “You must understand that campaigns in the northwest have always depended upon support from the southeast. Your duties in Shanghai will grow more important, and your standing with them. What happens depends on what a man makes of it.” He patted Hu’s shoulder. “You have not read the Record of Imperial Military Achievements and do not know Qianlong’s Ten Great Campaigns. In the past, frontier enterprises were entrusted to imperial relatives or great Manchu officials. Giving one to our Lord Zuo is no ordinary matter. Among the great commanders distinguished since the Taiping uprising, Lord Zeng has stood above the rest, with Zuo and Li next. From now on, Zeng and Zuo may well be spoken of together.” That last sentence awakened Hu. His anxiety vanished. His patron would be farther away, but greater and more firmly established. Once he understood that, there was no reason to fret.

“Thank you for enlightening me,” Hu said happily. “Let me trouble you further. Please explain the situation in the northwest in detail.” “First let us discuss the steamships,” Zuo said firmly. “However urgently the court presses me to go beyond the passes, I shall not leave Fujian until this undertaking has been settled under my own hand.” “Yes, sir,” Hu replied. “Once it is settled, to whom will you entrust it?” “Exactly! You have reached the crucial point. I have cherished this ambition for three years and worked hard for months to bring it this far. If I entrust it to the wrong man and it is abandoned halfway, I shall never be reconciled to it. I am still considering that. For the present, I must ask you to take more trouble.” “That goes without saying. But I can only attend to it until you leave Fujian.” “No. You must continue after I leave,” Zuo said. “The shipyard will remain your concern. I will never abandon this halfway and give Li Shaoquan occasion to laugh. I have considered it countless times: if it succeeds, it will surpass his foreign undertakings many times over.” His competitive motive was plain. No advice could diminish his desire to excel. Hu knew that he could not escape the burden; he must perform his part, however crushing the weight upon his head. Yet the crucial question remained unanswered, and he had to ask again. “Then I can only obey. But whom shall I have to serve in Fujian?” “Do not speak of serving. Xueyan, you are exceptionally clever. There is no one you cannot get on with. It is precisely because I shall entrust the work to this man that I need you still more—” He broke off. Hu understood perfectly: the chosen man would be difficult to serve. He was all the more eager to know. “Who is it?” “Shen Youdan.”

It was Shen Baozhen, the former governor of Jiangxi, now at home observing mourning. Hu was genuinely surprised. On reflection, the choice was sound, but the regulations forbade a man to serve as an official—particularly a high official—in his native province. Mourning created a further obstacle. What was Zuo’s intention? Hu remained silent. “I know what you are thinking. Let me show you a draft memorial.” The lengthy document argued the advantages of shipbuilding before reaching its principal point. The undertaking was imperative, it said; Zuo’s approaching departure from Fujian could not justify setting it aside. He and the foreign officers had negotiated the many complicated arrangements for establishing the works. Unless these were settled while he remained, his successor would face tangled affairs without anyone to consult; unclear agreements might also cause later controversy for which Zuo could not evade responsibility. He therefore had to remain another thirty days to bring the arrangements to completion. A successor must be chosen, but above all he must be able to remain at the work for a long time. Continuity would steady everyone’s purpose, make success attainable, and allow deeper study to bring greater mastery. After repeated consideration, Zuo could think only of Shen Baozhen. In office and at home, Shen had long enjoyed a reputation for integrity admired throughout the realm. His careful, exact judgment was already known to the emperor. At present he was at home attending to his family and might remain there for many years, unlike officials liable to frequent transfer. His high local reputation would strengthen his commitment to the enterprise. If given charge, he would certainly see it established. Yinggui and Xu Zonggan agreed. Zuo had visited him three times to discuss the matter, but Shen had repeatedly declined. Might the emperor, considering the project’s importance and near completion, graciously urge him to accept the public duty and appoint him director-general of shipbuilding? An official seal should be issued by the appropriate board, and he should have authority to memorialize directly on all shipbuilding matters, preventing obstruction by others.

The necessary funds would be obtained as required in consultation with the general, governor-general, and governor. Acting Provincial Treasurer Zhou Kaixi was to ensure that there was no delay. Hu Guangyong was to manage all materials, the engagement of foreign artisans and Chinese workers, and the opening of the technical school. Hu, the memorial explained, was capable, meticulous, experienced in foreign affairs, indispensable to the shipbuilding establishment, and already trusted by the foreigners. “Good! I entrust it to you.” Zuo rose and moved towards his writing table. “Now we must discuss the first great task.”

CHAPTER ELEVEN

The first great task was the western expedition. Any major campaign began with two questions: troops and funds. For several nights Zuo had remained awake, thinking beneath the lamp, and had written an outline of his plans. He now took it from a drawer and asked Hu to study it. The outline dealt first with troops, then with finance. Military arrangements had to suit local conditions. The terrain of the northwest differed completely from that of the southeast. Southern soldiers would find the food unfamiliar and the cold intolerable. The effective commanders and units with whom Zuo had fought across the southeast, particularly those recruited in Fujian and Guangdong, could therefore not be taken northwest. He intended to bring only three thousand and more men. Liu Dian, formerly assistant director of military affairs in Fujian and now recommended for the corresponding duties in Shaanxi and Gansu, would return to Hunan to recruit another three thousand men and lead them west. These six thousand and more would form Zuo’s personal force. The main fighting army he intended to raise locally, making common cause with the able men of Guanzhong.

At this point Hu exclaimed, “Your Excellency, if you proceed in this way, when can the Hui rebellion be suppressed?” “I do not quite understand you.” “Consider, sir. Recruiting an army is not the work of a day, and training it into an elite force is harder still. It will take a year or two.” “A year or two? Establishing the Western Regions will require no less than ten years.” “Ten years!” Hu was startled. “That would take—” He stopped, but Zuo knew he meant the cost. Smiling, he said, “Do not be anxious. I intend to establish agricultural colonies in the northwest. That is the way to lasting peace. As with the shipyard, one must not seek an immediate return. Once the results appear, you will see that I have planned correctly.” Hu set the outline down and bent his head in thought. “What are you considering?” “A long way ahead. I too am thinking eight or ten years into the future.” “Exactly!” Zuo clapped his hands in delight. “Our thoughts coincide. We must plan carefully and find provisions and pay for eight or ten years.” Hu did not answer immediately. He picked up the outline and continued reading until he understood the broad scheme. Zuo intended to train cavalry, build two-wheeled gun carriages, and establish a central bureau for agricultural colonies. The colonies would need implements, seed, carts, horses, and advances for all food and incidental expenses until the first harvest. The amount was considerable. “Your Excellency, cavalry and gun carriages are necessary for victory, and the court will certainly approve them. But might it regard the agricultural colonies as a matter that can wait?”

“That is where you do not understand. There are still many men of learning at court. They will understand.” Hu reddened, but spoke sincerely. “Yes, sir. I do not fully understand. Please instruct me.” Zuo outlined the restrictions upon campaigning in the Western Regions. Since the Qin and Han dynasties, armies had gone west in early spring and returned in autumn. When autumn came and the horses were fat, the enemy held the advantage; the severe cold that followed was also beyond the endurance of troops from within the passes. “Because of these difficulties, Emperor Wu of Han won victories almost every year against the Xiongnu in the early Yuanshuo period, yet had to send an army out again every year. The grass was cut, but the roots remained—a burden without end.” After citing precedents, Zuo returned to the present. “The Hui rebellion presents much the same problem. I could choose twenty or thirty thousand good troops, take them beyond the passes in spring, pursue the enemy through the summer, and return in autumn, as Wei Qing and Huo Qubing did. But would that settle the rebellion?” “Naturally not.” Hu understood. “As the verse says, wildfire cannot burn it all away; the spring wind brings it back. Only by thoroughly turning over the ground can the weeds be prevented from returning.” “Precisely! Your comparison is apt.” Zuo was pleased. “If you understand my purpose, I can rest easy. You will obtain what I need.” Praise from Zuo was precious, but it was also a weighty burden. Hu knew that he was being asked to bear the principal responsibility for raising funds. After concentrated thought, he felt that so great and complicated a matter required some definite answers first.

“Your Excellency, how many battalions do you intend to train?” “That is difficult to say. I must see the circumstances beyond the passes.” The very first question thus became a difficulty. Without the number of men, monthly expenditure could not be calculated. Hu could only estimate: at fifty thousand men, with pay, food, clothing, weapons, tents, cooking utensils, and other expenses kept within five taels per man, the total would be two hundred and fifty thousand a month. He asked a second question. “You will take six thousand men beyond the passes?” “Yes. About sixty-five hundred. Thirty-five hundred will set out from Fujian and Zhejiang. Another three thousand, once recruited in Hunan, will go directly west.” “What about travelling expenses? Would ten taels a man suffice?” “I think it should.” “That is sixty-five thousand, needed immediately.” Hu asked his third question. “How much cavalry do you propose to train?”

“I have never commanded cavalry and do not yet know its battalion organization very well. My preliminary intention is three thousand men.” “Then at least three thousand horses will be needed,” Hu said. “They must be bought at Zhangjiakou. That money is available; I can advance it.” “You have money in Zhangjiakou?” “Yes. A hundred thousand taels, originally intended for furs and medicinal materials. I shall have to use it for horses first.” “Excellent.” Zuo was delighted. “Then I can send a purchasing officer at once.” “Let me know whom you appoint, and I shall send someone with him.” Hu continued, “What about the two-wheeled gun carriages? How many?” “Like Han Xin with soldiers, the more the better. Beyond the passes the country is vast. In addition to swift cavalry, guns on carriages can sweep the ground clear at a stroke. There is no better instrument for destroying the enemy’s strongholds.” Hu’s heart grew heavy. He had often heard of government troops who cared nothing for civilian lives, turned their guns upon villages, and bombarded indiscriminately. Bandits hiding there might be killed, wounded, or driven out, but many innocent inhabitants suffered with them.

Most of the Western firearms and artillery in Zuo’s army had been purchased by Hu in Shanghai. Follow the matter back to its origin, and he himself had unknowingly contributed to wrongdoing. His mother had remonstrated with him countless times over his procurement of weapons that killed people. He was a devoted son and obeyed her in every detail of family affairs. Only in public business did he feel unable to follow her wishes. Fortunately, she understood: it was not deliberate disobedience, but a situation from which he saw no escape. She could only do her utmost to make amends for his “sins.” Besides her customary worship, she provided medical treatment, medicines, and cooling tea in summer, padded clothing and rice vouchers in winter. Anyone seeking help to repair bridges or roads, care for the old, or relieve the poor received a generous promise. Yet however many good deeds she performed, buying birds to release could not compensate for human lives. Every mention of arms purchases saddened her. Hearing Zuo speak so enthusiastically now, Hu saw his mother’s troubled face in his mind and remained silent.

“What is it?” Zuo naturally did not understand. “Do you think building the gun carriages will be difficult?” “No. I am wondering how many will be required and what each will cost. I cannot draw up that budget.” “That comes later. For now, we must allow a rough amount. I should like to set aside two hundred thousand taels.” “And the agricultural colonies? How much must be raised for those?” “Harder still to say. Fortunately, the colonies are not an undertaking of merely three or five years, and their burden will steadily diminish. I think five hundred thousand taels, used as a revolving fund, will certainly suffice.” Hu calculated silently for a moment, then exclaimed, “This is tremendous! Tremendous!” “How so?” “Let me reckon it for you.” He counted on his fingers. “Travelling expenses, sixty thousand. Horses with saddles and bridles, say a hundred and twenty taels each: three thousand horses make thirty-six thousand. Gun carriages, two hundred thousand. Half the agricultural fund to begin with, two hundred and fifty thousand. Pay and provisions for fifty thousand men at five taels per month, two hundred and fifty thousand monthly, or three million a year. Altogether, three million five hundred and forty thousand. That is what must be raised for the first year.” Zuo too was stunned. Raising three million five hundred and forty thousand taels was no easy matter. Even half—one million seven or eight hundred thousand—was a formidable sum.

“And with transport so difficult in the northwest, we must make generous allowances everywhere. The money has to be secured before you take it with you. This is not like Fuzhou and Shanghai, only two days apart by sea, where I can somehow provide assistance in an emergency. Thousands of miles away, amid snow and ice, if you run short of provisions and cannot make yourself heard, what use will frantic anxiety be?” “Exactly. That is precisely my thought. Xueyan, we must raise it beforehand. If not enough for a year, then at least enough to prevent shortage for six months.” “Provided there is a dependable source of military funds, I shall do everything I can to arrange advances and subsequent receipts.” Hu then explained why the western army could never be allowed even a temporary interruption. Elsewhere, a gap could be filled from likin, land revenue, or customs receipts, or at worst by an accommodation with a neighbouring province. The northwest was barren and poor, leaving no room to move money around. Only Shanxi offered a possible neighbouring resource, and that was limited. Communications were difficult; transporting silver might itself take a month. If funds failed and hungry soldiers mutinied, the situation could become irretrievable. Zuo had foreseen this in his own careful deliberations. He fully agreed that they must first secure funds guaranteed to arrive without a tael missing or a day’s delay: the contributions assigned to the various provinces for Gansu. Here he felt pleased with his foresight. Without driving out his relative by marriage, Guo Songtao, he would have had only Fujian and Zhejiang as bases of support. Now wealthy Guangdong was included. Those three provinces must be the starting point.

Fujian came first. It had been contributing forty thousand taels a month towards Zuo’s Zhejiang troops, with another ten thousand from Fujian Customs. After the Taiping remnants were cleared, the forty thousand had been reassigned to Gansu. It now naturally came under Zuo’s control. The customs contribution, however, had already been allocated to the shipyard. Since that was his own initiative, he could not reverse himself and had to relinquish those ten thousand. Next came Zhejiang. When Yang Yuebin became governor-general of Shaanxi and Gansu with responsibility for the western campaign, Zeng Guofan had helped arrange funds and assigned Zhejiang twenty thousand a month. In the tenth month of the previous year, Zuo had brought his army into Guangdong and achieved his plan of feeding it there. Under Hu’s indirect pressure, he had then had to honour his promise to relieve Zhejiang’s burden. That left Zhejiang with an additional hundred and forty thousand a month. Ma Xinyi, mindful of the wider situation, voluntarily increased the Gansu subsidy by thirty thousand, making fifty thousand monthly. “Zhejiang has treated me well,” Zuo said. “Ma Gushan is a fine man. Fifty thousand a month is no small contribution. But”—he paused—“there are two other sums already budgeted in Zhejiang. Taking those would add nothing to its burden. What do you think?” “That depends on their original destination.” “One is the promised shipbuilding contribution, ten thousand a month. The shipyard is now funded entirely from Fujian Customs and likin. Zhejiang’s ten thousand could be redirected to Gansu.” This was an indirect way of increasing Fujian’s burden. Hu found it privately amusing: Zuo’s calculations could be sharper than a petty merchant’s. But so long as Zhejiang did not suffer, he had no reason to object. “I do not think Governor Ma will oppose that.”

“The other ten thousand supports Lord Zeng’s cavalry. I think that should come to me too. Consider the reason.” “Lord Zeng originally assigned Jiangsu thirty thousand a month for Gansu, but I hear the full sum is never remitted. Do you propose to offset Zhejiang’s ten thousand against what Jiangsu owes?” “Precisely. It costs Zeng nothing, so why should the accounts not be adjusted?” On the merits, how could it be said to cost Zeng nothing? Hu wanted to advise against displeasing him for the sake of ten thousand taels. Then he reflected that any such advice would make Zuo launch another tirade against Zeng, bringing their serious business to a halt. He swallowed the words.

Next came Guangdong. Its existing contribution to Gansu was only ten thousand; another ten thousand was intended for shipbuilding. Following the Zhejiang precedent, both could go to Gansu, making twenty thousand. Zuo hoped to double that to forty thousand, matching Fujian. “That can certainly be done,” Hu said. “Governor Jiang owes his advancement to you and should exert himself for public and personal reasons alike. There is no time to lose; you should write at once.” “That hardly matters. Jiang Xiangquan cannot refuse me. We may include it in the budget now.” “Fujian forty thousand, Zhejiang seventy thousand, Guangdong forty thousand, and Jianghaiguan Customs another thirty thousand. That gives a dependable hundred and eighty thousand a month, only two million one hundred and sixty thousand a year. The gap is still large.” “There are others, of course. The Ministry of Revenue has designated seven further provinces. I shall speak in person to the authorities in Jiangxi, Hubei, and Henan while passing through on my way west. Jiangsu, Henan, Sichuan, and Shandong must wait until I reach Shaanxi. Taking everything together, I think we can certainly count on two million four hundred thousand a year.” “Then I shall raise half of that in advance for you,” Hu said as though it were a casual matter. “Half?” Zuo wondered if he had heard correctly. “Half is one million two hundred thousand taels.” “Yes. I shall have it ready for you to take.” “But—” Zuo scarcely knew what to say. “Where will you find such an enormous sum?” “I have a method. Naturally, it will require your approval. Once I have worked out the arrangements, I shall explain them.” Zuo could not press further. He was doubtful, but more confident than doubtful. Remembering that Hu had fulfilled every promise he had made, he felt reassured and pleased.

“When will you leave, sir, and when do you expect to pass beyond the frontier gates?” “I intend to leave early in the eleventh month. I shall travel slowly, discussing affairs with governors and governors-general along the way. I shall probably not reach the capital until the year’s end.” “The capital? Did the edict not direct you to proceed straight to the west?” “That was hardly the intention of Their Majesties. It was certain men using the authority of the realm to command its governors. They fear I shall cause trouble in the capital. I mean to be a nuisance to them. Before leaving, I shall request an audience. I do not believe the two empress dowagers will refuse.” Zuo paused. “As for passing beyond the gates, I cannot fix the date yet. Next spring at the earliest.” “Then we have another three or four months. The million two hundred thousand will be ready before you go. For immediate needs, I can still arrange two or three hundred thousand.” “Excellent! Xueyan, settle it as soon as you can, so that I may rest easy.” Hu needed no such reminder. He too wished to settle the matter quickly. Although he gave his plan an eight-in-ten chance, the silver was not yet in his hands. The proverb about a cooked duck flying away was an exaggeration, but where money was concerned, even an agreed arrangement could change at the last moment. A million two hundred thousand was no trifling sum. The western expedition’s success, and whether Zuo might rise from his noble title to a grand secretaryship, depended upon it. If the plan failed just before completion, how could Hu answer for it? He deeply regretted forgetting the warning: a full bowl of rice is easy to eat, but a sweeping promise is hard to fulfil. Since he could not retreat, he must throw all his strength into carrying it through.

The difficulty was that Hu could not yet get away. In their dealings with Chinese officials, Giquel and d’Aiguebelle came to him about everything, from the shipyard scheme to the smallest details of their daily lives. Zuo, for his part, was divided between trust and suspicion of foreigners. He also feared that, if he said certain things himself, their bluntness might lead them to contradict him outright, injuring his dignity and authority. An intermediary such as Hu, who could convey his meaning tactfully, was indispensable. It was a predicament. Hu turned it over in his mind without finding an answer and sat lost in thought. Zuo had never seen him like this. He was surprised, but could hardly ask what was wrong. Host and guest sat facing each other in silence. Even the guards below the hall were astonished: normally these two would be laughing and talking without pause. Why were they now staring blankly at each other? At last a trusted guard approached. “Will His Excellency Hu be staying for a simple meal?”

Hu came to himself. “No, no, thank you,” he said at once. “I must go to the wharf to see someone off.” “Who is it?” Zuo asked. “Brown, the Fuzhou Customs Commissioner.” “Ah, he is going to Shanghai.” “Yes. Brenier, the French Consul General in Shanghai, has asked to see him on official business.” “What business? Could it concern the shipyard?” A thought flashed through Hu’s mind. He nodded. “It might.”

“Then we must be careful,” Zuo said. “Foreigners have plenty of tricks. When Giquel and d’Aiguebelle first took up this project, they bypassed their consul general and approached people in France directly. Naturally Brenier was displeased. Now every contract requires his signature. He may obstruct us.” “Your Excellency has considered the matter with great foresight. You are quite right. I think—” Hu hesitated deliberately. “But it cannot be done. Never mind.” “What cannot be done?” Zuo asked. “It would be best if I went too and kept close to Brown. Only I cannot leave my work here.” Stroking his short, greying moustache, Zuo considered this for a while. “If you went and came back quickly,” he said slowly, “it need not interfere.”

Hu brightened immediately. “Yes! I shall follow Your Excellency’s instructions and return as quickly as possible. If Brown’s business has nothing to do with the steamships, I can be back in Fuzhou within three to five days.” “Very well. Go, then. I have many important matters still to discuss with you. Above all, until that million two hundred thousand taels is secured, I shall not have a day’s peace.” This time Hu dared make no sweeping promises. He merely agreed to return at the earliest opportunity. His only anxiety in Fuzhou concerned Giquel and d’Aiguebelle, who were already thinking of withdrawing. If difficulties arose without anyone to mediate, he feared an outright breach. Zuo told him not to worry and gave him his personal assurance that he would reason with them kindly and do his best to reassure them. Any differences they could not resolve would be set aside until Hu returned. Only with that promise could Hu feel at ease. He hurried back to his lodgings, packed, and reached the wharf in time to sail directly for Shanghai on Brown’s ship. His first business on arrival was a confidential visit to Gu Yingchun.

Gu’s career had taken another turn in recent years: he had become comprador of the British firm HSBC. Known in Chinese by a phonetic rendering of the foreign term, the comprador was the head of the bank’s Chinese staff. Nominally he managed accounts and miscellaneous business; in practice, every dealing with Chinese people, from relations with officials to the hiring of labourers, came under his charge. Chinese customers likewise had to go through him when doing business with a foreign bank. Gu consequently wielded considerable authority at HSBC. Able and industrious, he had won his foreign employers’ complete confidence; they listened to his advice. That was why Hu came to him first. “I want to invite the managers of several foreign banks to dinner,” he began. “Make me a list, will you?” “Little Uncle,” Gu asked, “is this about the shipyard?” “No. I want to borrow money from them.”

Ordinarily Hu could borrow a hundred or two hundred thousand taels from a foreign bank on his word alone. A special dinner invitation suggested a much larger sum. Gu thought for a moment, took out a directory of foreign firms for the fourth year of Tongzhi, and turned to the banks. “Shall we invite all ten?” Hu looked down the list: 1. Agra Bank; 2. Lizhong Bank; 3. Lishang Bank; 4. Huiquan Bank; 5. the Chartered Bank; 6. Huilong Bank; 7. the Mercantile Bank; 8. the French Bank; 9. HSBC; 10. the Oriental Bank. Now he hesitated. They were all called foreign banks, but their nationalities differed. Britain and France in particular were constantly manoeuvring against each other to extend their influence; Zuo’s shipbuilding project had sharpened their rivalry. Invited together, they might become suspicious and refuse to speak openly. Would that not waste everyone’s time? “What about inviting them separately?” he asked. “Certainly. But, Little Uncle, I would invite only those who can help. If we settle it with them at one sitting, we need not bother with the others.” “Which ones would be useful, then?” Without hesitation Gu marked numbers five, seven, and nine. Hu had expected as much. Gu would naturally include HSBC; with HSBC came the Chartered and Mercantile Banks, both British institutions with close connections to it.

HSBC, however, was not an exclusively British bank. Originally named the Hongkong and Shanghai Banking Company Limited, it had established its head office in Hong Kong in the third year of Tongzhi, with a capital of five million Hong Kong dollars. Investors included the British firms Jardine Matheson and Renji, the American firm Qichang, and merchants from Germany and the Middle East. Chinese merchants also held shares. Gu was among them, and this connection had helped him obtain the comprador’s position at the Shanghai branch. That branch opened a year after the head office. Its manager, a Briton named McLean, had known Gu for years and considered him capable and trustworthy. Gu’s position as a shareholder provided a further reason to engage him. His first suggestion after taking office concerned the bank’s name. A literal rendering of “Hongkong and Shanghai Bank” was accurate enough, but Chinese custom required a business, however small, to have an auspicious name. To name a bank after a place—indeed, two places—struck people as puzzling. Add “Shanghai Branch” after “Hongkong and Shanghai Bank,” and the result became more awkward still. Such an ungainly name could hardly become a celebrated commercial signboard.

McLean readily accepted the suggestion. Following the Chinese preference for auspicious associations, they adopted the name Hongkong and Shanghai Huifeng Bank, shortened to Huifeng Bank or simply Huifeng: “abundant remittances.” It sounded clear and resonant in both northern and southern speech, with little variation in pronunciation. The Chinese name Maijiali, used for the Chartered Bank, was less fortunate: its first two syllables sounded different in Shanghai from the way northerners pronounced them. Gu’s second proposal concerned the shareholders. Different nationalities brought different interests, producing disagreements and mutual obstruction that could prevent the bank from acting effectively. He therefore urged that British interests should predominate, gradually buying out shareholders of other nationalities, while alliances with friendly banks would strengthen their collective resources and provide mutual support. McLean welcomed this advice too.

The two banks HSBC cultivated were naturally British: the Chartered and the Mercantile. The Mercantile, established in the fourth year of Xianfeng, was Shanghai’s oldest foreign bank. It was one of Britain’s overseas banks, with its head office in London and branches in Bombay and Shanghai. The Chartered Bank was a joint-stock company authorised by royal charter to establish branches in India, Australia, and Shanghai. Its head office was in London; its Shanghai branch opened in the seventh year of Xianfeng. Cantonese speakers called it “Ja Da,” a phonetic rendering of “chartered.” Shanghai people found that name awkward to pronounce and instead called it Maijiali, after its first manager. It had been established specifically to facilitate British trade with India, Australia, and Shanghai. Thus, besides its dealings with Fukang as a fellow banking concern, it also did business with Hu through his sales to foreign trading houses.

“Those three banks would certainly be useful,” Hu said doubtfully. “I am afraid they may not be enough.” “Not enough?” Only now did Gu realise that, after all this discussion, he still did not know what the matter involved. They had known each other so long and understood each other so well that they had been talking on assumptions. It was rather absurd. He came to the point. “Little Uncle, how much do you want to borrow?” “At least a million two hundred thousand.” “The banks have never advanced such a large sum. For whom? His Excellency Zuo, of course?” “Of course.” “To build steamships?” “No. To pay for the western campaign.” Even Gu, for all his experience and knowledge of Chinese and foreign affairs, was taken aback. “I do not think I have heard of borrowing from foreigners to fight a war,” he said frankly. “Little Uncle, I fear this will be difficult.” “I know it will. But it must be done.” Gu did not argue further. Hu had never shrunk from difficulties; persuasion would be wasted. The only course open to him was to help Hu overcome them, without counting the chances of success or failure. “You must have worked out a scheme,” he said. “How will you borrow and repay? What interest, and for how long? Tell me first, and we can see whether it will work.”

“A million two hundred thousand. The interest can be a little high. A term of one year: interest only for the first six months, then repayment of the principal in six monthly instalments.” “What will you use to repay it?” “The provincial contributions for the western campaign.” Hu counted on his fingers. “Forty thousand from Fujian, forty thousand from Guangdong, seventy thousand from Zhejiang. That makes a hundred and fifty thousand, leaving fifty thousand. We can look to Jianghaiguan Customs for thirty thousand; the remaining twenty thousand can surely be found somehow.” “Little Uncle, that is a calculation of everything going your way. Provincial contributions are unreliable. What if they fall into arrears?” “Fukang will guarantee them.” “No!” Gu shook his head vigorously. “You have no reason to do that. Besides, foreigners like a straightforward arrangement. If Fukang provides the guarantee, they will say, ‘We may as well lend directly to Fukang. So long as you give us security, we do not care what you use the money for.’ You would be taking an enormous risk and attracting far too much attention. It will not do.” Hu considered this and nodded. He was always willing to accept sound advice. “You understand foreign banks and foreign habits far better than I do. What do you suggest? Provided the business can be arranged, I will accept any conditions.” Gu explained, “They handle things somewhat differently from us. We trade on good faith: a spoken promise is enough, and we seldom think very far about the consequences. They value good faith too, but attach greater importance to legal principles. They are also rather suspicious: instead of calculating how things will go well, they first consider how they might go wrong. In a loan, their first questions are whether the borrower will be able to repay and what happens if he cannot. You must settle those two points in your own mind before approaching them.” “I understand. First, repayment is certain. Each province will have a fixed contribution, formally reported to the court. The western campaign is a great undertaking. A province that withholds its remittance and obstructs military operations will incur a serious charge. Besides, His Excellency Zuo has his own men in Fujian, Guangdong, and Zhejiang. They will certainly cooperate. Those three provinces provide a hundred and fifty thousand—three quarters of what we need. There is no cause for concern.” “Very well. I can tell the foreigners that. What about security?”

“If Fukang cannot conveniently guarantee it, His Excellency Zuo must come forward himself.” “He can come forward as the borrower, not as his own guarantor.” “That makes it difficult.” An idea occurred to Hu. “Have the governors and governors-general of the contributing provinces guarantee it. They can issue sealed notes in advance, redeemable at the provincial treasuries when due. Surely that would do?” “Not necessarily. Still, it is a proposal. I doubt the provincial authorities would agree.” “You need not worry about that. His Excellency Zuo can manage it. Nobody is better at invoking the Son of Heaven’s authority to command the regional lords.” “If you are confident, we can begin discussions,” Gu said. “I suggest leaving the dinner until later. Let me first arrange a meeting with HSBC’s chief. How would that be?” Chinese employees commonly called the head of a foreign firm the “big boss,” shortened simply to “boss.” Hu had met McLean before. He was shrewd, but also, as Shanghai people said, a man who understood how things worked: if a proposal made sense, one could usually reach an agreement with him. Hu readily accepted. There were, however, a few more things he needed to explain.

“Old Gu, you know my affairs well enough. But during this past year, with your duties at HSBC, you have lost touch with some of my personal circumstances. I am now a grand gentleman with an empty purse. I have spread myself too far, and some of my troubles cannot be spoken of. In Fujian, cash assistance and advances for arms purchases together leave me two or three hundred thousand taels out of pocket. In Zhejiang, the accounts for handling the provincial treasury’s business are still unsettled. Some items cannot easily be claimed, and I cannot press them without embarrassing His Excellency Zuo. The advances to the Reconstruction Bureau must simply remain outstanding. That is another two or three hundred thousand. Altogether I have about half a million tied up in unpaid accounts. How can I stand it?” “That much outstanding!” Gu was startled. “Spring is almost upon us. Both the silk and tea markets will be busy, and you must advance large sums first. If Fukang cannot turn its funds over then, it would look very bad.” “Look bad? It would kill me! And speaking of appearances, unless I arrange one matter properly before the New Year, the truth will already be exposed. I am a circuit intendant assigned to Fujian. Strictly speaking, I ought never to have administered the northern Zhejiang salt business; it was only defensible because Zhejiang lay under the Fujian–Zhejiang Governor General. Now I am being transferred to serve the Shaanxi–Gansu Governor General. I shall probably be stationed permanently in Shanghai to forward arms, provisions, and military funds to the northwest. I must hand over the northern Zhejiang salt administration. But there is a deficit of over a hundred thousand taels. How can I hand it over without making that good?” “So you need over a hundred thousand before the year ends merely to clear that hurdle.”

“And that is only one item. There are others. Once the New Year begins, Fukang will need half a million just to keep its funds circulating. If this loan goes through and the money is remitted in batches, I can use some temporarily. By next summer, when the silk and tea seasons are over, payment for the goods will come pouring in and I shall have room to move again.” Gu breathed more freely. “Very well,” he said decisively. “I will find a way to make this loan succeed.” “With you helping, it certainly can. Old Gu, I have two more points. First, everything must be kept secret. Not a whisper must escape. One memorial from those censors in the capital could wreck it. Let me tell you: even His Excellency Zuo does not yet know of this method.” Gu was astonished. “Then suppose we negotiate successfully and he says no? Would that not make us ridiculous?” “Do not worry. There will be no such embarrassment. I am absolutely certain he will follow my advice.” “Very well. What is the second point?”

“The second is that, although the loan will be made in the name of foreign merchants, Chinese people willing to lend could take shares in it and earn some additional interest.” “That will depend on circumstances. It is too early to say.” “Just keep it in mind. His Excellency Zuo’s achievements and my own business both depend on this loan.” To preserve secrecy, Gu arranged for McLean to meet Hu at the newly established German Club. They went straight to the subject as soon as they sat down. McLean was a reserved man. Having heard the whole proposal, he neither accepted nor rejected it, but began a series of questions. “What is your court’s view of the matter?”

“Suppressing the Muslim rebellion is regarded in China as a matter of the first importance,” Hu replied through Gu. “If the commander can raise sufficient military funds himself, the court will naturally give him its full support.” “As I understand it, your military commanders each have their own sphere of influence. Lord Zuo’s appears to comprise only Shaanxi and Gansu, which are very poor provinces.” “That is not so.” Hu would not accept this division into personal domains. “The court’s authority extends to every province. Once it approves the loan and the arrangement for the provinces to share repayment, its orders will be obeyed. You need have no anxiety.” “Then why does your government not take out the loan itself?” “When Lord Zuo comes forward, he represents the Chinese government. Negotiations must be conducted between parties of equivalent standing. If our government acted directly, it should discuss the matter with your Treasury. It would not be for us to settle it here.” McLean nodded thoughtfully, then pressed the point. “Lord Zuo represents the Chinese government, and you represent Lord Zuo.
